# Sūrah al-Furqān (Chapter 25) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Named for the Line That Divides**

Sūrah al-Furqān takes its name from the first word of substance after the opening blessing: *al-Furqān*, the Criterion — that which distinguishes, separates, and makes a boundary. The same word is also one of the names of the Qur'an itself. Most commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, judge the sūrah Makkan; some hold that verses 68–70 belong to the Madinan period, while a minority reverse the attribution. The difference does not disturb the chapter's unity. Its argument is one argument from first verse to last: God has sent down a Book that divides truth from falsehood, and He has sent it upon a human messenger whose very humanity the Quraysh could not forgive.

The sūrah opens by blessing the One who sent the Criterion upon His servant, that he might be a warner to the worlds. It then answers, one by one, the objections that clustered around that servant in Makkah: that the Book was a fabrication helped by other people; that it was old fables written down and recited morning and evening; that a true messenger would not eat food or walk in the markets; that treasure or a garden or an accompanying angel should have marked him out; that the Qur'an should have descended as a single whole rather than over years. Each objection is met not with insult but with a principle — about God, about prophecy, about the Hereafter, and about the measured way revelation enters a living community.

Al-Qurṭubī records that the word *furqān* could also be rendered “the Divide,” and that this naming is itself a theology of reading: a believer does not come to this chapter to be entertained, but to be shown a line and asked which side of it he is standing on. Ibn Kathīr, treating the opening verses, notes that the whole of the sūrah’s later portrait of *ʿibād al-Raḥmān* is already implied here. If a Criterion has been sent down, there must be a people it produces. If a warner has been sent to the worlds, there must be a world that either takes the warning or takes the Book for foolishness. The chapter is therefore not two unrelated halves glued together. It is a single movement from the sending of the Book to the character of those who live under it.

**Where This Sūrah Sits in the Book and in the Mission**

In the written muṣḥaf al-Furqān stands as the twenty-fifth sūrah, after al-Nūr and before al-Shuʿarāʾ. The pairing with al-Nūr is not accidental in the reader’s experience. Al-Nūr legislates light into houses, streets, and testimony; al-Furqān names the light as a Criterion and then shows what happens when a town prefers mockery to that light. Al-Shuʿarāʾ, which follows, will unfold the stories of earlier messengers at length — Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, Shuʿayb, Moses — which this sūrah has already named in compressed form in verses 35–40. A reciter who moves through these three chapters in order hears law, then criterion, then the prophetic archive. The Makkan heat of al-Furqān is the middle term: the Book has been identified, the objections have been listed, and the servants of the Compassionate have been described before the long narratives begin.

The Prophet ﷺ’s own life supplies the occasion even where a specific *sabab al-nuzūl* is not attached to every verse. He was an unlettered man of Quraysh; he ate food; he walked in the markets of Makkah; he had no treasure thrown down to him and no palace of stone; he recited a Book that arrived portion by portion over years. Every one of those facts became, in the mouths of his people, a reason not to believe. This sūrah is the Divine reply to that entire cluster of reasons, spoken while the reply still had to be walked, not yet administered from a city-state. That is why al-Qurṭubī and others insist that verse 52’s “strive against them by means of it” is a striving with the Qur'an, not with the sword. The Criterion is still, in this chapter, a recitation that has to win its way into chests.

**A Sūrah of Two Halves: Accusation, Then Character**

The first half of the chapter (roughly verses 1–62) is forensic. It reconstructs the case the disbelievers made against Muhammad ﷺ, then dismantles it by appeal to God's sovereignty, to the history of earlier messengers, to the signs in shade and night and rain and the two seas, and to the coming Hour for which a Blaze has been prepared. Moses and Aaron, Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of al-Rass, and the town upon which the evil rain fell are summoned as witnesses: humanity has rejected human messengers before, and the ruins of that rejection still lie along the trade roads the Quraysh themselves traveled.

The second half (verses 63–77) turns from the accusers to the accused community and describes, with a patience that has made these verses among the most loved in the Qur'an, *ʿibād al-Raḥmān* — the servants of the Compassionate. They walk humbly. They answer ignorance with peace. They pass the night in prostration and standing. They fear Hell without despairing. They spend without waste and without miserliness. They do not kill, fornicate, or take a second god. They repent, and their evil deeds are replaced with good. They do not bear false witness. They pass idle talk with dignity. They hear the signs of their Lord without going deaf and blind. They ask that their families be a comfort and that they themselves be made imams for the reverent. The sūrah that began with a Criterion ends with a portrait of the people the Criterion produces.

Al-Rāzī observes that the Name *al-Raḥmān*, refused by the idolaters in verse 60, is the Name that titles this portrait. The theological point is sharp. A people who will not bow to the Compassionate cannot be described as His servants; a people who will bow are then described not first by their metaphysics but by their gait, their nights, their spending, and their tongues. Doctrine becomes character. Character becomes a *mustaqarr* — a dwelling — in the Garden. The forensic half without the portrait would leave the reader knowing what to reject; the portrait without the forensic half would leave him not knowing why this character is required. Together they are a complete education.

**The Recurring Music of the Chapter**

Several words and images return until they become a way of hearing. *Tabāraka* — Blessed is He — opens verses 1, 10, and 61, blessing the Sender of the Book, the Giver of possible palaces, and the Placer of the lamp and the moon. *Ittakhadha* — to take for oneself — describes the taking of false gods (verse 3), the taking of a way with the messenger that the wrongdoer failed to make (verse 27), the taking of a *fulān* as friend (verse 28), the taking of the Qur'an as abandoned (verse 30), the taking of caprice as a god (verse 43), and the taking of a way unto the Lord that is the only “wage” the Prophet ﷺ is allowed to want (verse 57). A life, this sūrah says, is a series of takings. The Criterion is the rule by which takings are judged.

*Sabīl*, way, is lost by those who multiply descriptions of the Prophet ﷺ (verse 9), wished for too late by the hand-biter (verse 27), further missed by those gathered on their faces (verse 34) and by those who thought the messenger was the one leading them astray (verse 42), and offered still, in verse 57, to whoever wills. *Nushūr*, resurrection, is denied in hope (verses 21, 40), owned by God alone against the idols (verse 3), and then given as a name for ordinary morning (verse 47). *Ḥijran maḥjūrā*, a barrier forbidden, is the cry of the guilty or the angels on the Day the angels are seen (verse 22) and the description of the divide God set between the two seas (verse 53). The same words, two offices: verdict and mercy. A reciter who notices these returns is already doing what verse 62 names as the purpose of night and day: *tadhakkur*, remembrance that connects.

**Prophetic Light on the Sūrah’s Rank**

The Qur'an itself is the *Furqān*, and the Prophet ﷺ spoke of the Book’s protective and intercessory power in terms that include every sūrah a believer holds to. Muslim records:

> *"Recite the Qur'an, for on the Day of Resurrection it will come as an intercessor for its companions."* (Muslim 804)

And he ﷺ described his own office, which this sūrah’s first verse names, in the ḥadīth of the five distinctions:

> *"I have been given five things that were not given to anyone before me… and prophets were sent only to their own people, while I have been sent to all of mankind."* (al-Bukhārī 335; Muslim 521)

Verse 1 is the Qur'anic form of that last clause. The commentary that follows treats that sending, and every objection to it, and every fruit of it in the servants of the Compassionate, as a complete discourse verse by verse. Nothing has been shortened because the chapter is seventy-seven verses rather than two hundred and eighty-six. Al-Baqarah’s own warning against haste in religion is honored here in the manner of the reading: unhurried, sourced, and complete, from *tabāraka alladhī nazzala al-furqān* to *fa-sawfa yakūnu lizāmā*.

**How to Read What Follows**

Each of the seventy-seven verses is treated as an independent writing task. Arabic vocabulary, roots, and morphology are explained in simple English. Qur'anic cross-references are given with the actual text. Authentic Prophetic reports are given with their wording and their collections. The views of al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn Taymiyyah, and others are brought in where they genuinely open the verse. ʿAqīdah, justice, human responsibility, and the Hereafter are developed where the verse is about those things. Psychological, social, and practical implications are not tacked on as slogans; they are the verse’s own logic walked into a life. Unique, context-specific mini-headings mark real turns of thought. Direct quotations appear as blockquotes.

The method is the method required, and the depth is the depth a chapter of this rank deserves: not a summary of al-Furqān, but al-Furqān opened, verse after verse, until the Criterion has been allowed to do what it was sent down to do.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:1

> **Blessed is He Who sent down the Criterion upon His servant that he may be a warner unto the worlds,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Blessing Spoken Over the Act of Revelation**

The sūrah does not open with a command, a story, or a warning. It opens with *tabāraka* — "Blessed is He." The root *b-r-k* carries the sense of growth, increase, and the multiplying of what is good. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both note this lexical range. A thing that is *mubārak* is not merely fortunate; it is a source from which good keeps coming. Applied to God, the word raises a theological caution that al-Qurṭubī records with care: some commentators preferred to hear *tabāraka* as "holy" or "transcendent," lest anyone imagine that God Himself undergoes the change implied by growth. The caution is sound as doctrine. The word, however, is not describing a change in God. It is describing the overflowing of good from God. The blessing is not that God increases; it is that everything that comes from Him increases whoever receives it.

The object of that blessing in this verse is a specific act: *alladhī nazzala al-furqān ʿalā ʿabdihi* — "Who sent down the Criterion upon His servant." Revelation is here named as the first of God's blessed works, before creation is mentioned (that comes in verse 2), before the Hour is mentioned (that comes in verse 11), before the signs in shade and rain (those come later still). The order is instructional. Before the reader is asked to look at the world, he is asked to look at the Book that teaches him how to look.

**What "Sent Down" Already Tells the Listener**

The verb is *nazzala*, the intensive form, used throughout the Qur'an for a sending-down in successive portions. The related verb *anzala* can describe a sending-down as a single event. The choice here is not accidental. This sūrah will later record the objection, "Why was the Qur'an not sent down upon him as a single whole?" (25:32). The first verse has already answered by the form of its verb: the Criterion was sent down as a process, portion upon portion, because that is how a living guidance enters a living people. A book dropped all at once is a monument. A book that arrives as events unfold is a companion.

The destination of that sending-down is *ʿalā ʿabdihi* — "upon His servant." The Prophet ﷺ is not here called messenger, prophet, or seal. He is called servant. The same title opens Sūrah al-Isrāʾ:

> *"Glory be to Him Who carried His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque."* (Qur'an 17:1)

And it is the title by which God addresses him at the height of revelation:

> *"Blessed is He Who sent down the Criterion upon His servant."* (Qur'an 25:1)

Servanthood is not a demotion from prophecy. It is prophecy's inner name. The one who receives the Book does not own it, author it, or stand above it. He carries it as a servant carries what his Master has placed in his hands. Ibn Taymiyyah observed across his writings on *ʿubūdiyyah* that the most honorable name God gives His messengers is the name of service, because it locates them exactly where a creature is most truthful: dependent, obedient, and unconfused about origin.

**Furqān: The Word That Cuts and the Word That Saves**

*Al-Furqān* is from the root *f-r-q*, to separate, to divide, to make a distinction. Al-Qurṭubī explains that it is called this because it separates the believers from the disbelievers and truth from falsehood, and because it teaches believers how to live by delineating what is lawful and what is prohibited. The word can also be rendered "divide" or "boundary": that which distinguishes, and that by which things are distinguished. A criterion is not merely a light. It is a light that judges.

The same word appears at decisive points in the Qur'an. Of Moses:

> *"And We gave Moses the Book and the Criterion, that perhaps you would be guided."* (Qur'an 2:53)

Of the month of the Qur'an's descent:

> *"The month of Ramadan in which the Qur'an was sent down as guidance for mankind, and as clear proofs of the guidance and the Criterion."* (Qur'an 2:185)

Of the day at Badr, when a small, poorly armed community met a larger force and the unseen distinguished them:

> *"If you believe in God and in what We sent down upon Our servant on the Day of the Criterion, the day the two hosts met."* (Qur'an 8:41)

And of Moses and Aaron together, in a verse that this sūrah will later echo:

> *"And We gave Moses and Aaron the Criterion, and a light, and a reminder for the reverent."* (Qur'an 21:48)

Al-Furqān in this opening verse is, by the agreement of the commentators, a name of the Qur'an. But the name is not a synonym chosen for variety. It tells the reader what the Book *does*. It does not flatten the world into one color. It draws a line. In an age that treats all distinctions as violence, the verse quietly insists that some distinctions are mercy: the distinction between true and false, between the worship of the Creator and the worship of what cannot create, between a life measured by the Hour and a life that hopes never to meet it.

**A Warner, Not a Court Entertainer**

The purpose clause is exact: *li-yakūna lil-ʿālamīna nadhīrā* — "that he may be a warner unto the worlds." A warner (*nadhīr*) is not a man who frightens for sport. He is a man who sees a danger the rest have not yet admitted and speaks before the danger arrives. The Qur'an pairs the title constantly with its complement, the bearer of glad tidings (*bashīr*), and this sūrah will itself say in verse 56:

> *"And We sent thee not, save as a bearer of glad tidings and as a warner."* (Qur'an 25:56)

Here, at the opening, only the warning is named. The reason is the audience. Makkah was not refusing good news. It was refusing the news that its gods, its pride, and its way of life stood under judgment. Warning is the first mercy to a people walking toward a cliff. Glad tidings become intelligible only after the cliff has been seen.

The Prophet ﷺ described the office with an image that has never gone stale. Al-Bukhārī (no. 6482) and Muslim record:

> *"My example and the example of the people is that of a man who kindled a fire. When it lit what was around it, moths and other creatures began to fall into it. He began trying to prevent them, but they overcame him and fell in. I am grasping you by your belts away from the Fire, and you are slipping from my hands."* (al-Bukhārī 6482; Muslim 2284)

That is a warner: not a man above the people, but a man with his hands on their belts, pulling against the very appetite that draws them to the flame.

**Unto the Worlds: The Widening of the Address**

*Al-ʿālamīn* can mean the worlds in the widest sense, or, more specifically, the human worlds — nations, collectivities, generations. Al-Qurṭubī records that here it is understood by some to refer to the human beings and the jinn to whom the Prophet ﷺ was sent. Al-Rāzī notes the reading that "warner unto the worlds" means he was sent to all creatures until the Day of Judgment. The Qur'an itself places beside this verse another, still more famous:

> *"And We have not sent thee except as a mercy unto the worlds."* (Qur'an 21:107)

Warning and mercy are not two missions. They are one mission named from two sides. The same man is mercy because he warns, and he warns because he is mercy.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the uniqueness of this widening in a ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (no. 335) and Muslim (no. 521):

> *"I have been given five things that were not given to anyone before me: I have been made victorious by terror for a distance of a month's journey; the earth has been made for me a place of prayer and a means of purification; the spoils of war have been made lawful for me; I have been given intercession; and prophets were sent only to their own people, while I have been sent to all of mankind."* (al-Bukhārī 335; Muslim 521)

The first verse of al-Furqān is the Qur'anic form of that last clause. Previous *furqāns* were given to particular communities. This Criterion is given upon a servant whose warning is addressed to the worlds.

**The Theological Dispute the Verse Does Not Need to Settle**

Al-Rāzī records that certain Muʿtazilite theologians argued from this verse that God desires all creatures to have faith and to do good and avoid evil, since a warning sent to the worlds would be pointless if some were created for unbelief. Ashʿarite theologians countered with:

> *"Certainly We have created for Hell many among jinn and men."* (Qur'an 7:179)

The dispute is real in the history of *kalām*. The verse itself, however, is doing pastoral work, not writing a treatise on predestination. It blesses the Sender, names the Book as Criterion, names the recipient as servant, and names the purpose as warning. Human responsibility is assumed throughout this sūrah: people take gods, fabricate charges, deny the Hour, bite their hands in regret, and — in the closing portrait — walk humbly, spend justly, and repent. A warning that did not assume the hearer's capacity to respond would not be a warning. It would be a decoration.

**What It Means to Live Under a Criterion**

A person who accepts this verse accepts that his life will be measured. The Book is not an ornament for the shelf and not a cultural heirloom. It is a divider. It will tell him, sometimes against his mood and his tribe and his appetite, what is true and what is not. The practical analogue is not a slogan but a habit: before a word is spoken, before a contract is signed, before a desire is fed, the servant asks what the Criterion has already said. Taqwā, in this light, is not a feeling of piety. It is the care of a driver on a mountain road at night, both hands on the wheel, because the drop is real and the markings on the road were painted by someone who has seen the drop.

The sūrah will spend seventy-six more verses showing what that care looks like, and what the refusal of it costs. It begins, however, where every rightly ordered life begins: by blessing the One who did not leave the worlds without a line they could see.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:2

> **He unto Whom belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth, and Who took not a child, and Who has no partner in sovereignty, and Who created everything, then measured it out with due measure.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Clauses That Fence the Throne**

Verse 1 named the blessed Sender of the Criterion. Verse 2 now says who that Sender is, in four clauses that function like four walls around the doctrine of God. He is the One to whom sovereignty (*mulk*) over the heavens and the earth belongs. He took not a child. He has no partner in sovereignty. He created everything, then measured it out with due measure. Each clause closes a door that human religion has, in every century, tried to open.

The first clause is ownership. *Alladhī lahu mulku al-samāwāti wal-arḍ* — "He unto Whom belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth." *Mulk* is kingship, dominion, the right to command and the power to execute. Sūrah al-Mulk opens with the same claim in a different key:

> *"Blessed is He in Whose hand is sovereignty, and He is over all things Powerful."* (Qur'an 67:1)

And elsewhere the Qur'an gathers the whole of existence into a single possessive:

> *"So glory be to Him in Whose hand is the dominion of all things, and to Him you will be returned."* (Qur'an 36:83)

The point is not that God is a larger version of an earthly king. Earthly kings borrow. They inherit thrones, lose them, share them with ministers, and die leaving them to sons. God's *mulk* is underived, unshared, and unending. Verse 2 states this before it denies child and partner, because those denials only make sense once true ownership has been named. You cannot take a partner in what you do not own, and you cannot bequeath what was never a property in time.

**Why "He Took Not a Child" Comes Second**

*Wa-lam yattakhidh waladan* — "and Who took not a child." The verb is *ittakhadha*, "to take for oneself," the same verb the Qur'an uses for the taking of protectors, of idols, of the calf. A child in this sentence is not an accident of biology. It is a theological claim: that something other than God shares His nature, His eternity, or His right to be worshipped. The Qur'an rejects that claim in every direction it has historically taken — the pagan Arabs' "daughters of God," the Christian confession of Jesus as son, and any later metaphysics that inserts an offspring, emanation, or demigod between the Creator and the world.

> *"They say, 'God has taken a child.' Glory be to Him! Rather, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth; all are devoutly obedient to Him."* (Qur'an 2:116)

> *"The Originator of the heavens and the earth — how should He have a child when He has no companion, and He created all things?"* (Qur'an 6:101)

The logic is tight. A child is needed by a parent who will die, who wishes to continue a name, who requires help, or who feels loneliness. None of these conditions touch God. To ascribe a child to Him is therefore not an honor. It is a category error about what God is. Al-Ikhlāṣ will later compress the same four walls into four verses: He is One; He is Self-Sufficient; He neither begets nor is begotten; and none is comparable to Him. Verse 2 of al-Furqān is that confession written in the syntax of sovereignty.

**No Partner in the Kingdom**

*Wa-lam yakun lahu sharīkun fī al-mulk* — "and Who has no partner in sovereignty." If "took not a child" closes the door on a family resemblance between God and creation, "no partner" closes the door on a board of directors. *Shirk*, association, is the Qur'an's name for the oldest religious mistake: admitting God as president while installing other beings as ministers with real portfolios — rain, fertility, victory, intercession, luck.

The denial is not only about statues. It is about jurisdiction. If sovereignty is unshared, then fear, hope, vow, sacrifice, and the last word in a decision belong in one place. The Prophet ﷺ taught this with a precision that reaches the interior life. Ibn ʿAbbās reported:

> *"If you ask, ask of God; and if you seek help, seek help of God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516)

That sentence is verse 2 turned into counsel. A partner in sovereignty would be someone else you could ask when God seemed slow, or someone else you could thank when the gift arrived. The verse allows neither.

**Created, Then Measured: The Two Acts Inside One World**

The fourth clause is the most technically rich: *wa khalaqa kulla shayʾin fa-qaddarahu taqdīrā* — "and Who created everything, then measured it out with due measure." Two verbs, in sequence. *Khalaqa* is to bring into being. *Qaddara* is to assign measure, proportion, limit, and course. The cognate noun *taqdīr* is placed after the verb for emphasis, a cognate accusative that in Arabic means "He measured it with a measuring" — thoroughly, not approximately.

Al-Rāzī notes that human *taqdīr* is an act of estimation: a builder estimates timber, a merchant estimates cost. Because God's knowledge is eternal and unchanging, Divine *taqdīr* is not a guess. It is God's knowledge of a creature's entire existence from beginning to end. Other commentators, among them Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī, emphasize *taqdīr* as ordainment: the bestowal upon each creature of everything it was, is, and will be. In that sense the word neighbors *qadar*, a person's "lot" or measuring-out, the term around which the theological discussions of predestination have turned.

The Qur'an repeats the pairing of creation and measure until it becomes a way of seeing:

> *"Indeed, We have created all things according to a measure."* (Qur'an 54:49)

> *"And He created all things and measured them with precise measure."* (Qur'an 25:2, the present verse)

> *"Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High, Who created, then proportioned, and Who measured, then guided."* (Qur'an 87:1–3)

Measure is why the world is livable. Lungs are measured to air, eyes to light, seeds to seasons, grief to the possibility of patience. A universe that was merely created, without being measured, would be a heap. A universe that is created and then measured is a house. The same doctrine that humbles the human will — you did not assign yourself your term, your gifts, or your hour of death — is the doctrine that makes science, medicine, agriculture, and trust in God possible. There is a grain in things. It can be studied because it was set.

**What This Does to Anxiety and to Pride**

Two human illnesses meet this verse and are treated by it. Pride says: I am self-made. The verse replies: you were created, then measured; the proportions you boast of were given. Anxiety says: nothing holds; tomorrow is chaos. The verse replies: nothing that exists exists without a measure upon it, including the thing you fear. Neither reply invites passivity. The measured world is the world in which work has meaning, because effects follow causes that God has made regular. The Prophet ﷺ held both edges in one sentence when he was asked whether medicine and protective measures change what is written:

> *"They are part of God's qadar."* (recorded by al-Tirmidhī 2148; Ibn Mājah 3437)

Treatment is not a rebellion against measure. It is one of the measures.

A person who recites this verse is being taught to walk through an ordinary day without two illusions: the illusion that he owns his life, and the illusion that his life has no Author who knows its length. Sovereignty without a child and without a partner means there is no committee to appeal to behind God's back. Creation with measure means there is no corner of the day that fell out of His knowledge. Between those two truths a human being can work, repent, plan, and sleep.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:3

> **But they have taken gods apart from Him, who create naught and are themselves created, and who have no power over what harm or benefit may come to themselves, and who have no power over death, or life, or resurrection.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Contrast That Makes Idolatry Look Like a Category Error**

Verse 2 described the One who owns the heavens and the earth, who took no child, who shares the kingdom with no one, who created everything and measured it. Verse 3 opens with *wa-ttakhadhū* — "But they have taken." The subject has shifted from God to the human habit of installing replacements. Al-Rāzī notes that this verse is widely thought to refer to idolaters in particular, and would not necessarily include Christians, whose error the previous verse had already addressed in the denial of a child. The structure is therefore two-sided: verse 2 closes the door on a "son of God"; verse 3 closes the door on gods carved, named, inherited, or imagined.

The taking is active. Idols do not storm the heart. They are adopted. The Qur'an uses the same verb for taking protectors, taking the calf, taking rabbis and monks as lords. Religion, in this diagnosis, is often less a discovery than a preference. A community *takes* what it will serve, and then calls the preference ancient, sacred, or necessary.

**Gods That Cannot Do the One Thing a God Must Do**

The first charge is devastating in its simplicity: *lā yakhluqūna shayʾan wa-hum yukhlaqūn* — "who create naught and are themselves created." A god, if the word means anything, is a source. These are products. The Qur'an repeats the argument until it becomes almost embarrassing to miss:

> *"Do they associate that which creates nothing, while they themselves are created?"* (Qur'an 7:191)

> *"And those they call upon besides God create nothing, and they themselves are created."* (Qur'an 16:20)

The logic does not require philosophy. A child can follow it. That which was made cannot be the Maker. That which depends cannot be the Independent. The idol — whether of stone, of a person, of a nation, of a market, or of the self — is always, in the last analysis, an artifact. Someone shaped it, named it, funded it, or feared it into authority. God, by contrast, is the One verse 2 has just described: uncreated, unpartnered, the measurer of every measure.

**Harm and Benefit: The Test Anyone Can Run**

The second charge is practical: *wa-lā yamlikūna li-anfusihim ḍarran wa-lā nafʿan* — "and who have no power over what harm or benefit may come to themselves." They cannot protect even their own existence. The Qur'an makes this a recurring test of worship:

> *"Say: Do you worship, apart from God, what has no power to benefit or harm you?"* (Qur'an 5:76)

> *"And they worship apart from God that which neither harms them nor benefits them."* (Qur'an 10:18)

> *"Say: Then do you take, apart from Him, protectors who have no power to benefit or harm themselves?"* (Qur'an 13:16)

A being that cannot help itself cannot be asked to help you. The argument is not cruel. It is kind. It saves the worshipper from spending a life at a door that does not open. Abraham said it to his father with the tenderness of a son and the steel of a prophet:

> *"O my father, why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees, nor avails you in anything?"* (Qur'an 19:42)

Modern idolatry often hides the same emptiness behind more sophisticated names. A career that cannot keep a diagnosis from arriving, a following that cannot keep a night of panic from coming, a political savior who cannot keep his own coalition from eating him — these are "gods" with no power over their own harm or benefit. The verse does not mock ambition. It mocks worship: the transfer of trust, fear, and ultimate hope to a thing that cannot carry them.

**Death, Life, Resurrection: The Three Keys No Idol Holds**

The third charge completes the case: *wa-lā yamlikūna mawtan wa-lā ḥayātan wa-lā nushūrā* — "and who have no power over death, or life, or resurrection." Three keys. Death: they cannot prevent it, delay it, or assign it. Life: they cannot originate it, restore it, or guarantee its provision. Resurrection: they cannot raise, gather, or repay. The Qur'an elsewhere attributes all three to God in a single breath:

> *"He it is Who gives life and causes death, and to Him you will be returned."* (Qur'an 10:56)

> *"How do you disbelieve in God, when you were dead and He gave you life; then He will cause you to die, then He will give you life, then unto Him you will be returned?"* (Qur'an 2:28)

*Nushūr*, resurrection, is the same word this sūrah will later use for the day that follows sleep (25:47). The echo is deliberate. If you cannot wake a sleeper except by God's leave, you certainly cannot raise the dead. Idols fail the smallest analogue of the greatest claim.

The Prophet ﷺ taught a sentence that places these three keys in the believer's daily speech. Upon waking he would say, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (no. 6312):

> *"Praise be to God who gave us life after He caused us to die, and unto Him is the resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6312)

Every morning is a small *nushūr*. The One who owns that small rising owns the great one. No statue, no ideology, no human beloved, and no version of the self owns either.

**Why the Quran Catalogues What Idols Cannot Do**

Al-Qurṭubī and the commentators note that the Qur'an points out, across many verses, the things idols cannot do: they cannot create, they cannot grant provision (16:73; 29:17), they cannot speak (21:63–65; and the implication of 78:37 in some readings of divine address), they cannot intercede with any independence (30:13; 39:43; 43:86). The catalogue is not repetitive for lack of material. It is repetitive because the human heart is repetitive. It keeps returning to the same empty door under new paint.

There is also a pastoral reason. A person leaving idolatry — ancient or modern — needs more than a prohibition. He needs to see that he is not losing a benefactor. He is losing a deafness that had been dressed as a friend. The verse is therefore an act of disillusionment in the service of freedom. Once you know that the thing you feared cannot kill you, and the thing you loved cannot raise you, you are available to the One who does both.

**The Inner Idol This Verse Also Names**

The later verse 43 will make the inner form explicit: "Hast thou considered the one who takes his caprice as his god?" Verse 3 is already walking toward that diagnosis. A god that is created, that cannot help itself, that has no power over death or life or resurrection, may sit on a shelf — or it may sit in the chest, under the name of *hawā*, caprice. The tests are the same. Does this thing create, or is it made by my wanting? Can it protect itself from harm? Can it keep me from dying, or raise me when I have died? If the answers are no — and they are always no — then the thing, however beloved, is not eligible for worship.

Living this verse is a practice of relocation. Fear is relocated to God. Hope is relocated to God. The last appeal in a crisis is relocated to God. The idols are not always smashed in a public square. Sometimes they are simply starved of the trust that had been feeding them, until they are seen for what this verse said they were: created things, empty-handed, with no key to the three doors that matter.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:4

> **And the disbelievers say, "This is naught but a lie that he has fabricated, and another people have helped him in it." They have indeed produced a wrongdoing and a calumny.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Formal Charge: Invention Plus Accomplices**

After naming God and exposing the emptiness of the gods, the sūrah turns to the case the Quraysh made against the Book itself. The opening charge is double. *In hādhā illā ifkun iftarāhu* — "This is naught but a lie that he has fabricated" — and *wa-aʿānahu ʿalayhi qawmun ākharūn* — "and another people have helped him in it." The Prophet ﷺ is accused, first, of authorship, and second, of plagiarism with help. He is too gifted to be dismissed as a fool, and too unlettered to be credited as a scholar, so the accusation splits the difference: he made it up, and others — people with books — fed him the material.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record that the "other people" were said to be certain Jews and Christians, or specific individuals in Makkah who had knowledge of the earlier scriptures. Al-Rāzī notes that some commentators named persons learned in the Torah who later followed the Prophet ﷺ, so that the disbelievers could point to them as tutors. The sociological shape of the charge is familiar in every age: when a man without the expected credentials produces something that exceeds his class, his enemies prefer a conspiracy to a gift.

The Qur'an had already named this tactic elsewhere:

> *"And We certainly know that they say, 'It is only a human being who teaches him.' The tongue of the one they refer to is foreign, while this is a clear Arabic tongue."* (Qur'an 16:103)

The rebuttal in 16:103 is linguistic: the alleged tutor does not even speak the language of the Book. The rebuttal in this verse is moral, and it comes in God's own voice: *fa-qad jāʾū ẓulman wa-zūrā* — "They have indeed produced a wrongdoing and a calumny."

**Ẓulm and Zūr: Injustice Joined to Perjury**

*Ẓulm* is wrongdoing in the sense of putting a thing in the wrong place — the root image is darkness and displacement. To call revelation a fabrication is to put the speech of God in the place of a man's invention, which is the most severe misplacement of speech possible. *Zūr* is falsehood in the specific sense of a decorated, presented, courtroom lie: perjury, calumny, a false witness dressed for belief. The two words together say that the charge is not a mistaken opinion. It is an injury and a false testimony at once.

God does not, in this verse, yet answer the content of the accusation with a miracle. He names the accusation. That naming is itself a judgment. Before the secret-knowing of verse 6 is invoked, the moral quality of the speech has already been weighed. A culture that treats every serious claim as a smear in waiting should sit with this: some statements are not "takes." They are *ẓulm* and *zūr*. They harm a person, and they falsify the world.

**Why the Charge of Human Help Was Plausible to Its First Hearers**

The accusation was not invented out of nothing. Makkah was not sealed from the world. There were people of the Book in the peninsula. There were storytellers. There were slaves and merchants who had heard of Moses and Jesus. A suspicious mind, watching an unlettered man recite accounts of Joseph, Pharaoh, Mary, and the Flood, would reach for the nearest human explanation. The Qur'an does not pretend that the materials of sacred history were unknown. It insists that the *source* of this particular recitation is not those materials, and that the man reciting did not sit in a school of them.

The Prophet ﷺ's life was public. His companions, his wives, his enemies, and the boy who served him in the house could all have exposed a hidden tutor. None did. Instead, those closest to him became the most certain. That sociological fact does not replace the verse's theological claim, but it explains why the calumny had to keep adding accomplices: a single secret is hard to keep in a small town; a conspiracy of "another people" is the story you tell when the single-secret story has already failed.

**The Psychological Use of a Smear**

A smear has a function. It relieves the hearer of the duty to consider the message. If the Qur'an is a lie cooked with helpers, then one need not ask whether its account of God, the Hour, and justice is true. One need only repeat the rumor. This is why God calls it *zūr*: it is a testimony offered in place of thought. Modern forms are easy to list without leaving the verse's logic — a teacher is "funded," a reformer is "coached," a scripture is "compiled by a committee." The pattern is the same: displace the content by attacking the pipeline.

The believer's discipline, reading this verse, is the opposite. He examines pipelines when evidence requires it, but he does not let a rumor about helpers become a substitute for standing in front of the speech itself. The Criterion, verse 1 said, distinguishes. One of the things it distinguishes is argument from calumny.

**Wrongdoing Against a Man, and Wrongdoing Against God**

There is a further depth. To say of the Prophet ﷺ that he fabricated the Qur'an is to accuse him of the gravest fraud a human being can commit: speaking in God's name what God did not say. The Qur'an treats that crime as unthinkable for him:

> *"If he had fabricated against Us any sayings, We would have seized him by the right hand, then cut his life-vein."* (Qur'an 69:44–46)

So the calumny is two injuries in one. It injures the man by calling him a liar of a kind that would damn him. It injures God by treating His speech as a human product. *Ẓulm* covers both. The verse therefore does not merely defend Muhammad ﷺ's reputation. It defends the boundary between revelation and literature — a boundary without which every tyrant can write scripture, and every scripture can be reduced to taste.

A person who lives under this verse learns a caution about the tongue. Repeating a charge against a messenger, a scholar, or an ordinary believer without knowledge is not "discourse." It may be the same two nouns God used here: a wrongdoing and a calumny. The Criterion begins its defense of itself by teaching that words about words are already a moral act.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:5

> **And they say, "They are fables of those of old which he has had written down, and they are recited to him morning and evening."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Charge: Not Invention, but Recycled Legend**

Verse 4 accused the Prophet ﷺ of fabricating a lie with helpers. Verse 5 shifts the charge. Now the Qur'an is not a new fraud. It is an old library. *Asāṭīr al-awwalīn* — "fables of those of old" — stories, legends, the entertainment of vanished peoples, copied out and read back to an illiterate man on a schedule: *bukratan wa-aṣīlā*, morning and evening.

The two accusations sit in a useful tension. One says: he made it up. The other says: he copied it. Together they reveal that the objectors were not working from a single coherent theory. They were working from a single need — to avoid the claim that God had spoken. Any story that served that need would do, even if the stories canceled each other.

The phrase *asāṭīr al-awwalīn* is a refrain of rejection in the Qur'an:

> *"When Our signs are recited to them, they say, 'We have heard. If we wished, we could say the like of this. These are only fables of those of old.'"* (Qur'an 8:31)

> *"And when it is said to them, 'What has your Lord sent down?' they say, 'Fables of those of old.'"* (Qur'an 16:24)

> *"There is nothing but our life of this world; we die and we live, and we will not be resurrected. These are only fables of those of old."* (Qur'an 23:83–84, in sense; cf. 23:83)

> *"When Our signs are recited to him, he says, 'Fables of those of old.'"* (Qur'an 68:15; 83:13)

The repetition is diagnostic. "Fables of those of old" is what a person says when a recitation has reached the nerve of resurrection, judgment, or moral claim, and the cheapest solvent at hand is to file it under folklore.

**Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith and the Competition of Stories**

Al-Ṭabarī and others connect these verses with Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith of the Quraysh, a traveled man who had heard the tales of the Persian kings and the legends of Rustam and Isfandiyār. He would sit after the Prophet ﷺ had spoken and say, in effect: come, I have better stories than his. The comparison was the point. If the Qur'an is one entertainment among others, then the market can decide. Naḍr's method is the method of every age that cannot refute a scripture's theology and therefore tries to out-narrate it.

The method fails for a reason the objectors did not want to see. The Qur'an does not compete as a story. It competes as a claim about God, the soul, and the Hour, using narrative as evidence and warning, not as pastime. Joseph's story ends not in applause but in a statement about the unseen and the mercy of God. Moses' story ends not in spectacle but in a judgment upon tyranny and a mercy upon the oppressed. A person who hears these as *asāṭīr* has heard the plot and missed the address.

**"He Has Had Written Down": The Illiterate Man and the Imaginary Scribes**

*Iktatabahā* — "which he has had written down." The verb is causative. They do not even claim he wrote. They claim he commissioned writing. The concession is involuntary: they knew he did not write. The Qur'an elsewhere makes that biographical fact a sign:

> *"And you did not recite before it any book, nor did you write it with your right hand. Otherwise the falsifiers would have doubted."* (Qur'an 29:48)

So the accusation has to invent a workshop: others write, others read, morning and evening, and the Prophet ﷺ memorizes the product. The schedule — *bukratan wa-aṣīlā* — is meant to make the workshop sound industrious and secret. It also, ironically, describes the actual life of revelation more truly than they knew. The Prophet ﷺ did receive recitation morning and evening, and at night, and in the pauses of the day. The source was not a hired reader. The source was the One verse 6 will name.

**Morning and Evening as a Picture of Obsession**

There is a psychological observation inside their own wording. They imagine the Prophet ﷺ as a man under a continuous drip of dictated legend. What they are actually describing is their own problem: the recitation would not go away. It was there in the morning. It was there in the evening. A message that can be filed as a fable should be easy to walk away from. Their need to explain its persistence — to invent a twice-daily tutoring — is evidence that the speech was doing something a fable does not do. It was following them.

This is how many people still handle a moral claim that has found them. They do not say, "I have weighed it and found it false." They say, "I have heard all this before." Familiarity is used as a refutation. The verse unmasks that move. Calling a thing old is not the same as answering it.

**What the Believer Does with Old Truth**

The irony, which the sūrah will deepen, is that the Qur'an does not deny its continuity with what came before. It affirms it.

> *"He has sent down upon you the Book in truth, confirming what was before it."* (Qur'an 3:3)

Confirmation is not copying, just as a later court confirming an earlier verdict is not plagiarism. The stories of Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus are told because they happened, because they teach, and because the same God is addressing a new audience through a new messenger. The difference between *asāṭīr* and revelation is not novelty of plot. It is the presence of the Speaker, the accuracy of the meaning, and the demand the recitation makes upon the hearer's life.

A practical fruit of the verse is a discipline of listening. When a passage of the Qur'an feels "already known," the servant does not file it under fable or under habit. He asks what in him has gone dull. Morning and evening recitation — the very schedule the accusers mocked — became, in the life of the Muslims, the remedy: prayer at the two ends of the day, with the Criterion in the mouth, so that the old words would not become old in the heart.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:6

> **Say, "He has sent it down Who knows what is secret in the heavens and on the earth. Truly He is Forgiving, Merciful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reply Is Not a Biography. It Is a Name.**

Verses 4 and 5 put two human explanations of the Qur'an in the mouth of the disbelievers: fabrication with helpers, and old fables dictated morning and evening. Verse 6 does not answer with a literary analysis, a list of witnesses, or a defense of the Prophet's ﷺ schedule. It answers with a command to speak: *qul* — "Say." The messenger's task is not to litigate his own innocence as a private man. It is to point to the Sender.

*Anzalahu alladhī yaʿlamu al-sirra fī al-samāwāti wal-arḍ* — "He has sent it down Who knows what is secret in the heavens and on the earth." Al-Qurṭubī's gloss is exact: God knows all that is hidden, and so the Prophet ﷺ does not need to be taught by another. The alleged workshop of verse 5 is unnecessary once the Speaker is the One from whom nothing is concealed. The helpers, the scribes, the morning readers — all of them are solutions to a problem that does not exist if God is God.

**Sirr: The Secret as the Measure of Knowledge**

*Al-sirr* is that which is concealed — the inner of a thing, the whispered plan, the thought not yet spoken, the event not yet public, the reality not yet apparent in the heavens or on the earth. To know the secret is to know more than the surface. The Qur'an makes this knowledge a Divine exclusive:

> *"He knows what is in the heavens and the earth, and He knows what you conceal and what you declare. And God is Knowing of what is in the breasts."* (Qur'an 64:4)

> *"And if you speak aloud — then indeed, He knows the secret and what is still more hidden."* (Qur'an 20:7)

Al-Rāzī notes the implicit threat: if God knows all secret things, He is aware of the thoughts and plots of those who oppose the Prophet ﷺ. The verse is therefore doing two works at once. It authenticates the Book by naming a Sender whose knowledge does not require human libraries. And it warns the accusers that the inner of their accusation — the envy, the politics, the fear of losing status — is already in the open before Him.

A Book sent down by the Knower of secrets will contain what secret-knowers would put in a book: the hidden of the soul, the hidden of history, the hidden of the Hour. This is why the Qur'an can describe the interior of the hypocrites, the private councils of the Quraysh, and the future states of the dead. The content matches the Author.

**Why Forgiving and Merciful Close the Verse**

After a verse that could have ended in threat, two Names: *innahu kāna ghafūran raḥīmā* — "Truly He is Forgiving, Merciful." The past-tense *kāna* here, as often in the Qur'an, does not mean He used to be and ceased. It means this is His established quality. The accusers have produced *ẓulm* and *zūr*. The Sender of the Book, who knows the secret of that *ẓulm*, still names Himself as the One who forgives and whose mercy reaches.

The pairing is a pattern. Knowledge that sees everything, without mercy, would be a reason to despair. Mercy without knowledge would be a reason to hide. Together they create the only climate in which repentance is rational: you cannot hide, and you do not need to, because the One who sees has not closed the door.

> *"And those who, when they commit an indecency or wrong themselves, remember God and seek forgiveness for their sins — and who forgives sins except God?"* (Qur'an 3:135)

> *"Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of God. Indeed, God forgives all sins. Indeed, it is He who is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 39:53)

The Prophet ﷺ reported the ḥadīth qudsī that sets the proportion:

> *"When God created the creation, He wrote in His Book, which is with Him above the Throne: 'My mercy prevails over My wrath.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7404; Muslim 2751)

Verse 6 is that proportion in miniature. The Knower of secrets is not first of all a prosecutor. He is *Ghafūr, Raḥīm*. The same God who exposes the calumny of verses 4 and 5 offers, in the same breath, the Names by which the calumniators themselves could still come in.

**What This Does to the Prophet's Heart, and to Ours**

For the Prophet ﷺ, the command *qul* is a relief as well as a duty. He is not required to clear his name by matching their story with a better story about himself. He is required to refer the Book to its Source. Many of the hurts a public servant carries are of this kind: accusations about origin, funding, influence, ghostwriters. The verse's method is still available. Point to the work's content and to God. Let the secret-Knower handle the secret.

For the reader, the verse trains two habits. The first is theological: when asking where the Qur'an came from, begin with who God is, not with who sat in which room. The second is moral: live as a person whose secrets are already known, and whose hope is not in remaining unknown but in being forgiven. The combination produces a particular kind of honesty — neither exhibitionism nor concealment, but the quiet of someone who has already been seen and has already been invited to mercy.

In daily terms, the verse is a check against two modern diseases. Conspiracy thinking explains every public good as a hidden workshop; verse 6 answers that the true hidden is with God, and that not every origin is a plot. Surveillance culture, on the other side, makes people desperate to curate what is known about them; verse 6 answers that the knowledge that actually matters is already complete, and that the Names waiting on the other side of that knowledge are Forgiving and Merciful. A life can be built on that. A smear cannot.

**He Knows the Secret in the Heavens and the Earth**

*Qul anzalahu lladhī yaʿlamu l-sirra fī l-samāwāti wal-arḍ* — say: He sent it down who knows the secret in the heavens and the earth. The answer to “he forged it, a people helped him” is not a literary defense first. It is a naming of the Sender as Knower of the hidden. A forgery is a human secret. This Book comes from the One to whom human secrets are not secret. *Innahu kāna ghafūran raḥīmā* — truly He has ever been Forgiving, Merciful. The two Names after the knowledge of secrets are a door. He knows, and He still forgives. Verse 70 will need those Names. The Prophet ﷺ said that God conceals the servant who conceals himself, and that a man may do a deed in secret that saves him. Verse 6 is the theology of that: the knowledge is already His; the Names on the other side of it are mercy. A person who fears the smear more than he fears the Knower has ranked the wrong audience.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:7

> **And they say, "What ails this Messenger, who eats food and walks in the markets? Why is there not an angel sent down unto him to be a warner with him,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objection That Could Not Forgive His Humanity**

The sūrah now records the objection that, more than any other, reveals the religious imagination of the Quraysh. *Māli hādhā al-rasūli yaʾkulu al-ṭaʿāma wa-yamshī fī al-aswāq* — "What ails this Messenger, who eats food and walks in the markets?" The demonstrative *hādhā*, "this," carries a slight of proximity, as one might say "this fellow." They do not deny that he claims messengerhood. They deny that messengerhood could look like a man buying grain.

A major theme of the Qur'an is this incredulity: that God would send an ordinary human being. The demand for an angel appears again and again:

> *"And they say, 'Why has an angel not been sent down to him?' If We had sent down an angel, the matter would have been decided, and then they would not be given respite."* (Qur'an 6:8)

> *"They said, 'Why do you not bring us the angels, if you are of the truthful?'"* (Qur'an 15:7)

> *"Or you have a house of ornament, or you ascend into the sky. And we will not believe in your ascension until you bring down to us a book we may read."* (Qur'an 17:93)

> *"And those who hope not to meet Us say, 'Why have not the angels been sent down unto us, or why have we not seen our Lord?'"* (Qur'an 25:21)

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the insult was also social. Great kings, rulers, and notables considered themselves above walking with ordinary people in the markets. A messenger who queued among shoppers could not, in that map of honor, be a messenger. Status had been confused with sacredness. The market, which is where human need is most visible — hunger, price, bargaining, dust — was judged too low for a man who spoke with God.

**Eating Food: The Body as a Scandal**

Food is the most ordinary proof of dependence. A being that eats is a being that will starve if the food stops. The Quraysh wanted a messenger who did not need. The Qur'an will answer in verse 20 with a historical fact:

> *"And We sent not any messengers before thee but that they ate food and walked in the markets."* (Qur'an 25:20)

And it will say of the messengers as a class:

> *"We did not give them bodies that did not eat food, nor were they immortal."* (Qur'an 21:8)

The scandal of eating is the scandal of the incarnation of a message in a needy body. Islam does not teach that God became a body. It teaches something that, to the proud, is almost more offensive: that God is willing to put His speech on the tongue of a man who gets hungry. The hungry messenger can be followed by hungry people. An angel who does not eat can be admired; he cannot be imitated in the kitchen, the market, or the marriage.

The Prophet ﷺ's own table was an answer in action. ʿĀʾishah reported that months would pass and no fire would be lit in the house, only dates and water (al-Bukhārī 6458; Muslim 2972). He was a messenger who ate food — little of it — and the little was part of the teaching. Need was not hidden. It was offered to God.

**Walking in the Markets: Prophecy Among Prices**

The market in Makkah was not a metaphor. It was the center of the town's pride, the place of the fairs of ʿUkāẓ and the trade that made Quraysh rich. To walk there was to be seen as one merchant among others. The objectors wanted a man whose feet did not touch that dust. The Qur'an wants a man whose feet do, because the religion being revealed is a religion for people who buy and sell.

Later Madinan legislation about weights, fraud, hoarding, and oaths in trade would be unintelligible if the messenger had been too exalted to know a market. The verse, even in its hostile mouth, testifies that his life was public and mixed. He was not a hermit whose revelation could be dismissed as cabin fever, and he was not a prince whose revelation could be dismissed as court ideology. He walked where everyone walked.

The Prophet ﷺ later said, in a ḥadīth that honors this very dust:

> *"The truthful, trustworthy merchant will be with the prophets, the truthful, and the martyrs."* (al-Tirmidhī 1209)

Markets are not the opposite of revelation. They are one of the first places revelation has to go, because that is where the heart's greed and the heart's honesty are both on display.

**"Why Not an Angel With Him?" The Demand for a Co-Warner**

The second half of their sentence — *lawlā unzila ilayhi malakun fa-yakūna maʿahu nadhīrā* — "Why is there not an angel sent down unto him to be a warner with him" — is a demand for theater. An angel would settle the status question. It would also, as 6:8 says, settle the matter of respite. A people who see the unseen unveiled in that way have come to the end of testing. The Quraysh thought they were asking for more evidence. They were asking, without knowing it, for the end of their chance.

There is a further irony. They already had a warner. They wanted a second, of another species, as though the problem were the lack of staff. The problem was the lack of will. A person who will not hear a man will not hear an angel either, except as a terror. Verse 22 will make that explicit: on the Day they see the angels, there will be no glad tidings for the guilty.

**What the Objection Still Sounds Like**

The demand has not left the world. It is heard whenever religion is expected to look like celebrity, wealth, or special effects, and is dismissed when it looks like a man doing his shopping. It is heard whenever a scholar's ordinary needs are treated as disqualifying, and whenever communities hide their teachers behind a glamour the Prophet ﷺ did not wear. The verse invites a re-education of awe. Awe belongs to the Sender. The sent one eats, walks, bargains, and bleeds. That is not the failure of prophecy. It is the form prophecy took, so that a human path could be walked by human feet.

Living the verse means refusing two errors: despising a teacher for being human, and despising the market as a place where God has nothing to say. The Criterion was sent down upon a servant who knew both hunger and price. The religion that followed him will always be, at its healthiest, a religion that can be found where bread is sold.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:8

> **or no treasure cast unto him, or no garden for him from which to eat?" And the wrongdoers say, "You follow naught but a man bewitched."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**If Not an Angel, Then at Least a Fortune**

Verse 7 demanded an accompanying angel. Verse 8 continues the same sentence of complaint with two further absences: *aw yulqā ilayhi kanzun* — "or no treasure cast unto him" — and *aw takūnu lahu jannatun yaʾkulu minhā* — "or no garden for him from which to eat." The imagination of the objectors is consistent. A messenger should be marked by exemption from ordinary need. If he must be human, he should at least be a human whom the sky has made rich.

The same demand was thrown at Moses:

> *"Why, then, have armlets of gold not been cast upon him, and why do angels not accompany him?"* (Qur'an 43:53)

And at Muhammad ﷺ in another Makkan recitation:

> *"Or why is not a treasure sent down to him, or an angel come with him?"* (Qur'an 11:12)

Al-Rāzī explains the social logic. They believed that a messenger who was not wealthy and did not have lands from which to sustain himself could not be superior to them, so they felt no imperative to follow him. Superiority, in that map, is purchasing power. The Israelites had used the same map against Saul:

> *"They said, 'How can he have kingship over us while we are more worthy of kingship than he, and he has not been given abundance of wealth?'"* (Qur'an 2:247)

The pattern is older than Makkah. It is the confusion of *mulk* as God has it — verse 2 — with *mulk* as men want it: treasure, land, a visible surplus that humiliates rivals.

**Treasure Cast, Garden Planted: Two Pictures of Unearned Exemption**

A treasure *cast* (*yulqā*) is wealth that arrives without work, thrown down as a sign. A garden to eat from is wealth that continues, a private ecology of ease. Together they sketch a messiah of luxury. The Quraysh were not asking whether the message was true. They were asking whether the messenger had been excused from the human condition that they themselves found undignified in a leader.

Verse 10 will answer that God, if He willed, could give His servant better than both — Gardens with rivers, and palaces. The answer is important because it does not say God cannot. It says God did not choose that costume. The withholding is itself part of the message. A prophet of treasure would gather the greedy. A prophet of gardens would gather the comfortable. A prophet who eats food in the market gathers whoever is willing to be guided, including the poor whom verse 20 will call a trial for others.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle as a fact of his own life. Al-Tirmidhī (no. 2347) records:

> *"My Lord offered to make the valley of Makkah gold for me. I said: No, O Lord. Rather I will go hungry one day and eat one day. When I am hungry I will humble myself to You and remember You, and when I eat I will thank You and praise You."* (al-Tirmidhī 2347; its chain has been discussed, but the meaning is massively attested in his biography)

Whether one leans on that particular chain or on the mass of reports about his poverty, the costume he wore was chosen. Treasure was not refused because it was unavailable. It was refused because it would have confused the office.

**"A Man Bewitched": When Wonder Is Filed Under Magic**

The verse then changes speakers. The wrongdoers say, *in tattabiʿūna illā rajulan masḥūrā* — "You follow naught but a man bewitched." *Masḥūr* is one upon whom magic has been worked, a man whose perception is no longer his own. The charge is a way of explaining the uncanny without kneeling. His recitation is not ordinary speech. His endurance is not ordinary patience. His effect on the young and the poor is not ordinary rhetoric. Therefore — so the diagnosis goes — he is enchanted, and you who follow him are enchanted with him.

The same word is thrown at him elsewhere:

> *"And We certainly know that they say, 'It is only a human being who teaches him.'"* (Qur'an 16:103) — a different charge, but the same need.

> *"And they say, 'You follow only a man bewitched.'"* (Qur'an 17:47)

Magic, in this usage, is the wastebasket of the proud. Anything that exceeds their theory of the possible, but that they will not call divine, is called a spell. The tragedy of the label is that it makes the labeled person unreachable. You do not debate a man you have declared bewitched. You quarantine him. The followers are not addressed as reasoners. They are pitied as patients.

**Wrongdoers, Not Merely Doubters**

The Qur'an names the speakers *al-ẓālimūn*, the wrongdoers. Doubt, in the Qur'an, can be a stage. Wrongdoing is a choice to injure truth in order to keep a social world intact. Calling the Messenger bewitched injured him, injured those who loved him, and injured the possibility of fair hearing. It was, like the *zūr* of verse 4, a word used as a weapon.

The Prophet ﷺ did, in a later Madinan episode, suffer an attempt at actual magic, and God cured him; the reports are in al-Bukhārī (no. 5765) and Muslim. That historical fact, far from confirming the Makkan smear, shows the difference. Magic is a real crime in the Qur'anic world, and it is a crime *against* prophets, not the source of their prophecy. The man who receives the Criterion is not a man whose mind has been stolen. He is a man whose mind has been entrusted with a trust.

**The Measure This Verse Gives a Community**

A community that has internalized verse 8 will not test its teachers by treasure or by gardens, and it will not explain spiritual effect as pathology. It will ask, as verse 1 taught it to ask, whether a criterion is being carried. Wealth may come to a believer; gardens may be planted by his hand. Those are not proofs against him. They are also not proofs for him. The proof is the Book, the character, and the congruence with what messengers have always been: eaters of food, walkers in markets, warners without a fee.

In modern dress the demand sounds like this: Why is this preacher not famous, not funded, not followed by spectacle? Or, from the other side: this movement's power over people is "cultic," "hypnotic," "a spell." Both sentences can be, and sometimes are, legitimate questions about abuse. Both sentences are also, in the mouth of the proud, the same old refusal. The verse trains discernment. Look at the life. Look at the Criterion. Do not let gold, or the absence of gold, or the word "bewitched," do your thinking for you.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:9

> **Look how they set forth descriptions of thee, and then go astray and cannot find a way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Look, Not a Command to Retort**

*Unẓur kayfa ḍarabū laka al-amthāl* — "Look how they set forth descriptions of thee." The Prophet ﷺ is told to look. God does not, in this verse, put a counter-slogan in his mouth. He invites him to see the pattern of what is being done. *Amthāl* here are not only parables. They are comparisons, descriptions, labels: fabricator, pupil of foreigners, reciter of old fables, too human to be a messenger, too poor to be chosen, bewitched. A cluster of pictures has been hung around one man, and the pictures do not agree with each other. Verse 9 asks him — and us — to notice that fact.

Al-Rāzī observes that extraordinary phenomena of the kind they demanded are not in any case a necessary mark of prophets, so their comparisons are, even on their own terms, a waste of thought. Al-Qurṭubī says they offer such comparisons for the purpose of denial. Al-Ṭabarī records the reading that they then go astray and cannot find a way *out of* the descriptions they have given: the labels trap the labelers. Once you have called a man both a cunning fabricator and a bewitched fool, you have not described him. You have described your need not to follow him.

**The Way That Closes When Speech Becomes a Maze**

*Fa-ḍallū fa-lā yastaṭīʿūna sabīlā* — "and then they go astray and cannot find a way." Going astray here is not a private mood. It is the loss of a road. A *sabīl* is a path that can be walked. Their descriptions were meant to be a road away from him. They became a thicket. This is what happens when a person multiplies explanations of a thing he will not face. Each new label buys a night's relief and costs a day's clarity. In the end he cannot find a way because he has used up his way-finding on the construction of exits.

The Qur'an elsewhere describes this self-made maze:

> *"Those who disbelieve, it is the same to them whether you warn them or do not warn them: they will not believe. God has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering."* (Qur'an 2:6–7)

The seal, in the theology of these verses, is not an arbitrary lock on a willing heart. It is often the end-state of a series of choices to prefer any description except the true one. Verse 9 is a snapshot of that process in its social form: a community workshop of *amthāl*, then a community that cannot find the road those *amthāl* were supposed to protect.

**Why Multiple Smears Are a Sign of Weakness, Not of Evidence**

In law, a witness who tells two contradictory stories is not twice as credible. He is not credible. The Quraysh said: he invented it; others wrote it; it is old legend; he is ordinary; he should have treasure; he is bewitched. A student of evidence hears in that list not a case but a mood. Verse 9 is teaching the Prophet ﷺ — who had to hear these words in the street — to see the list as God sees it: not as a weight of testimony, but as a symptom.

The practical analogue in a believer's life is the moment when criticism of the religion, or of a person, begins to multiply in incompatible directions. One should listen to a single coherent objection with respect. A swarm of incompatible objections is often not investigation. It is resistance looking for a language. The verse does not forbid answering. It forbids being intellectually bullied by volume.

**The Consolation Inside the Command**

"Look" is also consolation. The Prophet ﷺ is not being told that the descriptions are his fault, or that a better public-relations strategy would have prevented them. He is being told that God has seen the pattern, named it as going astray, and recorded that they cannot find a way. The recording is for him, so that his heart does not internalize the maze as a map of himself. Many a public servant has been destroyed not by an accusation but by the temptation to live inside the accusation, rearranging his soul to answer every picture. Verse 9 cuts that temptation. Look at them. Do not become them.

Ibn Kathīr, across his treatment of the Prophet's ﷺ patience, returns to the principle that God answers on behalf of His messenger. This short verse is one of those answers. It does not refute each *mathal* in turn. It judges the activity of making them.

**Finding a Way When Others Have Lost One**

The last gift of the verse is the implied opposite. If they go astray and cannot find a way, there is a way. The sūrah will later name it in the portrait of the servants of the Compassionate, and the Fātiḥah has already named it *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*. Ways are found by walking toward what is true, not by manufacturing descriptions of what one refuses to walk toward.

In ordinary life this has a simple application. When a family, a workplace, or a public conversation becomes a factory of labels — this person is a fool, a knave, a puppet, a magician, all at once — the believer's job is not to add a label. It is to look, to notice the loss of a road, and to keep to the one he has. Taqwā here is the care of a person who will not enter a maze just because the crowd is inside it shouting directions.

**Look How They Strike for Thee the Similitudes**

*Unẓur kayfa ḍarabū laka l-amthāl* — look how they have struck for thee the similitudes. The command is to the Prophet ﷺ: look. Not: answer every label in kind. Look. The looking is a seeing of the method. *Fa-ḍallū* — so they have gone astray. The parables are not a path. They are the loss of a path. *Fa-lā yastaṭīʿūna sabīlā* — so they cannot find a way. Verse 27’s *sabīl* with the messenger is the way they cannot find because they spent the finding-energy on *amthāl*. Poet, sorcerer, madman, forger, man who eats: each *mathal* is a refusal to take him as what verse 1 named, a warner. Verse 33 will promise that every new *mathal* will be met with truth and a better explanation. Verse 9 is the diagnosis before the promise: the similitude-factory is already a lost road. A later reader who lives on labels — of sects, of persons, of the Prophet ﷺ himself in modern mockery — is in the same factory. The way out is not a better label. It is *sabīl*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:10

> **Blessed is He Who, if He will, shall appoint for thee what is better than that—Gardens with rivers running below, and He will appoint for thee palaces.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Blessing, Turned Toward the Servant**

The sūrah opened with *tabāraka alladhī nazzala al-furqān* — Blessed is He Who sent down the Criterion. Verse 10 returns the same verb, *tabāraka*, and turns it toward the man who has just been described as too poor, too hungry, and too unadorned to be a messenger. Blessed is He Who, if He will, can give that man better than the treasure and the garden the objectors demanded. The repetition of *tabāraka* is a structural mercy. The God whose blessing was named in the sending of the Book is the God whose blessing can, at will, rearrange the furniture of the world.

*In shāʾa* — "if He will" — is the hinge. God is able. God is not compelled. The Quraysh treated wealth as a necessary sign. The verse treats wealth as a possible gift, held in a will that does not take instructions from them. Ability without compulsion is the meaning of lordship. A God who *must* gild His messenger to prove Himself is already a God on trial. The God of this verse remains the One of verse 2, whose sovereignty has no partner and no child and no committee of critics.

**Better Than That: The Hereafter as the True Surplus**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as stating that the rewards of the Hereafter are greater than the treasure and garden mentioned in verse 8. Al-Rāzī records the reading that, if God willed, He could bestow both gardens in the Hereafter and palaces in this world. The two readings do not fight. The grammar allows a gift here and a gift there. The emphasis, in either case, falls on *khayran min dhālik* — "better than that." Better than the costume they designed. Better than the proof they demanded. Better, finally, than any garden a Makkan could irrigate.

*Jannātin tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār* — "Gardens with rivers running below" — is the Qur'an's most repeated picture of the final success. It appears first in the second sūrah:

> *"And give glad tidings to those who believe and do righteous deeds, that for them are Gardens with rivers running below."* (Qur'an 2:25)

The image is not a bribe of luxury. It is the restoration of the first garden from which Adam was sent down, now made permanent, with water — the sign of life in an arid land — running where the feet walk. To a people whose wealth was measured in gardens at Ṭāʾif and wells at home, the picture was intelligible. To anyone who has known thirst, it still is.

**Palaces: What the Word Meant in Their Mouths**

*Quṣūr* are palaces. Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ṭabarī note that for the Quraysh the word could mean any dwelling made of stone, regardless of size. Stone, in a town of mud-brick and hair-tents, was already grandeur. The Thamūd were remembered for cutting houses in the mountains and raising *quṣūr*:

> *"And you carve out houses from the mountains, with skill."* (Qur'an 26:149, of Thamūd; cf. 7:74)

> *"How many a town have We destroyed while it was doing wrong, so that it is fallen down on its roofs, and how many a deserted well and lofty palace."* (Qur'an 22:45)

The last of those verses is a warning: palaces empty out. Verse 10 promises palaces that will not. The contrast with verse 8 is therefore complete. They wanted a garden to eat from in this world, as a badge. God speaks of Gardens that remain, and of palaces He Himself appoints. The appointing matters. *Yajʿal laka* — He will appoint for thee. The servant does not seize a palace as proof. The Lord assigns it as gift.

**Why the Prophet Was Not Given This Costume in Makkah**

If the palaces were possible, why were they withheld in the years of this recitation? The sūrah has already begun to answer and will answer more fully. A messenger of palaces would have confirmed the Quraysh's theology of status. The poor would have been shut out by the very sign that was supposed to invite them. The trial named in verse 20 — "We made some of you a trial for others; will you be patient?" — requires that the messenger share the condition of those who have no stone house. His poverty is not an oversight in the Divine plan. It is one of the plan's instruments.

The Prophet ﷺ later, in Madinah, would have booty, land, and gifts, and would give them away so thoroughly that he would leave this world with his armor pledged to a Jewish merchant for a measure of barley (al-Bukhārī 2916; Muslim 1603). The capacity for palaces was always there, in God's will and, later, in the community's wealth. The refusal to live as a king remained. Verse 10 is therefore not a consolation prize of the form "you will have palaces when you die." It is a statement of proportion. What they think is the ceiling is, in God's hand, a floor He can raise whenever He wills — and a floor He can leave unraised when the mission requires it.

**Living Beneath a Ceiling God Can Lift**

The verse has a pastoral use for anyone whose life looks, to others or to himself, underfurnished. The Quraysh pointed at a man without treasure and called him disqualified. God pointed at the same man and said: Blessed is He Who can give him better. The believer does not demand that lifting as a right. He knows it is possible, and he knows it may be delayed until a Garden where rivers run, and he works in the meantime without the itch of comparison.

Comparison is the illness this verse treats. The objectors compared the Prophet ﷺ to the kings they had heard of. People still compare their provision to a cousin's, a feed's, a rival's. *In shāʾa* relocates the comparison. The relevant will is not the market's and not the family's. It is the will of the One who blessed the sending of the Criterion. A person who has that Book has already been given what is better than treasure. Palaces, if they come, are extra. If they do not, the blessing named in verse 1 has not been withdrawn.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:11

> **Nay, but they deny the Hour, and We have prepared a Blaze for those who deny the Hour.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Real Objection Beneath the Costume Demands**

*Bal* — "Nay, but" — is a correction. The sūrah has spent several verses recording what the disbelievers *say*: he fabricated, he copied, he eats, he has no treasure, he is bewitched.
