Verse 11 says what the noise is actually about. *Kadhdhabū bil-sāʿah* — they deny the Hour. The angel, the gold, the garden, the charge of magic — these are the costumes of a deeper refusal. If there is no Hour, then a warner is a nuisance, a Book that distinguishes is an insult, and a man who speaks of Gardens and palaces after death is selling vapor. If there is an Hour, then all their descriptions of him are a sideshow.

Al-Qurṭubī identifies the Hour as the end of the world and the Day of Resurrection. The Qur'an names it *al-sāʿah* because, when it comes, it is a single hour that reorders every hour that came before it. It is "the Hour" the way a people say "the Day" of a battle that defined them. Everything in this sūrah — the Criterion, the humanity of the messenger, the portraits of Hell and of the servants of the Compassionate — hangs on whether that Hour is real.

> *"And the Hour is coming, no doubt about it, and God will resurrect those who are in the graves."* (Qur'an 22:7)

> *"Indeed, the Hour is coming — I almost conceal it — so that every soul may be recompensed for what it strives."* (Qur'an 20:15)

The concealment is part of the mercy. If the Hour's date were published, the moral life would collapse into panic or delay. Hidden, it produces the only honest stance: readiness without a calendar.

**Denial as a Moral Act, Not a Calendar Dispute**

*Kadhdhabū* is not "they are unsure of the date." It is "they have called it a lie." Denial of resurrection in the Qur'an is almost never a scientific shyness about bones. It is a refusal of accountability. The Makkan aristocrat who said he would not be raised was not protecting a philosophy of matter. He was protecting a life that would look different if he had to meet it again.

> *"And they say, 'There is nothing but our life of this world. We die and we live, and nothing destroys us except time.'"* (Qur'an 45:24)

> *"Does man think that We will not gather his bones? Yes — We are able even to proportion his fingertips."* (Qur'an 75:3–4)

Fingerprints, in that verse, are the sign of irreplaceable identity. The one who is raised is not a generic soul. He is the same person who did what he did. Denial of the Hour is, at bottom, the wish to be untraceable.

**A Blaze Prepared: Saʿīr as Flame and as Place**

*Wa-aʿtadnā li-man kadhdhaba bil-sāʿati saʿīrā* — "and We have prepared a Blaze for those who deny the Hour." *Saʿīr* means a flame, a kindled fire, and is in that sense a synonym of *nār*. Al-Rāzī notes that some consider it one of the names of Hell. The word recurs across the Book as the end of those who deny, betray, or take Satan as ally:

> *"Those who devour the property of orphans unjustly devour only fire into their bellies, and they will roast in a Blaze."* (Qur'an 4:10)

> *"Indeed, God has cursed the disbelievers and prepared for them a Blaze."* (Qur'an 33:64)

*Aʿtadnā* — "We have prepared" — is the verb that has furnished theologians with an argument about predestination. Al-Rāzī records the reading that, because the Blaze is "prepared," the saved were saved in their mother's womb, and the lost were lost there. The verse can also be read, and in the grain of this sūrah is better read, as saying that Hell is prepared as a destination for those who choose freely to deny the Hour. Preparation of a place is not the same as compulsion of a traveler. A city may build a prison without naming in advance every inmate; the building is a warning as well as a capacity.

The two readings should not be allowed to erase the verse's plain pastoral force. There is a fire. It is not improvised at the last minute. It is prepared. Denial of the Hour is not a harmless private opinion. It is a road with an end that has already been built.

**Why This Verse Must Interrupt the Debate About Palaces**

The placement is precise. Verse 10 spoke of Gardens and palaces God could give His servant. Verse 11 says the objectors' real problem is not that the servant lacks palaces. It is that they do not believe in the world where such palaces are assigned by justice. People who deny the Hour will always find the righteous underfurnished and the wicked overfurnished, and they will take that snapshot as the whole film. The Hour is the rest of the film.

This is why the Prophet ﷺ said, in a report al-Tirmidhī (no. 2306) and others transmit:

> *"The intelligent one is he who takes himself to account and works for what is after death, and the helpless one is he whose soul follows its desires and who then hopes in God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2459; graded ḥasan by some)

Intelligence, in that definition, is Hour-awareness. Folly is desire plus vague hope. Verse 11 draws the same line with a harder instrument: those who deny the Hour have a Blaze prepared.

**How a Person Stops Denying Without Becoming Paralyzed**

Belief in the Hour is not meant to freeze the hands. It is meant to direct them. The Qur'an, having named the Blaze, will immediately describe its raging and then, in verses 15 and 16, place beside it the Garden of everlastingness. Fear without hope is denial's twin; it also empties the Hour of the God who is Forgiving, Merciful (verse 6). The servants of the Compassionate, at the end of this sūrah, will pray to be spared Hell and will still walk, spend, and raise families. That is Hour-belief in working clothes: not a calendar obsession, not a theatrical dread, but a refusal to live as if the film ends when the body ends.

The practical test is simple and daily. Would this word, this click, this contract, this silence still be chosen if the Hour were tonight? Taqwā is driving with that question on the dashboard — not so that one never arrives, but so that one arrives having not gone off the road.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:12

> **When it sees them from a place far off, they will hear its raging and roaring.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hell as a Seer, Not Only as a Place**

*Idhā raʾat-hum min makānin baʿīd* — "When it sees them from a place far off." The subject is the Blaze of the previous verse. Hell sees. Al-Qurṭubī takes the clause in its apparent sense: when Hell sees them. Al-Rāzī records the other reading, that the custodians of Hell see them, and that Hell here is a metonym, as when one says "Ask the town" meaning "Ask the people of the town." Both readings are inside the tradition. Neither is a cartoon. The verse is giving the Fire a relation to its inhabitants that is not the relation of a passive oven to bread. It is ahead of them. It perceives them before they arrive.

The Muʿtazilite theologians, al-Rāzī notes, disliked the ascription of seeing, hearing, and speech to the Fire. Other theologians saw no problem in God's granting the Fire such faculties. The Qur'an itself is unafraid of this language. It will later have Hell ask whether there is more:

> *"On the Day We will say to Hell, 'Have you been filled?' and it will say, 'Are there any more?'"* (Qur'an 50:30)

And it describes the Fire as having a fury that almost bursts from rage:

> *"When they are cast into it, they will hear it inhaling as it boils up, almost bursting with rage."* (Qur'an 67:7–8)

Whether one reads these as literal created faculties or as a language of eschatological reality that exceeds ordinary physics, the pastoral meaning is the same. The Blaze is not an accident of geology. It is a prepared, aware destination.

**The Ḥadīth of the Neck of Fire**

Al-Qurṭubī glosses this verse with a ḥadīth describing the Fire on the Day of Resurrection as having a neck that sprouts with two eyes that see, two ears that hear, and a tongue that speaks, declaring that it has been entrusted with those who associated others with God, and that it will swallow them as a bird swallows a sesame seed. Reports of this kind appear in the collections of al-Tirmidhī (no. 2574) and Aḥmad, with wordings that the hadith critics have weighed differently; Ibn Kathīr cites them in the commentary of the eschatological verses. Even when a particular chain is discussed, the Qur'anic base is independent of it: the Fire sees from far off, and those approaching hear it.

The sesame-seed image, if one sits with it, is not gore for its own sake. It is scale. The self that seemed large in the market, large in the tribe, large in its descriptions of the Prophet ﷺ, is, next to what has been prepared, a seed. Pride is a mis-measurement of size. The Hour corrects sizes.

**Raging and Roaring: Two Sounds of One Arrival**

*Samiʿū lahā taghayyuẓan wa-zafīrā* — "they will hear its raging and roaring." *Taghayyuẓ* is the inner boil of anger, the fury that fills a chest before it spills. *Zafīr* is the sound of a breath forced out — a groan, a roar, the exhalation of a beast or a furnace. The pair is interior and exterior: rage felt, rage heard. The people who denied the Hour do not, in this verse, yet feel heat on the skin. They hear. The first punishment is auditory. Distance has not protected them. *From a place far off* the Fire is already in their ears.

This matches a wider Qur'anic pattern: the Last Day begins, for the damned, with sounds. The cry, the blast, the wail.

> *"And the Horn will be blown; that is the Day of the Threat."* (Qur'an 50:20)

> *"When the sky is rent asunder, and it hears its Lord and is due to do so."* (Qur'an 84:1–2)

Hearing is the sense a warner uses. The Prophet ﷺ was sent as *nadhīr*, a warner, a man of the voice. Those who would not hear his voice will hear the Fire's. The continuity is severe and just. A life is, among other things, a history of what one was willing to hear.

**Why "From Far Off" Is Part of the Mercy of the Warning**

The verse is still, for its first hearers and for us, a warning, not a tour. It describes what has not yet happened. The distance in the verse — Hell seeing from far off — has a counterpart in the present: the reader is still far off from it. The sound is not yet in the room. There is time to become a person whom the Fire does not see as its own.

The Prophet ﷺ used to seek refuge from the Fire in the prayer itself, in a supplication reported by al-Bukhārī (no. 832) and Muslim (no. 588):

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of the grave, and I seek refuge in You from the tribulation of the False Messiah, and I seek refuge in You from the trials of life and death. O God, I seek refuge in You from sin and from debt."* (al-Bukhārī 832; Muslim 589, with close wordings)

And more directly:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the Fire."* (Abū Dāwūd 792; al-Nasāʾī; authenticated)

The servants of the Compassionate, later in this sūrah, will make that refuge a part of their character: "Our Lord! Avert the punishment of Hell from us." Verse 12 is why they do. They have heard, while hearing is still a choice, what the raging and roaring would mean if they heard it then.

**A Note on Speech About the Unseen**

The verse also trains a way of speaking about Hell that is neither denial nor relish. Denial says the Blaze is a metaphor for bad conscience. Relish describes infernal detail as if the speaker were glad of it. The Qur'an does neither. It states, with a few nouns of sound and sight, that the prepared Fire is real, perceiving, and terrible, and then it moves on to the human choices that lead toward it or away. A believer who has to teach this verse — to a child, to a student, to himself — should copy that economy. Say what is there. Do not add theater. Do not subtract reality. Then spend the longer breath, as the sūrah will, on the people who walk humbly and sleep in prostration, for whom another dwelling has been prepared.

**When It Sees Them from a Far Place**

*Idhā raʾat-hum min makānin baʿīdin samiʿū lahā taghayyuẓan wa-zafīrā* — when it sees them from a far place, they hear it raging and roaring. The Fire is given sight and sound. *Taghayyuẓ* is fury; *zafīr* is a roar, an exhalation of wrath. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr take the seeing as real, as God may give perception to what He will; others allow a language of terror. Either way the verse is not a metaphor for mood. The prepared (*aʿtadnā*, 25:11) is awake. The people who denied the Hour as a fable (25:11) will be seen by what they denied before they arrive at it. Distance does not protect. The servants who ask *iṣrif ʿannā* (25:65) have believed this seeing while it was still a recitation. Belief while it is recitation is the only belief that can become a turning-away. Belief when the roar is heard is not belief. It is *thubūr*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:13

> **And when they are cast bound together into a narrow place thereof, they will, there and then, call to be destroyed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Distance to Constriction**

Verse 12 placed the Fire far off, seeing and roaring. Verse 13 closes the distance. *Wa-idhā ulqū minhā makānan ḍayyiqan* — "And when they are cast into a narrow place thereof." They are not invited. They are *ulqū*, thrown, the same verb the objectors used when they demanded that a treasure be "cast" to the Prophet ﷺ (25:8). What they wanted cast to him was gold. What is cast to them is themselves, into tightness.

Hell in the popular imagination is often a vast inferno, an open waste of flame. The Qur'an here emphasizes the opposite quality: *ḍayyiq*, narrow, constricted, a space that does not admit a full breath. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Qurṭubī records, described that constriction with the metaphor of a spearhead forced into the shaft of a spear. The image is of metal driven into a socket too small for it — pressure without remainder. The punishment is not only heat. It is the end of room.

This matches other Qur'anic language about the chest in this world:

> *"And whoever God wills to guide, He opens his breast to Islam; and whoever He wills to leave astray, He makes his breast tight and constricted, as though he were climbing into the sky."* (Qur'an 6:125)

The tightness of the next world is kin to the tightness a person already makes in himself when he refuses the opening of the chest. Eschatology, here, is moral psychology completed.

**Bound Together: Chains as Company**

*Muqarranīn* — "bound together." Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī record two readings. One: people will be chained to their individual satans. The other: their hands will be chained to their necks. Both may be true at once; the word allows companions in the bond and the binding of the body. The Qur'an elsewhere pairs a person with his shayṭān as a coupled mate:

> *"Whoever turns away from the remembrance of the Compassionate, We assign to him a devil, and he is for him a companion."* (Qur'an 43:36)

> *"And you will see the guilty on that Day bound together in shackles."* (Qur'an 14:49)

The company a person chose in denial becomes the company he cannot leave in punishment. Those who, in verses 27–29, will lament a friend who led them from the Reminder are already, in this verse, bound to what they chose. Freedom in this world includes the freedom to chain oneself. The next world discloses the chain.

There is a social warning in this that does not need the Fire to be useful today. People bind themselves together around a mockery, a vice, a rumor, a market in harm. The bond feels like friendship. Verse 13 says that some bonds are rehearsals. Choose company as a person who may have to keep it.

**Calling for Thubūr: Destruction as a Last Request**

*Daʿaw hunālika thubūrā* — "they will, there and then, call to be destroyed." *Thubūr* is destruction, ruin, the wish to be finished. Al-Ṭabarī notes that it is also thought of as a kind of lamentation, so that the cry is "Woe!" as much as "Let us be annihilated." In either hearing, the request is the same: stop this by stopping us.

The wish for non-being is the final inversion of the human creature. In the world, even the miserable generally wish to continue, to sleep and try again. Here the continuation is the punishment, and so the punished ask for the one thing God will not give the people of the Fire: an exit by extinction.

> *"They will cry out, 'O Mālik, let your Lord put an end to us!' He will say, 'You will remain.'"* (Qur'an 43:77)

> *"His shall be Hell, wherein he neither dies nor lives."* (Qur'an 20:74)

Neither death nor life: not the rest of death, not the freshness of life. Verse 13 is the moment they discover that annihilation, which they once used as a philosophy — "nothing destroys us except time" (45:24) — is the mercy they will not be granted. Those who denied the Hour because they wanted to be unaccountable now want to be unexistent. Both wishes are refused.

**"There and Then": The Collapse of Delay**

*Hunālika* — there and then — is a word of place and of moment. No intermediary. No committee. No appeal that buys a year. The constriction, the binding, and the cry arrive together. The Quraysh's whole method in this sūrah has been delay by description: call him a fabricator, a pupil, a fable-reciter, a hungry man, a bewitched man — anything to put off the Hour. *Hunālika* is the end of that method.

For a reader still in the wide place of this world, the verse is not a demand to feel the spearhead. It is a demand to use the room while there is room. Repentance, prayer, the unbinding of a bad companionship, the opening of the chest to the Criterion — these are wide-place acts. The servants of the Compassionate will later pray, "Avert the punishment of Hell from us," while they still have a night in which to stand and prostrate. That prayer is this verse heard in time.

**Bound, Constricted, Calling for Thubūr**

*Maqarranīn* — bound together, coupled, chained. The companions they chose (25:27–29) become the companions they cannot unchoose. *Makānan ḍayyiqan* — a narrow place. The earth was wide (the walking of 25:63 is still possible). They used the width to mock a man who walked in markets. The width is withdrawn. *Daʿaw hunālika thubūrā* — they call, there and then, for destruction. Not for a second chance. For annihilation. The request of verse 14 will be refused: do not call for one destruction; call for many. Al-Qurṭubī and others: *thubūr* is ruin, woe, a wish not to exist. The people who treated the Hour as a fable (25:5) now treat existence as the fable they wish would end. The servants who ask *iṣrif ʿannā ʿadhāba jahannam* (25:65) are asking before the place is narrow. That is the only time the asking is a way and not a *thubūr*.

**When They Are Cast into It, Bound, into a Narrow Place**

*Wa-idhā ulqū minhā makānan ḍayyiqan muqarranīna daʿaw hunālika thubūrā* — when they are cast into a narrow place thereof, bound together, they call there and then for destruction. *Muqarranīn*: the companions they chose become the companions they cannot unchoose. The earth was wide; they used the width to mock a man who walked in markets. The width is withdrawn. *Hunālika* is the end of delay-by-description. They called him fabricator, pupil, fable-reciter — anything to put off the Hour. *Thubūr* is ruin, a wish not to exist. People who treated the Hour as a fable now treat existence as the fable they wish would end. Use the wide place while it is wide: repentance, unbinding of a bad companionship, opening the chest to the Criterion. The servants’ *iṣrif ʿannā* is this verse heard in time.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:14

> **"Call not this Day for one destruction, but call for many destructions!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer That Multiplies the Cry**

Verse 13 ended with the thrown, the bound, the constricted calling for *thubūr* — destruction, woe, an end. Verse 14 is the reply, in the second person plural, spoken over them: *lā tadʿū al-yawma thubūran wāḥidan wa-dʿū thubūran kathīrā* — "Call not this Day for one destruction, but call for many destructions." The request is not refused by silence. It is refused by expansion. You asked for one ending. The Day you have entered is not a single ending. It is a series that does not series out.

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī explain: the punishment will not be a solitary event; it will be enduring and come in many forms, and there will be no relief from it, so that those experiencing it will call out to be destroyed — or call out "Woe!" — over and over again. If *thubūr* is read as the lament "Woe!", the verse becomes: do not say "Woe!" once; say it many times. Either reading yields the same structure. The cry is made repetitive because the cause of the cry is repetitive.

**Why One Death Would Have Been a Mercy**

A single destruction is, in human experience, a limit. The execution ends the fear of execution. The last breath ends the pain that asked for it. The people of the Fire are asking for that limit. The reply tells them that the grammar of this Day does not contain it. Other verses name the same unremittingness from other angles:

> *"As often as their skins are consumed, We shall replace them with other skins, that they may taste the punishment."* (Qur'an 4:56)

> *"They will not be finished so as to die, nor will its punishment be lightened for them. Thus do We recompense every disbeliever."* (Qur'an 35:36)

> *"And they who disbelieve will have the Fire of Hell. It is not decreed for them that they should die, nor will its torment be lightened for them."* (Qur'an 35:36, same verse)

Skin replaced so that tasting can continue; death not decreed; torment not lightened. "Many destructions" is that doctrine in the form of an address. It is not said to the reader as a taunt. It is said, inside the scene, to people who still think in the old arithmetic: one crime, one death, account closed. The Hour's arithmetic is not that. A life spent denying the Hour was a life of many acts, many mornings of refusal, many descriptions of the messenger, many mockeries. The reply matches plurality with plurality.

**Who Speaks?**

The verse does not name the speaker. It may be God. It may be the keepers of Hell, the angels who, in 43:77, answer "O Mālik, let your Lord put an end to us" with "You will remain." It may be the Fire itself, which verse 12 has already endowed with perception. The tradition allows the ambiguity because the authority behind the sentence does not depend on a nametag. No one in that place is in a position to offer a different sentence.

What matters for the living is that the speech is public in the Book now. We are allowed to overhear a reply we still have time not to earn. That is the odd mercy of eschatological verses: they publish the closed court while the court is still open.

**The Moral Logic of Intensity**

Some hearts recoil from this verse and call it excess. The recoil should be heard with respect, then answered with the sūrah's own logic. The people addressed here are not those who sinned and repented. They are those who denied the Hour, who called the Criterion a lie and a fable, who wanted a messenger of treasure, who will, a few verses from now, be shown that their works have become scattered dust. They were offered a warner unto the worlds. They answered with *ẓulm* and *zūr*. "Many destructions" is not a temper. It is the form justice takes when a creature has used a whole life to refuse the purpose of life.

The Qur'an is also, in the same chapter, the Book of a God who replaces evil deeds with good for those who repent (25:70), who is Forgiving, Merciful (25:6), who describes servants that pray to be spared this very punishment (25:65). Intensity of warning and width of mercy are not two religions. They are one religion addressing two possible uses of a lifetime.

**What a Living Person Does with a Verse Like This**

One does not dramatize it. One does not deny it. One lets it do the small, severe work it was sent to do: to make triviality harder. A person who has heard "call for many destructions" should find it slightly more difficult to treat the Hour as a metaphor, to treat a mockery of revelation as banter, to treat a bound companionship in sin as a lifestyle. The verse is a weight. The way to carry a weight of this kind is not to perform despair. It is to increase the acts that the rest of the sūrah will praise: humility on the earth, prayer at night, a just mean in spending, repentance that is actually repentance.

The Prophet ﷺ, who knew these verses as a man knows his own pulse, still smiled, still joked without lying, still raced ʿĀʾishah, still sat with the poor. Severity in the unseen did not freeze him. It ordered him. That is the model. Hear verse 14. Then, like him, become a mercy unto the worlds — beginning with the world of your own tongue, which has not yet been reduced to calling for *thubūr*.

**Do Not Call Today for One Destruction**

*Lā tadʿū l-yawma thubūran wāḥidan wa-dʿū thubūran kathīrā* — do not call today for one destruction; call for many destructions. The answer to verse 13’s cry is not comfort. It is a multiplication of the thing cried for. One *thubūr* would be a single end. *Kathīrā* is the refusal of that mercy of ending. Al-Rāzī: the kinds of punishment are many, so the cries are many. Ibn Kathīr: it will not be lessened, so the call will be repeated. The living reader is not being told to practice despair. He is being told that despair, if it is delayed until that Day, will not be a door. The door is now, and it is not *thubūr*. It is *tawbah* (25:70), *duʿāʾ* (25:65, 25:77), a way with the messenger (25:27). People who treat self-destruction as a philosophy are rehearsing the wrong cry. The servants who ask that the punishment be averted are rehearsing the right one. Same Day coming. Two tongues in training.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:15

> **Say, "Is that better, or the Garden of everlastingness which is promised to the reverent?" It is for them a reward and a journey's end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Restores Proportion**

After the constriction, the binding, the cry for destruction, and the reply that multiplies the cry, the sūrah does not continue in Hell. It turns, with the command *qul* — "Say" — and asks a question of comparison: *adhālika khayrun am jannatu al-khuld* — "Is that better, or the Garden of everlastingness?" The question is not information-seeking. No one is expected to say that the Blaze is better. The question is a restoration of the power to choose while choosing is still possible. It puts the two ends in one sentence so that a life can be aimed.

This is a Qur'anic habit. After terror, a fork.

> *"Is the one who is cast into the Fire better, or the one who comes secure on the Day of Resurrection?"* (Qur'an 41:40)

> *"Then is he who was a believer like him who was a defiant sinner? They are not equal."* (Qur'an 32:18)

The Prophet ﷺ is told to *say* the question, which means the comparison is part of the prophetic office. Warning without the Garden is half a warning. The same man who grasps people by their belts away from the Fire (al-Bukhārī 6482) is the man who describes what they are being grasped *toward*.

**Khuld: Everlastingness as the Quality of the Place**

*Jannatu al-khuld* — the Garden of everlastingness. *Khuld* is from the same root as *khālid*, "abiding," which the Qur'an uses for the inhabitants of both Paradise and Hell. It means to stay, to remain, and when used of the Hereafter it means to remain in a manner that does not run out. The Garden is not a holiday from which one is recalled. It is a home whose lease is not written in years.

The word also answers, by implication, the philosophy of verse 11's deniers. They denied the Hour because they wanted the world to be the only garden, and they knew that garden dies. *Khuld* is the name of a garden that does not. To prefer the world to this is not worldliness in the sense of enjoying a lawful fruit. It is a failure of comparison, which is exactly what the verse is repairing.

**Promised to the Reverent: Who the Offer Is For**

*Allatī wuʿida al-muttaqūn* — "which is promised to the reverent." The recipients are not named as the clever, the noble, the people of stone houses, or the people of treasure. They are *al-muttaqūn*, those who have *taqwā*. Taqwā is the care that keeps a person from God's displeasure — the walking of a dangerous path with attention, the driving in a storm with both hands on the wheel. It is awareness that has learned to obey.

> *"And hasten to forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden as wide as the heavens and the earth, prepared for the reverent."* (Qur'an 3:133)

The Garden is *promised*. A promise from God is not a campaign pledge. The theologians will argue, in the next verse, about whether a promise binds God as obligation or as the form of His own truthful will. What a worshipper needs is already here: the offer is public, the condition is named, the One promising is the One of verse 2 who does not take a partner and does not fail in measure.

**A Reward and a Journey's End**

*Kānat lahum jazāʾan wa-maṣīrā* — "It is for them a reward and a journey's end." Two nouns. *Jazāʾ* is recompense, the matching of a life with what it deserves, though in the case of Paradise the matching is always, by the Prophet's ﷺ teaching, exceeded by gift. Muslim (no. 207) records:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds alone." They said: Not even you, O Messenger of God? He said: "Not even I, unless God covers me with His mercy."* (Muslim 207; al-Bukhārī 5673 with close wording)

So *jazāʾ* here is reward without the illusion of wages that purchase God. The Garden is given as recompense, and the recompense is mercy.

*Maṣīr* is a place to which one arrives — a journey's end, an outcome, a becoming. The word is used in the Qur'an for both ends:

> *"And your journey's end is to the Fire."* (Qur'an 14:30, in sense of *maṣīr*)

> *"And the journey's end of the reverent is to the Garden."* (as here)

A life is a road. The question "Is that better, or the Garden?" is a question about destination, not about mood. People often choose a road by its scenery and are surprised by its end. The verse puts the ends first, so that the scenery can be judged in their light.

**How to Hear a Comparison Without Turning Religion into a Bargain**

There is a cheap hearing of this verse: be good in order to be paid with a garden. The cheaper hearing produces a hireling's worship, which the tradition has always ranked below the worship of love and gratitude. A more accurate hearing is: the two ends are real; preferring the Blaze is insane; preferring the Garden is sanity; and sanity, once restored, still has to walk the road of the servants of the Compassionate, who do not reduce their Lord to a paymaster. They walk humbly, they pray at night, they spend justly, *and* the Garden is their *maṣīr*.

The comparison also has a use in the middle of an ordinary temptation. Temptation is always a local improvement: this word would feel better, this glance, this fraud, this extra that is not ours. Verse 15 lifts the eyes from the local to the last. "Is that better?" can be asked of a thousand small things. The Garden of everlastingness is not only a future estate. It is a present standard of comparison, the way a traveler in a desert judges a mirage by remembering that a real city has wells.

**Say: Is That Better, or the Garden of Everlastingness?**

*Qul a-dhālika khayrun am jannatu l-khuld* — say: is that better, or the Garden of everlastingness? *Dhālika* points at the Fire just described, or at the whole package of denial’s outcome. The question is not information-seeking. It is a forced ranking. *Allatī wuʿida l-muttaqūn* — which the reverent were promised. *Kānat lahum jazāʾan wa-maṣīrā* — it has been for them a recompense and a becoming-place. Promise, recompense, destination: three names of one mercy. The *muttaqūn* are not yet fully painted; verses 63–76 will paint them. Already they have a promised house. The Quraysh had demanded palaces as a proof (25:10). God offers a Garden as a promise to people of taqwā, not as a spectacle for people of *nufūr*. The Prophet ﷺ described the Garden until the companions wept and longed. Verse 15 is that description as a comparative question. Every day a person ranks. This verse tells him which ranking is sane.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:16

> **Therein they shall have whatsoever they will, abiding. It is a binding promise upon your Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Whatsoever They Will: Desire at Last Educated**

*Lahum fīhā mā yashāʾūn* — "Therein they shall have whatsoever they will." The Garden of verse 15 is now described from the inside. Its inhabitants are not rationed. Will — *mashīʾah*, what one wishes — is met. The Qur'an repeats this hospitality in several places:

> *"For them therein is whatever they will. That is the recompense of the good-doers."* (Qur'an 39:34)

> *"And those who believe and do righteous deeds — in the meadows of the Gardens. They will have whatever they will in the presence of their Lord. That is the great bounty."* (Qur'an 42:22)

> *"They will have whatever they will, abiding. Your Lord is a sure fulfiller of prayers."* (Qur'an 25:16, this verse)

> *"Therein they will have whatever they wish, and with Us is more."* (Qur'an 50:35)

"Whatsoever they will" is not the chaos of an untrained appetite let loose. The people who arrive there are the reverent of verse 15. Their will has been schooled by the Criterion. What a *muttaqī* wants, when wanting is finally free, is not the harm he used to want. The Garden is the place where desire and good coincide. That coincidence is one definition of bliss.

This world is the place where they do not coincide, which is why *taqwā* is needed — the care that refuses a wish that would cost the soul. The verse, read from here, is not only a promise. It is a diagnosis of the present: you do not now have whatever you will, and that is a mercy, because you do not yet always will what would keep you.

**Abiding: The Gift That Does Not Expire**

*Khālidīn* — abiding. The word was already inside *jannatu al-khuld*. It is repeated as a state of the people, not only a quality of the place. They remain. The fear that spoils every earthly garden — that the afternoon will end, that the body will fail, that the beloved will leave, that the kingdom will be inherited by someone else — is absent. Abiding is not boredom. Boredom is a function of hunger that cannot be met and of time that threatens. When will is met and time does not threaten, remaining is the name of peace.

The Prophet ﷺ described an announcement that will be made in Paradise, in a ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (no. 6544) and Muslim (no. 2829):

> *"It will be said: 'O people of Paradise, you will have health and not be sick; you will live and not die; you will be young and not grow old; you will be in bliss and not be miserable.'"* (Muslim 2837; similar in al-Bukhārī)

That announcement is this verse in spoken form. Abiding, unpacked into health, life, youth, and bliss, each with its negation of the old tax.

**A Binding Promise: Masʾūl and the Argument About Obligation**

*Kāna ʿalā rabbika waʿdan masʾūlā* — "It is a binding promise upon your Lord." *Masʾūl* is a word that has generated a careful theological literature. Literally it means "asked for," "that which is inquired about," or "that for which one is held to account." Al-Ṭabarī records the reading that it is something human beings have asked for, rather than something God is obligated to do. Al-Rāzī notes the connection to the angels' supplication:

> *"Our Lord, make them enter Gardens of Eden that Thou hast promised them, and whoever was righteous among their fathers, their spouses, and their progeny."* (Qur'an 40:8)

Al-Qurṭubī interprets *masʾūl* as obligation in the sense of a promise that one keeps rather than a debt that one owes. The Ashʿarite concern is famous: God's will is supreme, and He is not "obligated" in the way a creature is obligated by a law above him. The Muʿtazilite and many other theologians read verses like this as indicating that those who receive Paradise are those who deserve it, and that God will not break His word.

The two concerns can be held without violence to the verse. God is not a debtor. God is also not a being whose promises are provisional. The Qur'an states the second with a clarity that does not require a school:

> *"The word of your Lord has been fulfilled in truth and in justice. None can change His words."* (Qur'an 6:115)

> *"Indeed, God does not fail in His promise."* (Qur'an 3:9)

"Upon your Lord" — *ʿalā rabbika* — is the language of a guarantee. A worshipper who is not a theologian is being told: this is not a hope you invented. It is a promise located on God. You may work, fail, repent, and work again, without the additional terror that the Garden might be a metaphor God is free to withdraw from the reverent.

**What "Whatsoever They Will" Does Not License Here**

The verse is about *therein* — *fīhā*. It is not a license for "whatever I will" as a rule of this life. The sūrah will later praise those who, when they spend, are neither prodigal nor miserly, and those who do not take caprice as a god. The education of will happens here; the satisfaction of will happens there. Confusing the two is how a person becomes, in verse 43's language, a worshipper of his own *hawā*.

A practical way to live the verse is to practice wanting what will still be wantable in a Garden: pardon, the face of God, the company of the righteous, the untying of a knot in the chest, a fruit that does not come from injustice. Wanting those things now is already a rehearsal of *mā yashāʾūn*. Wanting what could not survive the Criterion is a rehearsal of the other end.

And because the promise is *masʾūl*, the believer's prayer for Paradise is not presumption. It is the asking that the word itself contains. The Prophet ﷺ taught his companions to ask for the highest of it — *al-Firdaws* — because it is the middle of Paradise and the highest of it, and above it is the Throne (al-Bukhārī 2790). Asking is part of the promise's own structure. Verse 16, heard well, makes a person both more ambitious in hope and more careful in desire.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:17

> **And on the Day He gathers them and that which they worship apart from God, He will say, "Was it you who caused these servants of Mine to go astray, or did they [themselves] go astray from the way?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Gathering of Worshippers and of What Was Worshipped**

The scene shifts from the Garden's inner hospitality to a court. *Yawma yaḥshuruhum wa-mā yaʿbudūna min dūni Allāh* — "And on the Day He gathers them and that which they worship apart from God." Two populations are assembled: the human beings who directed worship away from God, and the objects of that worship. Al-Qurṭubī lists what those objects may be: angels, jinn, idols, even Jesus. The grammar *mā yaʿbudūn* — "that which they worship" — is wide enough for persons, spirits, and things. Idols will be made to speak, the commentators say, just as the parts of the human body will be made to speak:

> *"On this Day We shall seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will bear witness to what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 36:65)

If a hand can testify, a worshipped thing can be asked a question. The court of the Hour does not lack witnesses, and it does not lack defendants who thought they would be silent stone.

**The Question God Asks the Worshipped**

*A-antum aḍlaltum ʿibādī hāʾulāʾi am hum ḍallū al-sabīl* — "Was it you who caused these servants of Mine to go astray, or did they [themselves] go astray from the way?" The question is addressed to the objects of worship, not first to the worshippers. Its nearest Qur'anic twin is the question put to Jesus:

> *"And when God said, 'O Jesus, son of Mary! Didst thou say unto mankind, Take me and my mother as gods apart from God?' He said, 'Glory be to Thee! It is not for me to utter that to which I have no right.'"* (Qur'an 5:116)

The form is a form of justice that even a child recognizes: before the followers are sentenced, the leader is asked whether he led. God already knows the answer. The question is asked so that it will be heard — by the worshipped, by the worshippers, and by us who still have time to choose what we worship.

Notice *ʿibādī* — "these servants of Mine." Even in their astrayness they are named as God's servants. The possessive is a claim that was never cancelled by their misdirection. They were His, and they gave themselves to what was not Him. The tragedy of shirk is not that God lost property. It is that servants misfiled themselves.

**Responsibility Split Without Being Evaded**

The question offers two options: you led them, or they went. Verse 18 will have the worshipped take the second, with a third element: God granted the worshippers and their fathers enjoyment until they forgot. The theology that emerges is not a hunt for a single scapegoat. Leaders can mislead. Followers can choose to be misled. Ease can anesthetize. All three will appear. None will be allowed to cancel the others.

This is why the verse matters for ordinary moral life, far below the worship of idols. When a community goes wrong, the Hour's question is already the right question: did the admired ones lead, or did the admirers wander because wandering suited them? Human courts often pick one and ignore the other. God asks both, in the hearing of both.

**False Worship as a Relationship That Will Be Cross-Examined**

Worship, in this verse, is not only a ritual. It is a relationship that creates a file. Whoever you exalt — a person, a party, a dead saint beyond what God allowed, a living celebrity, a desire — you are creating a pair that God can gather. The gathering is the disclosure of the pair. Many relationships that felt like devotion will, in that light, look like a mistake about who owns the servant.

The Prophet ﷺ warned against the inner mechanics of this pairing when he said:

> *"Do not exaggerate in praising me as the Christians exaggerated in praising the son of Mary. I am only a servant. So say: the servant of God and His messenger."* (al-Bukhārī 3445)

He is protecting his community from becoming, in the sense of this verse, people who will be gathered with "that which they worship apart from God," even if the "that" were himself. Servanthood, his own title in 25:1, is the safe name. Any worship that cannot survive the question of verse 17 is already too much.

**Hearing the Question While the Pair Can Still Be Broken**

The practical work of the verse is an audit of attachments. What do I treat as if it could not be wrong? What do I follow after the Criterion has already spoken against it? What ease have I let become a proof? Those questions, asked now, are verse 17 performed in private, before the gathering makes them public. Breaking a false pair is called repentance. The sūrah will later say that God replaces evil deeds with good for those who repent, believe, and work righteousness. The worshipped things of this verse cannot offer that replacement. Only the One who calls the straying "My servants" can.

**Did You Lead My Servants Astray, or Did They Stray?**

The question is God’s, and it is fair to both sides of a false worship. Idols, angels, ʿUzayr, ʿĪsā, saints, desires, stars: whatever was taken besides Him will be asked. The worshipped who had no will — stones — will not answer as the living do. The worshipped who were righteous — prophets, angels — will deny the cult. The straying will be shown to have been the worshippers’ own *hawā* (25:43). *ʿIbādī hāʾulāʾi* — these My servants. Even in rebuke God names them His. Ownership is not cancelled by their infidelity. That is a terror and a mercy. Terror, because you cannot leave His jurisdiction by leaving His worship. Mercy, because the Name *Rabb* still covers the one being asked about. Al-Rāzī notes that the interrogation is for the establishment of proof, not for God’s need to learn. The Day makes public what verse 6 already knew in secret. Practically, the verse trains a person not to recruit a righteous name for a crooked path. If the righteous will disown you then, let them disown the use now.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:18

> **They will say, "Glory be to Thee! It does not befit us to take protectors apart from Thee! But Thou didst grant them and their fathers enjoyment until they forgot the Reminder. And they were a people ruined.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Worshipped Begin with Tasbīḥ**

The objects of worship answer first with *subḥānaka* — "Glory be to Thee!" The word is a clearing of God's field from every unbecoming ascription. Before they explain, they exalt. Jesus uses the same first word in 5:116. Angels use it when they are slandered with the idea that they would disdain God's command. *Subḥānaka* is the reflex of any being that knows God and has been asked whether it stole His right.

Then the denial: *mā kāna yanbaghī lanā an nattakhidha min dūnika min awliyāʾ* — "It does not befit us to take protectors apart from Thee." *Yanbaghī* is "it is not becoming, it does not belong, it is not our place." *Awliyāʾ* are protectors, allies, patrons, friends in the strong sense. The worshipped swear that they themselves never took such patrons besides God — so how could they have invited others to take *them*? Al-Qurṭubī notes that *awliyāʾ* can also mean friends or saints. The irony is sharp: human beings took created things as protectors, while those created things, if they can speak, protest that they never even allowed themselves such a taking.

**Enjoyment as a Road to Forgetting**

The explanation they offer is not "we ordered it." It is: *mattaʿtahum wa-ābāʾahum ḥattā nasū al-dhikr* — "Thou didst grant them and their fathers enjoyment until they forgot the Reminder." Enjoyment — *matāʿ*, the use of the goods of the world — is here named as a Divine grant. God gave them a long afternoon. The afternoon became a reason to forget the evening.

The Qur'an is not against enjoyment. It is against enjoyment that eats remembrance.

> *"But you took them in mockery, till it made you forget My remembrance, and you used to laugh at them."* (Qur'an 23:110)

> *"They forgot God, so He made them forget themselves."* (Qur'an 59:19)

> *"And do not be like those who forgot God, so He made them forget their own souls. Those are the defiantly disobedient."* (Qur'an 59:19)

Forgetting the Reminder is not a lapse of memory in the medical sense. It is a displacement. The *dhikr* — the remembrance of God, and also, as a name, the Qur'an itself — was crowded out by what they were given to enjoy. Fathers are mentioned because the crowding became a culture. A child inherited a full table and an empty reminder, and called the inheritance civilization.

**"They Were a People Ruined"**

*Wa-kānū qawman būrā* — "And they were a people ruined." *Būr* is land that is fallow, lifeless, uncultivated, spoilt. A people *būr* are a people whose inner ground has gone out of cultivation. The agricultural image is exact after "enjoyment." They consumed. They did not plant remembrance. The field, at the end, is waste.

The worshipped, in saying this, are not gloating. They are locating the ruin in the worshippers' own history: grant, enjoyment, forgetting, ruin. The sequence is one of the Qur'an's most repeated moral physiologies. Provision is a test that can look like a proof of being right. The people of gardens in Sūrah al-Kahf walked that sequence. The people of Sabaʾ walked it. Qārūn walked it. Verse 18 is the sequence spoken from the witness stand of the very things that were over-loved.

**Who Is Exonerated, and Who Is Not**

If the worshipped are angels or righteous beings — Jesus, ʿUzayr in the accusations of 9:30, the saints of a later folk-religion — this verse is their acquittal. They did not ask for altars. If the worshipped are idols, the speech is the speech God gives lifeless things on a Day when He makes them speak, and the acquittal is of a different kind: they never had the power to mislead, because they never had power at all (verse 3). If the worshipped are satans or misleading humans, other verses will not give them this clean *subḥānaka*; they will share the burden. The present verse, in the reading of the commentators who take it of angels and the righteous, is specifically the innocence of those who were worshipped without consent.

The worshippers, in every reading, are not innocent. Forgetting was their act. Ruin was their state. The grant of enjoyment, which they might wish to use as an excuse — "You made us rich, what did You expect?" — is turned into evidence. Mercy was used as a sedative.

**The Living Application: Prosperity as a Test of Memory**

A person who has been given enjoyment — health, a salary, a city that works, children who sleep through the night — is in the middle of verse 18's sequence, not at its end. The question is whether the grant is conducting him toward remembrance or toward forgetting. The Prophet ﷺ taught a sentence that keeps the field in cultivation:

> *"There are two blessings in which many people are cheated: health and free time."* (al-Bukhārī 6412)

Cheated, because the grant is spent on what makes one forget. The remedy is not to throw the grant away. It is to attach *dhikr* to it: the Name over the meal, the prayer in the time of ease, the alms from the surplus, the refusal to take the surplus as a protector. Protectors, this verse says, are not to be taken apart from God — not by the worshipped, and not by the worshipper. Ease that becomes a *walī* is already the beginning of *būr*.

**They Will Say: Glory Be to Thee**

The righteous among the wrongly worshipped — ʿĪsā, the angels, ʿUzayr as some claimed — will begin with *subḥānaka*. They clear God before they clear themselves. *Mā kāna yanbaghī lanā an nattakhidha min dūnika min awliyāʾ* — it was not for us to take protectors apart from Thee. Then the diagnosis of the worshippers: *wa-lākin mattaʿtahum wa-ābāʾahum ḥattā nasū l-dhikr* — but Thou didst grant them and their fathers enjoyment until they forgot the Reminder. Ease plus time plus forgotten *dhikr* equals *būr*, ruin, a people gone to waste. The Prophet ﷺ sought refuge from knowledge that does not benefit and from a heart that does not humble (Muslim 2722). Verse 18 is that refuge as a history of nations. Enjoyment is not the crime. Forgetting under enjoyment is the crime. The servants who thank in the succession of night and day (25:62) are people who have refused that forgetting while the grant is still in their hands.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:19

> **They have denied you in what you say; so you cannot avert [it] or [find] help." And whosoever among you does wrong, We shall make him taste a great punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Court Turns to the Worshippers**

Verse 18 was the speech of the worshipped. Verse 19 is the speech that follows, addressed now to those who had worshipped: *fa-qad kadhdhabūkum bi-mā taqūlūn* — "They have denied you in what you say." The pair is broken in public. The followers had a story: these beings, these idols, these patrons will intercede, will recognize us, will stand with us. The patrons have just said *subḥānaka* and located the ruin in the followers. God announces the obvious: they have given you the lie.

Al-Qurṭubī records this as the primary reading: the words are spoken to those who worshipped things apart from God; it is they who cannot avert the punishment or find any help against God. He also records another opinion: that the words are spoken to the Prophet ﷺ and refer to the disbelievers' rejection of him. A third reading puts "so they cannot avert" in the third person, the "they" being the idolaters. The first reading is the most continuous with verses 17 and 18. The court that asked the worshipped now tells the worshippers that their testimony has collapsed.

**No Averting, No Help**

*Fa-mā tastaṭīʿūna ṣarfan wa-lā naṣrā* — "so you cannot avert [it] or [find] help." *Ṣarf* is turning something away: a ransom, a trick, a redirection of the blow. *Naṣr* is help, the arrival of an ally. The two nouns cover the whole of what a desperate person looks for — a way to deflect, and a person to stand with. Both are gone.

Al-Rāzī notes that *ṣarf* is also read without an implied object, so that it would mean "to turn oneself," that is, to turn in repentance; or that they will be unable to engage in any ruse. On the Day described here, repentance as a turning has closed. The turning that would have been accepted in the wide place of the world is the turning they did not make when enjoyment had not yet become ruin.

> *"And beware of a Day when no soul will avail another soul at all, nor will intercession be accepted from it, nor will compensation be taken from it, nor will they be helped."* (Qur'an 2:48)

Verse 19 is 2:48 in the specific case of the idolaters whose patrons have just testified against them. The theology of intercession in Islam is not denied by this. Intercession, where it is real, is by God's leave, for those with whom He is pleased, and it is not owned by the worshipped as a right the worshipper can cash. Taking protectors apart from God is precisely the attempt to own intercession in advance. That attempt ends here.

**"Whosoever Among You Does Wrong"**

The verse then widens. *Wa-man yaẓlim minkum nudhiqhu ʿadhāban kabīrā* — "And whosoever among you does wrong, We shall make him taste a great punishment." The address is no longer only to the gathered idolaters of the scene. *Minkum* — among you — faces the living audience, including the community of the Prophet ﷺ. Wrongdoing (*ẓulm*) is not sealed as a pagan specialty. Whoever does it will taste.

The widening is a mercy and a severity together. A mercy, because it refuses the smugness of "that court is for them." A severity, because the great punishment is not a museum exhibit about ancient cults. *Nudhiqhu* — We shall make him taste — is the verb of a future that can still be avoided, and of a future that will not be avoided by tribal membership.

*Ẓulm* in the Qur'an is a wide word. Its peak is shirk:

> *"Indeed, association is a great wrong."* (Qur'an 31:13)

But it includes every putting of a thing in the wrong place: a right withheld, a word misplaced, a person consumed. The "great punishment" scales with the wrong, and the verse does not need to itemize. It needs to keep the door of fear attached to the door of hope that verse 6 already opened: He is Forgiving, Merciful. Tasting is for those who persist. Those who put the thing back in its place — that is repentance — are not the subject of this last clause.

**What It Feels Like to Be Denied by What One Trusted**

The human core of the verse is a betrayal. There is no betrayal quite like the silence, or the contradiction, of what one had exalted. People experience small versions of it when a party discards them, when a patron pretends not to know them, when a public they served does not serve them back. Those small versions are tutors. They teach, if one will learn, not cynicism but tawḥīd: do not put a created back where only God's back holds.

The Prophet ﷺ taught the positive form of this in the counsel to Ibn ʿAbbās already cited at verse 2:

> *"Know that if the whole community gathered to benefit you, they would benefit you only with something God had written for you; and if they gathered to harm you, they would harm you only with something God had written against you."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516)

Verse 19 is that counsel on the Day when the community that was supposed to benefit you has denied you. Help was never theirs to own. The tasting of a great punishment is what remains when a life has been built on the error that it was.

**They Will Deny You, and You Cannot Avert**

*Fa-qad kadhdhabūkum bimā taqūlūna* — they have denied you in what you say. The worshipped turn on the worshippers. Verse 17’s question is answered in the negative: we did not tell them to. *Fa-mā tastaṭīʿūna ṣarfan wa-lā naṣrā* — so you cannot avert, nor help. *Ṣarf* is a turning-away of the punishment (the very verb the servants will use in 25:65: *iṣrif*). *Naṣr* is the help they expected from their gods, their tribe, their *fulān*. Both fail. *Wa-man yaẓlim minkum nudhiqhu ʿadhāban kabīrā* — whoever among you does wrong, We shall make him taste a great punishment. *Minkum* keeps the warning inside the address. Wrongdoing is not only the Quraysh’s. The tasting is God’s, as the sending was God’s. Practically, the verse is a demolition of every “my people will stand for me” that has not first stood for the Criterion. The servants who walk without a need to be defended by a false pair have already received a *naṣr* of a different kind: the Lord as *naṣīr* in 25:31.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:20

> **And We sent not any messengers before thee but that they ate food and walked in the markets. And We made some of you a trial for others; will you be patient? And thy Lord is Seeing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Historical Answer to Verse 7**

The objection of verse 7 — "What ails this Messenger, who eats food and walks in the markets?" — is now answered with a law of history. *Wa-mā arsalnā qablaka min al-mursalīna illā innahum la-yaʾkulūna al-ṭaʿāma wa-yamshūna fī al-aswāq* — "And We sent not any messengers before thee but that they ate food and walked in the markets." The humanity that was treated as a disqualification is shown to be the job description.

The sentence is universal: *min al-mursalīn*, of the messengers, none excepted. Abraham hosted with a roasted calf (11:69). Moses asked for food and water in the wanderings. Jesus ate, and the Qur'an uses that eating against those who deified him:

> *"The Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger; messengers had passed away before him, and his mother was a truthful woman. They both ate food."* (Qur'an 5:75)

Eating, in 5:75, is proof that the eater is not God. Eating, in 25:20, is proof that the eater can still be a messenger. The two verses are one doctrine. God does not eat. Messengers do. The Quraysh wanted a messenger who resembled God in exemption from need. The Qur'an gives them messengers who resemble their people in need, and resemble the other messengers in office.

**Why Markets Belong in the Biography of Revelation**

Walking in the markets is repeated so that no one can claim that the true prophet is only the desert hermit or the palace sage. The market is the ordinary. A religion that cannot walk there cannot save anyone who has to. The Prophet ﷺ's later teaching about honest weights, the cursed usurer, the merchant who is with the prophets if he is truthful (al-Tirmidhī 1209), and the seller who conceals a defect — all of that is already justified by this verse. Revelation is not allergic to price.

There is also a humility in the image. A messenger in a market is a messenger who can be jostled, overcharged, recognized, and insulted. The Quraysh thought that was unbecoming. God thought it was the pattern.

**"Some of You a Trial for Others"**

*Wa-jaʿalnā baʿḍakum li-baʿḍin fitnah* — "And We made some of you a trial for others." Al-Rāzī understands the trial as, among other things, the scorn of the haughty Quraysh toward the impoverished Companions. They said, in the spirit of 46:11:

> *"Had it been good, they would not have outstripped us in [attaining] it."* (Qur'an 46:11)

Poverty in the first community became a veil over the eyes of the rich. The low social status of a Bilāl, a Khabbāb, a Ṣuhayb was, for the proud, evidence that the religion was not worth entering. The verse says that this arrangement was not an accident. God *made* some a trial for others. The poor were a test for the rich: will you kneel beside a man you used to own? The rich were a test for the poor: will you envy, hate, or lose patience? The healthy are a test for the sick, the sick for the healthy, the beautiful for those who are not, the gifted for the ordinary, the ordinary for the gifted.

Ibn Kathīr and others extend the *fitnah* to the general fact of differing gifts. Verse 2 said God created everything and then measured it. Verse 20 says the measurements are not equal, and the inequality is a test, not a ranking of souls.

**"Will You Be Patient?" as a Question That Is a Command**

*A-taṣbirūn* — "will you be patient?" Al-Qurṭubī notes that it is addressed to the believers, encouraging them to bear the taunts of the disbelievers and to endure the poverty and weakness they suffer; it is also understood implicitly as the command "Be patient." A question can be a command when the only sane answer is yes.

Patience here is not passivity. It is remaining on the road while the trial — the other person — does what trials do. The poor man remains in the prayer even when the rich man wrinkles his nose. The rich man remains in the prayer even when the poor man is the one who taught him the verse. Both are being asked the same question.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"How wonderful is the affair of the believer. There is good for him in all of it, and that is for no one except the believer. If something pleasing comes to him, he is grateful, and that is good for him. If something harmful comes to him, he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999)

Gratitude and patience are the two answers to the two faces of *fitnah*. Verse 20 asks for the second because the face in view is the hard one: insult, poverty, the market in which the messenger is not a king.

**"And Thy Lord Is Seeing"**

*Wa-kāna rabbuka baṣīrā* — "And thy Lord is Seeing." The Names close the verse as they closed verse 6. The Seeing here is not a threat only. It is the reason patience is rational. A trial that is not seen by anyone who can repay is just cruelty. A trial that is seen by the Lord who promised the Garden of everlastingness is a road. The poor Companion who walked in the same markets and ate the same kind of food as the Prophet ﷺ was seen. The proud man who would not walk beside him was seen. Nothing in the inequality of the measurements is off-camera.

For a modern reader the verse is almost suspiciously contemporary. Class, race, health, platform, beauty, and income still make some people a trial for others. The Qur'an does not pretend the differences are imaginary. It names them as *fitnah* and asks the only question that converts a social fact into a spiritual path: will you be patient? The Lord who asked is Seeing.

**Messengers Who Ate Food and Walked in Markets**

*Wa-mā arsalnā qablaka mina l-mursalīna illā innahum la-yaʾkulūna l-ṭaʿāma wa-yamshūna fī l-aswāq* — We sent not before thee any of the messengers but that they ate food and walked in the markets. The objection of verse 7 is answered as a law of sending, not as an apology. Humanity of the messenger is the design. *Wa-jaʿalnā baʿḍakum li-baʿḍin fitnatan a-taṣbirūn* — and We have made some of you a trial for others: will you be patient? The poor believer is a trial for the rich rejecter; the rich is a trial for the poor; the healthy for the sick. *Wa-kāna rabbuka baṣīrā* — and thy Lord is Seeing. The Seeing is the comfort of the patient and the threat of the scorner. The Prophet ﷺ sat in the market of Madinah, bargained, carried goods, and said the honest merchant is with the prophets (al-Tirmidhī 1209). Verse 20 is that life as revelation’s method. A religion that is ashamed of a messenger in a market has asked for the angel of verse 7 again.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:21

> **And those who hope not to meet Us say, "Why have not the angels been sent down unto us, or why have we not seen our Lord?" Indeed, they have waxed arrogant in their souls and were greatly insolent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Not Hoping to Meet: The Inner Name of Denial**

The objectors are now named by a spiritual fact rather than by a tribal one: *alladhīna lā yarjūna liqāʾanā* — "those who hope not to meet Us." *Rajāʾ* is hope, and also, in some of its uses, expectation. Al-Rāzī notes that "they hope not for a resurrection" can mean both that they are not hopeful for such a reality and that they do not anticipate it. In this verse the meeting is *liqāʾanā* — meeting Us, meeting God. They do not hope for it. They do not want it. They do not live as if it will happen.

The phrase is a Qur'anic diagnosis of a type:

> *"Indeed, those who do not hope to meet Us, and are pleased with the life of this world and at rest in it, and those who are heedless of Our signs — those, their refuge is the Fire for what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 10:7–8)

> *"And they say, 'If we had been listening or reasoning, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze.'"* (Qur'an 67:10) — a later confession of the same type.

To hope to meet God is not a taste for mystical experience. It is to treat the end of the road as a Person, not as a wall. People who do not hope for that meeting must demand that the Unseen be delivered now, on their terms, as spectacle. That is what they do next.

**"Why Not Angels to Us? Why Have We Not Seen Our Lord?"**

The demand of verse 7 is repeated and raised. There they wanted an angel with the messenger. Here they want angels sent to *them*, or the sight of the Lord Himself. The self has moved to the center. It is not enough that a prophet receive. We must receive. We must see.

The Children of Israel said it to Moses:

> *"O Moses, we will not believe thee till we see God openly."* (Qur'an 2:55)

And the thunderbolt took them. The Quraysh say it to Muhammad ﷺ, and the Qur'an, in 6:8 and 17:90–93, records their menu of marvels. Al-Rāzī and the commentators on this verse connect the demand to those passages. The pattern is one: a heart that will not meet God at the appointed meeting tries to force a meeting of its own design.

The irony is that they will see angels, and verse 22 will tell them what that seeing will be like. They will not see God in this world as an object among objects; Moses himself was told *lan tarānī* — "You will not see Me" (7:143) — when he asked. The demand to see the Lord as a condition of faith is therefore not boldness. It is a category error about this world and the next, dressed as a higher standard of evidence.

**Arrogance in the Souls, Insolence Made Great**

*Laqadi istakbarū fī anfusihim wa-ʿataw ʿutuwwan kabīrā* — "Indeed, they have waxed arrogant in their souls and were greatly insolent." Two verbs. *Istakbarū* is to consider oneself great, to refuse from pride. *Fī anfusihim* locates the pride in the souls: it is not only a public swagger; it is an inner inflation. *ʿAtaw* is insolence, rebellious excess, the crossing of a bound with contempt. *ʿUtuwwan kabīrā* is the cognate accusative again: they insolenced an insolence that was great.

The Prophet ﷺ defined the first of these with a definition that has never been improved:

> *"No one will enter Paradise in whose heart is an atom's weight of arrogance... Arrogance is rejecting the truth and looking down on people."* (Muslim 91)

Rejecting the truth (the Criterion, the human messenger, the meeting with God) and looking down on people (the poor Companions, the walking-in-markets Prophet ﷺ) is exactly the psychology of this verse. The demand for angels and for sight of the Lord is not curiosity. It is the soul's refusal to receive from a man who eats food.

**Why the Meeting Must Be Hoped For, Not Forced**

Hope for the meeting produces a life of preparation: prayer, honesty, the fear and longing that the servants of the Compassionate will display. Demand for the meeting produces a life of conditions: I will believe when You show me, when You honor me, when You exempt me from following a hungry man. The first is *īmān*. The second is a negotiation that God does not enter.

The believer who reads this verse should inspect his own conditions. Where have I said, in effect, "I will be serious when I see a sign that suits me"? Where have I treated the ordinary — a verse, a poor neighbor, a duty in a market — as beneath the religion I would respect? Those questions are the verse applied without angels having to descend.

And the hope itself can be practiced. The Prophet ﷺ taught a supplication for the meeting:

> *"O God, I ask You for joy in meeting You."* (part of a longer supplication in al-Nasāʾī 1305 and Aḥmad; the meaning is also in the well-known *Allāhumma innī asʾaluka...* reports)

Joy in the meeting is the opposite of "those who hope not to meet Us." It does not require vision now. It requires a heart that would not be embarrassed to be found, because it has spent the day as if the Finder were already Seeing — which, verse 20 said, He is.

**Those Who Hope Not to Meet Us**

*Alladhīna lā yarjūna liqāʾanā* — those who do not hope for / do not expect a meeting with Us. *Rajāʾ* in Arabic is hope and expectant waiting. They have cancelled the appointment. Therefore they can demand angels as a spectacle, because a meeting they do not believe in cannot judge the demand. *Aw taʾtiya maʿa rabbinā* — or that our Lord should come. The insolence is complete: not only angels, but God Himself as a visiting proof, on their timetable. *La-qadi istakbarū fī anfusihim* — they have grown arrogant within themselves. The arena of the arrogance is the self, which is why a public humbling will be needed. *Wa-ʿataw ʿutuwwan kabīrā* — and they have exceeded with a great excess. Verse 63’s walk *hawnan* is the contrary character. The servants hope for the meeting; these refuse it and then demand it as theatre. The Prophet ﷺ taught love of meeting God as a mark of the friend of God, and hatred of meeting God as a mark of the other (al-Bukhārī 6507; Muslim 2683). Verse 21 is that ḥadīth’s enemy-portrait.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:22

> **On the Day they see the angels, there shall be no glad tidings for the guilty that Day. And they will say, "A barrier, forbidden!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Seeing They Asked For, Arriving as the Opposite of News**

They asked, in verse 21, why the angels had not been sent down to them. Verse 22 tells them the Day on which they will see the angels, and what that seeing will not contain: *lā bushrā yawmaʾidhin lil-mujrimīn* — "there shall be no glad tidings for the guilty that Day." The gift they demanded as a condition of faith will arrive as the end of faith's opportunity. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note that the Day may be death or the Day of Resurrection — two meanings the word *yawm* can bear here. In both, angels are seen. In both, for the guilty, the seeing is not good news.

Glad tidings — *bushrā* — is the other half of the Prophet's ﷺ office. He is *bashīr wa-nadhīr*, bearer of good news and warner (25:56). The guilty refused the *bushrā* when it was a recitation they could answer. They receive, instead, a seeing without *bushrā*. The angels who come to the believers at death with "Do not fear and do not grieve, and receive glad tidings of the Garden" (41:30) are not the angels the guilty meet.

> *"If you could but see when the wrongdoers are in the flood of death, and the angels extend their hands: 'Discharge your souls. Today you are recompensed with the punishment of humiliation.'"* (Qur'an 6:93)

That extending of hands is a seeing of angels. It is not the seeing they had in mind.

**"A Barrier, Forbidden!" — Ḥijran Maḥjūrā**

*Wa-yaqūlūna ḥijran maḥjūrā* — "And they will say, 'A barrier, forbidden!'" The phrase is an Arabic exclamation used, al-Rāzī explains, when one encounters an enemy or attacker and wants the attack stopped or hindered — a verbal throwing-up of a wall: forbidden, barred, make a barrier. It is the language of someone who has run out of weapons and still has a mouth.

Who says it? One interpretation: the disbelievers, who, upon seeing the fearsome sight of angels, desire to be protected from them. Another: the angels themselves, keeping the disbelievers out of the gates of the Garden — "a barrier, forbidden" as a sentence of exclusion. Both readings are inside the commentary tradition. In the first, the guilty try to use a charm against the very meeting they demanded. In the second, the unseen itself uses the charm against them. The sūrah will later use the identical pair of words in verse 53 for the divide God set between the two seas: a barrier, forbidden. There it is a mercy that keeps waters in their kinds. Here it is a verdict that keeps the guilty from a Garden they did not live for.

**The Guilty: Mujrimūn as a Moral, Not a Tribal, Name**

*Al-mujrimūn* are those who have committed *jurm* — a crime, a cutting-off, a sin that has become a record. The word in the Qur'an is not a slur for "the others." It is a name for a state. The same Book that uses it of Pharaoh's people uses it of anyone whose day's work is crime against God and against the soul. Verse 22 does not say "no glad tidings for the Quraysh." It says "for the guilty." Membership in a believing nation does not, by itself, move a person out of the noun.

Other verses warn the same type about meeting the angels:

> *"Do they wait for naught less than that God should come in the shadows of clouds, with the angels, and that the matter should have been decreed?"* (Qur'an 2:210)

> *"Do they wait for anything but that the angels should come to them, or your Lord should come, or some of the signs of your Lord should come?"* (Qur'an 6:158)

Waiting for the unveiled, while refusing the veiled, is the error. The Criterion was the veiled, merciful form of the news. Angels at death are the unveiled form. The first could be answered with *āmannā*. The second, for the guilty, is answered with a barrier-cry.

**What a Believer Asks to Hear Instead**

The opposite of this verse is a report the Prophet ﷺ taught about the soul that comes out in a manner pleasing to God: angels with faces like the sun bringing a shroud from Paradise and saying, "O peaceful soul, come out to the mercy of God" (Aḥmad; Abū Dāwūd 4753, in the long ḥadīth of al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib). That is *bushrā*. It is not earned by demanding angels now. It is prepared by living as if they will come.

The practical hearing of verse 22 is therefore not a fascination with death-angels. It is a daily preference for the news that is still good: the recitation, the repentance that is accepted, the prayer that is a meeting in the unseen without the barrier-cry. People who will not accept good news in small form — a verse, a warning, a poor man in a market — are training themselves to meet the large form without *bushrā*. People who accept it are training the other meeting. The difference is made now, in the world where angels are not yet seen and the mouth can still say *subḥānaka* instead of *ḥijran maḥjūrā*.

**Ḥijran Maḥjūrā: The Barrier They Asked For**

They asked to see angels. They will see them, and the seeing will be a locked door. *Ḥijran maḥjūrā* is an old Arabian cry of refuge, a sacred enclosure, a “hands off.” On that Day the cry does not enclose them from the angels. It announces that glad tidings have been enclosed from them. Verse 53 will use the same two words for the *barzakh* between seas — a mercy-barrier. Here the barrier is a verdict. Same words, two offices, as the sūrah loves to do with *nushūr*, with *ṣabr*, with *imām*. The servants of the Compassionate, who hope for *bushrā* in the meeting (and who will be met with *salām* in 25:75), are people who accepted unseen angels as a report and did not demand them as a theatre. Their refuge-cry now is *aʿūdhu billāh*, which still works. *Ḥijran maḥjūrā* then will not.

**The Day They See the Angels: No Glad Tidings for the Guilty**

*Yawma yarawna l-malāʾikata lā bushrā yawmaʾidhin lil-mujrimīna wa-yaqūlūna ḥijran maḥjūrā* — the Day they see the angels, no glad tidings that Day for the guilty, and they will say, “A barrier, forbidden!” They asked to see angels (25:21). They will see them, and the seeing is a locked door. The same two words in verse 53 name a mercy-barrier between seas. Here they name a verdict. The soul that comes out pleasing to God is met with faces like the sun and “come out to the mercy of God” (Abū Dāwūd 4753). That is *bushrā*, prepared by living as if they will come, not by demanding them now. Accept good news in small form — a verse, a warning, a poor man in a market — or train yourself to meet the large form without *bushrā*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:23

> **And We shall turn to whatever work they have done, and make it scattered dust.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Works That Looked Like Works**

*Wa-qadimnā ilā mā ʿamilū min ʿamal* — "And We shall turn to whatever work they have done." God advances, as a king advances to inspect, toward the portfolio of their deeds. The guilty of verse 22 are not people who did nothing. They worked. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the works of filial piety which the guilty believed to constitute good deeds will come to naught because of their disbelief. Al-Rāzī says *work* may also be understood to refer to any action of theirs. Both are true to the Qur'an's wider teaching. There are deeds that look, in the world, like light — generosity, courage, keeping of kin, building of wells — and that, when brought to the scale without faith and without the right of God, do not weigh.

> *"And We shall regard what they have done of deeds, and make them scattered dust."* (Qur'an 25:23)

> *"Those are the ones who disbelieve in the signs of their Lord and in the meeting with Him, so their deeds have become worthless, and We will not assign to them on the Day of Resurrection any weight."* (Qur'an 18:105)

> *"And if they had associated others with God, whatever they did would have become worthless for them."* (Qur'an 6:88)

The doctrine is severe because the claim of tawḥīd is severe. A deed is not a free-floating unit of good. It is an act with a direction. Directed to God, in the manner He taught, it remains. Directed to reputation, to a rival deity, to the self as god (verse 43), it is a deed that has already spent its wage in the world.

**Habāʾ: The Dust You Only See in a Shaft of Light**

*Fa-jaʿalnāhu habāʾan manthūrā* — "and make it scattered dust." *Habāʾ* is the motes that float in the air and become visible only when a ray of sun crosses a room. Al-Rāzī and the lexicographers linger on that picture. The deeds are not even sand, which has weight. They are the glittering nothing that a person can see and cannot hold. *Manthūr* is scattered, strewn, unsweepable back into a pile.

The image is exact about certain lives. A public record, a body of philanthropy, a career of "impact," a family name — all of it visible, like motes in a shaft, and all of it, without the weight of *īmān* and right intention, ungraspable on the Day when grasping matters. The shaft of light that makes *habāʾ* visible is, in this verse, God's turning toward the work. Inspection is what reveals the weightlessness.

The Prophet ﷺ described a form of this inspection in a ḥadīth that should make every public worker swallow:

> *"The first of people to be judged on the Day of Resurrection will be a man who was martyred. He will be brought, and God will make known to him His favors, and he will recognize them. He will say: 'What did you do with them?' The man will say: 'I fought for You until I was martyred.' God will say: 'You have lied. You fought so that it would be said, a courageous man; and it was said.' Then he will be ordered dragged on his face into the Fire."* (Muslim 1905; the ḥadīth continues with the scholar and the generous man, same structure)

Martyrdom, knowledge, charity — the three deeds most likely to look like weight — become *habāʾ* when the direction was "so that it would be said." Verse 23 is that ḥadīth in one metaphor.

**Worthlessness Is Not the Same as Non-Existence**

The Qur'an, in other places, still allows that some worldly good of the disbeliever may be repaid in the world — a long life, a body of wealth, a name. The scattering is of the *Hereafter's* weight.
