This is why the verse is not a permission to deny the civic good of anyone who does not share the faith. It is a warning to the one who is accumulating a file he believes will count when he meets God. Count for whom? Directed to whom?

For the believer, the verse is also a warning, because 18:105 and 6:88 and the ḥadīth of the three men are not about "them" only. Association, showing-off, and denial of the meeting can infect a praying person's portfolio. The servants of the Compassionate, later in the sūrah, are described by deeds that are inward as well as outward: humility, night prayer, fear of Hell, a just mean, truthful witness. Those are deeds with direction. They are the opposite of *habāʾ*.

**How to Do Work That Will Not Scatter**

The recipe is not complicated, and it is not a technique. Intention for God; manner as He taught; a heart that hopes to meet Him (the opposite of verse 21); repentance when the direction was wrong. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Actions are only by intentions, and each man will have what he intended."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907)

That first ḥadīth of the Ṣaḥīḥ is the commentary of verse 23. Intention is the gravity that makes a deed more than a mote. Without it, God "turns to" the work and the work does not turn out to have been there.

A person can practice this in small rooms. Before a message, a gift, a post, a prayer: to whom is this directed? If the honest answer is a human face, the deed may still be lawful, but it should not be filed under "for Him." If the honest answer is God, then let the human face, if it praises or does not, be secondary. The scale is coming. The dust is not required.

**Scattered Dust: Works Without a Face Toward Him**

*Wa-qadimnā ilā mā ʿamilū min ʿamalin fa-jaʿalnāhu habāʾan manthūrā* — We shall proceed to what they have done of deeds and make it scattered dust. The verb *qadimnā* is arrival, approaching, a coming to the file. The deeds existed. They were not nothing in history. They are made nothing in the scale because they were not for the Face, or were mixed with *shirk*, or were the dust of show. The Prophet ﷺ said that the first who will be judged include a reciter, a giver, a martyr, each of whom used a good deed for a human face, and the sentence is: you have taken your wage (Muslim 1905). Verse 23 is that ḥadīth as a Makkan warning, before the community had reciters and martyrs in numbers. Intention is already the issue. The servants who will not bear *zūr* (25:72) and who spend without waste or miserliness (25:67) are trying to have deeds that will not be *habāʾ*. Dust is the opposite of the *ghurfah*. Same Day. Two outcomes of work.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:24

> **That Day the inhabitants of the Garden shall have the best dwelling place and the most beautiful rest.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Other Inspection, the Other Place**

Verse 23 turned toward the work of the guilty and found dust. Verse 24 turns, without a raised voice, to the other population of that Day. *Aṣḥābu al-jannati yawmaʾidhin khayrun mustaqarran wa-aḥsanu maqīlā* — "That Day the inhabitants of the Garden shall have the best dwelling place and the most beautiful rest." The comparison is implicit: better than the narrow place of verse 13, better than anything the guilty called a home, better — as some commentators note — than the "that" of verse 15's question, "Is that better, or the Garden of everlastingness?"

*Khayr* can be the comparative "better" or the superlative "best." Both readings serve. On a Day when dwellings are disclosed, the Garden is not one option among peers. It is *khayr mustaqarr*, the best as a place to settle.

**Mustaqarr: A Place That Holds**

*Mustaqarr* is a dwelling, a place of stability, a where-one-settles. The word is used in the Qur'an for the womb, for the earth, for a destination. It names not a hotel but a hold. The guilty were thrown into a narrow place; they did not settle. The inhabitants of the Garden have a *mustaqarr*. The difference between being thrown and being housed is the difference this sūrah has been tracing since verse 11.

The Prophet ﷺ described palaces and tents, markets of Paradise, and a tree whose shade a rider travels in for a hundred years:

> *"In Paradise there is a tree in whose shade a rider would travel for a hundred years without crossing it."* (al-Bukhārī 3251; Muslim 2826)

Those reports fill *mustaqarr* with furniture. The verse itself is content with the comparative: whatever the guilty have, this is better; whatever you have seen of dwellings, this is best.

**Maqīl: The Midday Rest and a Scholarly Question**

*Maqīl* is a place of rest, and more specifically, as the commentators note, a place where one takes the midday sleep, the *qaylūlah*. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Zamakhsharī all pause over this. Does one sleep in the Garden? Is there, during the Day of Judgment, a midday-like intermission when the reckoning is suspended and the people of the Garden already rest, while the people of the Fire are in their narrow place?

The questions are honest and not fully closed. Sleep in this world is a brother of death, as verse 47 will imply. In a Garden where "you will live and not die" (Muslim 2837), sleep as deficiency would be odd. Rest as delight would not. Arabic can use *maqīl* for a rest that is not a medical sleep: a cooling, a pausing in shade at the hour when the world is hottest. On a Day the Qur'an itself calls a difficult Day for the disbelievers (25:26), the inhabitants of the Garden have, at the hour that corresponds to heat, the most beautiful cool.

Sūrah al-Nūr mentions the three times of privacy, including "when you put off your garments at noon" (24:58). The culture that received verse 24 knew the *qaylūlah* as a mercy in a hot land. The verse takes that mercy and makes it eschatological. The most beautiful rest is not the nap after Friday lunch. It is the rest that cannot be invaded by a knock, a debt, a disease, or a fear.

**Best and Most Beautiful: Two Adjectives, Two Needs**

*Khayr* is goodness, benefit, the better as such. *Aḥsan* is beauty, excellence of form, the most fair. The dwelling is good; the rest is beautiful. A house can be useful and ugly; a rest can be pretty and unsafe. The Garden is described as answering both the need of the body for a place that holds and the need of the soul for a place that delights. Human beings, even in this world, are not satisfied with safety without beauty or with beauty without safety. The verse promises the marriage of the two.

**How This Verse Relieves Without Making One Useless**

A person still on the road can hear "best dwelling, most beautiful rest" as an excuse to despise his present room, or as a reason to endure it. The sūrah wants the second. The servants of the Compassionate will walk humbly on this earth, pass the night in prayer, and spend in a just mean. They are not homeless in the world. They are, however, people whose idea of home has been corrected. The best *mustaqarr* is not the stone *qaṣr* of verse 10 as the Quraysh meant it. It is the Garden as God means it.

The Prophet ﷺ used to ask for the Garden and seek refuge from the Fire in the same breath, many times a day, including after the last tashahhud (al-Bukhārī 832). Verse 24 is the content of the first half of that asking. It is legitimate to want the best dwelling. It is legitimate to want the most beautiful rest. Wanting them in God's way is not world-hatred. It is world-ranking: this room is a trust; that rest is the end.

**Best Dwelling, Most Beautiful Rest**

*Khayrun mustaqarran wa-aḥsanu maqīlā* is the Garden as real estate against verse 34’s *sharrun makānan*. *Mustaqarr* is a place of remaining. *Maqīl* is a place of midday rest, a siesta of the next world, used here for the rest that follows the standing of the Day. Al-Qurṭubī and others note that the people of the Garden rest while the people of the Fire do not. The contrast with the Qurayshī demand for a *qaṣr* (25:10) is quiet and complete. They wanted a palace as a proof in this world. God offers a dwelling as a mercy in the next. The first would have been a trial and a spectacle. The second is a home. The servants who walk humbly are people who have accepted the ranking. They may live in rooms. They do not need those rooms to be the argument for their religion. The argument is the Criterion. The room that matters is the one verse 24 names, and verse 75 will call a *ghurfah*.

**The Companions of the Garden That Day: Best Dwelling, Most Beautiful Rest**

*Aṣḥābu l-jannati yawmaʾidhin khayrun mustaqarran wa-aḥsanu maqīlā* — the companions of the Garden that Day are best as a dwelling and most beautiful as a rest. *Mustaqarr* is remaining; *maqīl* is the rest after the standing of the Day. Al-Qurṭubī: the people of the Garden rest; the people of the Fire do not. Against verse 10’s demanded *qaṣr*, God offers a home, not a spectacle. Against verse 34’s *sharrun makānan*, this is *khayrun mustaqarran*. The servants walk humbly on this earth without needing their present room to be the argument for their religion. The argument is the Criterion. The Prophet ﷺ asked for the Garden and sought refuge from the Fire in one breath (al-Bukhārī 832). Verse 24 is the first half of that asking. Wanting the best dwelling in God’s way is not world-hatred. It is world-ranking.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:25

> **And the Day when the heavens are split open with clouds and the angels are sent down in a descent,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sky That Had Seemed a Ceiling Becomes a Door**

*Wa-yawma tashaqqaqu al-samāʾu bil-ghamāmi* — "And the Day when the heavens are split open with clouds." The sky, which in ordinary life is the image of what does not break, breaks. *Tashaqqaqu* is to split, to be rent, to come apart along a seam one did not know was there. The Qur'an returns to this rending as one of the signatures of the end:

> *"When the sky is rent asunder."* (Qur'an 84:1)

> *"When the sky is split, and it becomes rose-red like oil."* (Qur'an 55:37)

> *"And the sky will be opened as if it were gates."* (Qur'an 78:19)

> *"The sky will be split, for that Day it will be frail."* (Qur'an 69:16)

What human beings treated as the most reliable furniture of the world — the blue lid, the place of weather, the direction of prayer's upward gesture — is shown to have been a construction, and constructions can be opened. Verse 2 said He created everything and then measured it. Verse 25 says the measure includes a term for the sky.

**With Clouds: Ghamām as the Vehicle of the Unveiling**

*Bil-ghamām* — with clouds, in clouds, by clouds. The word recalls a verse that has already been hovering over this passage:

> *"Do they wait for naught less than that God should come in the shadows of clouds, with the angels, and that the matter should have been decreed?"* (Qur'an 2:210)

Clouds in this world are mercy: shade, the glad tidings of rain that verse 48 will name. Clouds on that Day are part of the opening. The familiar is used to carry the unfamiliar, the way a language of markets and gardens has been used throughout the sūrah to speak of what no market has sold. The commentators differ on whether the clouds are the veil of the Divine manifestation, the dust of a world coming apart, or the canopy of the angels' descent. What they do not differ on is that the sky's opening is not empty. Something comes through.

**Angels Sent Down in a Descent**

*Wa-nuzzila al-malāʾikatu tanzīlā* — "and the angels are sent down in a descent." The cognate accusative again: they are sent down a sending-down, thoroughly, not a scout or two. The people who asked in verse 21 why angels had not been sent to them receive, here, more angels than they wanted. The descent of angels at the end is mentioned also in:

> *"And your Lord comes, and the angels, rank upon rank."* (Qur'an 89:22)

> *"Do they wait for anything but that the angels should come to them..."* (Qur'an 6:158)

*Nuzzila* is the same pattern of verb used for the sending down of the Criterion in verse 1 (*nazzala*). Revelation was a sending-down. This is another. The first was a mercy that could be recited and refused. The second is a sending-down that cannot be refused because the time of testing is over. The Book came down in portions so that a heart could be made firm (25:32). The angels come down in a descent when the heart's firmness or its ruin has already been written by a life.

**Why the Verse Is Almost All Image and Almost No Command**

There is no imperative here. No "say." No "will you be patient?" The verse is a window. The sūrah has been arguing with descriptions, demands, and denials. Now it simply shows a Day. The method is the method of a warner who has explained and now points. Some hearers are moved by a reason; some by a picture. The Qur'an, in its mercy, uses both. A person who was unmoved by the logic of verse 3 (idols cannot create) may still be moved by a sky splitting with clouds.

The Prophet ﷺ's own sermons, as the companions reported them, would sometimes take this turn: the voice would rise, the face redden, and he would speak of the Hour as a man speaks of something he sees (Muslim 867). Verse 25 is that turn in recitation.

**Living Under a Sky That Will Open**

The practical fruit is not to stare at weather for omens. The Prophet ﷺ forbade treating the sky's ordinary signs as independent causes, and verse 50 will rebuke those who attribute rain to stars. The fruit is a corrected relationship to permanence. Houses, states, reputations, and the blue lid are measured. A life aimed at them as last things is a life aimed at what will split.

The servants of the Compassionate, who walk on the earth now, walk under this sky knowing it is not the last ceiling. That knowledge does not make them careless of the earth. It makes them careful of the heart, which will have to stand when the sky is no longer a cover. Night prayer (25:64) is, among other things, a rehearsal: one stands under the still-unsplit sky and speaks to the One who will open it, before the angels of that descent arrive.

**The Sky Split, the Angels Sent Down**

*Wa-yawma tashaqqaqu l-samāʾu bil-ghamāmi* — the Day the sky is split with clouds. The sky of verse 25 is the same sky under which they demanded an angel in verse 7. The angel they wanted as a walking companion arrives as a descent they cannot refuse. *Wa-nuzzila l-malāʾikatu tanzīlā* — and the angels are sent down in a sending. The cognate accusative makes the descent total. This is not the single angel they would have inspected and then perhaps still denied. This is the sky opening. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr connect it to other verses of the Day: 69:16, 84:1, 78:19. The sūrah has been a book of sendings: a Criterion sent down (25:1), water sent down (25:48), a messenger sent (25:56). The last sending of the sequence is angels through a split sky. A person who treated earlier sendings as optional will not find this one optional. Belief now is the chance to receive a Book before one must receive a sky.

**The Day the Sky Is Split with Clouds and the Angels Are Sent Down**

*Wa-yawma tashaqqaqu l-samāʾu bil-ghamāmi wa-nuzzila l-malāʾikatu tanzīlā* — and the Day the sky is split with clouds and the angels are sent down in a sending. The sky under which they demanded an angel (25:7) opens. The angel they wanted as a companion arrives as a descent they cannot refuse. The cognate *tanzīlā* makes the descent total. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr connect 69:16, 84:1, 78:19. The sūrah has been sendings: a Criterion, water, a messenger. The last sending is angels through a split sky. Night prayer under the still-unsplit sky is a rehearsal: one speaks to the Opener before the angels of that descent arrive. The heart will have to stand when the sky is no longer a cover. Care that heart now.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:26

> **that Day the true sovereignty will belong to the Compassionate, and that will be a difficult Day for the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**True Sovereignty: What Was Always So, Made Unmistakable**

*Al-mulku yawmaʾidhin al-ḥaqqu lil-Raḥmān* — "that Day the true sovereignty will belong to the Compassionate." Verse 2 had already said that to Him belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth. Verse 26 does not add a new owner. It adds the adjective *al-ḥaqq* — the true, the real, the sovereignty that is sovereignty in truth. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī explain: on that Day there will be no illusion of any sovereignty other than God's, even though true sovereignty was always God's, and all other claims to sovereignty over anything will disappear.

The world is a place of borrowed *mulk*. Kings stamp coins. Boards sign. Fathers rule houses. The borrowing is real as a trust and false as an ownership. The Hour ends the borrowing.

> *"The Day they come forth, nothing of them being hidden from God: 'Whose is the sovereignty today?' 'God's, the One, the Irresistible.'"* (Qur'an 40:16)

> *"On the Day of Resurrection, God will seize the earth and fold up the heavens in His right hand, then He will say: 'I am the King. Where are the kings of the earth?'"* (al-Bukhārī 7382; Muslim 2787)

Verse 26 is that question and that ḥadīth in the vocabulary of this sūrah. The new element is the Name to which the true *mulk* is attached: *al-Raḥmān*, the Compassionate.

**Why the Compassionate, Not the Avenger, Holds the Kingdom That Day**

The Name is a surprise only to those who think judgment and mercy are enemies. The sūrah is named, in its later portrait, for the servants of the Compassionate. The disbelievers of verse 60 will refuse to prostrate to *al-Raḥmān* because they do not recognize the Name. Here the Name is placed over the Day they thought, if they thought at all, would be mere wrath.

The meaning is not that the Day is easy. The verse will immediately say it is difficult for the disbelievers. The meaning is that the One who owns the Day is the One whose mercy, as the ḥadīth qudsī says, prevails over His wrath (al-Bukhārī 7404; Muslim 2751). Justice that day is not the justice of a machine. It is the justice of *al-Raḥmān*. For those who met His compassion with refusal, the difficulty is theirs. For those who were His servants, the same Name is the reason the Garden of verse 15 was a promise and not a rumor.

Ibn Taymiyyah and the theologians of the Names insist that we do not divide God into a merciful half and a sovereign half. *Al-Raḥmān* is the one who owns. Sovereignty does not pass to another Name when the sky splits. The Compassionate is King. That is the doctrine that keeps a believer from despair without letting him bargain.

**A Difficult Day for the Disbelievers**

*Wa-kāna yawman ʿalā al-kāfirīna ʿasīrā* — "and that will be a difficult Day for the disbelievers." *ʿAsīr* is hard, tight, arduous, the opposite of *yasīr*, easy. The Qur'an uses the pair elsewhere of the Hour:

> *"The Hour has drawn near... and it will be, upon the disbelievers, a difficult Day."* (cf. 54:1 and 25:26's sense; also 74:9–10: "That Day will be a difficult day, for the disbelievers, not easy.")

Difficulty is not equally distributed. The sky splits for everyone. The angels descend for everyone. True sovereignty is disclosed to everyone. The difficulty is *upon the disbelievers*. The inhabitants of the Garden, verse 24 said, have the most beautiful rest on that same Day. One Day, two experiences. The difference was made by what a person did with the Criterion, the messenger who ate food, and the hope of meeting God.

**What It Means to Rehearse True Sovereignty Now**

A believer does not wait for the sky to split in order to live under the true *mulk*. He practices the end of illusion in advance: he does not give a boss, a state, a spouse, or an appetite the last word that belongs to God; he does not panic as if a human kingdom were ultimate; he does not despair as if wrath were the only Name. The legal and spiritual name of this practice is *tawḥīd*.

The Prophet ﷺ taught a phrase that is verse 26 in the mouth of a person going to sleep, which is a small death:

> *"O God, You are the King. There is no god but You. You are my Lord, and I am Your servant..."* (part of the long evening supplication, Muslim 2713)

Saying "You are the King" at night is training for the Day when there will be no other sentence. For the disbeliever that sentence is difficulty. For the servant of the Compassionate it is the disclosure of what he had already been living toward.

**The True Mulk, Against Every Borrowed Crown**

Verse 2 named *mulk* as His, without partner. Verse 26 names the Day on which that fact becomes the only public fact. *Al-ḥaqq* — the true — is an adjective that judges every other kingship as *bāṭil* when the sky is split. Pharaoh, the Qurayshī notables, the man who wanted a treasure poured on the messenger, the man who took his caprice as a god: all of them leased a little command. The lease ends. *Wa-kāna yawman ʿalā l-kāfirīna ʿasīrā* — a hard day upon the disbelievers. Hard because the *mulk* they denied is the *mulk* they now cannot escape. For the believer the same Day is the appearance of what he had been saying in *Allāhu akbar*. The Prophet ﷺ taught that the most truthful word a poet said was Labīd’s: “Everything save God is false.” Verse 26 is that line as eschatology. Practically, it trains a person not to rent his fear to temporary kings. Fear the King whose kingship does not become difficult for those who already lived as His servants.

**The True Kingship That Day Belongs to the Compassionate**

*Al-mulku yawmaʾidhin al-ḥaqqu lil-Raḥmān* — the true kingship that Day belongs to the Compassionate. Verse 2 named *mulk* as His. Verse 26 names the Day it becomes the only public fact. *Wa-kāna yawman ʿalā l-kāfirīna ʿasīrā* — and it will have been a hard day upon the disbelievers. Hard because the *mulk* they denied is the *mulk* they cannot escape. For the servant it is the disclosure of *Allāhu akbar*. Labīd’s line — everything save God is false — is this verse as eschatology. Do not rent fear to temporary kings. Saying “You are the King” at night is training for the Day when there will be no other sentence. The Name they would not bow to (25:60) is the Name on the kingship. Bow now.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:27

> **And that Day the wrongdoer will bite his hands, saying, "Would that I had taken a way with the Messenger!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Body Tells the Truth Before the Mouth Finishes It**

*Wa-yawma yaʿaḍḍu al-ẓālimu ʿalā yadayh* — "And that Day the wrongdoer will bite his hands." The Qur'an here uses a gesture that needs no translation. Hands are bitten in rage, in self-blame, in the pain of a moment that cannot be replayed. Two hands, not one: *yadayh*. The whole of his agency — what he did, what he took, what he refused to take — is between his teeth. *Al-ẓālim*, the wrongdoer, is the same family of words as the *ẓulm* and *zūr* of verse 4 and the "whosoever among you does wrong" of verse 19. Wrongdoing, which in the world can look like cleverness, ends as a man eating his own hands. The image is the opposite of the market-walker of verses 7 and 20. Those hands bought and sold and pointed and swore. These hands are the meal of regret. Al-Qurṭubī notes the gesture as one of the Qur'an's ways of making the unseen visible without theatre: a body already known, used in a way not yet seen.

**"Would That I Had Taken a Way with the Messenger"**

*Yā-laytanī ittakhadhtu maʿa al-rasūli sabīlā* — "Would that I had taken a way with the Messenger." *Yā-laytanī* is the particle of wish that arrives too late. He does not wish, here, for treasure, or for an angel, or for the garden he once thought a prophet should own. He wishes he had taken a *sabīl*, a way, with the messenger. The thing he refused when it was a dusty man in a market is the thing he would now sell the world to have. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī mention reports that tie these verses to a specific person misled by a friend; al-Rāzī prefers not to lock them to one name. The wish is generic because the error is generic. Other verses give the same cry from other throats:

> *"If you could but see when they are made to stand before the Fire and say, 'Would that we were sent back, and had not denied the signs of our Lord, and had been among the believers.'"* (Qur'an 6:27)

> *"On the Day when their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say, 'Would that we had obeyed God and obeyed the Messenger.'"* (Qur'an 33:66)

> *"He will say, 'Would that I had sent forth for my life.'"* (Qur'an 89:24)

The content of a wasted life, when it is finally admitted, is almost always this: I wish I had gone with the one who was going the right way.

**A Way With Him, Not Merely a Feeling About Him**

*Ittakhadhtu maʿa al-rasūli sabīlā* — taken a way *with* the messenger. The preposition matters. Not "believed in his existence," not "admired his character from a distance," not "quoted him when it suited the tribe." A way, taken, with him. The Prophet ﷺ’s own definition of that taking is the Sunnah: a road that can be walked in prayer, in trade, in marriage, in anger, in the market where he walked. He ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever obeys me has obeyed God, and whoever disobeys me has disobeyed God."* (al-Bukhārī 7137; Muslim 1835)

The wrongdoer had descriptions instead of a way (verse 9). He had a friend instead of a messenger (verses 28–29). He had enjoyment instead of remembrance (verse 18). On the Day of true sovereignty he can see, with a clarity that is itself a punishment, that only one taking would have mattered. Verse 57 had already offered that taking as the only wage the messenger would accept. The offer was public. The wish of verse 27 is the offer, remembered from the wrong side of the Hour.

**Regret as a Teacher While It Is Still Not Too Late**

The verse is recited in a world where *yā-laytanī* can still be followed by action. That is its mercy. A person can bite his hands now, lightly, over a year wasted, a prayer dropped, a companionship that is already pulling him from the Reminder, and then take the way. The same gesture in the next world is sterile. Here it can be the beginning of *tawbah*. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The one who repents from sin is like the one who has no sin."* (Ibn Mājah 4250)

And:

> *"Regret is repentance."* (Ibn Mājah 4252; Aḥmad)

Verse 27 shows regret when it is no longer repentance. The living are being shown that picture so that their own regret can still be the other kind. Taking a way with the messenger is not a single ceremony. It is a series of takings: this prayer, this apology, this refusal of a friend who wants the Reminder forgotten, this walk in a market without eating people. The wrongdoer’s wish, overheard now, is a map. Taqwā is using the map while the road still has an exit. The servants of the Compassionate are, among other things, people who took the way in time. Their *salām*, their night, their just mean, are the way, walked. Verse 27 is what not walking sounds like when walking is no longer possible.

**Would That I Had Taken a Way with the Messenger**

*Yā-laytanī ttakhadhtu maʿa l-rasūli sabīlā* is the sūrah’s most painful sentence, and it is a disbeliever who says it. The way was available. The messenger ate food and walked in markets (25:7, 20) so that the way would be walkable. *Maʿa l-rasūli* — with the messenger, not merely about him, not merely near him as a joke (25:41). Companionship in the way is the lost object. The servants of the Compassionate are, by contrast, people who took it while taking was possible: walking, night, spending, closed doors of 25:68, open ears of 25:73. Their *salām* is the sound of a way taken. His *yā-laytanī* is the sound of a way refused. The verse is recited so that the living may steal the wish and spend it now, when it is still *tawbah* and not yet *ḥasrah*. Ibn Kathīr notes that this is among the most terrifying descriptions of the Day in the Qur'an, because it is so ordinary: a man wishing he had walked with another man. The ordinary is the point. The way was not a palace. It was a road with a messenger on it.

**The Day the Wrongdoer Bites His Hands**

*Wa-yawma yaʿaḍḍu l-ẓālimu ʿalā yadayhi yaqūlu yā-laytanī ttakhadhtu maʿa l-rasūli sabīlā* — and the Day the wrongdoer bites his hands, saying: would that I had taken a way with the messenger. The sūrah’s most painful sentence is a disbeliever’s. The way was available. The messenger ate food and walked in markets so that the way would be walkable. *Maʿa l-rasūli* — with him, not merely about him, not as a joke (25:41). Ibn Kathīr: among the most terrifying descriptions because so ordinary: a man wishing he had walked with another man. Steal the wish now, when it is still *tawbah* and not yet *ḥasrah*. The servants’ *salām*, night, and just mean are the way, walked. Verse 27 is what not walking sounds like when walking is no longer possible. *Regret is repentance* (Ibn Mājah 4252) — while regret can still be that.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:28

> **Oh, woe unto me! Would that I had not taken so-and-so for a friend!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Messenger He Did Not Take to the Friend He Did**

Verse 27 was a wish for a way with the messenger. Verse 28 is a wish against a taking that did happen. *Yā-waylatā laytanī lam attakhidh fulānan khalīlā* — "Oh, woe unto me! Would that I had not taken so-and-so for a friend." The same verb, *ittakhadha* — to take for oneself — that described false gods in verse 3 and the untaken way in verse 27 now describes a friendship. Worship, road, and company are all, in this sūrah, matters of what one *takes*. Al-Rāzī declines to lock the verse to one Makkan scandal; Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī mention a specific man pulled back from Islam by a companion. *Fulān* is every name that has ever stood between a person and the Reminder. The anonymity is part of the warning. The friend who ruins you may not be famous. He may be the one who sits on your wall, at your table, in your ear, and makes the Criterion feel like a mood-killer.

*Khalīl* is not a casual acquaintance. It is a friend who has entered the *khalal*, the inner gap, the place in a life where counsel and laughter and loyalty live. Abraham is *khalīl Allāh*, the intimate friend of God. The word is high. Its misuse is therefore also high. To take someone as *khalīl* is to give him a key. Verse 28 is the sound of a man who remembers the key and the thief it belonged to. The Prophet ﷺ set the doctrine of company in a ḥadīth that every generation of teachers has used as commentary on this verse:

> *"A man is upon the religion of his intimate friend, so let one of you look at whom he befriends."* (Abū Dāwūd 4833; al-Tirmidhī 2378, graded ḥasan)

And the parable:

> *"The example of a good companion and a bad companion is like the bearer of musk and the blower of the bellows. The musk-bearer will either give you some, or you will buy from him, or you will find a good smell from him. The blower of the bellows will either burn your clothes or you will find a foul smell from him."* (al-Bukhārī 5534; Muslim 2628)

Verse 28 is the bellows, remembered from inside the Fire. The smell that seemed like atmosphere in the world is now named as the cause of woe.

**Woe as Self-Address Before the Lawsuit**

*Yā-waylatā* — "O my woe," "O my ruin." In verse 14, woe or destruction was called for as an exit. Here woe is called as a name for oneself. The wrongdoer is not, in this sentence, blaming the friend yet — that comes in verse 29. He is first standing in the wreck and addressing it. This is psychologically true of real regret. Before the lawsuit against the companion comes the recognition: I am the one who took him. That recognition, in this world, is the beginning of freedom. A person who can say "I took him" can also say "I will un-take him." A person who says only "he did this to me" remains, in a sense, taken. The Qur'an is precise about this order. Self-indictment, then specification of the other’s crime, then the naming of Satan as forsaker. A culture of blame that skips the first step never arrives at *tawbah*. It only arrives at a better *fulān* to replace the last one.

**The Social Form of a Spiritual Disaster**

Islam does not forbid friendship. It forbids the kind of inner taking that relocates guidance from the messenger to a *fulān*. The Qur'an praises the believers as allies of one another (9:71). It commands being with the truthful:

> *"O you who believe, fear God and be with the truthful."* (Qur'an 9:119)

The servants of the Compassionate will answer the ignorant with "Peace," which is a social skill, not a hermit’s. Verse 28 is not an argument for isolation. It is an argument for not handing the inner key to someone who does not want the Reminder in the house. In modern life *fulān* may be a person, and may also be a feed, a circle, a "community" whose only creed is appetite. The test is the test of verse 29: after this friendship, is the Reminder closer or further? If further, the friendship is already the kind that produces *yā-waylatā*. Ending it may look, in the world, like coldness. The Day this verse describes will not call it coldness. It will call it a way not taken with the wrong man.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Keep to the community, beware of division, for Satan is with the solitary, farther from two. Whoever wants the best of the Garden, let him keep to the community."* (al-Tirmidhī 2165, graded ḥasan)

Community, in that ḥadīth, is not any crowd. It is the crowd of the *jamāʿah*, the people of the way with the messenger. Verse 28’s *khalīl* is the counterfeit of that: an intimacy that isolates you from the Reminder while making you feel less alone. The loneliness of the Fire will disclose the counterfeit. The living are being shown the disclosure so that they can change keys while keys still turn.

**How to Un-take a Khalīl Without Theatre**

Quietly, if quiet is possible. Clearly, if the *fulān* is still recruiting. The servants’ *salām* (25:63) is available: a peaceful refusal to continue the inner taking, without a sermon that is actually a new *laghw*. Restoration, if a right was taken in the friendship’s name, is part of *matāb* (25:71). Replacement of company is not optional. An empty inner gap will be filled. Fill it with people who smell of musk — who remind you of the night prayer, the just mean, the open ear to signs — or the gap will take another *fulān*. Verse 28 is recited in a world where the wish can still be followed by an act. That is its mercy. In the next world the same wish is only woe.

**Woe to Me — Would That I Had Not Taken So-and-So as Friend**

*Yā-waylatā laytanī lam attakhidh fulānan khalīlā* — woe to me; would that I had not taken so-and-so as an intimate friend. After the wish for a way with the messenger (25:27) comes the wish against a specific companion. *Khalīl* is more than an acquaintance: an inner taking. Ibrāhīm is *Khalīl Allāh*; this man took a *khalīl* of *ḍalāl*. The contrast is intentional. Friendship is not optional in human life. The choice of friend is a choice of way. The Prophet ﷺ said a man is on the religion of his intimate friend, so let him look to whom he befriends (al-Tirmidhī 2378; Abū Dāwūd 4833). Verse 28 is that ḥadīth as a cry too late. *Fulān* is unnamed so that every living listener may insert the name that pulls him from the Reminder (25:29) and remove it while removal is still *tawbah*. The servants who say *salām* to the ignorant and pass *laghw* with dignity are people who have already begun that removal without waiting for *waylah*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:29

> **He did indeed cause me to go astray from the Reminder after its having come to me, and Satan is a forsaker of man."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Charge Against the Friend, and the Name Behind the Friend**

The wrongdoer now specifies the friend's crime: *la-qad aḍallanī ʿan al-dhikri baʿda idh jāʾanī* — "He did indeed cause me to go astray from the Reminder after its having come to me." The Reminder had arrived. Guidance was not withheld. A person, a *fulān*, then pulled him from it. The sequence matters. This is not the ruin of someone who never heard. It is the ruin of someone who heard and then let a friendship out-shout what he heard.

*Al-dhikr* is the Reminder, a name of the Qur'an and a name of the remembrance of God. Al-Rāzī notes both. In this sūrah the Criterion has been sent down, recited, called a fable, called a lie. For this man, it *came*. *Jāʾanī* — it came to me. Arrival happened. Remaining did not.

The Qur'an elsewhere describes people who receive and then let others un-receive them:

> *"And recite to them the news of the one to whom We gave Our signs, then he stripped himself of them, so Satan pursued him, and he became of those who went astray."* (Qur'an 7:175)

The friend of verse 28 and the Satan of 7:175 are doing the same work: making a person who had signs into a person who does not live by them.

**"And Satan Is a Forsaker of Man"**

*Wa-kāna al-shayṭānu lil-insāni khadhūlā* — "and Satan is a forsaker of man." *Khadhūl* is one who abandons at the needful moment, who withdraws support when support is costly, a traitor to the one he urged. The wrongdoer, having blamed the friend, now names the pattern of which the friend was an instance. Satan — whether Iblīs himself, or, as al-Rāzī notes, *shayṭān* in the extended sense of a human or jinn helper of his — does not keep friends in the Fire. He keeps recruitment.

> *"And Satan will say, when the matter is decided: 'God promised you the promise of truth, and I promised you, then I failed you. I had no authority over you except that I called you, and you responded to me. So do not blame me, and blame yourselves. I cannot help you, and you cannot help me.'"* (Qur'an 14:22)

> *"The promise of Satan is only delusion."* (Qur'an 4:120; 17:64)

Verse 29's last clause is 14:22 in one adjective. *Khadhūl*. He does not go with you into the consequence. The friend who made the Reminder feel optional will not be there to make the Fire feel optional. Company in sin is a temporary corporation. It dissolves at the Hour.

**After It Had Come: The Aggravation of Having Known**

The most painful phrase in the verse is *baʿda idh jāʾanī* — after it had come to me. Knowledge increases capacity, and capacity increases responsibility. The Qur'an is consistently harder on those who knew:

> *"Those who were given the Book did not differ until after knowledge had come to them, out of transgression among themselves."* (Qur'an 3:19, in sense; 2:213)

> *"And whoever contends with the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him, and follows a way other than the way of the believers, We shall turn him to what he has turned to."* (Qur'an 4:115)

The wrongdoer's file is not empty of revelation. It is a file in which revelation is an exhibit of what was left. That is why his cry is bitterer than the cry of someone who never heard a warner. The sūrah opened with a warner unto the worlds. This man is one of the worlds the warner reached. Then *fulān* reached him more.

**Un-taking the Friend While Satan Can Still Be Refused**

The living application is unsentimental. If a companionship pulls you from prayer, from the Book, from the hope of meeting God, from the humility of the servants of the Compassionate, the companionship is already *aḍallanī*. Waiting for the friend to become harmless is a strategy Satan enjoys, because it costs him nothing and costs you the years in which the Reminder is still close.

Refusing does not require a speech about Satan. It requires a new taking: a way with the messenger (verse 27), company with those who remember, the practical *naṣīḥah* of people who will not flatter you off the road. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The likeness of those who take companions — " and the musk and bellows parable already cited at verse 28.

And he taught seeking refuge in a formula Muslims still say in prayer:

> *"I seek refuge in God from Satan the expelled."* (the istiʿādhah before recitation; and in Muslim 2203, refuge from Satan's whisper and puff)

Verse 29 is why that refuge is not a formality. The forsaker is real. He uses friends. He leaves. The Reminder, if it is taken back after having come, is the friendship that does not leave — because its source is the One who is not *khadhūl*, but *al-Walī*, the Protector who does not abandon those who take a way with His messenger.

**The Friend Who Makes One Forget, Then Leaves**

*La-qad aḍallanī ʿani l-dhikri baʿda idh jāʾanī* — he led me astray from the Reminder after it had come to me. The crime is not ignorance. It is post-Reminder forgetting, induced by a companion. *Wa-kāna l-shayṭānu lil-insāni khadhūlā* — and Satan is, toward man, a forsaker. He uses the friend, then leaves the friend. Verse 28’s *fulān* and verse 29’s Satan are two names of one method. The Prophet ﷺ warned against bad sitting-companions as a blower of the bellows of the smith (al-Bukhārī 5534; Muslim 2628): either you burn or you take a bad smell. Verse 29 is that ḥadīth as a Day-of-Judgment confession. The living application is surgical. After the Reminder has come, the friendships that require you to put it down are not friendships. They are *khadhl* deferred. The servants who pass idle talk with dignity (25:72) and do not sit with those who receive the signs with deafness (25:73, in the positive) have already performed the surgery. Their *fulān* is the messenger, or those who walk his way. That friend does not leave at the biting of hands.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:30

> **And the Messenger will say, "O my Lord! Truly my people have taken this Quran for foolishness."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Complaint Filed with God, Not with the Crowd**

*Wa-qāla al-rasūlu yā-rabbi inna qawmī ittakhadhū hādhā al-qurʾāna mahjūrā* — "And the Messenger will say, 'O my Lord! Truly my people have taken this Quran for foolishness.'" Al-Rāzī notes that these words are understood to be spoken by the Prophet ﷺ in the Hereafter, and that they can also have occurred during his earthly life. Both timings are theologically possible. A messenger may cry to his Lord in the world, as Moses cried, as Noah cried. He may also testify on the Day when the wrongdoer bites his hands. The verse does not date the sentence because the sentence is true in both courts.

The address is *yā-rabbi* — O my Lord. He does not answer the people in kind. He does not, in this verse, even answer them. He turns, as verse 6 taught him to turn, to the One who sent the Book. The office of warning includes the right to report that the warning was treated as waste.

**Mahjūr: Abandoned, Isolated, Treated as Nonsense**

*Mahjūr* is from *hajr*, to desert, to forsake, to leave aside, and also to talk nonsense, to isolate by speech. Al-Ṭabarī records that they took it for foolishness when they either thought it was sorcery or simply called it a lie. The word's range is useful. A people can make the Qur'an *mahjūr* by declaring it raving; by declaring it a lie; by hanging it on a wall and not living it; by reciting it beautifully and walking past its commands; by using it as a seasonal decoration. Abandonment has more than one style.

> *"And the Messenger will say, 'O my Lord, my people have taken this Qur'an as a thing abandoned.'"* (Qur'an 25:30, as also translated)

The demonstrative *hādhā* — this Qur'an — has a tenderness and a grief in it. This, which was sent down upon me, which I recited to them morning and evening (their own wording in verse 5), they have taken as *mahjūr*. The taking, again. They did not merely fail to find it. They *took* it as abandoned. An active filing.

**My People: The Pain of the Possessive**

*Qawmī* — my people. Not "the disbelievers" in the third person of theology. My people. The Prophet ﷺ's belonging to Quraysh, to the Arabs, to the community he was sent from as well as to, is not cancelled by their refusal. Noah said *qawmī*. Hūd said *qawmī*. The possessive is the wound. A stranger's rejection is cheaper. A people's taking of the gift as foolishness is the wound this verse records.

The Prophet ﷺ wept over his people. He was seen after Taʾif, and in the night journeys of his prayer, and at graves, as a man whose warning had not been a performance. Muslim (no. 179) and al-Bukhārī record his likeness as the man pulling people from the Fire by their belts. Verse 30 is what that man says when the belts have been slipped.

**A Verse That Also Faces the People of the Qur'an**

It would be a mistake to hear *qawmī* as only Quraysh. The ummah that later took the Book as an inheritance can take it as *mahjūr* in new ways: unread, untranslated into conduct, used as a weapon in a faction, honored as sound and ignored as law. The messenger's report to his Lord is not a closed historical document. It is a possible testimony about us.

The cure is the opposite taking: taking the Qur'an as *dhikr*, as criterion, as the way with the messenger. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The best of you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it."* (al-Bukhārī 5027)

And he said, of the one who has nothing of it in his heart, that he is like a ruined house (al-Tirmidhī 2913). Learning and teaching are the contrary of *hajr*. So is the night recitation of verse 64. So is the just mean of verse 67, which is a legal fruit of the Book, not a slogan from it.

**What to Do When the Book Feels Heavy or Distant**

Sometimes *hajr* begins not in hostility but in fatigue. The Book is always there, like a relative one has stopped calling. The verse can be a mercy to that fatigue if it is heard as a grief, not only as an accusation. The messenger cares that the Qur'an not be abandoned. God cares. A person who has drifted can return without a press conference: a page, a prayer in which al-Fātiḥah is actually heard, a command actually done. The opposite of *mahjūr* is not a conference about the Qur'an. It is a life in which the Qur'an is still allowed to interrupt.

**Hajr as a Habit, Not Only as a Slogan**

Abandonment has more than one style. A people can make the Qur'an *mahjūr* by declaring it raving; by declaring it a lie; by hanging it on a wall and not living it; by reciting it beautifully and walking past its commands; by using it as a seasonal decoration. The messenger’s report to his Lord is not a closed historical document about Quraysh. It is a possible testimony about us. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The best of you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it."* (al-Bukhārī 5027)

Learning and teaching are the contrary of *hajr*. So is the night recitation of verse 64. So is the just mean of verse 67, which is a legal fruit of the Book, not a slogan from it. A community that hosts conferences about the Qur'an and does not let a verse interrupt a contract has not answered verse 30. The Criterion is either a companion or a relic. The servants of the Compassionate are people of the first.

**The Possessive That Wounds**

*Qawmī* — my people. The Prophet ﷺ’s belonging to them is not cancelled by their refusal. Noah said *qawmī*. Hūd said *qawmī*. The possessive is the wound. A stranger’s rejection is cheaper. This verse records the wound so that later callers will not think their grief is a professional failure. Grief is the office. The Lord who suffices as Guide and Helper (25:31) is the One to whom the grief is filed. File it there. Then recite. The recitation is still the great striving.

**My People Have Taken This Qur'an as a Thing Abandoned**

*Wa-qāla l-rasūlu yā-rabbi inna qawmī ttakhadhū hādhā l-qurʾāna mahjūrā* — and the Messenger will say / said: O my Lord, my people have taken this Qur'an as a thing abandoned. *Mahjūr* is deserted, boycotted, left as a thing one passes. Abandonment has degrees: not believing, not reciting, reciting without *taḥkīm*, using it as furniture of a ceremony. Al-Qurṭubī lists kinds of *hajr*. The complaint is filed with *yā-rabbi*, the same address the servants will use in 25:65 and 74. The Prophet ﷺ does not, in this verse, answer the abandonment with a matching abandonment of his people. He files. *Qawmī* remains *qawmī*. For later Muslims the danger is to assume the verse is only about Quraysh. A community with many copies and many *mahjūr* hearts is in the verse. The cure is the opposite taking: take the Qur'an as *ṣāḥib* in the grave (as the reports describe), as *tartīl* in the night (25:32, 64), as a judge in the dispute. That taking is how one is not named in the complaint.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:31

> **Thus did We make for every prophet an enemy from among the guilty, and thy Lord suffices as a Guide and a Helper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pattern That Keeps a Prophet from Thinking He Is the Exception**

Verse 30 was a cry: my people have taken this Qur'an as abandoned. Verse 31 is the frame God puts around the cry. *Wa-kadhālika jaʿalnā li-kulli nabiyyin ʿaduwwan min al-mujrimīn* — "Thus did We make for every prophet an enemy from among the guilty." The *kadhālika* — thus, in that manner — means: what you are tasting is not a unique humiliation. It is the shape of the office.

The Qur'an states the same law elsewhere, with a more explicit cast of characters:

> *"Thus have We made for every prophet an enemy — satans from among mankind and jinn, who inspire each other with flowery discourse in order to deceive."* (Qur'an 6:112)

And it shows the law in story after story of this Book: Noah mocked, Hūd called a fool, Ṣāliḥ met with arrogance, Lot with indecency's defenders, Shuʿayb with market-men who hated a just measure, Moses with a tyrant and a magician class, Jesus with those who plotted, Muhammad ﷺ with those who called him fabricator, pupil, fable-reciter, bewitched. Verse 31 is the title of that series. Verses 35–40 will give some of the names.

**"We Made": The Enemy as a Permitted Instrument**

*Jaʿalnā* — We made. The verb is the verb of verse 20, where some people were made a trial for others. God does not love the guilt of the guilty. He does make the existence of opposition part of how prophecy works in a free world. If a prophet had no enemy, two things would be unclear: whether the message had cost anything, and whether the followers were following the message or the weather of the town.

Ibn Kathīr often notes, at verses like this, that the presence of enemies is a sign of a prophet's truth, not of his failure. False teachers are sometimes universally praised in their hour. The messengers collect the guilty as opponents the way a lamp collects moths — not because the lamp is at fault.

The guilty are *al-mujrimūn*, the same noun as verse 22. Opposition to prophets is here named as a crime, not as a respectable school of thought. A person may have questions. A person who makes himself an *ʿaduww*, an enemy, of a prophet has left the territory of questions.

**Thy Lord Suffices as Guide and Helper**

*Wa-kafā bi-rabbika hādiyan wa-naṣīrā* — "and thy Lord suffices as a Guide and a Helper." After the law of enmity, the law of sufficiency. *Kafā bi-* is "it is enough, it suffices, no further resource is required." The two Names that suffice are *Hādī*, Guide, and *Naṣīr*, Helper. Guidance for the road; help against the enemy. The pair matches the human need exactly. A prophet (and a follower of a prophet) can be lost in the maze of descriptions (verse 9), or he can be overpowered. He is told that the Lord who sent the Criterion is enough for both dangers.

On sufficiency the Qur'an is almost blunt:

> *"O Prophet, God is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you."* (Qur'an 8:64)

> *"Is not God sufficient for His servant?"* (Qur'an 39:36)

> *"And whoever relies upon God — then He is sufficient for him."* (Qur'an 65:3)

The Prophet ﷺ taught a morning and evening word that is this clause in a sentence:

> *"O God, I have hoped in You, and I have not hoped in another. You are the sufficiency."* (meanings collected in the well-known *ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl*, which al-Bukhārī 4563 attaches to Abraham and to the Prophet ﷺ at Uḥud)

**What Sufficiency Is Not**

Sufficiency is not the promise that enemies will vanish. Verse 31 assumes they will exist for every prophet. Sufficiency is the promise that their existence does not require a second lord, a compromising edit of the message, or a despair that the work is in vain. The Guide still guides. The Helper still helps. The Qur'an still distinguishes. The servant still walks in the market.

For a believer who is not a prophet and who still has enemies — of his faith, of his honesty, of his household — the verse is a permission to stop counting allies as if salvation were a vote. Look to the Lord as *Hādī* when the descriptions multiply. Look to Him as *Naṣīr* when the guilty organize. Then do the next right act. The making of enemies is God's. The making of a way through them is also God's. The servant's share is not to be surprised, and not to switch Guides.

**Sufficiency as Guide and Helper, Named Together**

*Kafā bi-rabbika hādiyan wa-naṣīrā* — two Names for two dangers. A prophet (and a follower) can be lost in the maze of descriptions (verse 9), or he can be overpowered. Guidance for the road; help against the enemy. The pair matches the need exactly. The Qur'an is almost blunt:

> *"Is not God sufficient for His servant?"* (Qur'an 39:36)

> *"And whoever relies upon God — then He is sufficient for him."* (Qur'an 65:3)

Sufficiency is not the promise that enemies will vanish. Verse 31 assumes they will exist for every prophet. Sufficiency is the promise that their existence does not require a second lord, a compromising edit of the message, or a despair that the work is in vain. The Guide still guides. The Helper still helps. The Qur'an still distinguishes. The servant still walks in the market. Ibn Kathīr often notes that the presence of enemies is a sign of a prophet’s truth, not of his failure. False teachers are sometimes universally praised in their hour. The messengers collect the guilty as opponents the way a lamp collects moths — not because the lamp is at fault.

**Thus Have We Appointed for Every Prophet an Enemy from the Guilty**

*Wa-kadhālika jaʿalnā li-kulli nabiyyin ʿaduwwan mina l-mujrimīn* — thus have We appointed for every prophet an enemy from the guilty. *Jaʿalnā* is the same verb that made some people a trial for others (25:20). Opposition is not an accident of poor strategy. It is a Divine appointment. *Wa-kafā bi-rabbika hādiyan wa-naṣīrā* — and thy Lord suffices as Guide and Helper. After the complaint of verse 30, sufficiency. Guidance for the road, help against the appointed enemy. The two Names of function — *Hādī*, *Naṣīr* — answer the two needs of a messenger whose Book is being abandoned: how to go on, and how not to be crushed. The Prophet ﷺ was told this so that Makkah’s hostility would look like a pattern, not like a unique failure. Every prophet. The guilty. A Lord who suffices. Living the verse is to stop asking why a truth collects enemies, and to start using the sufficiency that was named for that exact situation.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:32

> **And the disbelievers say, "Why was the Quran not sent down upon him as a single whole?" It is so, that We may make firm thine heart thereby. And We have recited it unto thee in a measured pace.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for a Book That Would Not Walk With a Life**

A new objection, related to the old ones. If this is from God, why not all at once? *Lawlā nuzzila ʿalayhi al-qurʾānu jumlatan wāḥidatan* — "Why was the Quran not sent down upon him as a single whole?" A single volume, dropped, complete, like a stone. The demand sounds like a demand for authenticity. It is often a demand to be rid of a recitation that keeps arriving, answering, forbidding, and consoling as events unfold. A finished object can be shelved. A speech that continues can not.

The Qur'an elsewhere describes its own manner:

> *"And [We sent it down] as a recitation We have divided in parts, that thou mayest recite it unto men in intervals, and We sent it down in successive revelations."* (Qur'an 17:106)

The objectors wanted *jumlatan wāḥidah*, one lump. God chose intervals. The sūrah that began with *nazzala al-furqān* — the intensive, successive sending-down — now explains why that verb was used.

**"That We May Make Firm Thine Heart"**

*Kadhālika li-nuthabbita bihi fuʾādak* — "It is so, that We may make firm thine heart thereby." The first named beneficiary of gradual revelation is the Prophet's ﷺ own heart. *Thabbata* is to make firm, to fasten, to hold a thing so it does not skid. *Fuʾād* is the heart as the seat of feeling and meaning. A man who is insulted in the market, who is called bewitched, who watches his people take the Book as abandoned, needs not only a complete doctrine. He needs a word that arrives *tonight*.

The Qur'an says this of the stories of the messengers as well:

> *"All that We recount unto thee of the stories of the messengers is that whereby We make firm thine heart."* (Qur'an 11:120)

Gradualness is pastoral. It is God pacing His speech to a human chest. Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Rāzī all highlight the relationship of the Qur'an to the events of the Prophet's ﷺ life, the gradual legislation of commands and prohibitions, and the way successive revelations were both necessary for the establishment of the community and a source of support during his struggles. Al-Rāzī lists among the benefits: the gradual application of law, the memorization and understanding of one part at a time, and the responsiveness of revelation to the questions and states of the believers.

A community cannot jump from no prayer to five prayers, from no prohibition of wine to a dry city, from no public law to a complete code, without the heart being carried. The Qur'an carried it. The demand for "all at once" is, among other things, a refusal of being carried.

**Recited in a Measured Pace**

*Wa-rattalnāhu tartīlā* — "And We have recited it unto thee in a measured pace." *Tartīl* is recitation that is distinct, ordered, unhurried, every letter given its due. Some commentators, including Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī, understand the clause to mean also, "We have explained to thee its interpretation." The measured pace is not only sound. It is meaning arriving in an order that can be borne.

The Prophet ﷺ's own recitation, as described by his companions, was *tartīl*: he would lengthen, pause at verses, and pray with the Book in a way that could take hours over a short sūrah. The sending-down matches the reciting. God did not dump. The messenger did not gabble. A religion of *tartīl* is a religion that refuses both the stone of "all at once" and the noise of haste.

**The Two Sendings the Commentators Combine**

Ibn Kathīr and others describe a two-stage descent: the Qur'an descended all at once from the Preserved Tablet (85:22) or the Mother of the Book (13:39) to the heaven of this world, and then descended gradually to the Prophet ﷺ over twenty-two or twenty-three years. The objectors, if they had known that doctrine, still would not have been satisfied, because what they wanted was not a heavenly completeness. They wanted an earthly event they could treat as a spectacle and then leave. God gave them instead a companionship of twenty-three years.

**What This Teaches Anyone Who Is Being Formed**

A person who wants to be changed "all at once" — faith, character, knowledge, healing — is repeating the objectors' demand in a private form. Hearts are made firm *thereby*, by the portion that arrives for this week, this grief, this temptation. Measured pace is not delay as neglect. It is delay as pedagogy.

The practical imitation of the verse is to take the Book as it was given: a portion, with *tartīl*, attached to a life. Not a binge of pages that leave the *fuʾād* untouched. Not a wait for a perfect retreat when the whole can be absorbed. The night prayers of the servants of the Compassionate (25:64) are *tartīl* made into a schedule. So is any day in which one verse is understood and done. The heart is fastened by what it actually receives, not by what it owns as a downloaded whole.

**Tartīl as Pedagogy, Not as Delay**

The Qur'an describes its own manner:

> *"And [We sent it down] as a recitation We have divided in parts, that thou mayest recite it unto men in intervals, and We sent it down in successive revelations."* (Qur'an 17:106)

> *"All that We recount unto thee of the stories of the messengers is that whereby We make firm thine heart."* (Qur'an 11:120)

Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Rāzī all highlight the relationship of the Qur'an to the events of the Prophet’s ﷺ life, the gradual legislation of commands and prohibitions, and the way successive revelations were both necessary for the community and a source of support. Al-Rāzī lists among the benefits: gradual application of law, memorization of one part at a time, and responsiveness to the questions and states of the believers. A community cannot jump from no prayer to five prayers, from no prohibition of wine to a dry city, without the heart being carried. The objectors wanted a finished object they could shelf. God gave a companionship of twenty-three years. A person who wants to be changed "all at once" is repeating their demand in a private form. Hearts are made firm *thereby*, by the portion that arrives for this week, this grief, this temptation.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:33

> **And they come not to thee with any parable, but that We bring to thee the truth and a better explanation.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Mathal They Bring Is Already Outmatched**

*Wa-lā yaʾtūnaka bi-mathalin illā jiʾnāka bil-ḥaqqi wa-aḥsana tafsīrā* — "And they come not to thee with any parable, but that We bring to thee the truth and a better explanation." Verse 9 told the Prophet ﷺ to look at how they set forth descriptions of him. Verse 33 tells him what God does with those descriptions. They arrive with a *mathal* — a comparison, a slogan, a "gotcha," a picture meant to discredit. God arrives with *al-ḥaqq*, the truth, and with a *tafsīr* that is better.

Ibn Kathīr says *mathal* here refers to the arguments or challenges the disbelievers used in order to try to discredit the Prophet ﷺ and the Qur'an. The word's range — parable, symbol, comparison, description — is the range of rhetoric. They are fighting with images. God answers with reality and with a clearer unpacking of it.

**Tafsīr Before Tafsīr Was a Genre**

*Tafsīr* in later technical usage is the name of Qur'anic commentary as a discipline. Here it is the ordinary noun: explanation, unveiling, making a thing's meaning distinct. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī note that the explanation God brings is always better than the challenges presented by the opponents. Better, not merely louder. The Criterion does not win by volume. It wins by making the matter more lucid than the smear.

This is a promise to the messenger that no objection is the last word. It is also a description of how the Qur'an actually behaves. They say: why a human messenger? The Qur'an explains prophecy's humanity (25:20). They say: why not all at once? The Qur'an explains the firming of the heart (25:32). They say: fables of the old. The Qur'an explains that the Speaker is the Knower of secrets (25:6). Every *mathal* becomes an occasion for more light. Opposition, in this verse, is drafted into the service of explanation.

**Truth First, Then the Better Unpacking**

The order inside the verse is itself a method. *Al-ḥaqq* comes first. Then *aḥsan tafsīr*. God does not first offer a cleverer image. He brings the real. Explanation is of the real. A community that learns to fight only with counter-slogans has not learned this verse. It has learned the opponents' game with a different team shirt. The prophetic method is: state what is so, then make it clearer than the caricature.

The Prophet ﷺ's own practice matched this. When they called him *mudhammam* (the blamed) instead of Muhammad (the praised), he said, as al-Bukhārī (no. 3533) records in meaning, that they were satirizing a man named Blamed, while he was Muhammad. He did not build a larger satire. He pointed at the name that was true.

**What This Does to a Believer's Anxiety About "New" Objections**

Every age produces *amthāl* that feel unprecedented: the Qur'an as a human product of late antiquity, the Prophet ﷺ as a statesman-only, the law as cruelty, the unseen as a brain-state. Verse 33's grammar is universal: they do not come with any parable *except* that We bring you the truth and a better explanation. The "except" is a guarantee. It does not mean every individual believer will have the better explanation on his tongue in five seconds. It means the resource exists, in the Book and in the light God gives around the Book, and that panic is not required.

The scholar's work, the teacher's work, the parent's work, is to seek that *tafsīr* rather than to improvise a *mathal* of our own. Sometimes the better explanation is a verse already in this sūrah. Sometimes it is a ḥadīth. Sometimes it is a patience that lets a bad comparison collapse under its own contradictions, as verse 9 said they cannot find a way.

**Living as People of Explanation, Not as People of Reflex**

A practical fruit: when a mockery arrives — at a table, on a screen, in a chest — do not let the first reflex be the last word. Ask what *ḥaqq* the mockery is twisting. Then look for the clearer account. The servants of the Compassionate will, in verse 63, answer the ignorant with "Peace." Peace is not surrender. It is the refusal to let a *mathal* set the tone. The better explanation can be given when the chest is not already on fire. *Tartīl* (verse 32) and *tafsīr* (verse 33) are kin: both are unhurried clarity. The religion that was sent down at a measured pace is to be defended at a measured pace. Haste is how a person trades the better explanation for a worse slogan.

**Every Mathal Met, and None Left Unanswered**

*Lā yaʾtūnaka bi-mathalin* is a promise of coverage. No analogy they invent — poet, sorcerer, madman, forger, man who eats food, man without a treasure — will be left without *ḥaqq* and a better *tafsīr*. The Prophet ﷺ did not need to panic at a new slogan. God had already undertaken the reply. Later scholars who meet new *amthāl* — new philosophies, new mockeries, new “scientific” dismissals — are heirs of this promise if they seek the *ḥaqq* in the Book rather than inventing a worse slogan of their own. Al-Rāzī treats the verse as a principle of debate: the Qur'an’s answers are not only true but clearer. Clarity is part of the mercy of *tartīl*. A religion that wins by fog has left verse 33. A religion that answers every *mathal* with a curse and no *tafsīr* has also left it. The servants who say *salām* to the ignorant are not empty of explanation. They are people who wait for the chest to be able to hear the better one.

**They Bring Thee No Similitude but that We Bring Thee the Truth and a Better Explanation**

*Wa-lā yaʾtūnaka bi-mathalin illā jiʾnāka bil-ḥaqqi wa-aḥsana tafsīrā* — they bring thee no similitude but that We bring thee the truth and better as explanation. Coverage is promised. Poet, sorcerer, madman, forger, hungry man, man without treasure: each *mathal* is met. The Prophet ﷺ need not panic at a new slogan. Later heirs meet new *amthāl* the same way: from the Book, not with a worse slogan. Al-Rāzī: the answers are not only true but clearer. Clarity is of the mercy of *tartīl*. A religion that wins by fog, or by curse without *tafsīr*, has left verse 33. Peace to the ignorant (25:63) is not emptiness of explanation. It is waiting until the chest can hear the better one.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:34

> **[As for] those who are gathered upon their faces to Hell, their place is worse and they are further astray from the way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gathered Upon Their Faces: The Body Turned into a Verdict**

*Alladhīna yuḥsharūna ʿalā wujūhihim ilā jahannam* — "those who are gathered upon their faces to Hell." *Ḥashr* is the gathering of verse 17. The new element is the posture. They are not led on their feet. They are driven on their faces. The face, which in this world is honor, direction, and the place a person is recognized, is put to the ground as a means of travel. The Qur'an repeats the image:

> *"And We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces — blind, dumb, and deaf. Their refuge is Hell."* (Qur'an 17:97)

> *"The Day they are dragged into the Fire on their faces: 'Taste the touch of Saqar.'"* (Qur'an 54:48)

The face is singled out in punishment in other verses as well — 8:50, 14:50, 23:104, 27:90, 33:66 — because the face was the site of arrogance. They waxed arrogant in their souls (25:21). They turned their faces from the messenger in the market. Gathering on the face is the moral inverted into the physical. A companion asked how they would walk on their faces, and the Prophet ﷺ answered:

> *"The One who made them walk on their feet is able to make them walk on their faces."* (al-Bukhārī 4760; Muslim 2806)

The answer refuses a physics-quarrel. Ability is not the problem. The problem is the life that earned a face as a hoof.

**Their Place Is Worse**

*Ulāʾika sharrun makānan* — "their place is worse." Worse than the dwelling of the Garden’s people in verse 24; than any place they occupied in the world; than the "way" they thought they had when they described the Prophet ﷺ into a maze. *Makān* is place, rank, location. They competed for place in Makkah — a better house, a better seat at a council, a better standing than a man who ate food in the markets. The place they receive is *sharr*, worse, evil as a location. Hell in this verse is not only a temperature. It is a *makān*, and the *makān* is worse. Human beings understand this. A beautiful room in which one is despised can be a worse place than a poor room in which one is loved. Hell is the worst of both: no beauty, no love, no rank except the rank of being further from the way.

**Further Astray from the Way**

*Wa-aḍallu sabīlā* — "and they are further astray from the way." The sūrah has used *sabīl*, way, as the thing the objectors cannot find (verse 9), the thing the wrongdoer wishes he had taken with the messenger (verse 27), the thing the worshipped are asked whether the servants left (verse 17). Here the people gathered on their faces are *aḍall*, more astray, further from that way. The comparative is a moral geography. Astrayness has degrees. Cattle, verse 44 will say, are astray in one sense; these are further. A person who never heard is astray in one sense; a person who heard, described, mocked, and took a *fulān* instead of a messenger is further. Gathering on the face is the end of that further.

**Why This Verse Follows the Promise of a Better Explanation**

Verse 33 promised that every parable they bring will be met with truth and a better *tafsīr*. Verse 34 shows the people who refused the better explanation. The sequence is fair. Light was supplied. The gathering is of those who preferred their *mathal* to the *ḥaqq*. No one is on his face because God ran out of words. For the living, the verse is a check on the love of face — *jāh*, reputation, the need to be seen as the one who was right in the room. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever has in his heart an atom's weight of arrogance will not enter Paradise."* (Muslim 91)

Arrogance lives in the face: the turned cheek, the look down, the refusal to be seen walking with the poor. The cure is the posture of the servants of the Compassionate: they walk *hawnan*, humbly, upon the earth (25:63). A face that has learned to look down in humility in this world is a face that may be raised in the next. A face that could not bear to lose a room’s opinion is a face at risk of becoming a means of travel toward *jahannam*. The way is still findable. That is why the verse is in a Book recited on earth, not only on a Day when the gathering has already been done.

**Worse Place, Most Astray Way**

*Sharrun makānan wa-aḍallu sabīlā* answers verse 24’s *khayrun mustaqarran wa-aḥsanu maqīlā*. Two real estates, two ways. The sūrah is a comparison of dwellings. Palaces they demanded (25:10) are not even in the comparison. The comparison is Garden versus gathering-on-the-face. Al-Rāzī notes that being gathered on the face is both humiliation and a fitting of the organ they used for arrogance. They walked with faces lifted. They are driven with faces down. The Prophet ﷺ was asked how they would walk on their faces and answered that the One who made them walk on their feet is able to make them walk on their faces (al-Bukhārī 4760; Muslim 2806). Ability is the short answer. Justice is the long one. A face that refused to bow at the Name *al-Raḥmān* (25:60) is a face that has already begun the posture of verse 34. The servants who walk *hawnan* are practicing the opposite gathering. Humility now is not a social style. It is a rehearsal of a head that will, if God wills, be raised then.

**Those Who Are Gathered on Their Faces to Hell**

*Alladhīna yuḥsharūna ʿalā wujūhihim ilā jahannama* — those who are gathered on their faces toward Hell. The face they would not put on the floor for *al-Raḥmān* (25:60) becomes the walking surface. The Prophet ﷺ was asked how, and said the One who made them walk on their feet is able to make them walk on their faces (al-Bukhārī 4760). *Awlāʾika sharrun makānan wa-aḍallu sabīlā* — those are worse in place and more astray as a way. The ranking answers verse 24. Two places, two ways. Palaces are not in the comparison. Garden and this gathering are. Living the verse is a care of the face now: lower it in *sujūd*, lower it in a market, do not need it to win a room. The way is still findable. That is why the gathering is described in a Book recited on earth.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:35

> **And indeed We gave unto Moses the Book and placed with him his brother as a helper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Law of Enmity to a Named Messenger**

Verse 31 said that every prophet was given an enemy from among the guilty. Verse 35 begins the illustration. Moses is named first, as he so often is in the Qur'an, because his story is the largest mirror the Book holds up to Muhammad ﷺ: a Book given, a people addressed, a tyrant opposed, a brother beside him, a community that still, after rescue, found ways to refuse. *Wa-laqad ātaynā Mūsā al-kitāb* — "And indeed We gave unto Moses the Book." The verb *ātaynā*, We gave, is the verb of a completed bestowal. The Torah is not, in this verse, a human memory. It is a gift, as the Criterion is a gift. The two Books stand in the sūrah as kin. Verse 1 gave the Furqān to a servant. Verse 35 gave the Kitāb to Moses. The objectors who wanted a messenger unlike other men are shown a messenger very like theirs.

**A Brother Placed as Wazīr**

*Wa-jaʿalnā maʿahu akhāhu Hārūna wazīrā* — "and placed with him his brother as a helper." *Wazīr* is a helper who carries part of the load — from *wizr*, a burden. Moses asked for this in another recitation:

> *"And appoint for me a helper from my family, Aaron, my brother. Strengthen my back by him, and let him share my task."* (Qur'an 20:29–32)

God answered that prayer, and this verse records the answer as a completed act: We placed. The placing is God’s, even though the asking was Moses’. Help in the office of warning is not a betrayal of tawḥīd. Verse 2 denied a partner in *sovereignty*. It did not deny a brother in *mission*. Muhammad ﷺ, who is being addressed through this history, had Abū Bakr, had ʿAlī, had the Companions who carried burdens. He also, unlike Moses, is reminded in verse 51 that God could have sent a warner to every town and did not. The two facts sit together: prophets may be given helpers; they are not given an exemption from the weight. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the command to go to Pharaoh is sometimes in the singular (20:24) and sometimes in the dual. Verse 35 is the gift of the pair. Verse 36 will be the sending of the pair.

**Why the Helper Is a Brother**

The verse does not say "an assistant." It says *akhāhu*, his brother. Kinship here is in the service of revelation, not in the service of a dynasty. Aaron is not a co-god. He is a co-worker. The Qur'an will also record Aaron’s trial with the calf, so the helper is not an infallible charm. He is a mercy. A man who has to face Pharaoh alone is a man in one kind of danger. A man who has a brother who can speak (Moses said he was more eloquent, 28:34) is a man whose weakness has been provided for. The Prophet ﷺ praised brotherhood in the faith as a covenant that outlasts blood:

> *"None of you believes until he loves for his brother what he loves for himself."* (al-Bukhārī 13; Muslim 45)

Moses and Aaron are the prophetic form of that love: a shared task, a strengthened back, a Book in the house. The guilty of Pharaoh’s court would have preferred Moses isolated. God placed a brother. Isolation is not a proof of authenticity. It is sometimes a proof that a person has not yet been given, or has not yet accepted, a *wazīr*.

**What This Says to a Community That Wants Lone Heroes**

Some religious cultures love the solitary genius and distrust companionship in good. Verse 35 does not. The Book is given to Moses, and a helper is placed with him. Work that is from God can be shared without being diluted. The servant of the Compassionate who walks humbly (25:63) is not walking in a fiction of self-sufficiency. He is walking as part of a people who pray, spend, and repent together. For the Prophet ﷺ hearing this in Makkah, the verse is consolation of a specific kind. You have enemies (verse 31). So did Moses. You have a Book. So did Moses. You have, around you, people who are already beginning to carry. That carrying is a Divine placing, not a political accident. Sufficiency, verse 31 said, is in the Lord as Guide and Helper. One of the forms of that help is a human brother. The two helps do not compete. The second is a gift of the first.

**The Book in the House, and the Strengthened Back**

Moses is given the Book, then given a brother. Revelation is not a replacement for companionship; companionship is not a replacement for the Book. Communities that idolize texts and starve friendship produce brittle scholars. Communities that idolize friendship and starve texts produce warm error. Verse 35 keeps both gifts in one sentence. *Wazīr* is a word of weight: a back, a support, a vizier. The Prophet ﷺ himself, in a famous ḥadīth of ʿAlī, used the Moses–Aaron pairing as a likeness of closeness, while the same tradition is careful not to make ʿAlī a prophet. The point that survives every dispute about that ḥadīth is the structure: God does not always send a man alone. He ﷺ had Abū Bakr in the cave, ʿUmar as a strength, ʿUthmān as a shy generosity, ʿAlī as a young lion of the house, Khadījah as the first to believe, and a community that would, after him, carry. Verse 35 is the theology of that carrying. A servant who refuses every *wazīr* in the name of sincerity may be refusing a gift God still gives. A servant who collects *wuzarāʾ* as a court may be building Pharaoh’s furniture. The test is the Book: does the helper help the Book, or help the self?

**We Gave Moses the Book and Appointed with Him His Brother Aaron as Helper**

*Wa-la-qad ātaynā Mūsā l-kitāba wa-jaʿalnā maʿahu akhāhu Hārūna wazīrā* — We gave Moses the Book and appointed with him his brother Aaron as a vizier. Book first, then brother. Isolation is not a proof of authenticity. The guilty of Pharaoh’s court would have preferred Moses alone. God placed a *wazīr*.
