For the Prophet ﷺ in Makkah the verse is consolation: you have enemies (25:31), as Moses did; you have a Book; you have people beginning to carry. Sufficiency is in the Lord as Guide and Helper (25:31). One form of that help is a human back. Communities that starve friendship for texts, or starve texts for friendship, have split what verse 35 joined. The servants of the Compassionate walk as a people, not as a fiction of self-sufficiency.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:36

> **Then We said, "Go to the people who have denied Our signs." Then We destroyed them completely.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sending, Then a Destruction, in Two Sentences**

*Fa-qulnā idhhabā ilā al-qawmi alladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — "Then We said, 'Go to the people who have denied Our signs.'" The dual *idhhabā*, go (you two), addresses Moses and Aaron together, the pair verse 35 has just assembled. Their destination is not described as a people who have not yet heard. It is described as a people who have already denied the signs. The mission is to a refusal in progress.

Then, without a narrative of plagues, sea, or staff: *fa-dammarnāhum tadmīrā* — "Then We destroyed them completely." The cognate accusative again: We destroyed them a destruction. *Dammara* is used in the Qur'an for a ruin that leaves a people unmade — 7:137, 17:16, 26:172, 27:51, 37:136, 47:10. The whole of the Exodus confrontation, which other sūrahs unfold at length, is here a hinge between a command and a demolition. The brevity is the point. For the purposes of this sūrah, what matters is the pattern: Book given, helper placed, sending to deniers, complete destruction of the deniers.

**Denied Our Signs: The Crime That Precedes the Ruin**

*Kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — they called Our signs a lie. The same verb as verse 11's denial of the Hour. Signs, in Moses' story, were public: staff, hand, flood, locusts, frogs, blood, the sea itself. Signs, in Muhammad's ﷺ story, are the Criterion, the recitation, the character, the news of the unseen. The people of Pharaoh denied the first kind. The people of Makkah are denying the second. Verse 36 puts them in one sentence of history so that Makkah cannot say, "We have not seen a staff." Denial is denial of what God actually sent, not of what the denier would have preferred to be sent (angels, treasure, a book in one lump).

**Why the Destruction Is Narrated Without Glee**

There is no gloating adjective. No catalogue of screams. *Dammarnāhum tadmīrā* is a completed Divine act, stated as fact. The Qur'an, when it tells of ruined nations, is not entertaining. It is warning a living city that the act has happened before and that the God who placed Aaron as *wazīr* is the God who can unmake a people who deny.

The Prophet ﷺ passed, on the Tabūk expedition, by the dwellings of Thamūd and forbade his companions to enter the ruined places except weeping (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980). That is the etiquette of hearing a verse like 36. The destruction is God's. The listener's share is fear, not thrill, and not a casual "they deserved it" that forgets the same Lord is Seeing (25:20) of this town too.

**Go: The Dual Command Still Has a Single Point**

Al-Qurṭubī's note about the dual and the singular is useful here. Sometimes the burden is described as Moses'; sometimes as Moses-and-Aaron's. The command *idhhabā* in this verse is shared, and the destruction that follows is not credited to their military talent. They went. God destroyed. The sequence protects two errors: the error that messengers destroy by themselves, and the error that messengers need not go because destruction is God's anyway. They must go. He may destroy. Their going is obedience. His destroying is judgment. The two acts are not rivals.

**What a Later Community Does with a Verse of Demolition**

It does not appoint itself the demolition team on the basis of a Makkan story. Permission to fight, when it comes in Islam, comes with its own laws, and al-Qurṭubī will later note at verse 52 that this sūrah is Makkan and that striving "by means of it" is striving with the Qur'an. Verse 36 is a history of God's act toward a people to whom two messengers had gone. The living analogue is: go with the signs you have been given — the Book, the explanation, the character — to people who are already in denial, and leave the unmaking of nations to the One who unmade Pharaoh.

Patience (25:20) and measured recitation (25:32) are the mood in which this verse is carried. Destruction complete is a past tense about them. For us the present tense is still *idhhabā*: go, the two of you, the many of you, to those who have called the signs a lie, while the door of verse 6 — Forgiving, Merciful — has not yet been replaced by the door of *tadmīr*.

**Denied Signs, Then Complete Destruction**

*Kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — they called Our signs a lie. The same verb as verse 11’s denial of the Hour. Signs, in Moses’ story, were public: staff, hand, flood, locusts, the sea. Signs, in Muhammad’s ﷺ story, are the Criterion, the recitation, the character, the news of the unseen. The people of Pharaoh denied the first kind. The people of Makkah are denying the second. Verse 36 puts them in one sentence of history so that Makkah cannot say, "We have not seen a staff." Denial is denial of what God actually sent, not of what the denier would have preferred to be sent. The Prophet ﷺ passed the dwellings of Thamūd and forbade his companions to enter the ruined places except weeping (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980). That is the etiquette of hearing *tadmīr*. The destruction is God’s. The listener’s share is fear, not thrill, and not a casual "they deserved it" that forgets the same Lord is Seeing (25:20) of this town too.

**Go to the People Who Have Denied Our Signs**

*Idhhabā ilā l-qawmi lladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — go, you two, to the people who have denied Our signs. The dual is Moses and Aaron of verse 35. The sending is into a denial already in progress. Prophets are not always sent to blank slates. They are sent to *kadhdhabū*. *Fa-dammarnāhum tadmīrā* — then We destroyed them with a destruction. The cognate accusative, as in verse 25’s *tanzīlā* and verse 65’s intensity, makes the act complete. Between the *idhhabā* and the *tadmīr* sat the years of signs Pharaoh called magic. Makkah is being told the interval is not infinity. The Prophet ﷺ was told he had a *wazīr*-community forming, as Moses had Aaron, and that denial of signs has an end named *tadmīr*. Patience in the interval is verse 20’s *a-taṣbirūn*. The destruction is God’s, not the messenger’s. Verse 56 will keep him in the office of announcement. Verse 36 is history’s witness that announcement, if denied, is not the last Divine act.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:37

> **And Noah's people: when they denied the messengers, We drowned them and made of them a sign for mankind. And We have prepared for the wrongdoers a painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A People Named by Their Denial, Not by Their Craft**

The catalogue of destroyed nations continues. Moses and Aaron were sent and Pharaoh's people were demolished. Now: *wa-qawma Nūḥin lammā kadhdhabū al-rusula aghraqnāhum* — "And Noah's people: when they denied the messengers, We drowned them." The famous ship, the pegs, the oven that boiled, the son who would not board — none of that is here. What is here is the moral cause and the elemental means: denial, drowning.

They denied *al-rusul*, the messengers, in the plural, though Noah is the messenger named in their story. The commentators explain the plural in ways that all have weight: denying one messenger is denying the office and therefore the rest; Noah preached so long that he stood for a succession of warnings; they denied Noah and the messengers who would come, in principle. The Qur'an uses this plural of Noah's people elsewhere:

> *"The people of Noah denied the messengers."* (Qur'an 26:105)

A people can live in the time of one man and still be guilty of refusing a whole chain, because the chain is one message about one God.

**Drowning as a Sign, Not as a Spectacle**

*Wa-jaʿalnāhum lil-nāsi āyah* — "and made of them a sign for mankind." The drowned are not only a past tense. They are an *āyah*, a sign, something to be read. Water, which this sūrah will later praise as pure, reviving, distributed (verses 48–50), is here the instrument of unmaking. The same created thing is mercy or judgment depending on the will that uses it and the people it meets.

The Qur'an tells Noah's story at length in Sūrah 11, Sūrah 71, 7:59–64, 23:23–30, 26:105–22. The sign-status of the drowned is stated so that later people — including the Quraysh, including us — cannot treat the story as a children's boat. *Lil-nāsi*, for mankind. The audience of the sign is not only Israel, not only the Arabs. Mankind is to read water and remember that a people can be removed.

**A Painful Punishment Prepared**

*Wa-aʿtadnā lil-ẓālimīna ʿadhāban alīmā* — "And We have prepared for the wrongdoers a painful punishment." The drowning was in the world. The prepared punishment is, as in verse 11's Blaze, the further end. Worldly ruin does not exhaust the file. Al-Rāzī and others read such clauses as the Hereafter's share, so that no one says, "They already drowned; the account is closed." Wrongdoing that reaches the level of denying messengers has a worldly face and an unseen face. Both are God's.

The word *alīm*, painful, is an adjective the Qur'an uses without embarrassment. Pain is not a metaphor here. The religion that names the Garden's rest as beautiful (25:24) names the wrongdoer's punishment as painful. Proportion is not served by softening either noun.

**Noah's Length as the Hidden Backdrop**

Though this verse does not mention Noah's centuries of calling, the rest of the Qur'an does, and the reader of al-Furqān is expected to hear it. Denial in this verse is not a single evening's mistake. It is the settled posture of a people who heard a man so long that his warning became furniture. The Quraysh have not had Noah's centuries. They have had a messenger of their own tongue, walking their markets. If Noah's people became a sign for mankind, a people who refuse a shorter, clearer, final warning are not in a safer position.

The Prophet ﷺ said that he and the Hour have been sent like two fingers together (al-Bukhārī 6505; Muslim 867). The time is shorter. The sign of the drowned is therefore more urgent, not less. A sign is useful only if it is read before the water, or the Fire, or the sky's splitting, arrives.

**Reading the Sign Without Becoming Cruel**

A believer reads Noah's people as a warning about denial, and also as a warning about despair in calling. Noah was told, at last, that none further of his people would believe (11:36). Until that Divine report, he called. Verse 37's destruction is God's closing act, not a preacher's license to stop seeing his people as *qawmī* (25:30). The messenger's grief and the Lord's drowning are different offices. Ours, unless we are told otherwise, is still the first.

**Drowning as a Sign for Mankind**

*Wa-jaʿalnāhum lil-nāsi āyah* — the drowned are not only a past tense. They are an *āyah*, a sign, something to be read. Water, which this sūrah will later praise as pure, reviving, distributed (verses 48–50), is here the instrument of unmaking. The same created thing is mercy or judgment depending on the will that uses it. The Qur'an tells Noah at length in Sūrah 11, Sūrah 71, 7:59–64, 23:23–30, 26:105–22. This verse does not retell. It names the cause (denial of the messengers, in the plural, because denying one is denying the office) and the means (drowning) and the remaining use (a sign for *al-nās*, mankind). The Prophet ﷺ said that he and the Hour have been sent like two fingers together (al-Bukhārī 6505; Muslim 867). The time is shorter than Noah’s centuries. The sign of the drowned is therefore more urgent, not less. A sign is useful only if it is read before the water, or the Fire, or the sky’s splitting, arrives.

**Noah’s People: The Drowned as a Sign**

*Wa-qawma Nūḥin lammā kadhdhabū l-rusula aghraqnāhum* — and Noah’s people, when they denied the messengers, We drowned them. *Al-rusul* in the plural: to deny Noah is to deny the office, because the message is one. Centuries of calling, then water. *Wa-aʿtadnā lahum ʿadhāban alīmā* — and We have prepared for them a painful punishment. The drowning is not the whole file. A further punishment is prepared. Water can end a body and not end a case. The sūrah’s later praise of rain (25:48–50) must be read next to this verse or rain becomes a sentimental god. The Sender of water chooses the office: revival of a dead land, or covering of a denying people. *Āyah* — a sign for mankind — is the remaining mercy to those who were not on that boat and are not yet in that Fire. Reading the sign is *tadhakkur* (25:50, 62). Refusing it is *kufūr*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:38

> **And ʿĀd and Thamūd and the inhabitants of al-Rass, and many generations between them,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Names in a List, Which Is Itself a Teaching**

The verse is almost only names: *wa-ʿĀdan wa-Thamūda wa-aṣḥāba al-Rassi wa-qurūnan bayna dhālika kathīrā* — "And ʿĀd and Thamūd and the inhabitants of al-Rass, and many generations between them." No verbs of destruction yet; those come in verse 39. The list is a way of saying: the pattern is not two examples. It is a human habit with a long bibliography. Al-Rāzī prefers, at al-Rass, not to lock the warning to a file we can close with a map. The Qur'an is not a handbook of archaeology. It is a Book of signs. A people can be named enough to be a warning and not named enough to become a curiosity.

ʿĀd and Thamūd are among the Qur'an's most frequent national names. ʿĀd, the people of Hūd, were given stature and power in the windy south of Arabia. They said:

> *"Who is stronger than we?"* (Qur'an 41:15)

and a screaming wind unmade them. Thamūd, the people of Ṣāliḥ, carved homes in rock; they hamstrung the she-camel that was their sign, and a cry or a tremor unmade them. The Quraysh knew these names. They passed the carved dwellings on their northern and southern roads. The list is not foreign history. It is regional memory summoned as evidence. The Qur'an tells ʿĀd at 7:65–72, 11:50–60, 41:15–16, 54:18–21; Thamūd at 7:73–79, 11:61–68, 26:141–58, 54:23–31. This verse does not retell. It rolls the names, as a court clerk rolls prior convictions, so that Makkah cannot say "we are the first to find a human messenger implausible."

**Al-Rass: A Name That Refuses to Be a File We Can Close**

There is no agreement, al-Ṭabarī records, as to the identity of the people of al-Rass, or even whether the term is a proper name, since the word can mean "the well" or "the pit." Al-Rāzī lists several possibilities: a geographical location; a people descended from or part of Thamūd; a people who threw their prophet into a well. He prefers the opinion that none of these details are ascertainable, and that the significance of the story lies in the fact that these were people who, like ʿĀd and Thamūd, were destroyed because of their disbelief. Sūrah Qāf names them again among the deniers:

> *"The people of Noah denied before them, and the people of al-Rass, and Thamūd, and ʿĀd, and Pharaoh, and the brothers of Lot, and the companions of the thicket, and the people of Tubbaʿ. Each denied the messengers, so My threat was just."* (Qur'an 50:12–14)

The honesty of "we do not know" is itself commentary. Al-Rass stands as a well into which a messenger was thrown, or as a town we cannot place — and in either case as a reminder that God has destroyed more nations than our libraries have filed. A religion that required a complete dossier before it would warn would be a religion of historians, not of warners. Verse 38 is content with a name and a blank.

**Many Generations Between Them**

*Wa-qurūnan bayna dhālika kathīrā* — "and many generations between them." *Qarn* is a generation, a people of an age, a horn of time. The Qur'an will not list them. *Kathīrā*, many, is the word that should humble a historian of religion. Between the named, the unnamed. Between the famous ruins, the ruins that have gone back to sand. The objectors of Makkah, who thought their city too well-placed to fall, are being told that the space of history is crowded with the fallen, most of them without a sūrah.

> *"And how many a generation before them have We destroyed? Do you perceive of them anyone, or hear from them a sound?"* (Qur'an 19:98)

Verse 38 is 19:98 as a roll call that includes a blank. The blank is the more frightening line. A named people can be studied until they become a story. An unnamed many cannot. They are the majority of the human past. They denied, as 50:14 says of the named, and the threat was just. Makkah is being invited to notice that uniqueness is not available as a defense. You are not the first proud town. You are not even in the named list yet. You are being warned before the list grows.

**Why a List Is the Right Rhetoric Here**

After the intimate scenes of the wrongdoer biting his hands and blaming *fulān*, the sūrah pulls the camera back until persons become peoples, and peoples become a list, and the list ends in "many." The individual drama is not cancelled. It is located. Your *fulān*, your abandoned Qur'an, your demand for angels, are local forms of a thing ʿĀd did, Thamūd did, al-Rass did, and many did. You are not an unprecedented mind. You are, if you deny, in a well-attended category. The Prophet ﷺ used to seek refuge from being of the people of a destroyed town, and he wept passing Thamūd’s dwellings. Al-Bukhārī (3380) and Muslim (2980) record that he forbade his companions to enter those ruined places except weeping. That is the etiquette of this verse. Read the names. Do not enjoy them. Ask whether a generation that has the Criterion in its houses is using that gift in a way that would make a later list add us, or in a way that would make us, instead, the servants of the Compassionate who were patient when some of them were a trial for others (25:20).

**What a Modern Reader Does with ʿĀd, Thamūd, and a Blank**

He does not reduce them to climate events or to folklore. He also does not invent details God did not give. He lets the list do its work: pride of strength (ʿĀd), pride of craft (Thamūd), violence against a messenger (al-Rass, in one reading), and the unnamed many who did some version of the same. Then he asks where his own town’s pride lives — in technology, in markets, in the mockery of a hungry warner, in the taking of *hawā* as god. The list is not past. The list is a mirror with several frames. Verse 39 will say that each was given parables and each was ruined utterly. Verse 38 is the attendance sheet. Being on it, unnamed, is still being on it.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:39

> **for each We set forth parables, and each We ruined utterly.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Parables First, Ruin Second**

*Wa-kullan ḍarabnā lahu al-amthāl* — "for each We set forth parables." *Kullan*, each: ʿĀd, Thamūd, al-Rass, the many generations. None was destroyed in the dark. God *ḍarabnā lahu al-amthāl* — struck for them the similitudes, set forth the comparisons, gave them the pictures by which a people can understand. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī gloss this as exhortations and proofs. *Mathal*, as verse 33 used it of the disbelievers’ slogans, is here used of God’s own pedagogy. They came with parables to discredit. He had already come with parables to guide. The verb *ḍaraba* for parables is the Qur'an's usual verb:

> *"God sets forth parables for mankind, so that they may reflect."* (Qur'an 14:25)

> *"And these similitudes We strike for mankind, but none understands them except those who know."* (Qur'an 29:43)

A people that perishes in this Book is a people that was spoken to in a language of examples. The ruin that follows is therefore not a snatch in the night. It is the second act of a conversation they refused. Ibn Kathīr, at the stories of ʿĀd and Thamūd, always notes the sign they were given — the wind they were warned by, the she-camel they were told not to harm — before he notes the cry that unmade them. Verse 39 is that order as a law: *amthāl*, then *tatbīr*. No nation in this list is a victim of Divine silence.

**Ruined Utterly: The Verb That Crumbles**

*Wa-kullan tabbarnā tatbīrā* — "and each We ruined utterly." *Tabbara* is to break, to shatter, to reduce to fragments; the noun *tabār* is ruin. Al-Rāzī notes that the verb also connotes the sense of something crumbling. The cognate accusative — We ruined them a ruining — is the same intensive music as *dammarnāhum tadmīrā* in verse 36. Each nation is given its own verb of end in the Qur'an: drowning, wind, cry, rain of stones, swallowing by the earth. Here a single verb covers them all, because in this sūrah they are one argument. However the crumbling came, it came after parables.

> *"And how many a town have We destroyed while it was doing wrong, so that it is fallen down on its roofs, and how many a deserted well and lofty palace."* (Qur'an 22:45)

Palaces, which verse 10 promised the Prophet ﷺ if God willed, are here the leftover of *tatbīr*. The Quraysh’s demand for palaces as a proof of messengerhood is answered, in the long view of this verse, by palaces that did not save the people who had them. *Tatbīr* is not impressed by stone.

**The Justice of "Each"**

The repeated *kullan* — each, and each — is a refusal of the idea that some nations were toyed with and some were warned. Each received *amthāl*. Each was ruined. The equality is the equality of God’s way, not of their sizes. A small people of a well (al-Rass) and a proud people of pillars (ʿĀd) are under one grammar. Makkah, which is neither as ancient as ʿĀd in its own telling nor as obscure as al-Rass, is being invited into that grammar while the parables are still being struck — this very sūrah being one of them. The Prophet ﷺ is in the middle of striking *amthāl* for them (verse 33 promised a better explanation for every *mathal* they bring). Verse 39 tells them what happens when a people is finished with that process. The process, for Makkah, is not finished. That is the mercy of hearing the verse in time.

**Parable as a Last Mercy**

Why parables, and not only commands? Because a command can be heard as power, and a parable is heard as meaning. "This is what you are like" can reach a mind that "do this" has not reached. The Qur'an describes the world itself as a field of *āyāt*, signs, which are cousins of *amthāl*. Shade, night, sleep, rain, two seas — the sūrah will turn to those shortly (verses 45–53). They are parables that do not need a ruined city. The ruined cities are parables that do not need a new argument. God has not been silent in either language. The Prophet ﷺ struck parables constantly, as in the musk and the bellows, the man pulling belts at the Fire, the straight path with curtained doors. He was imitating, as a messenger, the Divine method this verse names.

> *"The example of a good companion and a bad companion is like the bearer of musk and the blower of the bellows..."* (al-Bukhārī 5534; Muslim 2628)

A teacher who will only threaten has not learned *ḍarabnā lahu al-amthāl*. A teacher who will only soothe has not learned *tabbarnā tatbīrā*. The verse holds both. First the picture. Then, if the picture is refused, the crumbling.

**Hearing This Without Fatalism**

A person can hear "each We ruined" as the news that ruin is inevitable and effort is cosmetic. That is not the verse. The parables are the evidence that effort was asked for. The servants of the Compassionate are, later in the sūrah, a people who heard the parables and walked. Their ending is the lofty abode (25:75), not *tatbīr*. The list of verse 38 is a list of those who did not walk. The present tense, for the reader, is still the tense of *amthāl*: you are being spoken to in examples. The crumbling is a past about them and a possible future about people like them. Between the two sits a life, which is the only place a parable can still work. Taqwā is walking that stretch of road with both hands on the wheel, because the markings — the *amthāl* — were painted by someone who has seen the drop.

**Each We Struck with Parables, and Each We Ruined**

*Wa-kullan ḍarabnā lahu l-amthāla wa-kullan tabbarnā tatbīrā* — each We struck for them the similitudes, and each We ruined with a ruining. Before *tatbīr*, *amthāl*. God does not destroy a people without first speaking to them in a language they can understand. Verse 9’s *amthāl* were theirs, false. These *amthāl* are His, true. The ruined towns of verses 36–38 were not uninformed. They were spoken to and then broken. *Tatbīr* is utter breaking, the root of *tabār* as ruin, cousin of verse 1’s *tabāraka* only as a sound: blessed is the One who sends; ruined are those who deny what was sent. The Prophet ﷺ, passing Ḥijr, told his companions not to enter the dwellings of those who wronged themselves except weeping, lest there befall them what befell those (al-Bukhārī 3380). Verse 39 is that ḥadīth as a law of history. Parable, then ruin. The reader is still in the parable-stage. That is the mercy of being alive in a world where Furqān is still recited.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:40

> **And indeed they passed by the town upon which fell the evil rain. Have they not seen it? Nay, but they hope not for a resurrection.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Ruin on a Trade Route**

*Wa-laqad ataw ʿalā al-qaryati allatī umṭirat maṭara al-sawʾ* — "And indeed they passed by the town upon which fell the evil rain." Al-Ṭabarī understands the town as the town of Lot. The evil rain (cf. 27:58) is a rain of stones, the destruction of the people who denied their messenger and assaulted the guests of God. The Quraysh, in their journeys for trade to the north — toward Syria, along the route that passed the Dead Sea lands — would have passed such ruins. Al-Rāzī emphasizes that fact. The sign was not in a book they had to take on credit. It was on the road they used to make money.

The Qur'an repeats this appeal to travel:

> *"Have they not journeyed upon the earth and observed how those before them fared in the end?"* (Qur'an 12:109; 30:9, 42; 35:44; 40:21, 82; 47:10)

Seeing, in this religion, is part of responsibility. A merchant who has loaded camels in a ruined valley and then denied resurrection is a merchant who has used his eyes for prices and not for signs.

**"Have They Not Seen It?"**

*A-fa-lam yakūnū yarawnahā* — "Have they not seen it?" The question is not requesting information. It is establishing that the defense of ignorance is closed. They have seen. The ruins were not hidden. What is missing is not a view.

Sight without interpretation is a Qur'anic theme. Cattle see. The people of verse 44 will be compared to cattle, then called further astray, because cattle use their eyes for what cattle need, and these used their eyes and refused the meaning. The town of evil rain is a test of whether a pair of human eyes is still a pair of human eyes.

**"Nay, but They Hope Not for a Resurrection"**

*Bal kānū lā yarjūna nushūrā* — "Nay, but they hope not for a resurrection." The *bal* is the same correction as verse 11. The issue is not insufficient ruins. The issue is the absence of *rajāʾ* for *nushūr* — hope, expectation, desire, for the rising. Al-Rāzī notes that "hope not" can mean they are not hopeful for such a reality and that they do not anticipate it. Verse 21 used the same structure about meeting God. Verse 40 uses it about resurrection. The two absences are one absence. A person who does not expect to be raised will not read a ruined town as a letter to himself. He will read it as landscape.

The language of not hoping to meet God or be resurrected appears across the Book: 4:104; 10:7, 11, 15; 25:21; 45:14; 71:13; 78:27. It is one of the Qur'an's preferred names for unbelief, because it locates unbelief in the future tense of the heart. What you do not hope for, you do not prepare for. What you do not prepare for, you call a fable when someone else prepares for it.

**Evil Rain and Pure Water: The Sūrah's Coming Contrast**

This sūrah will soon praise water that is *ṭahūr*, pure, sent down to revive a dead land (25:48–49). Here water — rain — is *sawʾ*, evil, harm, a rain that does not revive. The same sky, the same kind of falling, two wills, two towns. The people of Lot received a rain that matched what they had made of their town. The people who hope for resurrection receive, in the later verses, a rain that matches mercy. Weather is not an independent god. That is part of what the ruins say, and part of what verse 50 will say when people attribute rain to stars.

**What a Modern Traveler Does with Ruins**

People still pass ruins — of cities, of civilizations, of a house on their own street that was full and is empty. Tourism can be a form of *a-fa-lam yakūnū yarawnahā* without the seeing. Verse 40 asks that a ruin interrupt the itinerary. The interruption is not morbidity. It is *nushūr*-awareness. Bodies that lived here were raised in the unseen, or will be. My body will be. The town of evil rain is not only "them." It is a possible reading of any town that treats the messenger's warning as a fable of those of old (25:5).

Hope for resurrection is practiced. The morning word the Prophet ﷺ taught — "Praise be to God who gave us life after He caused us to die, and unto Him is the resurrection" (al-Bukhārī 6312) — is a daily refusal of *lā yarjūna nushūrā*. A person who says it and then walks past a ruin, or past a graveyard, or past his own sleeping children, has a chance to see what the Quraysh passed and did not see.

**Sight Without Interpretation**

*A-fa-lam yakūnū yarawnahā* — have they not seen it? The question establishes that the defense of ignorance is closed. They have seen. The ruins were on the trade road. What is missing is not a view. Sight without interpretation is a Qur'anic theme. Cattle see. The people of verse 44 will be compared to cattle, then called further astray, because cattle use their eyes for what cattle need, and these used their eyes and refused the meaning. *Bal kānū lā yarjūna nushūrā* — nay, they hope not for a resurrection. Al-Rāzī notes that "hope not" can mean they are not hopeful for such a reality and that they do not anticipate it. A person who does not expect to be raised will not read a ruined town as a letter to himself. He will read it as landscape. The morning word the Prophet ﷺ taught is the daily contrary:

> *"Praise be to God who gave us life after He caused us to die, and unto Him is the resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6312)

Hope for *nushūr* is practiced. Tourism can be a form of passing the town of evil rain without seeing it. Verse 40 asks that a ruin interrupt the itinerary.

**They Have Come Upon the Town That Was Rained On with an Evil Rain**

*Wa-la-qad ataw ʿalā l-qaryati llatī umṭirat maṭara l-sawʾ* — they have surely come upon the town that was rained on with the evil rain. The Quraysh’s caravans passed the ruins of Lot’s people. Seeing was available. *A-fa-lam yakūnū yarawnahā* — have they not been seeing it? *Bal kānū lā yarjūna nushūrā* — nay, they have not been hoping for a resurrection. The failure is not of the eye. It is of *rajāʾ* for *nushūr*. Without hope of being raised, a ruin is only landscape. With that hope, a ruin is verse 37’s *āyah* in stone and salt. The same water-root as the mercy-rain of 25:48 is here *maṭar al-sawʾ*. Rain is not a god. The Sender chooses. The Prophet ﷺ, on passing such dwellings, hurried and covered his head and forbade entering except weeping. Verse 40 is that discipline as a rebuke to people who passed and joked. A modern traveler who photographs a destroyed people without *nushūr* in his chest is in the verse’s *bal*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:41

> **And when they see thee they do naught but take thee in mockery: "Is this the one whom God sent as a messenger?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sight That Immediately Becomes Theatre**

*Wa-idhā raʾawka in yattakhidhūnaka illā huzuwan* — "And when they see thee they do naught but take thee in mockery." The seeing of verse 40 was a seeing of ruins that did not become reminder. The seeing of verse 41 is a seeing of the messenger that becomes only mockery. *In ... illā* is a restriction: they do nothing else with the sight of him. No pause. No inner argument. Sight, then *huzuʾ*.

*Huzuʾ* is mockery, taking a thing as a joke, using a person as entertainment. The Qur'an treats it as a signature of denial:

> *"And when they meet those who believe they say, 'We believe'; but when they are alone with their satans they say, 'We are with you; we were only mocking.'"* (Qur'an 2:14)

> *"And never did a prophet come unto them but that they mocked him."* (Qur'an 43:7)

> *"How many a prophet did We send to those of old? Yet never did a prophet come unto them, but that they mocked him."* (Qur'an 43:6–7)

Mockery is cheaper than argument. It recruits the room. It does not have to be true. It only has to be funny enough that the Criterion sounds, by contrast, like a lack of humor.

**"Is This the One?" — The Demonstrative as a Knife**

*A-hādhā alladhī baʿatha Allāhu rasūlā* — "Is this the one whom God sent as a messenger?" *Hādhā*, this, the near demonstrative, used as verse 7 used it: this fellow. The question is not investigation. It is a sneer shaped as a question. It implies a picture of what "the one God sent" should look like — treasure, garden, angel, stone house, a face the council would stand up for — and then points at the man who eats food and walks in the markets.

The same incredulity, from the other side of status, appears when the proud talk about the first believers:

> *"Had it been good, they would not have outstripped us in [attaining] it."* (Qur'an 46:11)

God's choice of Muhammad ﷺ and of his poor companions instead of the notables is, to this sneer, evidence against the choice. The verse lets us hear the sneer so that we will recognize it when it is aimed at any servant who does not match a room's costume of the sacred.

**Mockery as a Social Technology**

A lone doubter can be answered with verse 33's better explanation. Mockery is rarely lone. It needs a laugh from a second person. That is why it is so effective against a messenger in a market: the market is already a crowd. Taking him in mockery is a public act, a way of telling bystanders what face to wear. Many who might have listened first learn, from the laugh, what listening would cost.

The Prophet ﷺ endured poetry against him, nicknames, the throwing of offal, the roadside waiting of Abū Jahl's circle. He prayed, he did not become a clown in return, and he did not stop walking where they could see him. Verse 41 is recited to him while this is happening, which means God wanted him to hear that He had heard the laugh.

**The Question That Will Be Reversed**

On the Day the wrongdoer bites his hands, the question "Is this the one?" will reverse. The sneer will become "Would that I had taken a way with the Messenger" (25:27). The demonstrative that shrank him will be replaced by a title that the wrongdoer will suddenly know how to use. Mockery is not a stable position. It is a loan from a time when faces still seem to be in our control.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Woe to the one who speaks and lies to make people laugh — woe to him, woe to him."* (Abū Dāwūd 4990; al-Tirmidhī 2315)

And he said that a man may say a word to make his companions laugh and fall further than the distance between east and west (al-Bukhārī 6478; Muslim 2988, in meaning). Verse 41 is that woe at the highest possible target: making the messenger himself the joke.

**What a Believer Does When the Sacred Is Made Comic**

He does not become humorless about lawful things. The Prophet ﷺ joked without lying. He does become careful about where a laugh is aimed. If it is aimed at a messenger, a verse, a person praying, a poor believer, the laugh is *huzuʾ* and is not cheap. The servants of the Compassionate will pass idle talk with dignity (25:72) and answer the ignorant with peace (25:63). Dignity and peace are the contrary of mockery. They are also, in a mocking room, a form of courage. Remaining unamused when the messenger is the joke is part of taking a way with him.

**The Pronoun That Cuts**

*Hādhā alladhī* — this one who. The demonstrative is a reduction. They will not say his name with honor. They point. Mockery lives in pronouns: “this one,” “that fellow,” “your companion.” The Qur'an records the pointing so that later mockers can hear themselves. The servants of the Compassionate, when they mention him ﷺ, pray upon him. The difference between *hādhā* and *ṣallā Allāhu ʿalayhi wa-sallam* is the difference between verse 41 and taking a way. Ibn Kathīr notes that this was their custom when they saw him: ridicule as a social glue. A room that laughs together at a prophet has made *kufr* into fellowship. The Prophet ﷺ bore it without answering in kind. His patience is part of why verse 75 can promise *salām*. He was met with mockery and will be met, and those who took his way will be met, with greeting and peace. The inversion is exact.

**When They See Thee They Take Thee Only in Mockery**

*Wa-idhā raʾawka in yattakhidhūnaka illā huzuʾan* — when they see thee they take thee only as a mockery. *A-hādhā lladhī baʿatha Llāhu rasūlā* — is this the one God has sent as a messenger? The demonstrative reduces him. They will not honor the name. Mockery is a social glue; a room that laughs together at a prophet has made *kufr* into fellowship. The Prophet ﷺ warned that a man may speak a word to make his companions laugh and fall far (al-Bukhārī 6478). Verse 41 is that woe aimed at the highest target. The believer is not humorless about lawful things. He is careful where a laugh is pointed. Dignity and *salām* (25:63, 72) are the contrary of *huzuʾ*. Remaining unamused when the messenger is the joke is part of taking a way with him.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:42

> **He would indeed have nearly led us astray from our gods, had we not been steadfast to them." They will know, when they see the punishment, who is further astray from the way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Confession Disguised as a Boast**

*In kāda la-yuḍillunā ʿan ālihatinā lawlā an ṣabarnā ʿalayhā* — "He would indeed have nearly led us astray from our gods, had we not been steadfast to them." Al-Rāzī hears in this the seriousness and diligence with which the Prophet ﷺ continually presented proofs. They almost moved. They admit the almost. Then they name what saved them from moving: *ṣabarnā ʿalayhā*, we were patient, steadfast, stubborn, upon our gods.

Patience is a virtue this sūrah has already asked of the believers (25:20). Here the same family of words is used for clinging to idols. The virtue is not in the remaining. It is in *what* one remains upon. Steadfastness upon a falsehood is not *ṣabr* as God praises it. It is the *ṣabr* of a man holding a door closed against a fire-exit.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that they kept themselves loyal to their gods and remained firm. The firmness is real. Many denials are not casual. They are disciplined. A person can work at not being convinced. Verse 42 is that work overheard.

**"Our Gods" as an Identity**

*Ālihatinā* — our gods. The possessive is the whole problem. Gods that are "ours" are gods that have become a we. To leave them is not, in this psychology, to leave a mistake. It is to leave the tribe, the fathers, the self as known. That is why almost being led astray from them feels like almost dying. The Prophet ﷺ was not only offering a new theology. He was offering a new we. The almost is the moment when the new we nearly won. The steadfastness is the old we pulling the body back.

This is why mockery (verse 41) and steadfastness to gods (verse 42) sit together. The laugh is a tool of the old we. It makes the new we look like a joke, so that the almost can be forgotten.

**They Will Know, When They See**

*Wa-sawfa yaʿlamūna ḥīna yarawna al-ʿadhāba man aḍallu sabīlā* — "They will know, when they see the punishment, who is further astray from the way." Knowledge will come. It will come late. Sight of punishment will do what sight of the messenger and sight of the ruined town did not do. The question of who is *aḍall sabīlā*, further astray from the way, is the question they implied about him: he almost led us astray. The verse postpones the verdict to a seeing they cannot mock.

Verse 34 used the same phrase of those gathered on their faces: they are further astray from the way. The two verses rhyme so that the reader cannot miss the reversal. The one they accused of being a road into error was the road. The steadfastness they praised was the error. The Hour is, among other things, the end of reversed maps.

**Almost: The Most Important Word in Their Sentence**

For a preacher, *kāda*, almost, is a consolation. Hearts moved and then re-hardened. The work was not nothing. For the one who almost moved and then prided himself on not moving, *kāda* is a warning that may not come twice. The Qur'an describes people who come close and then let a pride, a friend, a god, a laugh, pull them back. Pharaoh almost, in some tellings of the drowning, believed with the water at his throat (10:90), and it did not count as the faith of this world.

The Prophet ﷺ said that hearts are between two fingers of the Compassionate, who turns them as He wills (al-Tirmidhī 2140; Muslim 2654 in meaning). Steadfastness to idols is not a proof that the heart is safe. It is a proof that the heart can be held shut. Asking God to keep a heart after it has opened is the believer's *ṣabr*. Asking one's gods to keep a heart shut is the *ṣabr* of this verse.

**What to Be Steadfast Upon**

The servants of the Compassionate will be "rewarded with the lofty abode for having been patient" (25:75). Same virtue, other object. Patience upon the prayer, upon the just mean, upon not answering ignorance with ignorance, upon the hope of meeting God — that *ṣabr* is a taking of a way with the messenger. Patience upon "our gods," our brand, our tribe's mistake, our appetite — that *ṣabr* is the almost, refused, and the punishment, not yet seen.

A person can use verse 42 as a diagnostic. Where am I proud of not having been moved? If the thing that almost moved me was the Criterion, the pride is a danger. If the thing that almost moved me was a *fulān* pulling me from the Reminder, then steadfastness is a duty. The difference is the object. The verse teaches the difference by showing the wrong object in a confident mouth, and then dating the confidence: until they see the punishment.

**Almost, If We Had Not Persevered**

*In kāda la-yuḍillunā* — he almost led us astray. They confess, without meaning to, the power of the message. Only *ṣabr* — their word for stubbornness — kept them. The Qur'an often uses *ṣabr* as a virtue of the believers. Here the same root is the vice of those who endured in falsehood. Patience is not self-justifying. Its value is its object. Patient in *ḥaqq* is verse 75’s *bi-mā ṣabarū*. Patient in *bāṭil* is verse 42’s boast. *Law-lā an ṣabarnā ʿalayhā* — had we not held to our gods. The sentence is a warning to every community that congratulates itself on not having been moved. Movement toward the Criterion is not instability. It is *hidāyah*. Remaining unmoved when the Criterion has come is not *ṣabr*. It is *nufūr*. They will know who is more astray as a way when they see the punishment. The knowledge will not then be useful. It is useful now, while *ṣabr* can still be redirected toward the way of the messenger.

**He Almost Led Us Astray from Our Gods, Had We Not Persevered**

*In kāda la-yuḍillunā ʿan ālihatinā law-lā an ṣabarnā ʿalayhā* — he almost led us astray from our gods, had we not held to them. They confess the power of the message and name stubbornness *ṣabr*. Patience is not self-justifying. Patient in *ḥaqq* is verse 75. Patient in *bāṭil* is this boast. *Wa-sawfa yaʿlamūna ḥīna yarawna l-ʿadhāba man aḍallu sabīlā* — they will know, when they see the punishment, who is more astray as a way. The dating makes the knowledge useless then, useful now. Movement toward the Criterion is not instability. Remaining unmoved when it has come is not *ṣabr*. It is *nufūr*. Redirect the endurance while it can still become a way with the messenger.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:43

> **Hast thou considered the one who takes his caprice as his god? Wouldst thou be a guardian over him?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Idol That Does Not Need a Shelf**

*A-raʾayta man ittakhadha ilāhahu hawāhu* — "Hast thou considered the one who takes his caprice as his god?" After stone gods, after "our gods" as a tribal we, the sūrah names the god that can survive the breaking of every statue: *hawā*, caprice, the self's inclination, the drift of wanting. *Ittakhadha* again — he takes. The god is installed. Worship is the obedience of the inner law "because I want it."

Ibn ʿAjībah and al-Zamakhsharī say the idolaters obey their passions to the point of worshipping them. Some commentators relate that some were so fickle that, upon seeing a better or more beautiful idol than the one they were worshipping, they would abandon the first and begin worshipping the second. That anecdote, whether of a few or of a type, is the psychology of *hawā*: the object can change; the taking of desire as law does not.

The Qur'an asks the same question in another recitation:

> *"Have you seen the one who takes his caprice as his god, and God has led him astray upon knowledge, and has set a seal upon his hearing and his heart, and put a covering over his sight?"* (Qur'an 45:23)

And it asks:

> *"Then who is more astray than he who follows his own caprice without guidance from God?"* (Qur'an 28:50)

**Hawā as a God: What That Means in Acts**

A god is what one obeys when obedience costs. If, when the Criterion says no, the person says yes because the want is loud, the want has been given the right of God. The right is *ulūhiyyah*, the right to be worshipped, which Ibn Taymiyyah and the jurists of tawḥīd locate not only in ritual but in obedience, love, fear, and the last appeal. *Hawā* as god is therefore not a metaphor for "he likes things." It is a diagnosis of sovereignty: the inner king is appetite.

Modern life has made this diagnosis almost too easy to illustrate. A culture that treats the sentence "I have to be true to myself" as the last sacred sentence is a culture that has named *hawā* as *ilāh*. The self, in that creed, is not a trust to be trained. It is an oracle. Verse 43 is not only about a Makkan bowing to a stone that he will replace with a prettier stone. It is about a human being bowing to a series of wants and calling the series authenticity.

**"Wouldst Thou Be a Guardian Over Him?"**

*A-fa-anta takūnu ʿalayhi wakīlā* — "Wouldst thou be a guardian over him?" The Prophet ﷺ is asked a question that is a mercy to him. You are not responsible for being the *wakīl*, the entrusted manager, of a man who has made his desire his deity. The office of warning is not the office of compulsion. A similar sentence closes Sūrah al-Ghāshiyah:

> *"Thou art not a warder over them."* (Qur'an 88:22)

And:

> *"You do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills."* (Qur'an 28:56)

The question protects the messenger from two illnesses: despair that he has not converted the man of *hawā*, and the temptation to coerce a soul that must itself un-take its god. Ibn ʿAjībah and al-Qurṭubī note that those who act on impulse and respond to their passions cannot be persuaded, in the ordinary way, to worship the One God. The verse says that without insult. It says it as a limit of the prophetic job.

**Caprice Is Not the Same as Desire**

Islam does not treat every wanting as idolatry. Hunger, love, rest, and the wish for the Garden (25:15–16) are wantings. *Hawā* is desire as legislator, desire unhooked from the Criterion. The servants of the Compassionate will still spend, marry, and ask for comfort in their spouses and progeny (25:74). They will not take the comfort as a god. The difference is the taking. A desire that can be told no remains a desire. A desire that cannot be told no has been enthroned.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"None of you believes until his desire follows what I have brought."* (al-Nawawī cites it in *al-Arbaʿīn* no. 41; its chain has been discussed, and its meaning is the meaning of 4:65)

Whether one leans on that chain or on the mass of verses about following the messenger rather than *hawā*, the training is the same. Belief is not the absence of wanting. It is the education of wanting by what was brought.

**How to Un-take the Inner God**

The work is daily and untheatrical. A no said to a glance, a post, a sale, a revenge, a sleep through Fajr — each no is a dethroning. A yes said because God said yes, even when *hawā* said no — work, apology, fasting in summer — is the same dethroning from the other side. The Prophet ﷺ described the strong person as the one who controls himself in anger (al-Bukhārī 6114; Muslim 2609), which is *hawā* in one of its heats. Strength, in that ḥadīth, is tawḥīd of the inner kingdom.

The messenger is not *wakīl* over the man of caprice. The man of caprice is, until he dies, still able to become a servant of the Compassionate. Verse 43 limits the Prophet ﷺ. It does not limit God. The Names of verse 6 remain: Forgiving, Merciful. A god of *hawā* can be taken down in an hour of repentance. The sūrah will later say that God replaces evil deeds with good. That replacement includes the evil of having worshipped a want.

**Have You Seen the One Who Takes His Caprice as His God?**

*Araʾayta mani ttakhadha ilāhahu hawāhu* — have you seen the one who has taken his caprice as his god? *Araʾayta* is a command to look, as in verse 9. The spectacle is not a stone idol. It is a will that has been enthroned. Al-Ṭabarī: he obeys his *hawā* as a man obeys a god. Ibn Kathīr: whatever he likes, he does. The sūrah’s other gods — idols, angels wrongly worshipped, stars, palaces — are outer. This one is inner, and it can baptize all the others as “what I wanted.” *A-fa-anta takūnu ʿalayhi wakīlā* — wouldst thou then be a trustee over him? The Prophet ﷺ is released from a job he was never given: custody of another man’s want. Guidance is God’s (25:31). The messenger announces (25:56). The man of *hawā* remains responsible. For the reader, the verse is a mirror before it is a weapon. Before diagnosing a culture of desire, diagnose the desire that still gives you orders at Fajr, in a glance, in a reply. Dethroning that god is tawḥīd at the scale of a day.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:44

> **Or do you suppose that most of them hear or understand? Truly they are but as cattle. Nay, they are further astray from the way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question to the Messenger About the Inner Equipment of a Crowd**

*Am taḥsabu anna aktharahum yasmaʿūna aw yaʿqilūn* — "Or do you suppose that most of them hear or understand?" After the man who worships his caprice, the lens widens to *aktharahum*, most of them. The Prophet ﷺ is being weaned from a natural supposition: that a human majority, hearing human speech, will process it as human beings process speech. *Yasmaʿūn* is hearing that takes in. *Yaʿqilūn* is binding a meaning, from *ʿaql*, the cord that tethers a camel — intelligence as the power to hold a thought still.

The verse does not say they lack ears or brains. It says the messenger should not calculate as if those organs were being used for their human work.

**Cattle: The Comparison, and Then the Correction**

*In hum illā kal-anʿām* — "Truly they are but as cattle." Al-Qurṭubī: they are like cattle in that they do not think and restrict their concerns to eating and drinking; they are even further astray, because cattle are not condemned for acting like this, but human beings are. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī: cattle follow their natures, know how to obey their herders, and can hear and understand insofar as they are animals, but disbelievers disobey God and have neither eyes nor ears for the truth.

Al-Zamakhsharī and Ibn ʿAjībah press further. Cattle do not possess knowledge, but they are not guilty of the compound ignorance of both not knowing and not knowing that they do not know. Cattle know what is good and bad for them; some human beings do not understand how good God has been to them and that Satan is their enemy, and thus they do not know how to seek the greater good of the Hereafter. Cattle, one might say, are professionals at being cattle. A human being who lives only at that level is an amateur at being human.

Other verses that compare humans to animals include 2:65; 7:176; 29:41; 31:19; 62:5. The most famous of them, 7:176, is the dog that pants whether driven away or left; 62:5 is the donkey carrying books. Verse 44 belongs to that family and then goes a step.

**"Nay, They Are Further Astray from the Way"**

*Bal hum aḍallu sabīlā* — the third time this sūrah has used the comparative of astrayness and a way (verses 34, 42, 44). Cattle are not on the *sabīl* because they were not asked to be. Humans who will not hear or bind a meaning are off a road they were asked to walk. Further astray is not a slur. It is a coordinate. A lost camel is a problem for its owner. A lost human who would not use *ʿaql* is a problem for his own Hour.

The comparison is therefore not contempt for animals. Islamic law and the Prophet's ﷺ teaching are full of mercy to beasts — the woman who entered the Fire for a cat (al-Bukhārī 3318; Muslim 2242), the man forgiven for a dog (al-Bukhārī 3467; Muslim 2244), the command to sharpen the blade and give rest to the slaughtered. The comparison is a compliment to cattle and an indictment of the human who falls below a beast's honesty about its own good.

**Most of Them: A Sentence That Must Not Become a License for Contempt**

*Aktharahum* is a hard word. The Qur'an says in several places that most people do not believe, do not thank, do not know. The Prophet ﷺ still spent himself on the most. Verse 44 is not a permission to stop warning or to sneer. It is a permission to stop being surprised, and to stop measuring the truth by a show of hands. Guidance is not a majority phenomenon in this verse. It is a gift that most, in a given crowd of deniers, will not use their hearing to receive.

The servants of the Compassionate, who walk among such crowds, answer with "Peace" (25:63). They do not answer with "cattle." The noun is God's to use as diagnosis. On a human tongue, used as an insult in the street, it easily becomes the arrogance of verse 21. The difference is the difference between a physician naming a condition and a bystander shouting a name.

**Hearing and Binding as Practices**

If the disease is not hearing and not ʿaql, the medicine is the opposite practice. Hearing the Qur'an as *tartīl* (25:32), not as background. Binding meanings: what did this verse require of me today? The Prophet ﷺ used to repeat a verse, sometimes through a whole night. That is ʿaql: holding a meaning until it tethers the soul. Cattle graze and move on. A human being can remain.

The verse also explains some of the failure of argument. Verse 33 promised a better explanation. Verse 44 says that most of this particular audience is not in a state to hear it. Both verses are true. One supplies the resource. The other supplies the realism. Together they produce a prophet who does not stop explaining and does not collapse when the explanation is chewed like grass and forgotten.

**Most of Them as Cattle, or More Astray**

*In hum illā kal-anʿām* — they are only like cattle — *bal hum aḍallu sabīlā* — nay, they are more astray as a way. Cattle follow a beneficial pasture. These follow a *hawā* that harms them (25:43). Cattle do not deny a Lord after a Criterion. These do. The comparison is not an insult for its own heat. It is a diagnosis of unused *samʿ* and *ʿaql*. God gave hearing and binding-intelligence as the human extra over cattle. Unused extras are worse than not having them, because they are a trust refused. The Prophet ﷺ sought refuge from knowledge that does not benefit. Verse 44 is that refuge as a picture of a people. For the living reader the verse is a fear, not a license to call neighbors cattle. The fear is: I have ears. I have a mind that can hold. If I graze and move on, I have used a human face to live a below-cattle life. The servants who, when reminded of the signs, do not fall upon them deaf and blind (25:73) are the human extra, used.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:45

> **Hast thou not considered thy Lord, how He spreads out the shade—and had He willed, He could have made it still—and then We make the sun an indicator of it;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Psychology of Denial to a Shade on the Ground**

The sūrah has been in courtrooms, ruins, and inner idols. Verse 45 turns the Prophet's ﷺ face — *a-lam tara ilā rabbika*, "Hast thou not considered thy Lord" — toward a daily thing so ordinary that only revelation would think to make it a proof: shade. *Kayfa madda al-ẓill* — how He spreads out the shade. Al-Ṭabarī takes the shade as the time from the break of dawn until sunrise, a state between total illumination and total darkness. Al-Rāzī calls it a state between two extremes. In that reading, *ẓill* is also twilight, the first spreading of distinguishable dark-and-light.

Shade in a desert is not decoration. It is survival. The God who is being denied in this sūrah is the God who lays that survival on the ground every morning without a petition. Consideration of the Lord, here, is consideration of a mercy that has been underfoot.

**If He Had Willed, It Would Not Move**

*Wa-law shāʾa la-jaʿalahu sākinan* — "and had He willed, He could have made it still." Ibn Kathīr: God could cause the twilight to last without the sun overtaking it and causing it to disappear. A still shade sounds, at first, like a gift — shade that does not shrink at noon. In fact a still shade would be a stuck world: no measuring of time, no noon, no lengthening toward evening, no sign by which a traveler knows the hour. The motion of shade is a mercy because it is a clock that the illiterate can read.

Verse 2 said He created everything, then measured it. Verse 45 is measure as a moving edge of dark. The *in shāʾa* of verse 10 (Gardens if He will) is here the *law shāʾa* of a world that could have been frozen and was not. Will is the reason the world is livable rather than stuck.

**The Sun as Indicator, Not as Lord**

*Thumma jaʿalnā al-shamsa ʿalayhi dalīlā* — "and then We make the sun an indicator of it." Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī: it is through opposites that things are known, and thus the shade is known in contrast to the shining of the sun. Al-Zamakhsharī: it is through knowing the sun's shining state that one knows the twilight. Ṭabāṭabāʾī and others note that just as the sun moves gradually through the sky, so the shade or twilight gradually changes.

*Dalīl* is a pointer, a guide, an evidence. The sun does not own the shade. It indicates it. In a culture that could slip toward sun-worship — and the Qur'an knows Abraham's argument with the sun (6:78) — this clause is a demotion of the sun to a finger pointing. The Lord spreads the shade. The sun is hired as a signpost.

The prayer times of Islam are, in part, a ritual reading of this verse. Ẓuhr when the sun has passed the zenith and shade begins to lengthen; ʿAṣr by the length of a shadow; Maghrib at the swallowing of the day. A Muslim who prays on time is a person who has used the sun as *dalīl* without ever taking it as *ilāh*. The jurists' discussions of shadow-lengths are not pedantry. They are verse 45 as a timetable.

**Why This Proof After "They Do Not Hear"**

Verse 44 said most of them do not hear or understand. Verse 45 offers a proof that does not require a book: look at the shade. If they will not hear a recitation, they might still see a line of dark moving on a wall. The sūrah is not giving up. It is changing registers. Some hearts that closed at "messenger" may still open at a mercy they already use.

The Prophet ﷺ's teaching of the solar and lunar eclipses — that they do not happen for anyone's death, but are signs by which God frightens His servants (al-Bukhārī 1044; Muslim 901) — belongs to the same education. Heavenly lights are indicators, not lords, and not omens tied to kings. *Dalīl* is their rank.

**Stillness as a Spiritual Danger, Motion as a Mercy**

A still shade is an image of a life that will not be indicated, will not be measured, will not be moved toward an evening. God could have left a soul in that stillness. He did not, as long as the sun of a reminder still points. The later mystical reading that Ibn ʿAjībah records at verses 45–46 — the soul as a shadow cast from the Unseen, the sun of knowledge indicating its nature, God drawing it to Himself — is not required for the verse to work. The plain reading is already a spiritual direction: you are living in a moving mercy. Do not take the pointer as a god. Do not take the motion for granted. Consider the Lord who spreads it.

**The Sun as Dalīl, Never as Rabb**

Verse 61 will bless the One who placed a lamp. Verse 45 already demotes that lamp to a pointer of shade. A culture that prays to the sun, or a modern habit that treats “nature” as a closed explanation, is the same error at two temperatures. The shade is spread. The sun indicates it. God could have made it still. Motion is therefore a chosen mercy, not a necessity imposed on a helpless god. *Alam tara* — hast thou not seen? — is addressed first to the Prophet ﷺ and then to every seeing person. Seeing here is not eyesight. Eyesight sees shade every day and thanks no one. Seeing is the recognition that a moving mercy has a Mover. The servants who walk humbly walk in that shade without worshipping it, and without despising it. They will, at night, give some of the covering back as prostration. Day’s shade and night’s garment are one mercy in two colors. Verse 45 is the day’s half.

**Hast Thou Not Considered Thy Lord, How He Spreads the Shade**

*A-lam tara ilā rabbika kayfa madda l-ẓill* — hast thou not seen toward thy Lord, how He has spread the shade? *Wa-law shāʾa la-jaʿalahu sākinan* — had He willed, He could have made it still. *Thumma jaʿalnā l-shamsa ʿalayhi dalīlā* — then We made the sun a pointer upon it. The sun is demoted to *dalīl* before verse 61 names it a lamp. Motion of shade is a chosen mercy, not a necessity imposed on a helpless god. Seeing here is recognition, not eyesight. Eyesight sees shade every day and thanks no one. A still shade would be a life without indication, without evening. God did not leave souls in that stillness while a reminder still points. Do not take the pointer as a lord. Do not take the motion for granted. Consider the Lord who spreads.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:46

> **then We withdraw it unto Ourselves, a gentle withdrawal.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shade Does Not Escape. It Is Taken Back.**

Verse 45 spread the shade and made the sun its indicator. Verse 46 completes the motion: *thumma qabaḍnāhu ilaynā qabḍan yasīrā* — "then We withdraw it unto Ourselves, a gentle withdrawal." *Qabaḍa* is to seize, to contract, to take into the hand. The shade that was spread is gathered. The subject is God, in the royal We. The destination of the gathering is *ilaynā*, unto Ourselves. Shade does not fade into nothing as an independent event. It is taken back by the One who laid it out.

Al-Ṭabarī: the twilight is withdrawn as the sun rises and the light erases the shade. Some interpret *yasīr*, gentle, as "quickly" or "in a hidden way"; others, including al-Zamakhsharī, take it to mean that the withdrawal is gradual. Both can be true of different hours. Dawn's shade can seem to leap. Afternoon's shade can seem to crawl. In both, the hand that takes is unhurried in the sense that matters: it is not violent, not chaotic, not a theft. It is a *qabḍ yasīr*, a taking that a world can bear.

**Gentle, and Also Easy**

*Yasīr* is easy as well as gentle. Al-Rāzī notes that this verse has connotations of the coming of the Hour, which is spoken of as being easy for God:

> *"That is easy for God."* (Qur'an 29:19; 35:17)

> *"That Day they will follow the Caller... and it will be easy for Us to bring them together."* (cf. 50:44: "That is easy for Us.")

The same adjective. Gathering shade is easy. Gathering souls will be easy. The daily withdrawal is a rehearsal, in a language of light, of the withdrawal that verse 25 named as a splitting sky. A person who has watched a shadow shorten and not seen a Lord in it is a person who may also miss what the Hour is made of.

**Unto Ourselves: The Destination of Every Borrowed Thing**

*Ilaynā* is the theological heart of the verse. Shade is a loan. Provision is a loan. The face's youth is a loan. Verse 2's *taqdīr*, measure, includes a term of recall. The gentle withdrawal is the ethics of that recall: God takes back what He spread, and the taking is not a failure of the spreading. Evening is not the defeat of morning. It is the other half of the same ownership.

> *"We belong to God, and to Him we are returning."* (Qur'an 2:156)

Shade returns. We return. The grammar is one grammar.

**The Spiritual Allegory the Commentators Permit**

Ibn ʿAjībah, as the source commentary notes, reads verses 45–46 as meaning that the human soul (and indeed every created thing) is a shadow cast from the Unseen; the sun of sacred knowledge points to its ultimate nature, and God draws it gradually to Himself until the soul is annihilated (*fanāʾ*) in Him. One does not have to take the technical language of *fanāʾ* to take the structure: a life is a spread shade; knowledge indicates what it is; God draws it. Death, in that reading, is *qabḍ yasīr* for the believer — a gentle taking — and a violent one for the guilty who are thrown (25:13). The Prophet ﷺ described the believer's soul coming out like a drop from a waterskin, and the disbeliever's like a tangled thorn torn from wool (the long ḥadīth of al-Barāʾ, Abū Dāwūd 4753). Gentle, or not. The adjective in verse 46 is the believer's hope for his own withdrawal.

**Living as a Shade That Knows It Will Be Gathered**

Practically, the verse is a teacher of non-panic and of non-possession. Non-panic: the shortening of a gift — health, a role, a season of ease — can be a *qabḍ yasīr* from a Lord, not a random cruelty. Non-possession: if it is withdrawn *unto Him*, it was His while it lay on the ground. The servants of the Compassionate, who spend without waste and without miserliness (25:67), are people who have understood loans. You do not clutch a shade. You use it, thank for it, and let it be taken.

The prayer at the turning of shadows is again the ritual form of this knowledge. As the shade withdraws toward night, the Muslim is called to Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ. The day is being gathered. The servant gathers himself. That correspondence is not a poetic extra. It is how a measured world was meant to be lived: pointer, motion, gentle recall, and a human being who takes the recall as a sign rather than as an insult.

**Gentle, Easy, and Unto Him**

*Yasīr* is easy as well as gentle. Al-Rāzī notes that this verse has connotations of the coming of the Hour, which is spoken of as being easy for God (29:19; 50:44). Gathering shade is easy. Gathering souls will be easy. The daily withdrawal is a rehearsal, in a language of light, of the withdrawal that verse 25 named as a splitting sky. *Ilaynā* is the theological heart: shade is a loan. Provision is a loan. The face’s youth is a loan. Verse 2’s *taqdīr* includes a term of recall. The Prophet ﷺ described the believer’s soul coming out like a drop from a waterskin, and the disbeliever’s like a tangled thorn torn from wool (Abū Dāwūd 4753, the long ḥadīth of al-Barāʾ). Gentle, or not. The adjective in verse 46 is the believer’s hope for his own withdrawal. Practically, the verse teaches non-panic and non-possession. The shortening of a gift can be a *qabḍ yasīr* from a Lord, not a random cruelty. If it is withdrawn unto Him, it was His while it lay on the ground. The servants who spend without waste and without miserliness have understood loans. You do not clutch a shade. You use it, thank for it, and let it be taken.

**Then We Draw It to Us with Easy Drawing**

*Thumma qabaḍnāhu ilaynā qabḍan yasīrā* — then We draw it to Us with a gentle / easy drawing. Shade is not annihilated into nothing. It is taken *ilaynā*, unto Us. The destination of a withdrawn mercy is God, which means the withdrawal is not theft. It is return. *Yasīr* comforts the believer: the Lord who spreads can gather without violence toward those who remember. The same root in other verses names the Hour as easy for Him. Daily evening is a small Hour of light. The servants who pray Maghrib when the shade has been gathered are people who have read the drawing. They let the day go unto Him and take the night-garment (25:47) as the next loan. A life of clutching — of refusing *qabḍ* in wealth, in youth, in status — is a life at war with this verse. *Tawakkul* (25:58) is, among other things, consent to *qabḍ yasīr*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:47

> **And He it is Who made the night a garment for you, and made sleep repose, and made day a resurrection.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Night as Clothing, Not as Enemy**

*Wa-huwa alladhī jaʿala lakum al-layla libāsan* — "And He it is Who made the night a garment for you." After shade, night. *Libās* is clothing, a covering that rests on the body, hides, warms, and makes a person fit for the world. Night is given that office. It covers the town. It covers the faults of faces. It covers the poor man and the rich man in a darkness that, for a few hours, is almost a kind of equality.

> *"And We made the night a garment."* (Qur'an 78:10)

> *"He made the night for rest and the day for seeing."* (Qur'an 10:67, in sense)

A garment can be thrown off, and a garment can be clung to. The servants of the Compassionate will throw part of this garment off in order to stand and prostrate (25:64). That is not a refusal of the gift. It is a use of the gift's inner hours for the Giver. The rest of the town may use the whole garment for sleep or for sin. The night itself is innocent. It is *libās*. What one does inside a garment is the moral question.

**Sleep as Repose: Subāt and the Brother of Death**

*Wa-al-nawma subātan* — "and made sleep repose." *Subāt* is rest, cutting-off, a pause in which the day's noise is severed. Al-Ṭabarī and others mention the proverb, "Sleep is the brother of death." The Qur'an itself almost says so:

> *"He it is Who takes your souls by night, and He knows what you have earned by day. Then He raises you up in it, that a stated term may be fulfilled."* (Qur'an 6:60)

> *"God takes souls at the moment of their death, and those who die not during their sleep. He withholds those upon whom He has decreed death, and sends the others back until a stated term."* (Qur'an 39:42)

Sleep is a small un-selfing. The will that verse 43 can make into a god is laid down without the will's permission. A proud man sleeps. His *hawā* does not keep watch. That is a mercy and a sign. The one who returns his soul in the morning is the one who was never its owner.

The Prophet ﷺ taught words at both edges of this death-brother. Upon sleeping:

> *"In Your name, O God, I die and I live."* (al-Bukhārī 6314)

Upon rising:

> *"Praise be to God who gave us life after He caused us to die, and unto Him is the resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6312)

Those two sentences are verse 47 as a daily liturgy. Night-garment, sleep-repose, day-resurrection: he named them all.

**Day as Nushūr: The Word Verse 40 Said They Do Not Hope For**

*Wa-jaʿala al-nahāra nushūrā* — "and made day a resurrection." *Nushūr* is the rising, the spreading out, the resurrection of verse 40 which they hope not for. Here it is applied to morning. Waking is named with the name of the Hour. The sūrah has already used *nushūr* of the resurrection idols cannot own (25:3). Now it uses it of the day. The pedagogical intent is almost blunt. You want a proof of rising? You rise.

People who deny resurrection while making appointments in the morning are people who use a sign as a convenience and refuse it as a meaning. Cattle also wake (25:44). The human use of waking is to remember the other waking. The garment of night is taken off. The soul is, as 6:60 says, raised. Work begins. That work will be gathered, as shade was gathered, *ilaynā*.

**A Complete Cycle in One Verse**

Garment, repose, resurrection. Covering, pause, rising. The three gifts are a full anthropology of twenty-four hours. No human invented them. *Jaʿala*, He made. The same verb that made some people a trial for others (25:20) here makes the structure in which the trial is lived. A person who thanks for a shirt and does not thank for the night, who seeks a mattress and does not see *subāt* as a named mercy, who drinks coffee to be raised and does not say *al-ḥamdu lillāh* for the raising, is a person who has been given the cycle as a tool and not as a sign.

The servants of the Compassionate live inside this verse with a modification: they give some of the garment back. Standing at night is a chosen small death of sleep, for the sake of a better rising. They believe in the great *nushūr*, so they can afford to lose a little of the small one. People who hope not for the great one cling to the small one — sleep, entertainment, the night as a garment for deeds they would not do in the day. Same three gifts. Two uses. The verse names the gifts so that the uses can be judged.

**Sleep as a Small Death, Waking as a Small *Nushūr***

The Prophet ﷺ taught, on waking:

> *"Praise be to God who gave us life after He caused us to die, and unto Him is the resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6312)

Verse 47 is that ḥadīth as cosmology. Night is a garment — covering the eye, the market, the shame, the tired body. Sleep is *subāt*, a cutting-off, a pause in which the soul is taken in a manner short of death (39:42). Morning is a *nushūr* that trains the tongue for the greater one. A person who sleeps without the Name and wakes without praise has used two of the three gifts as a beast uses them. The servants who pass the night prostrating (25:64) have taken some of the garment and given it back as worship. They do not despise sleep. They refuse to let sleep be the only use of night. Between garment and resurrection sits the choice that will show on the Day whose *nushūr* this daily one was announcing.

**He It Is Who Made the Night a Garment, Sleep a Rest, and the Day a Rising**

*Wa-huwa lladhī jaʿala lakumu l-layla libāsan wal-nawma subātan wa-jaʿala l-nahāra nushūrā* — He it is who made for you the night a garment, and sleep a rest, and made the day a rising. Three gifts, one anthropology of twenty-four hours. *Subāt* is a cutting, a pause short of death (39:42). The waking ḥadīth (al-Bukhārī 6312) is this verse as a morning sentence. The servants give some of the garment back as *sujūd* (25:64). People who hope not for the great *nushūr* (25:40) cling to the small one as entertainment and as covering for deeds they would not do in the day. Same gifts. Two uses. *Jaʿala* — He made — is the same verb as the trial of verse 20. The structure of the trial is itself a mercy. Thank for the shirt, the pause, and the rising, or use them as a beast uses them.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:48

> **And He it is Who sends the winds as glad tidings ahead of His Mercy. And We send down from Heaven pure water,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Winds as News Before the Gift**

*Wa-huwa alladhī arsala al-riyāḥa bushran bayna yaday raḥmatih* — "And He it is Who sends the winds as glad tidings ahead of His Mercy." After night and day, weather. *Bushr* is glad tidings, the same family as the *bushrā* the guilty will not receive from angels (25:22). Here the winds are *bushr*: good news walking in front of rain. A people of dry valleys knew this in their bodies. The air changes. Dust lifts or settles. A smell arrives. Then water. God could have dropped rain without announcement. He sends a herald. Mercy likes to be preceded by hope.

> *"And it is He who sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy, until, when they have carried heavy clouds, We drive them to a dead land and send down thereby water."* (Qur'an 7:57)

Winds as signs and blessings appear also in 2:164; 15:22; 27:63; 30:46; 45:5. The same winds, in other verses, are a scream upon ʿĀd. Created things, as the evil rain of verse 40 already showed, have no independent moral. They take the office God gives them that day. Today, in this verse, the office is good news.

**Mercy Named as Rain**

*Raḥmatihi* — His Mercy. Rain is not only meteorology. It is a Name made visible. Al-Raḥmān, who will be refused as a Name in verse 60 and who owns true sovereignty in verse 26, is here felt on the skin as wetness that will become grain. The sūrah is teaching its audience to attach phenomena to Names, so that they will not attach them to stars (verse 50) or to idols (verse 3).

The Prophet ﷺ, when he saw rain, would uncover part of his body to receive it and say it was newly come from his Lord (Muslim 898). That is *fiqh* of this verse: the water is a visitor from Mercy. You do not merely run out of it. You greet it.

**Pure Water from Heaven**

*Wa-anzalnā min al-samāʾi māʾan ṭahūrā* — "And We send down from Heaven pure water." *Ṭahūr* is both pure and purifying — a thing clean in itself and able to clean other things. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī both note the two senses; the phrase could be rendered "purifying water." The Qur'an had already used the idea in a battlefield mercy:

> *"He ... sent down upon you water from the sky to purify you thereby, and to remove from you the defilement of Satan, and to strengthen your hearts and set your feet firm."* (Qur'an 8:11)

Commentators use the present verse, as the source notes, to discuss the purifying properties of water and whether other substances share them. The jurists' later work on *ṭahārah* — what lifts ritual impurity — grows from this adjective. Water is the ordinary means God chose for the body's return to prayer. A religion that prays five times a day is a religion that cannot treat water as a mere chemical.

**Heaven as the Direction of the Gift**

*Min al-samāʾ* — from the sky, from Heaven. The direction matters. Wells can be owned. Rivers can be diverted by the strong. Rain, at the moment of falling, is still nobody's. It is the daily rebuttal of the man who thinks provision is a tribe's achievement. Qārūn said, "I was given it on account of knowledge I have" (28:78). Rain does not respect that sentence. It falls on the field of the grateful and, as verse 50 will say, is distributed in a way that should produce reflection, not astrology.

**Glad Tidings in a Sūrah That Has Been Full of Warning**

This chapter has spent many verses on Blaze, constriction, biting of hands, ruined nations. Verse 48 is not a contradiction of that. It is the other half of the same Lord. The winds as *bushr* teach the heart that warning is not His only weather. A person who has only heard the Fire may serve from fear and then dry up. A person who has also stood in a wind that smells of rain knows why the servants at the end of the sūrah are servants of *al-Raḥmān*. Mercy has a herald. It also has a substance, *māʾ ṭahūr*.
