Both come down. Both can be refused, as signs are refused. But they come down, which is why refusal is a choice and not a fate imposed by a silent sky.

**The Ḥadīth of Newly Come Water**

Muslim records Anas:

> *"We were with the Messenger of God ﷺ when rain fell. He uncovered his garment so that the rain would fall on him. We said: Why did you do that? He said: 'Because it has newly come from its Lord.'"* (Muslim 898)

The uncovering is a fiqh of greeting. Water is not only stored, priced, and argued over. It is received. A civilization that can pipe water into every house and still not uncover anything — not a face, not a thanks, not a Name — has gained convenience and lost the verse. The servants of the Compassionate, who will spend in a just mean, are people who know that the water in the cup was *bushr* before it was plumbing.

**Winds That Warn and Winds That Herald**

The same root of wind unmade ʿĀd. The Qur'an is not confused. Created things take offices. A wind can be *bushr* and a wind can be *ʿāṣif*. The people who attribute rain to a star (verse 50) also, in other moods, attribute disaster to bad luck. Both attributions retire the Sender. Verse 48 trains the first attribution: when the air changes toward mercy, say *Raḥmah*. Verse 40 trained the second: when rain is evil, it is still His. Tawḥīd of weather is not a rural piety. It is the refusal to let meteorology become a second lord. Science may describe densities and fronts. Description is reading. It becomes *kufūr* when the description is used to close the book.

**What a Person Does When the Herald Arrives**

He thanks. He does not hoard what was sent as nobody’s. He uses *ṭahūr* for *ṭahārah* — he returns to prayer with the same water that greened the land. He remembers the dead land of verse 49 as a picture of his own chest, and asks that the inner land be revived too. The Prophet ﷺ’s rain-supplications, and the prayer for rain (*istisqāʾ*), are this verse as liturgy. Distribution is His, so request is to Him. The herald has come. The Name is visible on the skin. The servants are people who do not let that visibility pass as furniture.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:49

> **that We may revive a dead land thereby and give drink to many cattle and men We created.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Purpose Clause of Rain**

Verse 48 sent winds as glad tidings and water as *ṭahūr*. Verse 49 names why: *li-nuḥyiya bihi baldatan maytan* — "that We may revive a dead land thereby." The *li-* is purpose. Rain is not a decoration of the sky and not a random kindness. It has a job: to make a dead place live. The same purpose is stated elsewhere with the same astonishment:

> *"And We sent down from the sky blessed water, and We produced thereby gardens and grain of harvest, and towering date palms... as provision for the servants, and We gave life thereby to a dead land. Thus is the emergence."* (Qur'an 50:9–11)

> *"And He who sends down water from the sky in measure, and We revive thereby a dead land — thus will you be brought forth."* (Qur'an 43:11)

*Thus.* The revival of land is the analogical proof of the revival of bodies. Verse 40 said they hope not for *nushūr*. Verse 47 called day a *nushūr*. Verse 49 now puts *nushūr* in the field: brown, then green. A man who has seen a wadi after rain and still says bones will not rise is a man who has refused an analogy his eyes already accepted.

**Dead Land: Mayt as a Feminine Agreement and a Theology**

The Arabic *baldatan maytan* treats the land as dead with a form the grammarians discuss; the meaning is not in dispute. Land dies. Land is raised. Death, in the Qur'an's vocabulary, is not only what happens to animals. A field can be a corpse. The God of verse 3, who alone owns death, life, and resurrection, here exercises all three on dirt. Idols do not green a valley. Caprice does not green a valley. *Al-Raḥmān* does.

The spiritual extension is invited by the text without needing a forced allegory. A chest can be a dead land. Knowledge and repentance are water. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The example of the guidance and knowledge with which God has sent me is like abundant rain falling on the earth..."* (al-Bukhārī 79; Muslim 2282)

The ḥadīth divides soils: some accept water and grow; some hold water for others; some are smooth and barren. Verse 49 is the ḥadīth's landscape. The Book that was taken as *mahjūr* (25:30) is rain that a people can refuse to be soil for.

**Drink for Cattle and for Men**

*Wa-nusqiyahu mimmā khalaqnā anʿāman wa-anāsiyya kathīrā* — "and give drink to many cattle and men We created." The order is striking. Cattle first, then human beings. *Anʿām* are the livestock of verse 44's comparison. The animals that were used as an image of the heedless are, in the economy of rain, mentioned before *anāsī*, human beings. Provision is not a human monopoly. The Lord of the worlds (the title implied since 25:1's "worlds") waters what He created, and He names the beasts in the purpose of His rain.

*Kathīrā*, many — not all in one place, not all at once. Verse 50 will explain the distribution. Here the adjective is already a hint: many, not a chosen tribe only. Cattle of a pagan and cattle of a believer drink. That common drinking is part of *al-Raḥmān*'s universal mercy, the mercy that verse 1 of al-Fātiḥah named as reaching the worlds. The particular mercy of guidance is another rain. This verse is the first rain.

**Created: The Reminder Inside the Gift**

*Mimmā khalaqnā* — from what We created. The drinkers are not independent customers. They are created drinkers, of created water, on created land, revived by the Creator. The purpose clause is therefore also a tawḥīd clause. Every green patch is a sentence: I made this, I killed it with drought, I raised it, I watered what I made. The people who passed the town of evil rain (25:40) and did not see are the people who walk in a green field and see only feed prices.

**How to Drink as a Human, Not Only as Cattle**

Cattle drink and that is piety for cattle. Human beings drink and must remember. The Prophet ﷺ's etiquette of drinking — naming God, sitting, praising after — is the human extra. So is the right of others in the water, the forbidden hoarding of surplus at a well, the law of *sāʾibah* and public water. A dead land revived is a common mercy that can be turned into a private empire. Qārūn and the owners of the garden in Sūrah al-Qalam did that. The servants of the Compassionate, who spend in a just mean, are the opposite use of a revived land: eat, drink, feed, and do not take the green as a god.

The verse's last gift is proportion. Rain's purpose is life — land, beast, human. A culture that pollutes the *ṭahūr* or withholds drink is fighting the purpose clause of revelation. That is not a modern political extra pasted onto a Makkan verse. It is the meaning of *li-nuḥyiya*: so that We may revive. To help revival is to walk with the purpose. To poison it is to take a side against the reason the winds were sent as glad tidings.

**Dead Land, Cattle, Many Humans**

*Li-nuḥyiya bihi baldatan maytan* — that We may revive thereby a dead land. The same verb will, in the next world, revive bodies. Rain is a rehearsal of resurrection, as sleep was a rehearsal of death (25:47). *Wa-nusqiyahu mimmā khalaqnā anʿāman wa-anāsiyya kathīrā* — and give it as drink to many cattle and humans We have created. Cattle are named first. The order is a mercy to the beast and a check on human monopoly. Water is not only for the one who can buy a well. *Anāsiyya kathīrā* — many humans — not all in one city, not all of one tribe. Distribution (25:50) will make this explicit. The servants who spend in a just mean (25:67) are the human response to being named in this list: we were given drink among many. Hoarding the drink is a denial of *kathīrā*. The verse is agriculture, eschatology, and ethics in one purpose clause. A person who can say *al-ḥamd* for rain and still poison a river has said *al-ḥamd* with the mouth only.

**That We May Revive Thereby a Dead Land, and Give Drink to Many Cattle and Humans**

*Li-nuḥyiya bihi baldatan maytan wa-nusqiyahu mimmā khalaqnā anʿāman wa-anāsiyya kathīrā* — that We may revive thereby a dead land, and give it as drink to many cattle and humans We have created. Rain rehearses resurrection as sleep rehearsed death. Cattle are named first: a check on human monopoly. *Anāsiyya kathīrā* — many humans, not one tribe. Distribution (25:50) will make this explicit. The servants who spend in a just mean are the human response to being named among many. Hoarding drink is a denial of *kathīrā*. Cattle drink and that is piety for cattle. Humans drink and must remember: Name over the cup, right of others at the well, no turning a revived land into a private empire. *Li-nuḥyiya* is the purpose. Help revival. Do not poison it. That is walking with the reason the winds were sent as glad tidings.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:50

> **And indeed We have distributed it among them, that they may reflect. But most of mankind refuse to be aught but ungrateful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Distribution as a Sign, Not as Randomness**

*Wa-laqad ṣarrafnāhu baynahum li-yadhdhakkarū* — "And indeed We have distributed it among them, that they may reflect." *Ṣarrafa* is to turn about, to vary, to distribute in different forms and places. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī: the rain does not always fall in the same place, coming down on different lands at different times. Ibn ʿAjībah: even though rain falls every year, God chooses to bless certain lands with it some years and other lands in other years. The map of wet and dry is a sentence to be read.

The purpose of the distribution is *li-yadhdhakkarū* — that they may remember, reflect, take a reminder. Uneven rain is not, in this theology, a proof of Divine absence or of stellar control. It is a proof of a Will that allocates. A world where every field received the same water on the same day would be a world with less *tadhakkur*, because there would be less to ask. Why here and not there? Why this year and not last? The questions are meant to end at a Face, not at a chart of stars.

**Water as a Symbol of What Else Is Distributed**

Ibn ʿAjībah, as the source notes, reads water in its various Qur'anic forms as also symbolizing spiritual blessings that provide life to the soul through knowledge and wisdom. The distribution of rain then becomes a parable of the distribution of guidance: some years a community is soaked in scholars and openings; some years it is dry and is asked to remember. The plain sense does not need the symbol in order to stand. The symbol does not fight the plain sense. Both kinds of water are *ṣarrafnāhu*.

**Most Refuse to Be Anything but Ungrateful**

*Fa-abā aktharu al-nāsi illā kufūrā* — "But most of mankind refuse to be aught but ungrateful." *Abā* is refusal, a stubborn declining. *Kufūr* is ingratitude, and the word is kin to *kufr*, unbelief. Covering a gift is the root image. Most people, the verse says, will not take the distribution as reminder. They will take it as a complaint ("why not us"), or as a pride ("our climate"), or as a science without a Giver, or as astrology.

Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī record that the lack of gratitude in the face of beneficent rain was interpreted to refer specifically to attributing blessings like rain to astrological events such as the setting of a star, rather than to the Mercy and Power of God. The Prophet ﷺ addressed that habit after a night's rain:

> *"Do you know what your Lord has said?" They said: God and His Messenger know best. He said: "He has said: This morning some of My servants have entered morning as believers in Me and some as disbelievers. As for whoever said, 'We have been given rain by the grace of God and His mercy,' that is a believer in Me, a disbeliever in the star. And whoever said, 'We have been given rain by such-and-such a star,' that is a disbeliever in Me, a believer in the star."* (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71)

Verse 50 is that ḥadīth as a Makkan verse. Distribution should produce *tadhakkur*. Most produce *kufūr*. The star is only one of the covers. A modern cover might be a system, a luck, a "that's just how it is." The covering is the point.

**Most: Again the Realism of This Sūrah**

Verse 44 said most of them do not hear or understand. Verse 50 says most of mankind refuse except ingratitude. The sūrah is consistent in not flattering the species. It is also consistent in not silencing the minority. The servants of the Compassionate are about to be described at length. They are not "most." They are enough to make the distribution worth it, and enough to show that *tadhakkur* is possible.

**How to Be of Those Who Reflect**

When rain falls, name God, as the ḥadīth taught. When rain does not fall, ask for it as *istisqāʾ*, the prayer for rain, which is a ritual of this verse: distribution is His, so request is to Him. When another land is green and yours is not, refuse the two idolatries of envy and of contempt. When your land is green, refuse the idolatry of "our star, our soil, our technique" as first cause. Technique is a means inside *taqdīr* (25:2). It is not a replacement for *ṣarrafnāhu*.

Reflection, *tadhakkur*, is a practice of connecting. This water, that dry, my cup, their drought, the dead land of verse 49, the dead heart I have felt in myself — one Lord turning them about. Ingratitude is the practice of disconnecting: treating each wetness as a separate luck. The verse is short. The habit it asks for takes a life. Most refuse. The verse tells you that so that you will not use "most" as your guide when you decide whether to thank.

**The Ḥadīth of the Star and the Rain**

The Prophet ﷺ addressed the habit of attributing rain to a star after a night’s rain:

> *"This morning some of My servants have entered morning as believers in Me and some as disbelievers. As for whoever said, 'We have been given rain by the grace of God and His mercy,' that is a believer in Me, a disbeliever in the star. And whoever said, 'We have been given rain by such-and-such a star,' that is a disbeliever in Me, a believer in the star."* (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71)

Verse 50 is that ḥadīth as a Makkan verse. Distribution should produce *tadhakkur*. Most produce *kufūr*. The star is only one of the covers. A modern cover might be a system, a luck, a "that's just how it is." The covering is the point. When rain falls, name God. When rain does not fall, ask for it as *istisqāʾ*. When another land is green and yours is not, refuse the two idolatries of envy and of contempt. Technique is a means inside *taqdīr* (25:2). It is not a replacement for *ṣarrafnāhu*.

**We Have Distributed It Among Them That They May Remember**

*Wa-la-qad ṣarrafnāhu baynahum li-yadhdhakkarū* — We have surely distributed it among them that they may remember. Rain, in the near sense; the turning-about of signs, in the wider sense the word *ṣarrafnā* has in the Qur'an. Distribution is a mercy and a test. Not every land drinks at once. *Fa-abā aktharu l-nāsi illā kufūrā* — but most of mankind refuse except ingratitude / covering. *Kufūr* is the covering of a gift with a name that is not God’s. Verse 50 is the statistics of *shukr*: most will not. The servants who desire to thank (25:62) are the minority the verse implies without naming. A caller who needs majority-thanks has not read *akthar*. A caller who stops distributing reminder because most cover it has not read *li-yadhdhakkarū*. The purpose clause remains. Distribute. Let most do what most do. Be of those who name the rain.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:51

> **And had We willed, We would have sent a warner to every town.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Counterfactual That Magnifies One Messenger**

*Wa-law shiʾnā la-baʿathnā fī kulli qaryatin nadhīrā* — "And had We willed, We would have sent a warner to every town." God is able to staff the earth with warners, a *nadhīr* in each *qaryah*. He did not, in this will, do so. The verse is understood, Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī note, as a magnification and praise of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ: God singled him out and deemed him worthy to carry the task of establishing the final religion in a way that would reach beyond one town. Al-Qurṭubī hears the implication that, unlike the rain that descends upon all lands (verse 50), the Prophet ﷺ is sent to one land — and through that one land, as verse 1 said, to the worlds. The juxtaposition is the verse’s first teaching. Physical mercy is broadcast. The mercy of a specific prophetic sending is concentrated, then meant to spread by the concentrated one’s reach.

Al-Ṭabarī reads a pastoral meaning for the Prophet ﷺ himself: it might have been easier on him if more messengers were sent to other towns, but now he has to bear a greater burden, and the verse reminds him not to obey the desires of the disbelievers. Ibn ʿAjībah notes that the Prophet ﷺ was necessarily possessed of exceptional wisdom and insight to convey a message that would give the different people of the world all that they needed. The plain sense also allows a reading that singles out Makkah as the town chosen for the sending. All readings agree on the grammar of *law shiʾnā*: this design is a will, not a lack of staff, not a failure of logistics, not an accident of seventh-century geography.

**Rain Everywhere, Warner Not Everywhere: Two Mercies, Two Designs**

Verse 50 said the rain is distributed among them so that they may reflect. Verse 51 says warners are not distributed one per town. If every town had its own *nadhīr* of the same rank, the unity of the last Book and the last law would be harder to see. One Criterion, one servant (25:1), many towns as destination. This does not cancel the earlier prophets. Verse 31 said every prophet had enemies; verses 35–40 named several. They were sent to their peoples. Muhammad ﷺ is being told, in the grammar of *law shiʾnā*, that the present design — one warner, many towns in his address — is a choice. The Qur'an states the uniqueness of that choice in the Prophet’s ﷺ own report:

> *"I have been given five things that were not given to anyone before me… and prophets were sent only to their own people, while I have been sent to all of mankind."* (al-Bukhārī 335; Muslim 521)

Verse 1 named him a warner unto the worlds. Verse 51 explains that this was not because God could not have done otherwise. It was because He willed this. A will that could have multiplied warners and did not is a will that has loaded one chest with a world’s worth of warning. That is honor. It is also weight. Verse 52 will draw the practical conclusion: so do not obey the disbelievers, and strive with it a great striving.

**The Burden Inside the Honor**

A magnification is also a weight. If God could have sent others and did not, the one sent is not free to put the load down because a town has laughed (25:41) or demanded an angel (25:21) or asked for the Book in one lump (25:32). The counterfactual "We could have sent many" is a way of saying "We sent you." The Prophet ﷺ felt the weight. The reports of his grey hair from Sūrah Hūd and its sisters, of his prayer until his feet swelled, of his grief at Taʾif, are the biography of a man who understood that other towns would not be receiving a parallel messenger of their own while he rested.

> *"A sūrah was revealed to me that made my hair grey." They said: Which, O Messenger of God? He said: "Hūd and its sisters."* (transmitted in the commentary tradition; al-Tirmidhī 3297 records related wordings)

Grey from a recitation is the body of verse 51. One warner. Many towns. A Book that does not stop arriving. The Lord who could have staffed every *qaryah* chose instead to make one man’s night prayer (the Sunnah that verse 64 will praise in his followers) carry a world’s warning. That is why verse 20’s "will you be patient?" is not a small question.

**What This Does Not Mean for Later Teachers**

It does not mean that no one after him warns. He left a Book and a Sunnah, and he ﷺ said:

> *"The scholars are the heirs of the prophets. The prophets did not leave a dinar or a dirham as inheritance; they left knowledge. Whoever takes it has taken a great share."* (Abū Dāwūd 3641; al-Tirmidhī 2682)

It means that the office of *rasūl* with a new criterion is closed, and that the concentration of the sending is part of the mercy: one road, not a warner-per-town of conflicting books. The rain’s variety (25:50) is a sign. The message’s unity is a different sign. For a believer in a town that has no scholar, or a town that has many, the verse is a reason to be grateful that the warner’s words reached at all. The *qaryah* that did not receive its own *nadhīr* received, if the Book arrived, the same Criterion. Distance from Makkah is not distance from the sending, once the sending has been defined as "unto the worlds."

Ingratitude, in verse 50, was refusal to read rain. Ingratitude, after verse 51, would be refusal to read the honor of having one messenger whose warning was designed to be enough. A person who says "if only a prophet had come to my city, my century, my language-community in a new sending" is repeating, in a softer voice, the Quraysh’s demand for a customized spectacle. God could have. He willed otherwise. The Criterion is the sufficient sending. The great striving of the next verse is the sufficient response.

**Living as People of One Warner**

Practically this verse forbids two opposite errors. The first is despair that one’s town is too far, too late, too unlike Makkah to be addressed. It was addressed. The worlds were addressed. The second is the invention of new warners who behave as if verse 51 had gone the other way — as if God had in fact sent a *nadhīr* to every sect and every age with a new book. The servants of the Compassionate, who take a way unto their Lord (25:57) with the messenger they were given (25:27), are people of one Criterion. Their imamate (25:74) is an imamate inside that Criterion, not a competing sending. Verse 51, heard well, makes a community both smaller and larger: smaller, because it does not need a prophet in every street; larger, because the one it was given was given to the worlds.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:52

> **So obey not the disbelievers, but strive against them by means of it with a great striving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The "So" That Follows from One Warner**

*Fa-lā tuṭiʿi al-kāfirīn* — "So obey not the disbelievers." The *fa-* draws a line from verse 51. Because God could have sent a warner to every town and instead sent you, do not take instructions from the people who want you to shorten, sweeten, or stop. Obedience here is not the obedience of a subject to a king. It is the obedience of a messenger to a room's pressure: omit the Hour, make the gods a small issue, bring a book all at once, walk less in the market, be more like us. *Lā tuṭiʿ* is a fence around the office.

The Qur'an repeats this fence when the pressure is theological:

> *"So do not obey the deniers. They wish that you would compromise, so they would compromise."* (Qur'an 68:8–9)

Compromise is the form obedience to disbelievers often takes in a man who is not seeking their wealth. He wants the laugh to stop (25:41). He wants the almost (25:42) to become a conversion by means of a softer verse. Verse 52 forbids that path without forbidding gentleness. The servants of the Compassionate will still say "Peace." Peace is not obedience. Obedience would be editing the Criterion to earn the peace.

**Strive Against Them by Means of It**

*Wa-jāhidhum bihi jihādan kabīrā* — "but strive against them by means of it with a great striving." *Bihi*, by means of it. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī understand the pronoun as the Qur'an. Al-Qurṭubī and others allow "by means of Islam." Al-Qurṭubī and others dismiss the opinion that this refers to the use of force, since this is a Makkan sūrah and the permission to use force had not been granted yet. The striving is a striving of recitation, argument, endurance, and public holding of the message — a *jihād kabīr*, a great one, without a sword.

The adjective *kabīr* is a consolation and a demand. Great, because the enemy is not small: it is *hawā* as a god, mockery as a social technology, the memory of ruined towns unused, the demand for angels. Great, because the instrument is great: the Criterion itself. A person who thinks words are weak has not understood this verse. God names a word-struggle a great jihad.

The Prophet ﷺ said, in a ḥadīth that the commentators of jihad often cite for the wider meaning:

> *"The mujāhid is the one who strives against his own soul."* (al-Tirmidhī 1621; Aḥmad; graded ḥasan)

And he returned from an expedition and said, in a widely quoted though chain-discussed report, that they had returned from the lesser jihad to the greater. Whether one accepts that particular wording or not, verse 52 in its Makkan setting is already a greater jihad in the sense of a struggle whose weapon is *it* — the Book.

**What Obedience to Disbelievers Looks Like in a Soft Age**

It looks like silencing a verse because a room will call it harsh. It looks like making the Hour a metaphor to keep a guest. It looks like taking *hawā* as a consultant on what the religion should now mean. It looks like abandoning the Qur'an as *mahjūr* (25:30) while still naming oneself after the messenger. The verse's first clause is therefore not only for a prophet in Makkah. It is for anyone who carries what he carried.

The second clause is the alternative to resentment. Do not obey them; do not merely sulk; *jāhidhum bihi*. Use the Book. Recite it as *tartīl*. Bring the better explanation (25:33). Be patient in the trial of class and insult (25:20). Walk in the market. The great striving is a life, not a moment of volume.

**Great Striving and the Coming Portrait of Gentleness**

There is no contradiction between verse 52 and verses 63–77. The servants who walk humbly and say "Peace" are not people who have obeyed the disbelievers. They are people who have striven with the Book until the Book has shaped their walk. Humility is not surrender of content. The great jihad of the Qur'an produces, if it succeeds in a chest, the character the sūrah is about to describe. A striving that produces arrogance has used some other *bihi*. A striving that produces the abandonment of the message has obeyed the first clause's forbidden object. The two verses at the two ends of this stretch — 52 and 63 — are the same religion: do not obey; strive with it; then walk humbly, because the "it" you strove with is a Book whose Lord is the Compassionate.

**Jihād Kabīr Without a Sword, and Why That Is Not Softness**

Al-Qurṭubī’s insistence that this is Makkan striving with the Qur'an is not a later embarrassment about force. It is a recognition of this chapter’s moment. The Criterion is still winning chests. A sword in this verse would have confirmed the Quraysh’s picture of a man seeking a kingdom. A Book in this verse confirms verse 1. The great striving is recitation, argument, endurance, public holding, night prayer that keeps the reciter from collapsing, and the refusal to edit. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The mujāhid is the one who strives against his own soul."* (al-Tirmidhī 1621; Aḥmad)

That inner striving is not a replacement for the public one. It is its condition. A man who has not striven with his *hawā* (25:43) will, under pressure, obey the disbelievers in the name of strategy. Verse 52 forbids the strategy. Hold the Book. Let the Book do the great work. Walk humbly while you hold it. That combination is this sūrah’s definition of strength.

**Do Not Obey the Disbelievers, and Strive Against Them with It a Great Striving**

*Fa-lā tuṭiʿi l-kāfirīna wa-jāhidhum bihi jihādan kabīrā* — so obey not the disbelievers, and strive against them with it a great striving. *Bihi* — with it — is, in the mass of commentators, with the Qur'an. The great jihād of this Makkan verse is a jihād of argument, recitation, steadfastness, the refusal to edit the message to buy a room’s peace. *Jihādan kabīrā* names it great so that no one will think a war of words is small. The Prophet ﷺ said the mujāhid is the one who strives against his own soul (Aḥmad; al-Tirmidhī 1621, in meaning). Both strivings are in the verse: do not obey them, and use the Book. Obedience to disbelievers can wear the face of courtesy, of “strategy,” of a silence that is actually *mahjūr*. Verse 52 forbids that face. Hold the Criterion. Let it be the weapon. The servants who do not fall deaf on the signs (25:73) are the inner condition of this public striving.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:53

> **And He it is Who mixed the two seas, one sweet, satisfying, the other salty, bitter, and set between them a divide and a barrier, forbidden.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Seas, One Mixing, a Forbidden Line**

*Wa-huwa alladhī maraja al-baḥrayn* — "And He it is Who mixed the two seas." *Maraja* is to mix, to let flow one into the other, to stir together. Al-Ṭabarī: one flows into the other. Some, al-Qurṭubī notes, understand the verb as "to allow to flow," so that God caused the two seas to flow. The picture is of adjacency without annihilation: waters that meet and do not, in the meeting, become one untyped flood.

*Hādhā ʿadhbun furātun wa-hādhā milḥun ujāj* — "one sweet, satisfying, the other salty, bitter." *Furāt* is water so sweet it slakes. *Ujāj* is water so sharp it burns the mouth. Rivers and springs on one side; oceans on the other, in the reading of al-Ṭabarī. The Qur'an returns to the two seas as a sign:

> *"And it is He who has merged the two seas, this one palatable and sweet, this one salty and bitter, and He has made between them a barrier and a forbidding partition."* (Qur'an 25:53, this verse)

> *"He released the two seas, meeting; between them is a barrier they do not transgress."* (Qur'an 55:19–20)

> *"And the two seas are not alike: this one palatable, sweet, pleasant to drink, and that one salty, bitter."* (Qur'an 35:12)

**Barzakh and Ḥijran Maḥjūrā: The Phrase We Have Heard Before**

*Wa-jaʿala baynahumā barzakhan wa-ḥijran maḥjūrā* — "and set between them a divide and a barrier, forbidden." *Barzakh* is a barrier, an isthmus, a between. It appears in 23:100 as the barrier after death, and in 55:20 as the barrier between seas. Eschatologically it names the interval of the grave. Here it is geographic and also more than geographic: a God-made between that keeps kinds kind.

*Ḥijran maḥjūrā* is the exact exclamation of verse 22, when the guilty or the angels cry "A barrier, forbidden!" There it was a verdict. Here it is a description of a mercy in creation. The same words, two offices. A barrier can be a punishment (you shall not pass to the Garden) or a gift (the sweet water shall not be lost in the salt). The Lord of both barriers is one. The people who asked to see angels (25:21) are being told to see, in the meantime, a *barzakh* they can visit at a river-mouth.

**Law and Truth, Hope and Fear: The Readings That Do Not Cancel the Seas**

Ibn ʿAjībah, as the source records, reads the sweet water as the Law (*sharīʿah*), easy to attain, and the salty as ultimate truth (*ḥaqīqah*), which comes only through spiritual effort; or, in the heart, hope and fear, neither of which should overpower the other. Al-Qushayrī's line of commentary has similar interior readings. These are not replacements for the two seas. They are what a heart trained by the two seas may notice in itself. A religion of only sweet water — all hope, all ease — becomes a swamp. A religion of only salt — all fear, all unattainable truth — becomes undrinkable. God mixed them and set a *barzakh*. The servant's inner work is not to abolish one sea. It is to keep the divide God set.

**What the Sign Does to Human Pride**

No engineer set the estuaries' first law. The people who take *hawā* as a god (25:43) cannot, by wanting, make the ocean drinkable or the river preserve itself in the gulf without the measure God set. Verse 2's *taqdīr* is here a taste: sweet, salt, and the line. Science can describe the densities and the tides. Description is a form of reading. It becomes *kufūr* (25:50) when the description is used to retire the Describer.

**Forbidden as a Mercy One Can Imitate**

A *ḥijr maḥjūr* in the soul is the ability to say no at a boundary: this far, not into that. The servants of the Compassionate will not kill, not fornicate, not bear false witness, not linger in idle talk. Those are *barzakhs*. A person without them is two seas with no isthmus, a flood of sweet appetite into bitter consequence. The verse, then, is not only a nature-sign. It is a pattern. God mixes. God divides. Human wisdom, when it is wisdom, copies both: it does not pretend the sweet and the salt are the same, and it does not pretend they do not both belong to Him.

**Barzakh as Mercy That Can Be Copied**

*Barzakh* appears in 23:100 as the barrier after death, and in 55:20 as the barrier between seas. Eschatologically it names the interval of the grave. Here it is geographic and more than geographic: a God-made between that keeps kinds kind. *Ḥijran maḥjūrā* is the exact exclamation of verse 22. There it was a verdict. Here it is a description of a mercy in creation. The same words, two offices. Ibn ʿAjībah reads the sweet water as the Law, easy to attain, and the salty as ultimate truth, which comes only through spiritual effort; or, in the heart, hope and fear, neither of which should overpower the other. These are not replacements for the two seas. They are what a heart trained by the two seas may notice in itself. A religion of only sweet water — all hope, all ease — becomes a swamp. A religion of only salt becomes undrinkable. God mixed them and set a *barzakh*. The servant’s inner work is not to abolish one sea. It is to keep the divide God set. No engineer set the estuaries’ first law. Verse 2’s *taqdīr* is here a taste: sweet, salt, and the line.

**He It Is Who Has Let Loose the Two Seas**

*Wa-huwa lladhī maraja l-baḥrayni hādhā ʿadhbun furātun wa-hādhā milḥun ujāj* — He it is who has let loose the two seas, this one sweet, palatable, and this one salt, bitter. *Wa-jaʿala baynahumā barzakhan wa-ḥijran maḥjūrā* — and placed between them a barrier and a forbidden obstruction. The words of verse 22’s verdict are here a mercy in creation. God mixed without confusing. Kinds remain kinds. A heart may notice, as Ibn ʿAjībah did, hope and fear, Law and ultimate truth: neither should drown the other. The plain reading is already enough. You drink. You sail. You do not pretend the waters are one, and you do not pretend they have two lords. One *Qadīr* (25:54) set the line. The servants keep inner *barzakhs* too: between *laghw* and speech, between *zinā* and *ṣihr*, between waste and miserliness. Copying the barrier is tawḥīd as a habit of distinction.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:54

> **And He it is Who created a human being from water, and made of him lineages and [kinships through] marriages. And thy Lord is Powerful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Water of the Seas to the Water of a Body**

The sūrah has just spoken of two seas. Now: *wa-huwa alladhī khalaqa min al-māʾi basharan* — "And He it is Who created a human being from water." The *māʾ* is the same word as the pure water of verse 48, now as the stuff of a person. The Qur'an says this in several registers: every living thing from water (21:30), every beast from water (24:45), man from a drop, from clay mixed with water. Here the statement is simple and therefore large. The creature who demanded angels and palaces is a water-creature. The messenger he mocked for eating food is a water-creature. The Lord who mixed the seas mixed, from water, a *bashar*.

*Bashar* is human being as skin, as surface, as the mortal visible. The word is the word disbelievers use when they say "you are only a *bashar* like us." The verse takes their noun and honors it as a Divine making. Being *bashar* from water is not a disqualification from prophecy (25:20). It is the condition in which prophecy, lineage, and marriage all happen.

**Lineages and Marriage-Bonds**

*Fa-jaʿalahu nasaban wa-ṣihrā* — "and made of him lineages and kinships through marriages." Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī: *nasab* is kinship by blood; *ṣihr* is kinship by marriage. From one water-creature, two kinds of web. Blood is the given. Marriage is the made alliance. Together they are the human form of the mixing and the barrier: families flow into families without becoming an undifferentiated flood; a *barzakh* of law (who may marry whom, who is *maḥram*, who inherits) keeps the kinds of relation distinct.

> *"O mankind, reverence your Lord, who created you from a single soul and from it created its mate, and from the two has spread abroad a multitude of men and women."* (Qur'an 4:1)

Verse 54 is 4:1 in the vocabulary of water and of the two bonds. The people who take caprice as a god treat both bonds as optional decorations of the self. The Qur'an treats them as a *jaʿl*, a making by God. You did not invent kinship. You entered it. You may, by marriage, extend it. You may not, by *hawā*, pretend you are unoriginated water.

**Marriage as a Sign in a Sūrah That Will End with Spouses**

The servants of the Compassionate will pray, "Grant us comfort in our spouses and our progeny" (25:74). That prayer is already justified by this verse. Spouses are not a worldly distraction from tawḥīd. They are one of the two webs God made from water. Comfort in them, when it is comfort in faith, is a use of the making. Fornication, which verse 68 will forbid, is an attempt to take the mixing without the *barzakh* of *ṣihr*. The law of marriage is therefore not a tribal leftover. It is the ethics of verse 54.

**Thy Lord Is Powerful**

*Wa-kāna rabbuka qadīrā* — "And thy Lord is Powerful." After water, blood, and in-laws, a Name of ability. *Qadīr* is the One who can. Creating a speaking, relating, marrying creature from water is not a small trick. Measuring the two seas and then making, from the same substance, a being who can deny the Maker is a power that includes the power to raise that being from a second waterlessness in the grave.

The Name also sits over broken webs. A lineage can be cruel. A marriage can be a trial (25:20: some of you a trial for others). Power is not the promise that every *nasab* and *ṣihr* will feel like shade. It is the promise that the Maker of the webs can repair, replace, and judge them. The believer marries, honors parents, and keeps ties of kinship (a command the Prophet ﷺ tied to the Name *al-Raḥmān*, al-Bukhārī 5988) under this Name: my Lord is *Qadīr*, so the web I did not invent I will not treat as a god, and the web I cannot fix alone I will not despair of.

**Water, Again, as the Sūrah's Quiet Thread**

Pure water from heaven (48), revival of dead land (49), distribution (50), two seas (53), human from water (54). The chapter that began with a Book sent down is now a chapter of things sent down and mixed and measured. The intellectual point is one: the Sender of the Criterion is the Sender of water in all its offices. To worship a desire, a star, a statue, or a tribe is to forget that you are, in the end, a thirsty arrangement of *māʾ* whose lineages will not help on the Day the wrongdoer bites his hands — unless they were part of a way taken with the messenger.

**Nasab and Ṣihr as Trusts, Not as Idols**

Lineage in Arabia was a religion. Verse 54 names it a creature of water and of *Qadīr*. You may honor it. You may not worship it. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Learn enough of your lineages to maintain your ties of kinship."* (al-Tirmidhī 1979)

That is the lawful use. The unlawful use is the boast that will be dust on the Day the wrongdoer bites his hands and does not bite them because his tribe was small. Marriage, *ṣihr*, is the other web. The servants will ask for coolness of eyes in spouses and offspring (25:74). They will not commit *zinā* (25:68). Between those two verses sits this one: God made the webs. Use them as He made them. Do not invent a third web of desire without covenant, and do not make the first two into gods who can save a face on a Day of gathering. *Qadīr* over that is the same *Qadīr* who mixed two seas and set a *barzakh*. Families have *barzakhs* too: laws of who may marry whom, rights of wives, rights of kin. Crossing them is not freedom. It is a small *kufūr* of a large gift.

**He It Is Who Created from Water a Human, and Made Him Kin by Blood and by Marriage**

*Wa-huwa lladhī khalaqa mina l-māʾi basharan fa-jaʿalahu nasaban wa-ṣihrā* — He it is who created from water a human being and made him kinship and marriage-kin. *Wa-kāna rabbuka qadīrā* — and thy Lord has ever been Powerful. After two seas, a third mixing: a child from *māʾ*. Lineage in Arabia was a religion. Verse 54 names it a creature of water and of *Qadīr*. Honor it; do not worship it. Marriage is the other web. The servants will ask for coolness of eyes in spouses and offspring (25:74) and will not commit *zinā* (25:68). Between those verses this one: God made the webs. Use them as He made them. A lineage will not help on the Day of bitten hands unless it was part of a way with the messenger. *Qadīr* over broken houses is the same *Qadīr* who set a *barzakh*. Families have laws. Crossing them is not freedom.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:55

> **And they worship apart from God that which neither benefits them nor harms them. And the disbeliever is a partisan against his Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Old Test, Applied Again After Water and Kin**

After seas, rain, and marriage-bonds — proofs that should have made a worshipper look up — the sūrah returns to the absurdity of verse 3: *wa-yaʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi mā lā yanfaʿuhum wa-lā yaḍurruhum* — "And they worship apart from God that which neither benefits them nor harms them." The test of benefit and harm has been running through the Book:

> *"Say: Do you worship, apart from God, what has no power to benefit or harm you?"* (Qur'an 5:76)

> *"And they worship apart from God that which neither harms them nor benefits them, and they say, 'These are our intercessors with God.'"* (Qur'an 10:18)

> *"He calls upon one whose harm is nearer than his benefit."* (Qur'an 22:13)

The repetition after the water-signs is a rhetorical sigh. You drink what He sent. You marry by a web He made. You sleep under a garment He spread. Then you bow to what cannot move a drop toward you or away.

**Ẓahīr: Partisan, Helper, or One Who Holds God Cheap**

*Wa-kāna al-kāfiru ʿalā rabbihi ẓahīrā* — "And the disbeliever is a partisan against his Lord." *Ẓahīr* is a helper, a backer, one who lends his back (*ẓahr*) to a cause. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī: they help or support Satan against God. Some, al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note, understand *ẓahīr* as "left behind" or "disregarded," so that the verse would mean the disbeliever deems his Lord unimportant, as something at his back, not before his face.

Both senses cut. In the first, unbelief is not a private vacancy. It is a taking of sides. The disbeliever becomes, in effect, staff for the other camp — the camp of *hawā*, of the *fulān* who pulls from the Reminder, of the mockery in the market. In the second, unbelief is a ranking: the Lord is put behind, like a forgotten load. Verse 18 said they forgot the Reminder in enjoyment. *Ẓahīr* in the "behind" sense is that forgetting as a posture.

**Against His Lord: The Odd Combat**

*ʿAlā rabbihi* — against his Lord, upon his Lord, over against the One who made him from water. The mismatch is the verse's shock. A water-creature, partisan against the Powerful (25:54). A sleeper in a gifted garment, backing the one who cannot raise him. The Qur'an is willing to call this what it is: not a respectable alternative philosophy, but a taking of the wrong side in a contest that is not a contest. God is not endangered by a partisan. The partisan is.

> *"Those who disbelieve are allies of one another."* (Qur'an 8:73)

> *"And he who takes Satan as a protector apart from God has certainly suffered a manifest loss."* (Qur'an 4:119)

Verse 55 is those verses in the vocabulary of this sūrah's argument: worship of the useless, plus active backing of the opposition to the Useful.

**What Partisanship Looks Like Without a Statue**

A person can be *ẓahīr* against his Lord by defending a mockery of the messenger, by legislating *hawā*, by attributing rain to a star, by taking the Qur'an as *mahjūr* and calling the taking sophistication. The partisan is often busy. Unbelief, in this adjective, is not always laziness. It is energy pointed the wrong way.

The contrary is *walāyah* with God, the taking of Him as *Walī*. The Prophet ﷺ said, in the ḥadīth qudsī of approaching God:

> *"Whoever shows enmity to a friend of Mine, I have declared war upon him."* (al-Bukhārī 6502)

Enmity toward the friends is a form of being *ẓahīr ʿalā rabbīhi*. Love of the servants of the Compassionate is a form of getting off that side. The sūrah is about to describe those servants at length, as if to give the partisan a picture of the other camp — not a camp against people, but a camp of walking humbly, night prayer, and a just mean.

**Benefit and Harm as a Daily Audit**

The first half of the verse is a question one can actually ask of any attachment: can this thing, in itself, benefit me or harm me independently of God? If no, it is not eligible for worship. It may be eligible for use, love, and care — a spouse, a medicine, a teacher — under Him. The moment it is asked to do God's work (guarantee, last protection, last meaning), the worship of verse 55 has begun, and with it the risk of becoming *ẓahīr* against the Lord who was already doing the work.

**Partisan Energy and the Quiet of Tawḥīd**

Unbelief in this adjective is often busy. It defends a mockery, legislates a desire, attributes rain to a star, takes the Qur'an as *mahjūr* and calls the taking sophistication. The contrary is not laziness. It is *walāyah* with God, the taking of Him as *Walī*. The Prophet ﷺ reported:

> *"Whoever shows enmity to a friend of Mine, I have declared war upon him."* (al-Bukhārī 6502)

Enmity toward the friends is a form of being *ẓahīr ʿalā rabbihi*. Love of the servants of the Compassionate is a form of getting off that side. The sūrah is about to describe those servants at length, as if to give the partisan a picture of the other camp — not a camp against people, but a camp of walking humbly, night prayer, and a just mean. A person who has been *ẓahīr* can still take the *illā* of verse 70. The Names of verse 6 have not been withdrawn. The audit of attachments is the day’s work: can this thing, apart from God, benefit or harm? If no, it is not eligible for worship. Use it. Do not bow to it. That is verse 55 as a habit.

**They Worship, Apart from God, What Neither Benefits nor Harms**

*Wa-yaʿbudūna min dūni Llāhi mā lā yanfaʿuhum wa-lā yaḍurruhum* — they worship, apart from God, what neither benefits them nor harms them. The idols’ job-description is a void. Benefit and harm are God’s (as the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿAbbās in al-Tirmidhī 2516 already taught). *Wa-kāna l-kāfiru ʿalā rabbihi ẓahīrā* — and the disbeliever is a partisan against his Lord. *Ẓahīr* is a helper, a backer, one who takes a side. Kufr is not only a private opinion. It is taking sides against the Lord in the matter of worship, of rain, of the messenger. The servants of the Compassionate take the Lord’s side without being His protectors — He needs none — by refusing to back what cannot benefit or harm. Verse 55 is tawḥīd as non-partisanship with the useless. A modern *ẓahīr* may back a desire, a market, a tribe, a theory. The test is the same: can it benefit or harm apart from Him? If not, do not worship it, and do not become its lawyer against the Criterion.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:56

> **And We sent thee not, save as a bearer of glad tidings and as a warner.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Office Reduced to Two Words, Which Are Enough**

*Wa-mā arsalnāka illā mubashshiran wa-nadhīrā* — "And We sent thee not, save as a bearer of glad tidings and as a warner." After the partisan against his Lord, a sentence that puts the messenger back in his job-description. *Illā*: nothing else. Not a king of treasure (25:8). Not an angel (25:7). Not a guardian over the man of caprice (25:43). Not a demolisher of towns in his own name (25:36). A *mubashshir* and a *nadhīr*. The pairing is one of the Qur'an's most stable descriptions of prophecy:

> *"We have sent you as a witness, a bearer of glad tidings, and a warner."* (Qur'an 33:45)

> *"And We send not the messengers except as bringers of glad tidings and warners."* (Qur'an 6:48)

> *"O People of the Book, there has come to you Our messenger, making clear to you... lest you say, 'There came not to us any bringer of glad tidings or a warner.'"* (Qur'an 5:19)

Verse 1 named only the warning, because Makkah needed the cliff named. Verse 15 asked whether the Blaze was better or the Garden. Verse 56 now states the office as both halves. A messenger who only warns produces despair or defiance. A messenger who only congratulates produces the ingratitude of verse 50. Both halves are one sending.

**Glad Tidings After So Much Fire**

This sūrah has been heavy. The reader who has walked from the Blaze’s roaring through drowned nations may have forgotten that the same mouth is a mouth of *bushrā*. Verse 56 is a structural kindness. The Garden of everlastingness (25:15), the best dwelling (25:24), the winds as glad tidings (25:48), the promise that God is Forgiving, Merciful (25:6) — these are not asides. They are half of why he was sent. The Prophet ﷺ said to people he was sending out:

> *"Give glad tidings, and do not drive people away. Make things easy, and do not make them difficult."* (al-Bukhārī 69; Muslim 1734)

He was teaching them his own *illā*: you are not sent as something else. The great striving of verse 52, done with the Qur'an, includes the striving to keep both notes audible. A community that can only threaten has obeyed a mood, not a sending. A community that can only smile has done the same with a different mood.

**What the Restriction Protects**

*Mā arsalnāka illā* protects the messenger from roles others will try to hang on him, and from roles he might, in exhaustion or in hope, take up. He is not responsible to convert by force the man of *hawā*. He is not responsible to produce the angelic theatre they demanded. He is responsible to announce. The Hour, the Criterion, the Garden, the ruined towns, the shade, the rain — announce them. God does the rest: guidance, destruction, gentle withdrawal, replacement of evil deeds with good. This restriction is a mercy to every later caller. The heir of the prophets (Abū Dāwūd 3641) is also not sent as a king, a celebrity, or a warder over souls. When a teacher begins to need obedience to himself rather than hearing for the Book, he has left the *illā*. When a teacher despairs because most refuse (25:44, 25:50), he has forgotten that he was sent as announcer, not as a guarantor of soil.

**Warner Unto the Worlds, and Still Only This**

Verse 1’s "warner unto the worlds" and verse 56’s "only a bearer of glad tidings and a warner" look, if one is careless, like a shrinking. They are a focusing. The worlds are the audience. The two functions are the job. The size of the audience does not change the job into sovereignty. *Mulk* remains God’s (25:2, 25:26). The servant remains a servant (25:1). Glad tidings and warning are the servant’s share of the *mulk*: speech, not the throne. A person who loves the Prophet ﷺ can show it by copying the *illā*. Speak hope where hope is true. Speak danger where danger is true. Do not obey the disbelievers’ request to drop one of the two. Do not invent a third office that feels more impressive. The impressive thing, in this verse, is that God considered two kinds of speech enough to send a man for, unto the worlds. The servants of the Compassionate, who say *salām* and who cry *iṣrif ʿannā ʿadhāba jahannam*, are people who have received both halves. Their character is the fruit of a sending that was never only one note.

**Bushrā That Does Not Flatten Warning**

Glad tidings in this sūrah are specific: Gardens of everlastingness as a promise (25:15), replacement of evil deeds (25:70), the *ghurfah* and *salām* (25:75). Warning is specific: roaring Fire (25:12), regret of a bitten hand (25:27), *gharām* (25:65), multiplied punishment (25:69). The messenger is sent with both. A caller who only congratulates has dropped half the sending. A caller who only threatens has dropped the other half. The Makkan air wanted neither: they wanted a king, or a poet they could hire, or silence. Verse 56 refuses the job-change. For later centuries the verse is a charter of *daʿwah*: your office is announcement. Results belong to the Owner of *mulk*. Despair because “most do not believe” is a trespass on God’s statistics (25:50). Pride because some do believe is a trespass on verse 58’s *tawakkul*. The middle is to keep saying what was sent.

**Only a Bearer of Glad Tidings and a Warner**

The restriction is the mercy. He ﷺ is not charged with splitting the sky, pouring a treasure, or converting by force the man of *hawā*. He is charged with two speeches. Glad tidings in this chapter are Gardens, replacement of evil, the *ghurfah*. Warning is the Fire, the bitten hand, *gharām*. Dropping either half to please a room is leaving the *illā*. Verse 1’s “warner unto the worlds” is the audience. Verse 56 is the job. The size of the audience does not promote the messenger to king. *Mulk* remains God’s. A later caller who needs to be more than an announcer has asked, in his own person, for verse 7’s angel and verse 8’s treasure. The servants of the Compassionate received both halves and became a walk, a night, and a just mean. That is what announcement produces when it is believed.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:57

> **Say, "I ask not of you any reward for it, save that whosoever will may take a way unto his Lord."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Price of Revelation Is No Price**

*Qul mā asʾalukum ʿalayhi min ajrin* — "Say, 'I ask not of you any reward for it.'" The messenger who was accused of seeking status, who was measured by treasure and gardens (25:8), is commanded to announce a fee of zero. *Ajr* is wage, compensation, the thing a hired tongue waits for. He is not a hired tongue. The same announcement is put in the mouths of prophet after prophet in Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ:

> *"I do not ask you for any reward for it; my reward is only from the Lord of the worlds."* (Qur'an 26:109, 127, 145, 164, 180)

And:

> *"Say: I do not ask you for any reward for it, except love among kin."* (Qur'an 42:23 — a different exception, for a different context)

> *"Follow those who do not ask of you any payment, and they are rightly guided."* (Qur'an 36:21)

Al-Rāzī: the Prophet ﷺ is asking people to believe for their own sake, not his. Al-Zamakhsharī: all he desires is for them to receive what he is giving them. A paid warning is always under suspicion. An unpaid one still can be rejected, but the rejection cannot honestly say, "He is selling this."

**The Exception That Is Not a Fee**

*Illā man shāʾa an yattakhidha ilā rabbihi sabīlā* — "save that whosoever will may take a way unto his Lord." Al-Qurṭubī records the grammatical reading in which *illā* is a break, so that the verse would run: I ask no reward, but let whoever wills take a way to his Lord. Al-Rāzī notes the reading that the "reward" is that they spend in the way of God, or that taking a way *is* through charitable giving. In all readings, what he wants from them is not a transfer to his pocket. It is a taking — *yattakhidha* again — of a *sabīl* to their Lord.

The wrongdoer will wish he had taken a way with the messenger (25:27). Verse 57 is the messenger, in this world, asking for that taking as the only "payment." You do not pay me. You walk. The walk is your gain. That is the inverted economy of prophecy.

**Whosoever Will: Mashīʾah as the Door That Is Not Forced**

*Man shāʾa* — whoever wills. The man of caprice was a man whose will had been made a god (25:43). Here will is invited to become a way. God does not, in this sentence, drag. The warner warns; the willing take a road. Verse 43 asked, "Wouldst thou be a guardian over him?" Verse 57 is the same limit from the other side: I am not even asking a wage that would give me a client's hold on you. I am asking that, if you will, you walk toward Him.

This is not the theology of a God who has no will over hearts. It is the ethics of a messenger who is forbidden to collect. The two levels — Divine decree and prophetic non-coercion — have been running through the sūrah since the Muʿtazilite and Ashʿarite dispute was mentioned at verse 1. Verse 57 lives at the level of the human encounter: no fee, a road offered, will addressed.

**Taking a Way unto His Lord, Not unto the Messenger**

*Ilā rabbihi* — unto his Lord. The way with the messenger (25:27) and the way unto the Lord are one way with two names, because the messenger does not collect roads to himself. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Do not exaggerate in praising me as the Christians exaggerated in praising the son of Mary. I am only a servant."* (al-Bukhārī 3445)

Verse 57 is that ḥadīth as a business practice. No reward; a way to *his* Lord, the listener's Lord. A later teacher who makes the way end at himself has started asking for the *ajr* this verse forbade, even if he asks in the currency of veneration rather than of coins.

**How a Community Keeps This Verse Alive**

It pays its teachers a living when needed, as the later law of *rizq* from the treasury allowed, without turning the *dīn* into a market of fatwas-for-fees. It suspects the caller who cannot speak without a product. It also suspects the listener who uses "I ask no reward" as a reason to leave knowledge unfunded and scholars hungry. The verse is about the messenger's inner non-attachment and the message's non-sale, not about the ummah's right to starve its teachers.

The personal application is cleaner. When you speak a truth of this Book, notice whether you are waiting for a wage: a like, a status, a debt of thanks. If you are, you have drifted from *qul mā asʾalukum*. When you hear the Book, notice whether you are waiting to be done with the speaker or ready to take a *sabīl*. The second is the only payment he was commanded to want.

**The Economy of Prophecy, Inverted**

The same announcement is put in the mouths of prophet after prophet:

> *"I do not ask you for any reward for it; my reward is only from the Lord of the worlds."* (Qur'an 26:109)

> *"Follow those who do not ask of you any payment, and they are rightly guided."* (Qur'an 36:21)

Al-Rāzī: the Prophet ﷺ is asking people to believe for their own sake, not his. A paid warning is always under suspicion. An unpaid one still can be rejected, but the rejection cannot honestly say, "He is selling this." *Man shāʾa* — whoever wills. The man of caprice was a man whose will had been made a god (25:43). Here will is invited to become a way. God does not, in this sentence, drag. The warner warns; the willing take a road. *Ilā rabbihi* — unto his Lord. The way with the messenger (25:27) and the way unto the Lord are one way with two names, because the messenger does not collect roads to himself. He ﷺ said:

> *"Do not exaggerate in praising me as the Christians exaggerated in praising the son of Mary. I am only a servant."* (al-Bukhārī 3445)

Verse 57 is that ḥadīth as a business practice.

**I Ask of You No Wage Except That Whoever Wills May Take a Way to His Lord**

*Qul mā asʾalukum ʿalayhi min ajrin illā man shāʾa an yattakhidha ilā rabbihi sabīlā* — say: I ask of you no wage for it, except that whoever wills may take a way to his Lord. The *illā* is not a wage. It is the only return he will accept: that a person, willing, take a road. *Man shāʾa* honors the will that verse 43 showed being worshipped. Here will is invited to become a *sabīl*. The way to the Lord and the way with the messenger (25:27) are one road. Unpaid prophecy is a sign of the office (26:109; 36:21). A later teacher who needs a wage as the condition of reminder has left this *qul*. Lawful payment for lawful work is another chapter. Making the Criterion a product is this verse’s opposite. The servants who spend in a just mean (25:67) have learned the same economy from the other side: money is not the point of the road.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:58

> **And trust in the Living, Who dies not, and hymn His praise. And God suffices as One Aware of the sins of His servants,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tawakkul Relocated to the One Death Does Not Touch**

*Wa-tawakkal ʿalā al-ḥayyi alladhī lā yamūt* — "And trust in the Living, Who dies not." After asking no wage and offering a way, the messenger is told where to lean. *Tawakkal* is to entrust, to put one's back against a reality that can hold. The object is not the outcome of the warning, not the conversion of the partisan, not the silence of the mockers. It is *al-Ḥayy*, the Living, with a relative clause that is almost a definition: who does not die.

The Name *al-Ḥayy* stands in the greatest verse with *al-Qayyūm*:

> *"God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Self-Subsisting."* (Qur'an 2:255)

Everything the sūrah has offered as a false lean — idols that cannot create, friends who forsake, stars that do not send rain, caprice that dies with the sleeper, palaces that empty (22:45) — dies. The Living does not. Trust in a dying thing is not trust. It is a delay of collapse. Trust in the Living is the only *tawakkul* that is not a misfiling.

The Prophet ﷺ's morning and evening words include:

> *"O Ever-Living, O Self-Subsisting, by Your mercy I seek help; set right all my affairs, and do not leave me to myself for the blink of an eye."* (al-Nasāʾī in ʿAmal al-Yawm wal-Laylah; al-Ḥākim; graded ḥasan by some)

Not left to oneself even for a blink: that is *tawakkul* as a refusal of the inner god of verse 43.

**Hymn His Praise: Tasbīḥ as the Sound of Trust**

*Wa-sabbiḥ bi-ḥamdih* — "and hymn His praise." *Tasbīḥ* is declaring Him far from every unbecoming thing; *ḥamd* is praise for what He is and what He gives. The two together are the tongue's version of *tawakkul*. A person who has really leaned on the Living will not then spend the day listing what the Living has "failed" to do on the person's timetable. He will declare Him free of failure and praise Him in the timetable he does not own.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Two words, light on the tongue, heavy on the Scale, beloved to the Compassionate: Glory be to God and by His praise; Glory be to God, the Magnificent."* (al-Bukhārī 6406; Muslim 2694)

Beloved to the Compassionate: the Name this sūrah is about to put over a whole portrait of servants. *Tasbīḥ* is not a decoration around trust. It is how trust breathes when it has a mouth.

**God Suffices as Aware of the Servants' Sins**

*Wa-kafā bihi bi-dhunūbi ʿibādihi khabīrā* — "And God suffices as One Aware of the sins of His servants." Sufficiency again, as in verse 31. There He sufficed as Guide and Helper. Here He suffices as *Khabīr*, Aware, of *dhunūb*, the sins of ʿibād, His servants. The messenger who has been mocked, called bewitched, told that his Book is fables, does not need to keep a private archive of the mockers' crimes. The archive is already held by One whose knowledge of secrets was named in verse 6.

This is a release. Revenge is often the claim that no one else saw. *Kafā bihi khabīrā* says someone saw, and that someone is enough. The Prophet ﷺ is being told not to become, in his hurt, a competing recorder. The servants, a few verses later, will be people who say "Peace" when the ignorant address them. That peace is easier if the file is not in their hands.

**Sins of His Servants: The Possessive Again**

*ʿIbādihi* — His servants. Even the sinners are His. The partisan of verse 55 is still, in the grammar of ownership, a servant in revolt, not an independent kingdom. Awareness of their sins is therefore not a journalist's file. It is a Master's knowledge of a household. That knowledge, paired with verse 6's Forgiving, Merciful, is why repentance in verses 70–71 will be possible. The One who suffices as *Khabīr* is the One who can replace evil deeds with good. If He were only aware, the verse would be terror. He is the Living who does not die, praised, aware — and, the sūrah has not forgotten, merciful.

**How to Practice This Verse When the File in the Chest Is Heavy**

Trust: do the next right act, and do not stay up owning outcomes. Praise: put *subḥān Allāh wa-bi-ḥamdih* in the mouth when the mind wants to rehearse insults. Sufficiency of awareness: when the urge to expose, to archive, to "make sure they pay" rises, remember that payment is already in a better hand. The great striving of verse 52 continues. It continues without the poison of being the accountant of other people's *dhunūb*. That accountant, if he is not God, becomes a small partisan of his own.

**The Living Who Dies Not**

The Name *al-Ḥayy* stands in the greatest verse with *al-Qayyūm*:

> *"God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Self-Subsisting."* (Qur'an 2:255)

Everything the sūrah has offered as a false lean — idols, friends who forsake, stars, caprice, palaces — dies. The Living does not. Trust in a dying thing is a delay of collapse. The Prophet ﷺ’s morning and evening words include:

> *"O Ever-Living, O Self-Subsisting, by Your mercy I seek help; set right all my affairs, and do not leave me to myself for the blink of an eye."* (al-Nasāʾī, ʿAmal al-Yawm wal-Laylah; al-Ḥākim)

*Tasbīḥ* is the tongue’s version of *tawakkul*. Two words, light on the tongue, heavy on the Scale, beloved to the Compassionate (al-Bukhārī 6406; Muslim 2694). Sufficiency of awareness of sins is a release: revenge is often the claim that no one else saw. *Kafā bihi khabīrā* says someone saw, and that someone is enough. The servants who say *salām* when the ignorant address them find that peace easier if the file is not in their hands.

**Put Thy Trust in the Living Who Does Not Die**

*Wa-tawakkal ʿalā l-ḥayyi lladhī lā yamūt* — and put thy trust in the Living who does not die. Every other lean dies: idols, *fulān*, stars, palaces, the self. *Wa-sabbiḥ bi-ḥamdih* — and glorify with His praise. Trust has a tongue. *Wa-kafā bihi bi-dhunūbi ʿibādihi khabīrā* — and He suffices as One Aware of His servants’ sins. The file of sins is not in the neighbor’s mouth and not in the enemy’s. It is with the Aware, who is also, verse 6, Forgiving, Merciful. *Tawakkul* here is a release from being the accountant of other people’s ledgers and of one’s own panic. The Prophet ﷺ’s *ḥasbunā Llāh* is this verse in a battlefield. The servants’ *salām* to the ignorant is this verse in a market. Both are trust in the One who does not die.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:59

> **He Who created the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them in six days, then mounted the Throne, the Compassionate [is He]. So ask, regarding Him, One Who is aware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Six Days, Then the Throne: Creation as a Stated Act**

The Living of verse 58 is now identified by acts: *alladhī khalaqa al-samāwāti wal-arḍa wa-mā baynahumā fī sittati ayyām thumma istawā ʿalā al-ʿarsh* — "He Who created the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them in six days, then mounted the Throne." The formula is one the Qur'an repeats:

> *"Indeed, your Lord is God, who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then mounted the Throne."* (Qur'an 7:54; 10:3; 32:4; cf. 11:7; 50:38; 57:4)

Six days: a measured creating, not an unformed instant in our sight, though He could have said "Be" once. Al-Rāzī prefers, as the source notes, not to convert these days into the "thousand years" of 22:47 without need, and considers some of these questions among the imponderables — like why five prayers — since God could have created in an instant. The point the verse wants is not a calendar of cosmic hours. It is that creating was an act with a measure (25:2), and that after creating comes *istiwāʾ* upon the Throne: rule, establishment of kingship, the *mulk* of verses 2 and 26 in a spatial-theological image the early Muslims received without carving it into a body.

Ibn Taymiyyah and the school of the *salaf* insist on *istiwāʾ* as a real attribute befitting God, without asking how. Others among the theologians interpreted. The verse, in a commentary for a wide audience, can say what all agree: the Creator is not absent from what He made. He mounted the Throne. The world is not a machine left running. It is a kingdom.

**The Compassionate Is He**

*Al-Raḥmān* — the Compassionate. The Name is read, as al-Rāzī notes, in several grammars: as the subject of the whole sentence; as an appositive to the Living of verse 58; as the start of a new sentence, "As for the Compassionate, ask..." In every grammar the Name is placed over creation and Throne. The King who created in six days and rules is *al-Raḥmān*. Verse 26 said true sovereignty that Day belongs to the Compassionate. Verse 59 says the sovereignty of the days before that Day belongs to Him too.

This is the Name the next verse will show the idolaters refusing. The sūrah is installing it, with full cosmic credentials, immediately before their refusal, so that the refusal will be heard as ignorance of a Name that has just been attached to the Throne.

**"So Ask, Regarding Him, One Who is Aware"**

*Fa-sʾal bihi khabīrā* — "So ask, regarding Him, One Who is aware." Al-Rāzī records the readings. "Ask, regarding it" — the creation — and the Aware is God Himself, the Prophet ﷺ being commanded to rely on God for knowledge of such matters. Or the Aware is Gabriel. Or, less likely if the command is to the Prophet ﷺ, a wise human. The safest pastoral reading, and the one that matches verse 58's *khabīrā*, is that knowledge of this Lord is to be sought from the One who is Aware — God — through what He has said, not from the guesswork of a people who cannot read rain.

The verse, heard that way, is a method. About God, ask the Aware. Do not ask caprice (25:43). Do not ask the fathers' idols (25:55). Do not ask a star (25:50). Revelation is the asking-place. The Prophet ﷺ, who did not write with his right hand (29:48), is himself the one who asked and was answered. We ask by following what he brought.

**Why Six Days Matter to a Person Who Will Never Settle the Physics**

They matter because they teach patience of Divine action. The God who could have made the world in a blink made it in a stated span, and the God who could have sent the Book in one lump (25:32) sent it in *tartīl*. Measure is His habit of mercy toward creatures who cannot bear unmediated totality. A person who resents the slow — slow guidance, slow provision, slow justice — is a person at odds with the six days and the twenty-three years. *Al-Raḥmān* is not slow as neglect. He is measured as care.

They also matter because "whatsoever is between them" includes the shade, the night-garment, the two seas, the water of a child. The Throne is not a reason to despise the between. The Compassionate who mounted the Throne is the One who spread the shade. Asking the Aware about Him should produce, if the asking is real, a life that can walk in a market without thinking the Throne is empty.

**Ask the Aware: A Method, Not a Slogan**

*Fa-sʾal bihi khabīrā* is permission to ask and a restriction on whom to ask. Ask one who knows. The Prophet ﷺ is the first referent for his community. After him, those who inherited his knowledge without selling it. The verse does not send the reader to the stars of verse 61, nor to the caprice of verse 43, nor to the mockers of verse 41. Knowledge of the six days and the Throne is not a hobby of cosmology. It is *khabar* from the *Khabīr*. A person who builds a theology from his own *hawā* and then pastes “the Compassionate” on it has not asked the Aware. He has asked himself. The servants who will soon be described are people whose asking has become walking, night-prayer, a just mean, and a cry against Hell. That is what asking the Aware looks like when it graduates from a question into a life.

**He Created the Heavens and the Earth and What Is Between Them in Six Days**

*Alladhī khalaqa l-samāwāti wal-arḍa wa-mā baynahumā fī sittati ayyāmin thumma stawā ʿalā l-ʿarsh* — who created the heavens and the earth and what is between them in six days, then mounted the Throne. The six days teach measured action, kin to the *tartīl* of verse 32. *Al-Raḥmān* is the Name on the Throne, the Name they refused in verse 60. *Fa-sʾal bihi khabīrā* — so ask, about Him, one who is aware. Ask the Prophet ﷺ, then those who inherited knowledge without selling it. Do not ask the stars, the caprice, or the mocker. The servants who follow are what asking looks like when it has become a walk. The Throne does not empty the market. The Compassionate who mounted it is the One who spread the shade. Live in the between as a person who has asked.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:60

> **And when it is said unto them, "Prostrate before the Compassionate," they say, "And what is the Compassionate? Shall we prostrate before that [to] which you command us?" And it increases them in aversion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Name They Will Not Bow To**

*Wa-idhā qīla lahumu usjudū lil-Raḥmān* — "And when it is said unto them, 'Prostrate before the Compassionate.'" The command is simple. The Name is the Name verse 59 just attached to the Throne. Their reply is *wa-mā al-Raḥmān* — "And what is the Compassionate?" The *mā* can be contemptuous ("what is this 'Compassionate'?") or ignorant ("we do not know this name"). Most commentators, al-Rāzī notes, believe they were questioning the name *al-Raḥmān*, not the existence of that to which it referred.

Ibn Kathīr records the incident of Ḥudaybiyyah: the Prophet ﷺ instructed that the treaty be written "In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful," and the Qurayshī negotiators objected that they did not know *al-Raḥmān*; they wanted "In Thy Name, O God" (*bi'smika Allāhumma*). Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī note that some of the idolaters said they only knew of one *raḥmān*, Musaylimah of Yamāmah, who had taken the title Raḥmān al-Yamāmah. The Name, in other words, was either unfamiliar as a proper name of God, or it had been stolen by a false prophet, or both. In any case they would not *sajdah* to it.

**"Shall We Prostrate to What You Command Us?"**

*A-nasjudu li-mā taʾmurunā* — "Shall we prostrate before that [to] which you command us?" Some read *yaʾmurunā*, "to which he commands us." The resistance is not only to a Name. It is to being commanded. Prostration is the body admitting a hierarchy. A people who have taken *hawā* as a god (25:43) and who mock a messenger who eats food (25:41) will hear a command to bow as an insult to the inner king. The *mā*, "what," reduces the Compassionate to an object among objects, a "that" which a man is ordering them to worship. They cannot hear that the order is from the Throne, through a servant.

**It Increases Them in Aversion**

*Wa-zādahum nufūrā* — "And it increases them in aversion." *Nufūr* is shying away, the startle of a beast, flight from a thing. The command to prostrate, which increases the believers in humility, increases these in flight. Al-Rāzī records that upon the revelation of this verse the Prophet ﷺ and the Companions prostrated, and the idolaters imitated them in mockery, which is what "it increases them in aversion" meant in that report. Whether the report is the occasion or a later illustration, the pattern is Qur'anic: the same recitation increases some and hardens others.

> *"And We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers, and it does not increase the wrongdoers except in loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

> *"And when He sends down a sūrah, some of them say, 'Which of you has this increased in faith?' As for those who believe, it increases them in faith."* (Qur'an 9:124)

Verse 60 is that law in the matter of a Name. *Al-Raḥmān* is healing if you bow. It is *nufūr* if you will not.

**Why This Name, at This Moment**

Because the sūrah is about to define *ʿibād al-Raḥmān*, the servants of the Compassionate. Before the portrait, the refusal. The reader will know that the people in verses 63–77 are people who accepted the Name others mocked. Prostration before *al-Raḥmān* is the inner door to the character that follows. A person who will not bow at the Name will not walk humbly, will not spend in a just mean, will not fear Hell as they fear it. The portrait is not a generic ethics. It is the ethics of people who know who *al-Raḥmān* is.

The Qur'an elsewhere makes the Name a complete alternative to *Allāh* in address:

> *"Say: Call upon God or call upon the Compassionate. Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names."* (Qur'an 17:110)

The Quraysh's "what is the Compassionate?" is therefore not a small lexical gap. It is a refusal of a Name God had made equivalent, in invocation, to the supreme Name. The servants who will not refuse it are about to be described. The increase of aversion in verse 60 is the dark that makes their light visible.

**Prostration as the Test That Still Works**

A body that will not go down has already chosen a god that must not be outranked. The Prophet ﷺ said that the closest a servant is to his Lord is while prostrating (Muslim 482). Verse 60's people will not take that closeness because they have asked "what is He?" as a shield. The living test is the same. When the Name is said, does the chest move toward the ground or toward *nufūr*? The answer, over a life, is the difference between this verse and the next section of the sūrah.

**The Name They Would Not Write at Ḥudaybiyyah**

Ibn Kathīr records that at Ḥudaybiyyah the Prophet ﷺ instructed that the treaty be written "In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful," and the Qurayshī negotiators objected that they did not know *al-Raḥmān*; they wanted "In Thy Name, O God." Al-Qurṭubī notes that some of the idolaters said they only knew of one *raḥmān*, Musaylimah of Yamāmah. Most commentators believe they were questioning the name, not the existence of that to which it referred. The Qur'an had made the Name a complete alternative in address:

> *"Say: Call upon God or call upon the Compassionate. Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names."* (Qur'an 17:110)

Their "what is the Compassionate?" is therefore not a small lexical gap. It is a refusal of a Name God had made equivalent, in invocation, to the supreme Name. The same recitation increases some and hardens others (17:82; 9:124). Prostration before *al-Raḥmān* is the inner door to the character that follows. A person who will not bow at the Name will not walk humbly, will not spend in a just mean, will not fear Hell as they fear it. The portrait is the ethics of people who know who *al-Raḥmān* is.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:61

> **Blessed is He Who placed constellations in the sky and placed therein a lamp and a shining moon.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Tabāraka: Blessing Spoken Over the Night Sky**

The sūrah has used *tabāraka* of the One who sent the Criterion (25:1) and of the One who can give His servant better than treasure (25:10). Now: *tabāraka alladhī jaʿala fī al-samāʾi burūjan* — "Blessed is He Who placed constellations in the sky."
