The blessing that opened the Book's sending and the servant's possible palaces now opens the sky as a made thing. The people who asked "what is the Compassionate?" are given, if they will look up, a sky that Name arranged.

*Burūj* are constellations, towers, the twelve signs of the band through which sun, moon, and planets move. Al-Zamakhsharī connects the word to *tabarruj*, a display of adornment, because of the radiance of the constellations. They can also mean towers (4:78), and in that sense the "houses" where the seven visible lights — five planets, sun, and moon — are said to reside. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī both record this. The Qur'an names *burūj* also in 15:16 and 85:1. The sky is not a blank. It is a placed architecture.

**A Lamp and a Moon That Shines**

*Wa-jaʿala fīhā sirājan wa-qamaran munīrā* — "and placed therein a lamp and a shining moon." Most commentators take the lamp as the sun, which is what it is called in 71:16 and 78:13. Some, al-Qurṭubī notes, read *sirāj* in the plural *suruj* and understand stars. The lamp can also, in a quiet allusion, recall the Prophet ﷺ, who is called a luminous lamp in 33:46. The plain sense is the sun: a lamp, not a god. Verse 45 already demoted the sun to a *dalīl*, an indicator of shade. Verse 61 names it a *sirāj*, a tool of light someone placed.

The moon is *munīr*, shining with a light that, as other verses make explicit, is a borrowed light. Night's garment (25:47) is not absolute dark. A shining moon is part of the garment's mercy, a lamp for travelers and for people who will rise to pray (25:64) and need a sky that is not a wall.

**What This Verse Forbids and What It Does Not**

The source commentary notes that although cosmological sciences, of which astrology is a part, were common in Islamic civilization, Islamic law forbids predictive astrology and astrological magic, while not forbidding the symbolic and descriptive study of the sky. Verse 50 has already rebuked those who attribute rain to a star. Verse 61 blesses the Placer of *burūj*, which is the opposite of blessing the *burūj* as lords. You may map them. You may not ask them for a child's fate. The difference is tawḥīd.

Ibn ʿAjībah, as the source records, reads the zodiacal signs as sometimes symbolizing states of the soul and stations of virtue. That reading is a contemplation, not a horoscope. A soul may notice, in a night sky, the variety of lights and remember the variety of gifts. It may not hand its *tawakkul* (25:58) to a house of Saturn.

**Blessed Is He: The Sky as a Reason to Bow**

Verse 60 refused prostration to *al-Raḥmān*. Verse 61 is, among other things, a reason they should have bowed. The One who placed towers of light and a lamp and a shining moon is not a "what" to be sniffed at. He is *tabāraka*, the One from whom good increases. A person who sleeps under that sky, in the garment of night, and then asks "what is the Compassionate?" is a person who has used the *sirāj* to live and refused the Name of the One who hung it.

The Prophet ﷺ taught a supplication at seeing the new moon, and he taught looking at the sky in the opening of night prayer. The servants who pass the night prostrating and standing (25:64) are people who have taken verse 61 as a miḥrāb. The lamp is above them. The moon is *munīr*. The Placer is the Compassionate. Their aversion did not increase. Their *sajdah* did.

**A Sky That Will Split, Named First as a Gift**

Verse 25 split the heavens with clouds. Verse 61, later in recitation but earlier in the world's ordinary days, names the heavens as a placed beauty. Both are true. The sky is a mercy now and a door then. People who only hear the splitting may not love the Lord. People who only hear the constellations may not fear Him. The sūrah has given both. *Al-Raḥmān* is the Name that can hold a lamp and a splitting in one sovereignty. Blessed is He who placed. Blessed is He who will open. The servant who has said *tabāraka* over both is ready for the portrait that begins two verses from now.

**A Lamp, Not a Lord**

Most commentators take the lamp as the sun, which is what it is called in 71:16 and 78:13. Verse 45 already demoted the sun to a *dalīl*, an indicator of shade. Verse 61 names it a *sirāj*, a tool of light someone placed. The moon is *munīr*, shining with a light that other verses make explicit as borrowed. Night’s garment (25:47) is not absolute dark. A shining moon is part of the garment’s mercy, a lamp for travelers and for people who will rise to pray (25:64). Islamic law forbids predictive astrology and astrological magic. Verse 50 has already rebuked those who attribute rain to a star. Verse 61 blesses the Placer of *burūj*, which is the opposite of blessing the *burūj* as lords. You may map them. You may not ask them for a child’s fate. The difference is tawḥīd. The Prophet ﷺ taught looking at the sky in the opening of night prayer. The servants who pass the night prostrating are people who have taken verse 61 as a miḥrāb. The lamp is above them. The Placer is the Compassionate. Their aversion did not increase. Their *sajdah* did.

**Blessed Is He Who Placed in the Sky Mansions, a Lamp, and a Moon Shining**

*Tabāraka lladhī jaʿala fī l-samāʾi burūjan wa-jaʿala fīhā sirājan wa-qamaran munīrā* — blessed is He who placed in the sky mansions, and placed therein a lamp and a moon shining. *Tabāraka* again, as in verses 1 and 10. Blessedness attached to placing. *Burūj* are towers, constellations, great signs of the sky. Mapping them is not worship. Asking them for a child’s fate is. *Sirāj* and *qamar munīr* demote sun and moon to furniture of a mercy. The people who increased in *nufūr* at the command to prostrate to the Compassionate (25:60) are here given more of His sky to refuse, or to read. The servants who will stand at night (25:64) read it. A lamp is above them. They do not pray to it. They pray to the Placer. That is tawḥīd in a posture.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:62

> **And He it is Who made the night and the day successive, for whosoever desires to reflect or desires to be thankful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Khilfah: Each One Taking the Other's Place**

*Wa-huwa alladhī jaʿala al-layla wal-nahāra khilfatan* — "And He it is Who made the night and the day successive." *Khilfah* is succession, each coming behind the other, one taking the place the other leaves. Some, al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note, understand it as "opposed": He made them opposites, one dark and one light. Both senses are in the word. They follow each other, and they are not the same. The garment and the resurrection of verse 47 are here a relay. The Qur'an cites this relay as a sign in many places:

> *"Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the night and the day are signs for those of understanding."* (Qur'an 3:190)

> *"He makes the night enter into the day and the day enter into the night."* (Qur'an 22:61)

Entering, succeeding, opposing: the vocabulary of a measured motion no idol owns. Verse 2’s *taqdīr* is here a clock that the illiterate can read. A still world, as verse 45 said of a still shade, would be a world without this mercy. The relay is how a human being is given more than one hour in which to become someone else.

**For Whoever Desires to Remember, or Desires to Thank**

*Li-man arāda an yadhdhakkara aw arāda shukūrā* — "for whosoever desires to reflect or desires to be thankful." The relay is not for everyone as a benefit of meaning. It is *for* the one who wants *tadhakkur* or wants *shukr*. Al-Ṭabarī: one desires to reflect on or desires to be thankful for the blessings of the day and night. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī also record the reading that whatever one could not accomplish of worship in the day one could accomplish at night, and vice versa. The succession is a second chance built into the clock. Ibn Kathīr mentions the ḥadīth:

> *"God, glorified and exalted, extends His Hand by night so that He might forgive the one who sinned during the day, and He extends His Hand by day so that He might forgive the one who sinned during the night, until the sun rises from its setting-place."* (Muslim 2759)

That ḥadīth is verse 62 as a theology of time. Night is not only a garment for sleep. It is a Hand extended. Day is not only a *nushūr* for work. It is a Hand extended. The person who desires to remember or thank will find, in whichever half he missed the other, a place to stand. The person who does not desire will find only a schedule.

**Desire: Irāda as the Human Key Again**

*Arāda* — he desired, he willed. Verse 57: whoever wills may take a way. Verse 62: the night and day are for whoever desires remembrance or thanks. The signs are public. The use is willed. A person can live through ten thousand relays of dark and light and never remember and never thank. Cattle do that (25:44) without blame. Humans who do it have refused a *li-man arāda* that was aimed at them. Remembrance and thanks are not the same act, and the verse honors both. Some hearts wake to meaning (*tadhakkur*). Some hearts wake to gratitude (*shukr*). Both are accepted as reasons the relay was made. A scholar’s night of reflection and a laborer’s morning of *al-ḥamdu lillāh* are both inside the *li-man*. The sūrah will not require every servant of the Compassionate to be a thinker of the same kind. It will require that they not be people who let the relay pass as if it were furniture.

> *"If you are grateful, I will surely increase you; but if you disbelieve, indeed My punishment is severe."* (Qur'an 14:7)

*Shukr* in that verse is a law of increase. *Tadhakkur* in this verse is a law of meaning. Night and day are the field of both. Ingratitude, verse 50 said, is what most people refuse to be anything but. Verse 62 offers the alternative as a desire anyone can begin at the next turning.

**A Bridge into the Portrait**

Verse 62 is the last verse before *ʿibād al-Raḥmān*. The placement is a craftsman’s. The sky has been blessed, the lamp hung, the night and day made successive for remembrance and thanks. Now: who are the people who actually remembered and thanked? The next fifteen verses answer with a walk, a word of peace, a night of prayer, a fear of Hell, a just hand in money, a refusal of the great sins, a repentance, a dignity in speech, an open eye to signs, a prayer for family and for leadership in piety. They are the *man arāda* of this verse, written out as a community. The reader is being told, before the list, that the list is not an elite blood. It is a use of night and day. Anyone who desires to remember or thank has already stepped toward that community. The successive dark and light will not stop for him. That is the mercy. He can begin at the next Maghrib, the next Fajr, the next missed deed that a later hour can still repair. The servants who pass the night prostrating (25:64) have taken this verse at its word. The people who took the Qur'an as *mahjūr* let ten thousand relays pass. Same clock. Two uses. The verse names the clock so that the uses can be judged.

**Whoever Desires to Remember, or to Thank**

*Li-man arāda an yadhdhakkara aw arāda shukūrā* — for whoever desires to remember, or desires to be grateful. Desire is named twice. Night and day are not an automatic factory of remembrance. They are an opportunity for a will. Verse 43’s man of *hawā* also desired. The difference is the object. Here the object is *dhikr* and *shukr*. The successive alternation is a mercy to that desire: if you miss the night, a day comes; if you miss the day, a night comes. The Prophet ﷺ said the two cool prayers — Fajr and ʿAṣr — are a special keeping (al-Bukhārī 574; Muslim 625), and that whoever prays the two cool ones enters the Garden. Verse 62 is the cosmology of those two cool ones: a night that can be stood in, a day that can be thanked in. The portrait that follows is simply what a human looks like when *arāda* has chosen those two objects long enough to become a character.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:63

> **The servants of the Compassionate are those who walk humbly upon the earth, and when the ignorant address them, say, "Peace,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Portrait Begins with Feet, Not with a Title**

*Wa-ʿibādu al-Raḥmāni alladhīna yamshūna ʿalā al-arḍi hawnan* — "The servants of the Compassionate are those who walk humbly upon the earth." After the Name was refused (25:60) and the sky was blessed (25:61) and night and day were made for remembrance (25:62), the sūrah answers "what is the Compassionate?" with a people. You want to know the Name? Look at His servants. They are not introduced by genealogy, office, or miracle. They are introduced by a walk.

*Hawnan* is gentleness, unhurriedness, humility of gait, a lack of stamping. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī: with dignity, tranquility, and forbearance. Al-Qurṭubī: it refers not only to walking but to the general conduct of one's life. The earth in this clause is the same earth whose sovereignty belongs to God (25:2), the earth the messenger walked in markets upon (25:7, 20). The servants do not float. They walk. They walk as if the ground is not theirs to punish.

The Qur'an had already forbidden the other walk:

> *"And do not walk upon the earth exultantly. Indeed, you will never tear the earth apart, and you will never reach the mountains in height."* (Qur'an 17:37)

Ibn Kathīr mentions ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb seeing a young man walking meekly, as if ill, and asking if he were sick; when the man said no, ʿUmar commanded him to walk with strength. *Hawnan* is not weakness, not a performed slouch, not the affectation of piety as a limp. It is the absence of swagger. ʿUmar's correction protects the verse from becoming theatre.

The Prophet ﷺ said, in a ḥadīth Ibn Kathīr also brings to this territory:

> *"When you come to the prayer, do not come in haste. Come to it with tranquility. Pray what you catch, and complete what you miss."* (al-Bukhārī 636; Muslim 602)

Tranquility of approach to prayer is *hawn* in a ritual form. The walk of verse 63 is that tranquility extended to the whole earth.

**When the Ignorant Address Them**

*Wa-idhā khāṭabahum al-jāhilūna qālū salāmā* — "and when the ignorant address them, say, 'Peace.'" *Jāhil* here is not the unlettered. It is the person of *jahl*, the heated ignorance that insults, jests, and picks a fight. The servants are addressed — *khāṭabahum* — they are not the seekers of the quarrel. Their reply is one word of a kind: *salāmā*.

Al-Ṭabarī: though they are subjected to ignorant behavior, they should not respond in kind. Some interpret *salām* as speaking what is appropriate and correct (*sadād*). Some, as al-Qurṭubī records, took *salām* as "I am free of you," a disassociation, and then claimed the verse was abrogated by the verses of fighting. Al-Qurṭubī, after mentioning that opinion, states that no question of abrogation need arise: the verse is commanding an attitude of restraint and forbearance, which is not a legal command of the kind that abrogation governs. If *salām* were rewritten as "I am free of you" across the Qur'an, God would be saying it to the people of the Garden (36:58), and Abraham to his guests (51:25), which is intolerable.

Peace as the servants' answer is therefore what it sounds like: a refusal to let *jahl* set the tone. It is not a treaty of surrender of the Criterion. Verse 52 already forbade obeying the disbelievers. Verse 63 forbids imitating the ignorant. Both can be true in one person: a great striving with the Book, and a walk that does not stomp, and a mouth that says *salām* when a mouth of *jahl* opens.

**Why the Portrait Starts Here**

Because the sūrah's opponents have been people of mockery (25:41), of descriptions (25:9), of loud almost-and-then-steadfastness to idols (25:42). The first mark of the Compassionate's servants is that they do not become that. They walk. They greet ignorance with peace. A religion that began this portrait with a creed-formula would have been accurate and less searching. A religion that begins it with gait and with a reply to insult is a religion that knows where *hawā* actually lives: in the stride and in the retort.

The Prophet ﷺ was asked for a definition of *righteousness* and pointed, in various reports, to good character. He said he was sent to complete good character (al-Muwattaʾ 8.8; Aḥmad). Verse 63 is that completion in two clauses. The Name *al-Raḥmān* produces, in a human body, a walk that does not bruise the earth and a word that does not bruise the ignorant more than truth requires.

**Peace as a Piece of the Garden Brought Forward**

*Salām* is the greeting of Paradise (10:10; 13:24; 14:23; 19:62; 33:44; 36:58; 56:26). The servants use it now, on the earth, when *jahl* speaks. They are practicing the climate they hope to die into. The ignorant, if they persist, will meet a Day of no *bushrā* (25:22). The servants refuse to make this day into that Day in advance by their own tongues. Saying *salām* is not naivety. It is eschatology of the mouth: I will not rehearse Hell's roar in a street argument.

In a culture of instant reply, verse 63 is almost violently practical. The ignorant address by comment, by slight, by a laugh at the messenger. The servant's first tool is not a better insult. It is peace, then — if explanation is due — the better *tafsīr* of verse 33, at a measured pace. The walk continues. The earth is not torn. The Name is not shamed by its servant's gait.

**The Servants of the Compassionate Walk Humbly**

*Wa-ʿibādu l-Raḥmāni lladhīna yamshūna ʿalā l-arḍi hawnan* — and the servants of the Compassionate are those who walk upon the earth humbly. The Name they refused to bow to in verse 60 is the Name that owns this portrait. *Hawnan* is gentleness of gait, absence of *marah* (17:37), a body that does not announce itself as a god. *Wa-idhā khāṭabahumu l-jāhilūna qālū salāmā* — and when the ignorant address them, they say “Peace.” Not the *salām* of greeting the righteous only: the *salām* of disengagement, of a word that does not feed the *jahl*. The Prophet ﷺ walked as if he were coming down a slope, and was more modest than a virgin in her chamber (al-Bukhārī 3562; Muslim 2320, in the descriptions). Verse 63 is that gait as the first mark of a people. Before night prayer, before the just mean, before the closed doors of 25:68: a walk and a word. The earth can tell. The ignorant can tell. The Compassionate has already told.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:64

> **and who pass the night before their Lord, prostrating and standing [in prayer],**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Night That Was a Garment, Used as a Miḥrāb**

Verse 47 made the night a garment. Verse 62 made night and day successive for whoever desires to remember or thank. Verse 64 shows the servants taking the garment off enough to stand: *wa-alladhīna yabītūna li-rabbihim sujjadan wa-qiyāmā* — "and who pass the night before their Lord, prostrating and standing." *Yabītūna* is to spend the night, to pass the *baytūtah*. They do not merely nod a prayer at the night’s edge. They *bayt* in a state of *sujūd* and *qiyām*. *Li-rabbihim* — for their Lord, before their Lord, toward their Lord. The night’s audience is not a reputation. No market sees this walk. The humble gait of verse 63 could still, in theory, be a social performance. The night-watch cannot. This is why the portrait places it second. The public peace and the private prostration authenticate each other.

**Prostrating and Standing: The Body’s Two Sentences**

*Sujjadan wa-qiyāmā* — prostrating and standing. The order, in some hearings, puts the more honorable posture first, or the more frequent inner state first: they are people of *sajdah*. Standing is the recitation, the length, the *tartīl* of verse 32. Prostrating is the nearest a servant comes to his Lord. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The closest a servant is to his Lord is while he is prostrating, so increase supplication."* (Muslim 482)

Together they are the prayer as a whole, especially the night prayer, *qiyām al-layl*, which the Prophet ﷺ kept until his feet swelled. The Qur'an had already legislated the extra of the night for him and recommended it for his community:

> *"And from [part of] the night, pray with it as extra for you; it may be that your Lord will raise you to a praised station."* (Qur'an 17:79)

> *"O you wrapped in the cloak, arise [to pray] the night, except a little."* (Qur'an 73:1–2)

The Prophet ﷺ rose most nights, to the point that some authorities considered a form of night prayer a prescribed act specific to him, while the ummah received it as the most beloved extra. Verse 64 is the ummah’s share of that rising, named as a mark of the Compassionate’s servants — not as a fatwā that every servant who misses tahajjud is expelled from the Name, but as a description of the type. They are people in whose nights God is an addressee.

**Why Night, When Day Was Named a Resurrection**

Because the night is when *hawā* wants the garment for itself: sleep, or sin, or the screen that is a third thing. Giving part of the night to *qiyām* is a dethroning of the inner god of verse 43. It is also when the great striving of verse 52 is quiet enough to hear the Book. The Qur'an is, in the Prophet’s ﷺ life, a night-companion as much as a day-argument. The servants who were told the Book was taken as *mahjūr* (25:30) are people who do not abandon it in the hours when abandonment is easiest. ʿĀʾishah described his night: he would stand until his feet cracked, and when she asked why, given that God had forgiven him, he said:

> *"Shall I not be a grateful servant?"* (al-Bukhārī 4836; Muslim 2820)

*Shukr*, which verse 62 named as a reason for night and day, is here a reason to stand. Gratitude is not a mood. It is a use of the garment. He ﷺ also said that the best night prayer after the obligatory is the prayer of Dāwūd: sleep half, pray a third, sleep a sixth (al-Bukhārī 1131; Muslim 1159), and that whoever recites the last two verses of al-Baqarah at night, they suffice him (al-Bukhārī 5009). The verse is a direction, not a single quantity. Begin. Stand. Go down. Let the night be, for a portion, *li-rabbihim* rather than for the self.

**A Community’s Hidden Architecture**

A town that contains such nights is a town with a hidden architecture. The markets of verses 7 and 20 may not know. The ignorant of verse 63 may not know. The Lord who is *Khabīr* of sins (25:58) is *Khabīr* of these prostrations too. The Blaze that sees from far off (25:12) is not the only seer. The servants spend the night being seen by the better Seer. The portrait will add fear, spending, and public morals. It begins the inner list with a body that already knows the floor. A spirituality that cannot find the floor at night will not find the just mean in the market. The two are one taqwā in two hours. Verse 64 is the hour the crowd does not applaud. That is why it is here, second, immediately after the walk that the crowd can see. What is seen is to be believed; what is unseen is to be more believed. The Compassionate’s servants are people of both.

**Sujūd and Qiyām as the Night’s Two Halves**

They spend the night *sujjadan wa-qiyāmā* — prostrating and standing. Not only one. Standing is the recitation, the long holding of the Book. Prostration is the face on the floor, the answer to verse 60’s *nufūr*. The people who increased in aversion when told to prostrate to the Compassionate are answered, two verses later, by people who prostrate to Him when no one is scoring it. The Prophet ﷺ said the nearest a servant is to his Lord is while he is prostrating, so increase *duʿāʾ* therein (Muslim 482). Verse 65 will put that *duʿāʾ* on their tongues: *Rabbanā iṣrif ʿannā*. The order is pedagogical. First the body learns the floor. Then the tongue asks for the Fire to be turned away. A *duʿāʾ* against Hell from a body that will not bow is a contradiction the sūrah does not entertain. Night prayer is therefore not a decoration on the portrait. It is the engine. Without it the walk of verse 63 becomes a daytime performance. With it the walk is what a bowed back does when it rises and goes to the market.

**Who Pass the Night for Their Lord, Prostrating and Standing**

*Wa-lladhīna yabītūna li-rabbihim sujjadan wa-qiyāmā* — and those who pass the night for their Lord prostrating and standing. *Li-rabbihim* — for their Lord, not for a record a neighbor can see. Standing is recitation; prostration is the face that answers verse 60’s *nufūr*. The Prophet ﷺ said the nearest a servant is to his Lord is while prostrating, so increase *duʿāʾ* therein (Muslim 482). Verse 65 will put that *duʿāʾ* on their tongues. First the floor, then the cry. A town that contains such nights has a hidden architecture. The Lord who is *Khabīr* of sins (25:58) is *Khabīr* of these prostrations. What the market can see (25:63) is to be believed; what it cannot see is to be more believed. The Compassionate’s servants are people of both.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:65

> **and who say, "Our Lord! Avert the punishment of Hell from us! Truly its punishment is inescapable.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Night Prayer Does Not Make Them Casual About the Fire**

The servants who stand and prostrate are not, in the next breath, people who feel finished. *Wa-alladhīna yaqūlūna rabbanā iṣrif ʿannā ʿadhāba jahannam* — "and who say, 'Our Lord! Avert the punishment of Hell from us!'" *Iṣrif* is turn away, avert, the same root as the *ṣarf* the idolaters could not accomplish in verse 19. They ask God to do, for them, the turning they cannot do for themselves. The people of verse 13 called for destruction as an exit. These people call, while they still have a night, for the punishment not to arrive. The sequence is a theology of the heart. Prayer, then fear. Not fear as a replacement for prayer, and not prayer as a replacement for fear. Al-Ghazālī named hope and fear the two wings. Verse 64 is a wing of love and gratitude (the night given back). Verse 65 is a wing of fear. The Fātiḥah placed mercy before the Master of the Day. This portrait places night-service before the cry about Hell. Both orders are true. A servant who only prays and never asks to be spared has not seen what verse 12 heard. A servant who only asks to be spared and never stands has not seen what verse 64 gave the night for.

**"Truly Its Punishment Is Gharām"**

*Inna ʿadhābahā kāna gharāmā* — "Truly its punishment is inescapable." *Gharām* is a clinging, a debt that will not leave, an infatuation that binds. Al-Zamakhsharī connects it to being so enamored that one cannot free oneself, and to a penalty, since a *gharīm* is a debtor. Al-Ṭabarī records the sense of a lasting evil. The servants do not describe Hell as a mood. They describe it as a thing that, if it takes, keeps. This is the knowledge that produces a serious *iṣrif*. If the Fire were a slap and then a release, the cry would be smaller. Because it is *gharām*, a clinging punishment, the people who know the Compassionate still beg to be kept out of it. There is no contradiction. *Al-Raḥmān* is the One whose mercy prevails:

> *"When God created the creation, He wrote in His Book, which is with Him above the Throne: 'My mercy prevails over My wrath.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7404; Muslim 2751)

and Hell is real, prepared (25:11), seeing (25:12), narrow (25:13). The servants believe all of this at once. That is why their speech in the night is not "we are safe because we stood," but "avert it from us." The Prophet ﷺ used to seek refuge from the Fire in the prayer itself:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of Hell, and I seek refuge in You from the punishment of the grave..."* (al-Bukhārī 832; Muslim 588)

Verse 65 is that command as a mark of a type. They are not too spiritual to mention *jahannam*. They are too honest not to.

**Our Lord: The Possessive of People Who Still Need**

*Rabbanā* — our Lord. The same address as the messenger’s *yā-rabbi* in verse 30. Servants do not, by serving, graduate from need. They become more precise about what to ask. The ignorant ask, in effect, for the laugh, the status, the treasure. These ask for a turning-away of *gharām*. The request is negative in form — avert from us — and that negativity is a wisdom. Sometimes the first mercy is not a palace (25:10) but a non-arrival of what was prepared for deniers. Ibn Kathīr notes that the most intelligent believers are those who remember death most and prepare best for what is after it. This cry is that intelligence in a sentence. It does not require a vision of the Fire. It requires belief in the housing report of verses 12–14 and 66.

**Fear That Does Not Freeze the Walk**

A person can hear *gharām* and become useless, or theatrical, or despairing, which is another form of denying mercy. The servants, in the next verses, will still spend, still marry, still pass idle talk with dignity. Their fear is a *taqwā* that keeps the hands on the wheel, not a panic that drives off the road. The cry "avert it" is itself an act of hope: they would not ask if the Compassionate did not avert. Verse 6 named Him Forgiving, Merciful, immediately after naming Him Knower of secrets. Verse 65 is that pair in a night-sentence. Living the verse is simple to state and hard to keep: after worship, do not inflate. Ask. Name Hell as clinging. Use the Name *Rabb* as a person uses a protector he actually believes in. Then, as verse 66 will add, name the Fire as a bad place to live — and go on walking humbly in the only earth that is still a place to walk. A religion that cannot say this sentence in the night has become a religion of self-esteem. A religion that can say only this sentence has become a religion of paralysis. The servants say it, and then they spend in a just mean. That is the portrait’s sanity.

**Gharām as the Fire That Stays**

*Inna ʿadhābahā kāna gharāmā* — its punishment is a clinging, an attachment, a debt that will not unstick. The word is kin to a creditor who does not leave, and to a love that ruins. Hell, in this naming, is not a passing heat. It is a companionship they did not want and cannot dismiss. The servants ask *iṣrif ʿannā* because they have believed the adjective. People who treat Hell as a metaphor, or as a brief scare for children, cannot pray this prayer except as theatre. The Prophet ﷺ sought refuge from the Fire in the last tashahhud, after almost every prayer (al-Bukhārī 832). Verse 65 is that ḥadīth as the night-speech of a whole class of people. They have just been on the floor (25:64). The first words up from the floor are not a request for a palace. They are a request for a non-arrival. That order of asking is a rank of intelligence. Ibn ʿAbbās and others: they pray this in their night worship. The portrait is not a list of daytime virtues only. It is a record of what a bowed body says when no market is listening.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:66

> **What an evil dwelling place and station!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Completes the Cry**

Verse 65 asked for the Fire to be averted and named its punishment *gharām*. Verse 66 is the evaluation that justifies the cry: *innahā sāʾat mustaqarran wa-muqāmā* — "What an evil dwelling place and station!" *Sāʾat* is "how evil it is," a verdict on a place. The servants are not discussing a metaphor and not performing severity. They are discussing real estate of the unseen, and they have found it unlivable. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both linger on the pair *mustaqarr* and *muqām*: a place to settle and a place to remain. The cry of verse 65 would be incomplete without this clause, because a punishment that was only a passing heat might be endured as a fever is endured. A dwelling that is evil as a dwelling — evil to enter, evil to stay — is what *gharām* named from another angle. Clinging evil. Unlivable remaining.

The vocabulary is deliberately paired with verse 24. There, the inhabitants of the Garden had *khayr mustaqarr* and *aḥsan maqīl* — the best dwelling, the most beautiful rest. Here Hell is *sāʾat mustaqarran wa-muqāmā*. Same kinds of noun. Opposite verdict. The sūrah wants the listener to choose a house the way a person who has seen a slum and a garden chooses, not the way a person chooses a mood. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the servants’ speech here is itself an act of *īmān*: they evaluate what they have not yet entered, on the report of the One who does not lie. That is the opposite of verse 40’s people, who passed a ruined town and hoped not for resurrection. These people have not passed Hell with their feet. They have passed it with the Book, and they have believed the housing report.

**Mustaqarr and Muqām: Settling Versus Remaining**

*Mustaqarr* is where one settles, the hold of a place, used in the Qur'an for the womb, for the earth, for a destination. *Muqām* is a standing, a residence, a stay, a station. Some commentators distinguish them as the first arrival and the long remaining, or as the inner of the Fire and a rank within it. The pair, without over-parsing, says: as a place to go, it is evil; as a place to stay, it is evil. There is no hour of Hell that becomes a home. Verse 13’s narrow place is this *mustaqarr* in another noun. Verse 14’s many destructions are this *muqām* in another verb. The servants have heard those verses — they pray at night with this Book (25:64) — and they have converted hearing into a real-estate judgment. That conversion is *ʿaql*, the binding of meaning verse 44 said most do not do.

> *"It is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

A heart that can still call a place evil that the culture around it has made a joke, or a metaphor, or a children’s threat, is a heart that has not gone blind. Verse 66 is that seeing, put on the tongue in the first person plural of people who still say *rabbanā*.

**Why the Portrait Includes a Sentence of Pure Evaluation**

Because some religious styles skip judgment of the Fire in order to seem gentle, and some skip everything else in order to seem severe. The servants do neither. They walk humbly, they pray, they evaluate. *Sāʾat* is a moral adjective on a place, which is a kind of courage. In a culture that treats every destination as a lifestyle, calling a destination evil is almost a forbidden act. The Qur'an requires it. Not all dwellings are valid. Not all stations are tastes. Ibn Taymiyyah’s insistence that love and hate for God’s sake are part of faith finds a simple home here: the servants hate a house God has named evil, and that hate is not a defect of mercy. It is mercy’s clarity. A physician who will not call a poison poison is not kind.

The Prophet ﷺ saw Paradise and Hell in prayer and in the eclipse prayer, and he came out having seen both. In the eclipse he said, after describing what he had been shown:

> *"I saw the Fire, and I have never seen a sight more terrible..."* (al-Bukhārī 1052; Muslim 901)

His religion is a religion of having seen, by revelation, that one house is *khayr* and one is *sāʾat*. Verse 66 is the ummah being taught to share the evaluation without having had the vision. We take his report. We say what the servants say. The saying is not a performance of fear. It is an alignment of the tongue with the real.

**Dwelling as a Choice That Is Made in Advance**

A *mustaqarr* in the next world is prepared by a *mustaqarr* of the heart in this one. People already dwell: in mockery, in *hawā*, in the remembrance, in the night prayer, in the just mean. Verse 66 trains the tongue to refuse one future dwelling so that the present dwelling can be refused when it is a rehearsal of it. If I am living in a station of clinging sin, I am already tasting a small *gharām*. The cry "what an evil dwelling" should then land on that station too, not only on a Fire I have not yet seen. This is the logic of *taqwā* as careful walking: you do not wait for the drop to call the drop a drop. You call it while the road still has a shoulder.

The servants will, in verse 76, hear the opposite evaluation of their own end: "What a beautiful dwelling place and station!" The two verses are mirrors. 25:66 is what they say about Hell. 25:76 is what God says about their Garden. They judge the evil house. He judges the beautiful one. Their share is to hate what He has named evil. His share is to house them where they could not house themselves. Between the two sentences a life of walking, praying, and spending is enough to make the second sentence, if He wills, theirs. The Prophet ﷺ used to seek refuge from the Fire in the prayer itself:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of Hell..."* (al-Bukhārī 832; Muslim 588)

Verse 66 is that refuge as a mark of character, not only as a formula. The servants do not consider themselves too advanced for the sentence. They consider the sentence too true to outgrow.

**What an Evil Station Teaches a Good Life**

It teaches proportion. A joke at the messenger’s expense (25:41), a friendship that pulls from the Reminder (25:28–29), a taking of caprice as god (25:43), a Qur'an taken as abandoned (25:30) — all of these can look, in a market, like small weather. Verse 66 puts a house at the end of that weather and names the house evil. The naming is supposed to make the weather less casual. It is also supposed to make repentance (25:70) urgent rather than decorative. A person who believes this verse and still delays a turning is a person who has heard a housing report and not yet packed. The servants pack. That is why, after this sentence, the portrait can move to spending, to the three fences, to *tawbah*. Evaluation, then conduct. Conduct, then a beautiful *mustaqarr*. The order is the order of a mind that has believed the Hour.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:67

> **and who, when they spend, are neither prodigal nor miserly—and between them is a just mean—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hand, After the Night and the Cry**

The portrait now leaves the mosque of the night and enters the ledger. *Wa-alladhīna idhā anfaqū lam yusrifū wa-lam yaqturū* — "and who, when they spend, are neither prodigal nor miserly." *Anfaqū* is spending, the making of wealth go out. The servants have wealth that goes out. They are not, as a type, a community of hermits without a coin. They spend. The question is how.

*Isrāf* is prodigality, waste, crossing the bound in giving or in using. *Iqtār* is tightness, miserliness, chaining the hand. The Qur'an had already drawn the same two ditches for the Prophet ﷺ himself:

> *"And let not your hand be shackled to your neck, nor stretch it forth entirely, lest you sit blameworthy, destitute."* (Qur'an 17:29)

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that any expenditure in disobedience is waste, and they cite the proverb: "There is no doing good in waste, and no waste in doing good" (*lā khayr fī al-isrāf wa-lā isrāf fī al-khayr*). Opinions on waste range from spending on what is haram to being excessive even in what is good. The just mean will later be a school of ethics; here it is a mark of a servant.

**Between Them a Qawām**

*Wa-kāna bayna dhālika qawāmā* — "and between them is a just mean." *Qawām* is a standing, a balance, a just measure, the state in which a thing is upright. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī cite the ethical tradition that a virtue lies between two vices, a good between two evils. The idea that the best of a thing is its middle is common in Islamic thought: the *ummah wasaṭ* of 2:143, the middle way of the Fātiḥah's straight path.

Spending is a perfect place to see this geometry. Give nothing, and you have taken wealth as a god (or taken fear of poverty as a god). Give without bound, and you have taken a gesture as a god, or taken praise, or taken your own impulse as legislator (25:43). The servants' hand is a *barzakh* (25:53) between two seas of error. Sweet generosity, salt restraint, a forbidden line that keeps both from flooding.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The upper hand is better than the lower hand."* (al-Bukhārī 1427; Muslim 1033)

And he forbade waste. And he praised the one who is content. And he left an estate so small that his armor was pledged (al-Bukhārī 2916). The Sunnah is itself a *qawām*: not the chained neck, not the emptied house as a theatre of zuhd that starves a family. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr was told to fast some days and not others, to pray some of the night and sleep some, because his family had a right (al-Bukhārī 1975). That ḥadīth is verse 67 in another file.

**Spending as a Test That the Poor and the Rich Both Sit**

The poor servant spends a little, and *isrāf* for him may be a showy gift that leaves his child hungry. The rich servant spends much, and *iqtār* for him may be a fortune that does not notice the neighbor. The *qawām* is relative to what God has measured (25:2) into a hand. This is why the verse says *idhā anfaqū*, when they spend — not "when they are wealthy." The mark is the manner of the outgoing, not the size of the chest.

Zakāt is a floor, not the *qawām*'s ceiling. Charity beyond the floor is asked, with the left hand not knowing the right (al-Bukhārī 1421). Waste in a wedding, waste in a plate, waste in a building for the ego, tightness with a relative, tightness with a scholar, tightness with the Book's own poor — all of these are the two ditches. The servants live in the road between.

**Why Money Enters the Portrait of the Compassionate's People**

Because *al-Raḥmān* is a Name of giving, and His servants, if they are His, give without becoming the giving, and withhold without becoming the withholding. A spirituality that cannot touch a coin is not this sūrah's. The messenger walked in markets. His servants' holiness includes a market-holiness: a just mean. In an age of consumer *isrāf* on one side and anxiety-hoarding on the other, verse 67 is as contemporary as a bank app. The app will not teach *qawām*. The night prayer of verse 64, if it is real, should. A person who has asked God to avert Hell will find it harder, if he is awake, to throw money into a vanity or to lock it against a right. The cry of verse 65 and the hand of verse 67 are one taqwā in two organs.

**The Upper Hand and the Family’s Right**

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The upper hand is better than the lower hand, and begin with those you support."* (al-Bukhārī 1427; Muslim 1033)

And he told ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, who wished to fast every day and pray the whole night, that his family had a right (al-Bukhārī 1975). Verse 67 is that ḥadīth as a mark of the type. *Qawām* includes the people who eat from the hand. A zuhd that starves a child is *isrāf* in the cloak of piety. A generosity that is a performance for a room and a tightness at home is *iqtār* dressed as *karam*. The servants’ just mean is tested at the table no guest sees.

**Two Ditches, One Road, Daily Accounting**

Every outgoing is a small judgment: is this waste, tightness, or *qawām*? The night prayer of verse 64, if it is real, should make that judgment easier, because a person who has stood before his Lord is less hungry for a room’s applause and less terrified of a future God has already measured. ʿUmar’s *muḥāsabah* — bring yourselves to account before you are brought to account — includes the ledger. Verse 67 is that accounting as character. The lofty abode (25:75) is for those who were patient. Patience in money is as heavy as patience in insult. The Compassionate’s servants are named by both.

**When They Spend, They Are Neither Wasteful nor Miserly**

*Wa-lladhīna idhā anfaqū lam yusrifū wa-lam yaqturū wa-kāna bayna dhālika qawāmā* — and those who, when they spend, are neither wasteful nor miserly, and there is, between that, a just stand. *Qawām* is a standing, a balance, a middle that can bear weight. The sūrah that forbade obeying disbelievers and named a great jihād also cares how a hand opens. Money is a *fitnah* (25:20) and a sign of the *mulk* that is God’s (25:2). Waste is a little lordship. Miserliness is a little atheism of provision. The Prophet ﷺ forbade both excess and tightness, and said the upper hand is better than the lower (al-Bukhārī 1427; Muslim 1033). Verse 67 is that ḥadīth as a mark of *ʿibād al-Raḥmān*. The just mean is not a spreadsheet only. It is a character that has stood at night (25:64) and therefore does not need to buy a self with waste or to hide from God with hoarding.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:68

> **and who call not upon another god along with God, and slay not the soul that God has made inviolable, save by right, and who fornicate not—for whosoever does that shall meet requital,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Refusals That Fence a Human Life**

The portrait, having described gait, speech, night, fear, and spending, now names three things the servants do *not* do: *wa-lā yadʿūna maʿa Allāhi ilāhan ākhara* — they do not call upon another god with God; *wa-lā yaqtulūna al-nafsa allatī ḥarrama Allāhu illā bil-ḥaqq* — they do not slay the soul God has made inviolable except by right; *wa-lā yaznūn* — and they do not fornicate. Tawḥīd, the sanctity of blood, the sanctity of the loins. A theology, a law of killing, a law of sex. The servants of the Compassionate are not only mild walkers. They are people whose mildness is fenced. The same three appear as a cluster in the counsel to Ibn Masʿūd, who asked which sin is greatest:

> *"That you set up a rival with God, while He created you." He said: Then which? He said: "That you kill your child for fear that he will eat with you." He said: Then which? He said: "That you commit adultery with your neighbor's wife."* (al-Bukhārī 4477; Muslim 86)

And the Qur'an had already joined them in the commandments of al-Isrāʾ (17:22–32) and in the bequest of 6:151. Verse 68 is that cluster as a mark of a people. A spirituality that keeps the walk and the night and loses these three has kept the costume and lost the body.

**No Other God: The First Fence Is the Sūrah’s Whole Argument**

Not calling upon another god with God is verses 2–3, 43, 55, 60, gathered into a habit. The servants have un-taken the statues, the *hawā*, the partisan backing of Satan. *Duʿāʾ*, calling, is worship. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Duʿāʾ is worship."* (al-Tirmidhī 2969; Abū Dāwūd 1479)

Where a person calls in the last instance is where his god is. The servants’ last instance is God, without a partner in *mulk*. This is not a small interior. It is the whole of tawḥīd as a daily refusal to transfer last hope, last fear, and last appeal. Verse 57 offered a way unto the Lord as the only wage. Verse 68 says the servants do not call that way to a second address.

**The Soul Made Inviolable, Except by Right**

*Al-nafs allatī ḥarrama Allāh* — the soul God has made *ḥarām*, inviolable, sacred, set off. The sanctity is God’s making, not a social contract. *Illā bil-ḥaqq* — except by right: the right of a just qiṣāṣ, a judicial killing that the law, not *hawā*, has named. The prohibition is in 4:29, 4:92–93, 5:32, 6:151, 17:33. The servants are not a people of private blood. In a time of Jāhiliyyah’s raids and of every later rage that picks up a weapon without a right, this clause is the Compassionate’s mark on a hand that also spends in a just mean. The same hand. The Prophet ﷺ said, in his Farewell Sermon:

> *"Your blood, your property, and your honor are sacred to you, like the sacredness of this day of yours, in this month of yours, in this city of yours."* (al-Bukhārī 1739; Muslim 1679)

Verse 68 is that sermon in the portrait. *Illā bil-ḥaqq* is not a hole for revenge. It is a recognition that God has named some takings of life as justice, and that those takings are not the servant’s to invent.

**They Do Not Commit Zinā**

*Wa-lā yaznūn* — and they fornicate not. *Zinā* is sex outside the *ṣihr* God made from water (25:54). It is the mixing of two seas without the *barzakh*. The servants, who will ask for comfort in spouses (25:74), refuse the counterfeit of that comfort. The Qur'an calls *zinā* a grave indecency and an evil way (17:32). The portrait is not embarrassed to put sex next to tawḥīd and murder. A life is one. The loins, the blood, and the altar are three doors *hawā* loves to enter. The servants have shut them. *Athām*, requital, is the cost of opening them. Some said *athām* is a valley in Hell, as some said *wayl* is a valley. The idiomatic meaning is enough: he shall meet the cost. The three refusals are not a style. They are a fence over a drop. Verse 69 will multiply the punishment. Verse 70 will open a door the size of repentance. The portrait is honest about both the drop and the door.

A community that keeps these three — tawḥīd, inviolable blood, inviolable sex — is a community that can afford to walk humbly and say *salām*. A community that loses them will find that humility and peace become decorations on a violence. The Compassionate’s servants are defined by what they will not call upon, will not kill, and will not lie with. That is not a small spirituality. It is the minimum of a human world in which a Criterion can be lived.

**The Three Doors Hawā Loves**

Tawḥīd, blood, and sex are named together because *hawā* (25:43) loves all three doors. A god other than God is the first door. A life taken without right is the second. A bed taken without covenant is the third. The servants close them. The Qur'an in 6:151 and 17:31–32 gives the same cluster. Furqān is not a new law here. It is the law as the character of people who serve the Compassionate. *Yulqa athāmā* — he shall meet requital. The meeting of verse 75 is *taḥiyyah* and *salām*. The meeting of verse 68 is *athām*. Two meetings, two lives. Verse 69 will multiply. Verse 70 will replace. The portrait refuses to lie about either. A preacher who skips 68 to reach 70 produces cheap hope. A preacher who skips 70 to remain in 68 produces despair. The Compassionate’s servants are people who fear 68 enough to stop, and believe 70 enough to return if they have not stopped in time.

**They Call Not upon Another God, Nor Kill, Nor Fornicate**

*Wa-lladhīna lā yadʿūna maʿa Llāhi ilāhan ākhara wa-lā yaqtulūna l-nafsa llatī ḥarrama Llāhu illā bil-ḥaqqi wa-lā yaznūn* — and those who call not upon another god with God, and slay not the soul God has made sacred except by right, and fornicate not. Three doors *hawā* loves. The cluster is the same as 6:151 and 17:31–32. Furqān here is not a new law. It is the law as the character of the Compassionate’s people. *Wa-man yafʿal dhālika yalqa athāmā* — whoever does that shall meet requital. Two meetings in the portrait: *athām* here, *taḥiyyah* and *salām* in verse 75. Verse 69 will multiply. Verse 70 will replace. A preacher who skips 68 to reach 70 sells cheap hope. A preacher who skips 70 sells despair. The servants close the three doors, and they believe the door of *tawbah* is still a door.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:69

> **and the punishment shall be multiplied for him on the Day of Resurrection. He shall abide therein, humiliated,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Multiplication, Abiding, Humiliation: Three Weights on One Cluster**

Verse 68 ended with meeting *athām*. Verse 69 unpacks the meeting: *yuḍāʿaf lahu al-ʿadhābu yawma al-qiyāmati wa-yakhlud fīhi muhānā* — "and the punishment shall be multiplied for him on the Day of Resurrection. He shall abide therein, humiliated." Three verbs' worth of consequence: doubled or multiplied, remaining, disgraced. The verse is still inside the portrait of the servants, as the shadow by which their refusals are understood. They do not call upon another god, do not kill without right, do not fornicate — *because* whoever does that meets this. Al-Rāzī reads the multiplication as the compound of disobedience and rejection of faith, or as unremitting intensity. Ibn Kathīr leans toward intensity without relief. The doubling can also be for those who do evil and lead others to it — the *fulān* of verse 28 as well as the one who took him. A sin that is also a recruitment is not a single file.

> *"For them the punishment will be doubled, for they were unable to hear and they did not see."* (Qur'an 11:20)

> *"O wives of the Prophet, whoever of you commits a clear indecency, the punishment will be doubled for her, and that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 33:30)

The second verse shows that multiplication is a known Divine grammar: capacity and rank increase the file. The first shows that inability to hear — the cattle of 25:44, the deafness of 25:73’s opposite — is itself part of what is doubled. Verse 69 sits in that grammar. The three crimes of verse 68 are not private stumbles only. They unmake tawḥīd, blood, and lineage, which are the three pillars of a human world. Multiplication is proportion, not temper.

**Abiding: Khulūd as the Opposite of the Garden’s Khuld**

*Yakhlud fīhi* — he shall abide therein. The same root as *jannatu al-khuld* (25:15). Abiding is not a monopoly of bliss. Remaining is the structure of both houses. The difference is the house. The people who wanted annihilation (25:13–14) receive, if they die on the three crimes without the repentance of verse 70, a remaining they did not want, in a *mustaqarr* they themselves would have called evil if they had been among the servants who say verse 66. Al-Qurṭubī and the theologians of *waʿīd* (the threat) debate whether every killer and every fornicator among the people of tawḥīd abides forever, or whether *khulūd* here is a long remaining that God’s *illā* in the next verse can still interrupt. The debate is real. The verse’s face, however, is the threat attached to “whoever does that.” The threat is meant to be heavy enough to fence a life. It is not meant to close the door that verse 70 will open with *illā*. A commentary that softens verse 69 to protect verse 70 has not understood how medicine works. A commentary that uses verse 69 to nail verse 70 shut has not understood the Compassionate.

> *"They will not be finished so as to die, nor will its torment be lightened for them. Thus do We recompense every disbeliever."* (Qur'an 35:36)

That is *khulūd* from the inside of denial. Verse 70 is the exit offered while the body still breathes. Both sentences are the same Lord.

**Muhān: Humiliation as a Pain Distinct from Heat**

*Muhānā* — humiliated, made low, stripped of dignity. The servants walk *hawnan* (25:63), a humility they chose. The criminal of verse 69 is *muhān*, a humiliation he did not choose. The two humilities are not cousins. One is a servant’s gait. The other is a face gathered to Hell (25:34). Pride, which verse 21 named as the inner of the demand for angels, ends as *hawān*, disgrace. The Qur'an is consistent: whoever exalts himself against the truth is put down by it.

> *"Those who oppose God and His Messenger — they will be among the most humbled."* (Qur'an 58:20)

The Fire is not only heat. It is a social death, a stripping of the face that mocked (25:41). For a culture of honor like Quraysh, *muhānā* may have been as loud a word as flame. For a culture of image, it still is. Ibn Kathīr, at the gathering on the faces, connects posture and disgrace: the organ of arrogance becomes the organ of travel toward *jahannam*. Verse 69 names the inner of that posture: *hawān*. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"No one will enter Paradise in whose heart is an atom's weight of arrogance... Arrogance is rejecting the truth and looking down on people."* (Muslim 91)

Rejecting the truth (shirk, denial) and looking down on people (the poor Companion, the hungry messenger, the slain soul, the used body) is exactly the psychology this cluster punishes. Humiliation in the next world is not an arbitrary extra. It is arrogance, inverted.

**Why the Portrait Pauses on Terror Before the Exception**

Because a door of repentance that is opened too early, before the drop is seen, becomes a shrug. Verse 69 makes the drop visible: multiplied, abiding, humiliated. Verse 70 will then say *illā*. The order is the order of a physician who shows the disease before the medicine, not to torment, but so that the medicine is taken as medicine. The servants who cry "avert from us the punishment, it is *gharām*" (25:65) are people who have believed verse 69. Their cry is this verse converted into *duʿāʾ*. Al-Ghazālī’s two wings — hope and fear — require this wing to have weight. Hope without this verse is the hope of verse 40’s people who hope not for resurrection, dressed in religious language. Fear without verse 70 is despair, which is another form of denial of *al-Raḥmān*.

For the living who have already touched one of the three fences — a shirk of the inner kind, a hand in unjust harm, a zinā — verse 69 is not the last word they are allowed to hear. They are commanded to hear it, fully, and then to walk into verse 70 without delay. Delay is how *athām* is met. Immediate repentance is how multiplication is, by the Compassionate’s own promise, replaced. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The one who repents from sin is like the one who has no sin."* (Ibn Mājah 4250)

That likeness is verse 70’s work. Verse 69 is why the likeness is a mercy and not a loophole. A loophole would not need a turning, a faith, and a righteous deed. A mercy names the drop and then names the bridge.

**Living Between the Threat and the Door**

The practical stance is not to dramatize Hell in others and not to dismiss it in oneself. It is to keep the three fences as fences, not as tastes; to treat a slip toward any of them as a fire-drill, not as a lifestyle; and to refuse the two cheap readings — "I am safe because I say *lā ilāha illā Allāh* while the fences are down," and "I am finished because I have touched a fence." Both cheap readings ignore the grammar of these two verses. 69 is true. 70 is true. The servants live inside both, which is why they still walk humbly after they have prayed, and still ask to be spared after they have walked.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:70

> **save for those who repent and believe and perform righteous deeds. For them, God will replace their evil deeds with good deeds, and God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Is a Second Sending of Mercy**

*Illā man tāba wa-āmana wa-ʿamila ʿamalan ṣāliḥan* — "save for those who repent and believe and perform righteous deeds." After multiplied punishment, abiding, humiliation: *illā*. The sūrah that opened with a blessed sending-down of a Criterion now sends down, inside the portrait of the servants, a door as wide as a life that still has breath. Al-Ṭabarī records that some idolaters were inclined to Islam but felt they could never escape previous sins of the kind verse 68 named, and wanted to know if there were expiation; this verse was revealed.

The three conditions are three. *Tāba* — he turned, the literal sense of *tawbah*. *Āmana* — he believed, he entrusted himself. *ʿAmila ʿamalan ṣāliḥan* — he did a righteous deed, a deed that is *ṣāliḥ*, fit, repairing. Turning without faith is a mood. Faith without deeds is a claim. Deeds without turning can be the dust of verse 23. The three together are a new taking of a way (25:27, 25:57).

**God Will Replace Their Evil Deeds with Good Deeds**

*Fa-ulāʾika yubaddilu Allāhu sayyiʾātihim ḥasanāt* — "For them, God will replace their evil deeds with good deeds." *Yubaddilu* is to substitute, to exchange. Al-Ṭabarī: not that evil becomes good in its essence, but that through repentance one's evil deeds are wiped and a good is recorded, or that they will be transformed in the Hereafter. Al-Rāzī: punishment replaced by reward; or, after repentance, God leads from idolatry to faith, from adultery to wholesome marriage, from bad actions to good ones in this life, and then the reward of those deeds is given.

All these readings are inside a mercy that is almost difficult to believe, which is why the verse must say it. The Prophet ﷺ rebuked Abū Hurayrah, in the account al-Ṭabarī records, when Abū Hurayrah told a woman who had committed zinā and killed her child that she had no repentance; the Prophet ﷺ asked whether he knew these verses, and Abū Hurayrah went to find the woman with the news. The story, whether every isnād of it is at the highest grade or not, is the verse's own spirit: do not close a door God has named open.

> *"Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of God. Indeed, God forgives all sins."* (Qur'an 39:53)

> *"Except for those who repent, believe, and do righteous work. For those, God will change their evil deeds into good deeds."* (Qur'an 25:70)

The replacement is God's act, not a psychological rebranding. *Allāh yubaddilu*. The servant turns, believes, works. The exchange is Divine.

**Forgiving, Merciful: The Names of Verse 6 at the Door**

*Wa-kāna Allāhu ghafūran raḥīmā* — the same two Names that closed the reply to the calumny of verses 4–5. The sūrah has not changed Lords. The Knower of secrets who was Forgiving, Merciful to people who called the Book a lie is Forgiving, Merciful to people who called on another god, killed, and fornicated, if they turn. Some theologians, as the source notes, discussed whether 4:93 (the willful killer of a believer) abrogates this verse; al-Ṭabarī and others preserve the debate, and the mainstream of mercy is that no sin is unforgivable in an absolute sense if repentance is sincere, with shirk requiring a return to tawḥīd. The verse's own voice is not the voice of a closed file.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The one who repents from sin is like the one who has no sin."* (Ibn Mājah 4250)

And God says, in the ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O son of Adam, were your sins to reach the clouds of the sky and then you asked Me for forgiveness, I would forgive you."* (al-Tirmidhī 3540)

Verse 70 is that ḥadīth as Qur'an, with the extra of *tabdīl*: not only erasure, but replacement. A person who had a file of *sayyiʾāt* may find, if the three conditions are met, a file of *ḥasanāt* in its place. That is more than pardon. It is a rewriting.

**What Replacement Asks of a Community**

That it not tattoo people with their worst day. The woman who came, the idolater who feared he had too much blood on his hands, the fornicator who thought he was finished — they are the audience God chose to address with *yubaddilu*. A community of the Compassionate's servants, if it is real, is a community that believes in this verb. It fences the three crimes (25:68). It does not fence the criminal out of *tawbah*. The just mean in spending (25:67) has a cousin in the just mean in judgment of persons: neither the *isrāf* of making sin a style, nor the *iqtār* of making repentance a rumor.

For the person whose own night is full of verse 68's ghosts, the work is named: turn, believe, do a fit deed — today, not when the self feels worthy. Worthiness is what God replaces *into*. It is not the ticket in.

**Replacement as a Divine Act, Not a Rebranding**

Al-Ṭabarī records that some idolaters were inclined to Islam but felt they could never escape previous sins of the kind verse 68 named; this verse was revealed. The three conditions are three. Turning without faith is a mood. Faith without deeds is a claim. Deeds without turning can be the dust of verse 23. *Yubaddilu* is God’s act:

> *"Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of God. Indeed, God forgives all sins."* (Qur'an 39:53)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"O son of Adam, were your sins to reach the clouds of the sky and then you asked Me for forgiveness, I would forgive you."* (al-Tirmidhī 3540, ḥadīth qudsī)

Verse 70 is that ḥadīth as Qur'an, with the extra of *tabdīl*: not only erasure, but replacement. A community of the Compassionate’s servants, if it is real, is a community that believes in this verb. It fences the three crimes. It does not fence the criminal out of *tawbah*. Some theologians discussed whether 4:93 abrogates this verse; the verse’s own voice is not the voice of a closed file. The Names that close it are the Names of verse 6: Forgiving, Merciful.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:71

> **And whosoever has repented and works righteousness does indeed repent to God with true repentance.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Defines the Turning It Has Just Offered**

Verse 70 opened the door: repent, believe, work righteousness, and God replaces evil deeds with good. Verse 71 names what a real walking-through looks like: *wa-man tāba wa-ʿamila ṣāliḥan fa-innahu yatūbu ilā Allāhi matābā* — "And whosoever has repented and works righteousness does indeed repent to God with true repentance." Al-Rāzī: the first *tāba* can be turning from idolatry, the second *yatūbu* turning toward God. The verse can also be read as a present and future: whoever repents and does good, he is repenting to God a [real] repentance. The cognate accusative *matābā* is the same intensive music as *tadmīrā* and *tartīlā*: he turns a turning, thoroughly. Nothing in the sūrah’s style is accidental. When God wants a demolition complete He says *tadmīrā*. When He wants a recitation measured He says *tartīlā*. When He wants a repentance that is not a mood He says *matābā*.

The definition is protective. A *tawbah* that is a tear without a next deed is not the *matāb* of this verse. A deed without a turning of the chest toward God can be the scattered dust of verse 23. The servants’ repentance is *ilā Allāh*, to God, not to a public, not to a self-image, not to a *fulān*. Direction again. The whole sūrah has been about what one takes and toward whom. Al-Ṭabarī, at 2:37, already taught that Adam’s receiving of words and God’s turning to him are a two-sided act. Verse 71 is that two-sidedness as a definition of human turning: you have not repented *to God* until the work follows, and the work is not this verse’s work until it is aimed at Him.

**True Repentance as a Path, Not a Moment**

The English "true repentance" is an attempt at *matābā*. The Arabic is a noun of a completed, intensive turning. Later writers on *tawbah* — Ibn al-Qayyim in *Madārij al-Sālikīn* most famously — listed conditions that this verse already contains in two verbs: leaving the sin, regret, resolve not to return, and, if a right of a person was taken, restoration; then *ʿamila ṣāliḥan*, a fit deed. The work is the evidence that the turning had a body. The turning is the evidence that the work has a face. Ibn Taymiyyah’s definition of *ʿibādah* as everything God loves of inward and outward sayings and deeds is the atmosphere of this verse. *Tawbah* is not a third thing beside worship. It is worship’s repair.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Regret is repentance."* (Ibn Mājah 4252; Aḥmad)

Regret is the inner of *tāba*. Verse 71 adds the outer: *ʿamila ṣāliḥan*. Together they are *matāb*. The wrongdoer’s regret in verse 27 is regret without a next deed on a Day when deeds are closed. The servant’s regret in this world is regret with a next prayer, a next restitution, a next night of *sujūd*. Same tear. Different tense. That is why this verse is good news that still has a spine. It will not let a person call a feeling a finished *tawbah* and return to the three fences as if the *illā* of verse 70 were a subscription.

> *"God loves those who turn much, and He loves those who purify themselves."* (Qur'an 2:222)

> *"Then Adam received words from his Lord, and He turned toward him. Indeed, He is the Oft-Turning, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 2:37)

God’s turning (*tawbah* as a Divine act) meets the servant’s turning. Verse 71’s *yatūbu ilā Allāh* is the servant’s side of a two-sided Name: He is *al-Tawwāb*, the One who turns, again and again. The intensive pattern of *tawwāb* matches *matābā*. He turns much. The servant is asked to turn a turning that is real. The love in 2:222 is attached to those who do this much, not once as a ceremony.

**To God: The Preposition That Keeps Tawbah from Becoming Self-Help**

*Ilā Allāh* — to God. Not "to a better version of me." The replacement of evil with good (25:70) is His act. The turning is toward Him. A culture that treats repentance as a brand of self-optimization will miss the preposition. The servants are not becoming their own project. They are returning a property — the self — to its Owner. Verse 2’s *mulk* includes this return. Verse 43’s inner god is dethroned by it. Al-Rāzī’s distinction — turning from, then turning to — is the map of a chest: you can leave a sin for fear of scandal and never arrive at God. That leaving may still spare a family. It is not yet *matāb ilā Allāh*. The Criterion is not satisfied with damage control. It wants a face.

On this the Prophet ﷺ taught a practical sequence in many reports of *istighfār*: a saying of *astaghfiru Allāh*, a *ṣalāh* of repentance, a restoration of a right. Abū Bakr reported that he ﷺ said:

> *"There is no one who commits a sin, then purifies himself, then prays two rakʿahs, then asks God for forgiveness, but that God will forgive him."* (Abū Dāwūd 1521; al-Tirmidhī 406, graded ḥasan)

Two rakʿahs are *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* in miniature. Forgiveness is God’s. The verse’s definition is satisfied in that small complete act, and then the life has to keep satisfying it. *Matāb* is not a certificate. It is a direction that remains a direction.

**How to Know If the Repentance Was Matāb**

Not by a feeling of cleanliness that may be a trick of the self or of the forsaker of verse 29. By the *ṣāliḥ* that follows: a prayer that was dead and now lives, a debt paid, a gaze lowered, a mockery left, a Qur'an that is no longer *mahjūr*, a walk that has less stomp, a friendship with a *fulān* ended or repaired toward the Reminder. The verse does not require perfection after the turning. It requires direction and work. The servants still, in verse 65, ask to be spared Hell. They are not people who think *matāb* made them fireproof. They are people whose turning is real enough to keep asking.

For a reader who has used verse 70 as a comfort without verse 71 as a task, this verse is a kindness. It will not let comfort become a new *hajr* of the Book. Repent, and work. Then you have repented to God a repentance. The sentence is almost circular, and the circle is the point: the only *tawbah* that counts as *tawbah* is the one that has both the turn and the work, and both are toward Him. In the portrait, this verse is the hinge between the three fences (68–69) and the tongue (72). A community that believes in replacement (70) without definition (71) will either despair or become casual. The servants do neither. They turn a turning, and then they pass idle talk with dignity, because a mouth that has just come back to God is not in a hurry to go back to *zūr*.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:72

> **As for those who do not bear false witness, and who, when they pass by some idle talk, pass by with dignity,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Tongue and the Ear, After the Great Sins and After Tawbah**

The portrait, having fenced altar, blood, and loins, and having opened the door of *tawbah*, returns to speech: *wa-alladhīna lā yashhadūna al-zūr* — "As for those who do not bear false witness." *Zūr* is the same word as verse 4’s calumny, the decorated lie. Ibn Kathīr: to testify to a lie. Some, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note, take it as "to witness what is false," which would include idolatry, lewd talk, and lascivious song — not looking at or listening to such false things by choice. Both the courtroom and the room of entertainment sit inside the word. The servants’ testimony is not *zūr*, and their presence is not a witnessing of *zūr* as a pastime. False witness is a destroyer of cities. The Prophet ﷺ said, when asked about the great sins, "associating others with God, and disobedience to parents," then he sat up and said:

> *"and false speech, and false witness," and he kept repeating it until the companions wished he would be silent.* (al-Bukhārī 2654; Muslim 87)

He would not be silent because *zūr* is how *ẓulm* gets a stamp. Verse 4’s people produced a wrongdoing and a *zūr* against the messenger. Verse 72’s people refuse to be that production. A community of night-prayers that still signs lies in a council has not yet become *ʿibād al-Raḥmān* in the tongue.

**Idle Talk: Laghw as a Climate, Not Only as a Sentence**

*Wa-idhā marrū bil-laghwi marrū kirāmā* — "and who, when they pass by some idle talk, pass by with dignity." *Laghw* is vain speech, wasted sound, the talk that has no weight and often a negative weight. Praise for turning from *laghw* is also in:

> *"Those who turn away from idle talk."* (Qur'an 23:3)

> *"And when they hear idle talk, they turn away from it and say, 'For us our deeds and for you your deeds. Peace be upon you. We do not seek the ignorant.'"* (Qur'an 28:55)

The servants do not, in this clause, sit and reform every circle. They *pass*. *Marrū* — they go by. The verb is a motion. Dignity, here, includes the dignity of not being detained. *Kirām* is nobility, generosity, high-mindedness, a refusal to be cheapened. Al-Rāzī’s range of *zūr* as including lewd song makes this clause a cousin: they do not make the idle the climate of their sitting. In a culture of *laghw* as industry — feeds, mockery as content, the messenger as a joke (25:41) — passing *kirāmā* is a full-time ethics. It is not snobbery. Snobbery stays to sneer. *Karam* leaves without taking the stain or adding to the noise.

**Passing, Not Performing**

There is a false piety that enters every idle circle in order to be seen leaving it. Verse 72 is quieter. When they pass — the situation is already a passing, a coming-upon. They do not make the *laghw* their destination. If they must be there, their passing is *kirām*: no laugh that signs the lie, no testimony that oils it. The servants who said *salām* to the ignorant (25:63) are the same people. Peace to the *jāhil*, dignity past the *laghw*. Two techniques for two rooms. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

Silence is a way of passing *kirāmā*. Good speech is the other way. *Zūr* is neither. He ﷺ also said:

> *"Woe to the one who speaks and lies to make people laugh — woe to him, woe to him."* (Abū Dāwūd 4990; al-Tirmidhī 2315)

That woe is verse 28’s *waylatā* in a comic key. The servants will not buy a room’s laugh with a lie. Their *karam* costs them that laugh. The Garden’s *salām* (25:75) will not.

**Why This Comes After Repentance**

Because a person newly turned (25:70–71) is often still in rooms of *laghw*. The verse does not demand a hermitage. It demands a passing. The new servant may still hear the old jokes about the Book. His mark is that he does not bear them as witness, does not sign them with a laugh, does not stay to be made smaller. *Karam* is a social *barzakh* (25:53): you may be adjacent to the idle as the sweet sea is adjacent to the salt, without becoming the salt. In practice this is one of the most daily verses in the portrait. False witness may be rare in a court and common in a character-assassination over tea. Idle talk is the air of many workplaces. The servants’ difference is not that they never encounter either. It is that they do not *yashhadūna* the first and do not camp in the second. They have a night prayer to get to (25:64), a just spending to do (25:67), a family to ask for (25:74). *Laghw* is a thief of those. Passing it with dignity is how the thief is refused without a sermon at every table.

A community that keeps this verse will sound different. Not grim. Not empty of lawful humor — the Prophet ﷺ joked without lying. Different in what it will not sign, and in what it will not sit in until the sitting becomes a second nature. *Zūr* and *laghw* are how a Criterion becomes *mahjūr* (25:30) without anyone announcing the abandonment. The servants announce nothing. They pass. The passing is the announcement.

**They Do Not Bear False Witness, and If They Pass by Idle Talk They Pass with Dignity**

*Wa-lladhīna lā yashhadūna l-zūr* — and those who do not bear false witness / do not attend falsehood. The verb covers the court and the gathering. *Zūr* is the bent word, the idol-festival, the lie dressed as testimony. *Wa-idhā marrū bil-laghwi marrū kirāmā* — and when they pass by idle talk, they pass with dignity. Two motions: they do not go to *zūr*; if *laghw* is in the road, they pass. They do not camp. *Karam* is the same root as the noble recording angel, as the generous. Here it is a gait. The walk of verse 63 (*hawnan*) and the passing of verse 72 (*kirāmā*) are one body in two streets. The Prophet ﷺ said a sign of a person’s Islam is his leaving what does not concern him (al-Tirmidhī 2317). Verse 72 is that ḥadīth as a mark of the Compassionate’s servants. A community of such passers will have fewer scandals to repent of in verse 70, because they were not in the room where the scandal was being composed.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:73

> **and who, when they are reminded of the signs of their Lord, fall not deaf and blind against them,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Reminder That Meets Open Organs**

*Wa-alladhīna idhā dhukkirū bi-āyāti rabbihim lam yakhirrū ʿalayhā ṣumman wa-ʿumyānā* — "and who, when they are reminded of the signs of their Lord, fall not deaf and blind against them." *Dhukkirū* — they are reminded, the passive of the *tadhakkur* verses 50 and 62 asked for. The signs are *āyāt*: verses of the Book, and signs in the shade, the rain, the two seas, the ruined town. When these are brought back to the servants’ notice, they do not *yakhirrū ʿalayhā* — they do not collapse upon them, fall over them — *ṣumman wa-ʿumyānā*, deaf and blind. *Kharra* is to fall down. The Qur'an uses it of those who fall in prostration when the recitation reaches them. Here the falling is a falling *deaf and blind*, a slump of inattention, a theatrical or actual deadness. The servants do not do that. They do not use the reminder as a pillow.

Spiritual deafness and blindness are a refrain of this Book:

> *"Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is hearts within breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

> *"Deaf, dumb, and blind — so they will not return."* (Qur'an 2:18)

> *"They have hearts with which they do not understand, eyes with which they do not see, ears with which they do not hear. They are like cattle. Rather, they are further astray."* (Qur'an 7:179)

Verse 44 of this sūrah called most of the deniers cattle, further astray. Verse 73 is the opposite population: when reminded, they are not deaf and blind. They are the *man arāda an yadhdhakkara* of verse 62, in the act. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the danger named here is not first hearing. It is later numbness. A person can walk humbly, pray at night, and still, over years, begin to hear the Qur'an as *laghw*’s cousin: sound without binding. Verse 73 is a fence against that inner *hajr*.

**Against Them: ʿAlayhā as a Hostile Slump**

The preposition *ʿalayhā* can be heard as "upon them" or "against them." Falling deaf and blind *against* the signs is a resistance, not only an absence. Some people use a recitation as a background that they actively keep meaningless — a familiar sound that must not be allowed to mean. The servants refuse that use. The Book that was taken as *mahjūr* (25:30) is, in their case, a Book that is allowed to interrupt. The Prophet ﷺ, when a verse of mercy was recited, would ask; when a verse of punishment, would seek refuge; when a verse of *tasbīḥ*, would glorify. Ḥudhayfah described that night prayer:

> *"He prayed... when he passed a verse of tasbīḥ he glorified, when he passed a verse of asking he asked, when he passed a verse of refuge he sought refuge."* (Muslim 772)

That is the opposite of falling deaf. Each *āyah* gets an organ. Verse 73 makes that responsiveness a mark of the type, equal in the list to night prayer and the just mean.
