A scholar who cannot be reminded, a worshipper who cannot be warned, an activist who cannot hear a verse against his *hawā*, has fallen *ṣumman wa-ʿumyānā* no matter how humble his walk looks.

**Signs of Their Lord: The Possessive of People Who Already Know**

*Āyāti rabbihim* — the signs of their Lord. The Lord is already theirs; the signs are a reminder, not a first introduction. This is why the verse is in the portrait of servants, not in the address to the mockers. The mockers’ problem was refusal of a first hearing. The servants’ danger is the second deafness, the deafness of familiarity. Ibn ʿAjībah and the spiritual commentators treat this as the disease of the *ʿārif* who has stopped being addressed. The plain sense is enough: a Muslim household in which the Qur'an is always on, and never in. The cure is the one the verse implies: when reminded, respond as if the sign were new. The shade is still spreading (25:45). The rain is still distributed (25:50). The verse of Hell is still *gharām* (25:65). The verse of replacement is still *yubaddilu* (25:70). Falling not deaf is a choice to let one of these actually land.

**A Community That Can Still Be Reminded**

The most dangerous community is the one that has many reciters and many deafnesses. Verse 73 makes openness to reminder a mark of the Compassionate’s people. The servants are people whose *āyāt* still have rights over their faces: the face does not go blank. It listens, or it weeps, or it changes a deed. That is *ʿaql*. That is the opposite of cattle. That is how a Criterion remains a criterion in a life that has already believed. Practically: when a verse is recited in prayer, do not ride it as sound. When a poor reminder comes from a spouse or a child or a stranger, do not fall deaf because the source is not impressive. When a sign in weather or in a ruin or in a morning *nushūr* presents itself, do not file it under furniture. The ignorant address, and the servants say *salām* (25:63). The Lord reminds, and the servants do not say nothing. They hear. Hearing is the first act of the warner’s audience that still has a chance. These people have decided to remain that audience until they die.

**Not Deaf, Not Blind, When Reminded**

*Idhā dhukkirū bi-āyāti rabbihim lam yaqirrū ʿalayhā ṣumman wa-ʿumyānā* — when they are reminded of the signs of their Lord, they do not fall upon them deaf and blind. *Lam yaqirrū* is a refusal to settle in the bad posture. They may have been inattentive; they do not take inattention as a home. Al-Ṭabarī: they do not persist in turning away. Contrast verse 44’s cattle, verse 60’s *nufūr*, verse 72’s *zūr*. The servants have open senses for the *āyāt*. The Prophet ﷺ wept at recitation, and commanded beautifying the Qur'an with the voices (al-Bukhārī 5049). Verse 73 is not a demand for tears on cue. It is a demand that a reminder be allowed to arrive. A believer who has heard Sūrah al-Furqān fifty times and can no longer be moved by verse 27’s *yā-laytanī* has begun to *yaqirr* in a deafness. The cure is the verse’s own verb: be reminded, and do not settle.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:74

> **and who say, "Our Lord! Grant us comfort in our spouses and our progeny, and make us imams for the reverent,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Prayer That Refuses to Be Only Private**

The servants who have walked, prayed, feared, spent, fenced the great sins, repented, guarded the tongue, and kept the signs open, now ask for a house and for a rank: *wa-alladhīna yaqūlūna rabbanā hab lanā min azwājinā wa-dhurriyyātinā qurrata aʿyunin wa-ijʿalnā lil-muttaqīna imāmā* — "and who say, 'Our Lord! Grant us comfort in our spouses and our progeny, and make us imams for the reverent.'" *Hab*, grant, is the verb of a gift, not a wage. They ask as people who still know that *nasab* and *ṣihr* (25:54) are His making. The portrait does not end in a hermitage. It ends in a household and in a hope of being useful to the reverent. That is the Compassionate’s servants: private enough to pass the night on a floor, public enough to want an imamate of taqwā, domestic enough to want cool eyes at home.

**Coolness of the Eyes: Qurrat Aʿyun as a Spiritual Joy**

*Qurrat aʿyun* is literally the coolness of the eyes, the opposite of a hot tear, the rest of a gaze that has found what it was looking for. Al-Rāzī insists that this comfort is of a spiritual nature, not mere worldly enjoyment of children and spouses, regarding which the Qur'an also warns (64:14). They pray that their families will join them in worship and obedience. The coolness is perfected when they join their families in the Garden. Al-Ṭabarī: they experience joy in seeing them worship God and perform acts of obedience. The prayer is therefore not "make my house easy." It is "make my house a place my eyes can rest because You are worshipped in it." A spouse who is a trial (25:20) can still be a spouse; the servant asks for the other possibility: a spouse who is *qurrah*. Children who are *fitnah* in 8:28 and 64:15 are, in this prayer, asked to be the opposite — a test that is a gift.

> *"Our Lord, grant us from our spouses and our offspring comfort of the eyes, and make us imams for the reverent."* (Qur'an 25:74)

The Prophet ﷺ used to pray for his family, and he said:

> *"The best of you is the best to his family, and I am the best of you to my family."* (al-Tirmidhī 3895)

Verse 74 is that Sunnah as a mark of the type. The night prayer of verse 64 does not cancel the household. It is asked to overflow into it. A spirituality that cannot pray this prayer has become a spirituality of the isolated self, which is another *hawā*.

**Make Us Imams for the Reverent**

*Wa-ijʿalnā lil-muttaqīna imāmā* — "and make us imams for the reverent." Al-Rāzī: they desire to achieve a level of devotion that will become a standard for others; some understand *imām* as "proof" or "example." He cites Abraham:

> *"And make for me faithful renown among later generations."* (Qur'an 26:84)

And he notes that some consider a desire to be exalted in matters of religion to be a positive thing, since such leadership comes through true knowledge and righteous action. This is not the *mulk* of verse 8’s treasure. It is not the face that walks exultantly (17:37). It is a request to be a *sabīl* that others can take, the way the wrongdoer wished he had taken a way with the messenger (25:27). The reverent, *al-muttaqūn*, are the people for whom the Garden of everlastingness was promised (25:15). To be an imam for them is to be a leader in the thing that saves, not in the thing that inflates. ʿUmar is reported to have understood that he had been answered when he asked to be an imam for the *muttaqīn*, because God would not make a man an imam for the reverent unless He had given him to be among them. Whether the attribution is exact, the logic is sound. You cannot lead the reverent from outside reverence.

**Ambition, Purified**

Verse 74 is a purification of ambition. The Quraysh wanted palaces as proof. The servants want to be examples in taqwā. The first ambition eats the earth. The second, if granted, cools eyes and guides walkers. A person who is afraid to ask for leadership in good may be hiding a laziness, not a humility. A person who asks for it without the walk, the night, the just mean, and the open ear to signs, is asking for a costume. The portrait has already described the costume’s absence. This prayer is the last inner motion of people who have the substance. Practically, the verse is a daily *duʿāʾ* that can be taken as written. Ask for coolness in the people you married and the people born from you — coolness of faith, not only of ease. Ask to be, in some small ring — a house, a class, a shop — an imam of the reverent: the one whose way can be taken without regret. Then accept that the granting is *ijʿalnā*, make us. He makes. The servant asks and walks. The coolness, if it comes, is a mercy from the Name this whole portrait has been serving.

**Coolness of Eyes, Not Coldness of Houses**

*Qurrata aʿyun* is a phrase of tears of joy, of a sight that cools. They do not ask first for wealth of children, nor for children who will be a *fitnah* as in 8:28 and 64:15. They ask for a coolness that is, in the commentators, righteousness seen in spouses and offspring. Ibn ʿAbbās and others: make them people of obedience. The request is therefore a request that the two webs of verse 54 become paths to verse 75, not paths to verse 27. *Wa-jʿalnā lil-muttaqīna imāmā* — and make us an imam for the reverent. Al-Ṭabarī prefers the reading that they ask to be leaders in good, followed in taqwā. Some grammarians read *imām* as a plural-sense noun. Either way the ambition is purified: not Pharaoh’s *imām* of 28:41, who call to the Fire, but an imam whose way, if taken, will not produce a *yā-laytanī*. A house that makes this *duʿāʾ* after the night of 25:64 is a house that has understood what children are for.

**Our Lord, Give Us from Our Spouses and Offspring Coolness of Eyes**

*Wa-lladhīna yaqūlūna rabbanā hab lanā min azwājinā wa-dhurriyyātinā qurrata aʿyunin wa-jʿalnā lil-muttaqīna imāmā* — and those who say: Our Lord, grant us from our spouses and offspring coolness of eyes, and make us an imam for the reverent. Coolness is righteousness seen, not only ease. The two webs of verse 54 are asked to become paths to verse 75, not to verse 27. *Imāmā* is leadership in taqwā, not Pharaoh’s *imām* of 28:41 who call to the Fire. Ambition is purified: the Quraysh wanted palaces; the servants want to be a *sabīl* that will not produce *yā-laytanī*. ʿUmar understood that God would not make a man an imam for the reverent unless He had given him to be among them. Ask, then walk. He makes (*ijʿalnā*). The servant does not appoint himself.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:75

> **it is they who shall be rewarded with the lofty abode for having been patient, and they will be met therein with salutations and [greetings of] peace,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait’s Wage: A Room Above**

After the long description — walk, peace, night, fear, spending, fences, repentance, tongue, hearing, family, imamate — the sūrah names what they receive: *ulāʾika yujzawna al-ghurfata bi-mā ṣabarū* — "it is they who shall be rewarded with the lofty abode for having been patient." *Ghurfah* is the upper chamber, the high room of a dwelling, and here, as al-Rāzī notes, the Garden itself, the highest of it. Other verses use the same noun:

> *"And those who believe and do righteous deeds — We shall surely house them in lofty chambers of Paradise beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein. How excellent is the reward of the workers."* (Qur'an 29:58)

The people who did not ask for palaces as a proof in this world (25:8–10) are given an upper room as a recompense. The *jazāʾ* of verse 15 returns. Reward, and — as the ḥadīth of "none enters by his deeds alone" already taught — reward as mercy:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds alone." They said: Not even you, O Messenger of God? He said: "Not even I, unless God covers me with His mercy."* (Muslim 207; al-Bukhārī 5673)

Patience is the quality of the people the promise was for. It is not a lever that purchases God.

**For Having Been Patient: Ṣabr as the Name of the Whole Portrait**

*Bi-mā ṣabarū* — because of what they endured, for having been patient. The entire list is here gathered under one noun. Walking humbly when mockery is easy is *ṣabr*. Night prayer when the garment wants sleep is *ṣabr*. Fearing Hell without despairing is *ṣabr*. The just mean when *isrāf* and *iqtār* are both loud is *ṣabr*. Not killing, not fornicating, not bearing *zūr* is *ṣabr*. Passing *laghw* is *ṣabr*. Remaining able to be reminded is *ṣabr*. Asking for a cool household and an imamate of taqwā, then working without owning the grant, is *ṣabr*. Verse 20 asked, "will you be patient?" Verse 75 answers, about these people: they were. The wrong people in verse 42 were "patient" upon their gods. The word is not magic. The object of patience is the magic. These were patient upon the Criterion, upon the way with the messenger, upon the Name *al-Raḥmān* that others refused to bow to. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Patience is light."* (Muslim 223)

And:

> *"Whoever remains patient, God will grant him patience."* (al-Bukhārī 1469)

The lofty abode is patience made into architecture.

**They Will Be Met with Taḥiyyah and Salām**

*Wa-yulaqqawna fīhā taḥiyyatan wa-salāmā* — "and they will be met therein with salutations and greetings of peace." *Yulaqqawna* is they will be received, encountered, met as a guest is met. The house is not empty. It has a greeting. *Taḥiyyah* is a salutation of life, a "may you live." *Salām* is the peace they used to say to the ignorant (25:63), now said to them as a climate.

> *"Their greeting therein will be: Peace."* (Qur'an 10:10)

> *"Peace be upon you. Enter the Garden for what you used to do."* (Qur'an 16:32)

The angels at death already begin this meeting for the righteous. Verse 75 is that meeting as the furniture of the *ghurfah*. The people who refused to make the earth a roar of *jahl* are received in a room that speaks their language. Peace was not a tactic. It was a mother-tongue. The Garden turns out to have been speaking it all along.

**Patience Is Not the Price That Purchases God**

*Bi-mā ṣabarū* can be misheard as wages. The sūrah has already, in verse 16, called the Garden a binding promise, and in verse 70, called replacement of evil a Divine act. The servants did not walk humbly in order to force an upper room. They walked because they were *ʿibād al-Raḥmān*. The upper room is what such walking is worth in the mercy of the One who promised. Living toward verse 75 is therefore not a grim accumulation of patience-points. It is remaining in the portrait because the portrait is true, and letting the *ghurfah* be His business. The meeting with *salām* will not embarrass anyone who practiced *salām*. That is a reason to practice it that does not require a calculation. You are rehearsing the greeting of your own possible house.

**The Upper Room as Architecture of a Character**

*Ghurfah* is height. The servants did not climb by trampling. They walked low on the earth and are housed high. That inversion is the sūrah’s moral physics. Those who wanted to be gathered with faces lifted (the arrogance answered in 25:34) are lowered. Those who lowered their gait are raised. *Yulaqqawna* — they are met, received, encountered. They do not storm the room. The room comes to them as speech: *taḥiyyatan wa-salāmā*. Greeting and peace. The two words they used in markets when the ignorant addressed them. God is not bored of their vocabulary. He returns it as the weather of their house. Ibn Kathīr and others mention that the angels greet them, and that God greets them. Both readings honor the same truth: peace is not their invention. It was always His, loaned to their tongues, then given back as a home. Living toward this verse is to make *salām* so habitual that a room of *salām* would not feel like a foreign language.

**Those Shall Be Requited with the Upper Room Because They Were Patient**

*Ulāʾika yujzawna l-ghurfata bi-mā ṣabarū wa-yulaqqawna fīhā taḥiyyatan wa-salāmā* — those shall be requited with the upper room because they were patient, and shall be met therein with greeting and peace. *Bi-mā ṣabarū* is patience as the inner of the whole portrait: patience in the walk, the night, the just mean, the closed doors, the passing of *laghw*, the open ear. It is not a wage that forces God. It is the character to which He promised a house. *Yulaqqawna* — they are met. They do not storm. The language of their market (*salām*, 25:63) becomes the weather of their room. Ibn Kathīr: angels greet them; and God greets them. Peace was never their invention. They borrowed it and are given it back as a home. Practice *salām* now. You are rehearsing the greeting of a possible house.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:76

> **abiding therein. What a beautiful dwelling place and station!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mirror of Verse 66, Spoken Over the Other House**

*Khālidīna fīhā ḥasunat mustaqarran wa-muqāmā* — "abiding therein. What a beautiful dwelling place and station!" The evaluation that the servants themselves spoke over Hell — *sāʾat mustaqarran wa-muqāmā* (25:66) — is now spoken, in the Divine voice or in the voice of completed truth, over their Garden. Same two nouns. Opposite adjective. The sūrah’s architecture wants this rhyme heard with the same care a reciter gives to a pair of rhymed verses at the end of a sūrah, because the pair is not decoration. It is the whole moral world reduced to housing. Two houses, two verdicts, one human life spent walking toward one of them. Al-Rāzī notes that *ghurfah* in the previous verse already named the upper chamber of the Garden; verse 76 then names the quality of remaining there and the beauty of remaining there, so that no one imagines the lofty abode as a hotel from which one is later moved. *Ḥasunat* is a completed verdict. The place has been seen, as it were, and found fair.

*Khālidīna fīhā* — abiding therein. The *khuld* of verse 15 and the *khālidīn* of verse 16 are now attached to the people of the portrait, not only to the Garden as a promised estate. Remaining, which was a terror in verse 69 (*yakhlud fīhi muhānā*), is a rest here. The grammar of remaining does not change. The remaining’s where changes, and the remaining’s company changes: greetings of peace (25:75), whatsoever they will (25:16), a Lord who is the Compassionate and who has not handed the kingdom to anyone else (25:26). Ibn Kathīr, treating similar closings, insists that *khulūd* in Paradise is not a philosophical puzzle about infinite duration so much as a pastoral announcement: the fear that spoils every earthly garden — that the afternoon will end — is here formally retired.

**Beautiful: Why the Qur'an Is Not Shy of Ḥusn**

Verse 24 promised the best dwelling and the most beautiful rest. Verse 76 confirms with *ḥasunat*. Beauty is not a pagan extra the Qur'an is embarrassed to name. The Garden is not only safe. It is fair to the eye and to the inner eye. The servants who passed *laghw* with *karam* (25:72) and walked *hawnan* (25:63) are people who already preferred a certain beauty in this world: the beauty of a clean word, a just hand, a night that belongs to God. The *mustaqarr* that is *ḥasan* is that preference, completed. Al-Qurṭubī, at verses of this kind, notes that the Qur'an’s eschatology is never only a court. It is also a home. A religion that can only threaten has not read this verse. A religion that can only decorate has not read verse 66. The two verses together are the two eyes of the Hereafter.

The Prophet ﷺ reported the ḥadīth qudsī that every later description of Paradise is only a footnote to:

> *"I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and what has not occurred to the heart of a human being."* (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824)

And he ﷺ gave the remaining its spoken form:

> *"It will be said: 'O people of Paradise, you will have health and not be sick; you will live and not die; you will be young and not grow old; you will be in bliss and not be miserable.'"* (Muslim 2837)

Verse 76 is that preparation and that announcement as a two-word housing report: beautiful dwelling, beautiful station. The rest is beyond the verse’s job. The verse’s job is to make the choice of house a choice of beauty against the evil of 25:66, not only a choice of safety against pain. A person who wants only not to burn has not yet wanted what the servants wanted. They wanted a *mustaqarr* that could be loved.

**Station: Muqām as Rank That Does Not Require Stomping**

*Muqām* is a place of standing, a residence, a rank. The Quraysh wanted a *muqām* of treasure and a messenger who did not walk in markets. The servants are given a *muqām* that is beautiful because they did walk, and walked humbly. Rank, in the end, is not abolished. It is transfigured. The imamate they asked for among the reverent (25:74) is answered with a station that does not humiliate anyone else — a room of *salām*, not of *muhān*. Ibn Taymiyyah’s principle that the most honorable name of the prophets is *ʿabd* is here given its eschatological counterpart: the highest station is still a servant’s station, now housed. No one in that *muqām* needs to tear the earth or reach the mountains (17:37). The earth has already been left behind as a trial; the mountains of pride have already been walked off.

> *"And those who believe and do righteous deeds — We shall surely house them in lofty chambers of Paradise beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein. How excellent is the reward of the workers — those who were patient and who trust in their Lord."* (Qur'an 29:58–59)

The echo of *ṣabr* and of lofty housing is the echo of 25:75–76. Patience was the name of the whole portrait. Beauty is the name of its end.

**Abiding as the End of the Fear of Loss**

Every beautiful *mustaqarr* in this world is haunted by verse 46: We withdraw it unto Ourselves, a gentle withdrawal. The Garden’s beauty is named together with *khālidīn* so that the haunting ends. No gentle withdrawal of this shade. No night that is a brother of death, except as a completed mercy. The sleep that was *subāt* (25:47) is replaced by a living that does not die. The servants’ cry to avert Hell (25:65) is answered not only with non-Hell but with a house that can be loved without the love being a future grief. This is why the Qur'an can speak of the Garden’s fruits as never cut off and never forbidden (56:32–33), and of its companions as having no fear upon them nor shall they grieve. Fear of loss is the tax of time. *Khulūd* is the lifting of that tax.

A person still in the withdrawing world can use verse 76 as a standard of homesickness. Not a hatred of the earth — the servants walk on it humbly, they marry, they spend. A ranking: this room is a trust; that room is the beautiful station. When a present beauty is taken back *ilaynā* (25:46), the taking is not the end of beauty. It is a reminder that the *ḥasunat mustaqarran* has not yet been entered, and that patience (25:75) is still the work of the day. The Prophet ﷺ taught his companions to ask for *al-Firdaws*, because it is the middle of Paradise and the highest of it, and above it is the Throne (al-Bukhārī 2790). Asking for the beautiful station is not greed. It is the *duʿāʾ* without which, verse 77 will say, a human being has no weight. Verse 76 is what that *duʿāʾ*, if accepted, looks like from the inside.

**How a Life Rehearses a Beautiful Station**

The rehearsal is not interior decoration. It is the portrait itself. Walk so that the earth is not punished by your gait. Answer *jahl* with *salām* so that your mouth already knows the greeting of 25:75. Give the night a portion *li-rabbika*. Spend between the two ditches. Keep the three fences. Turn when you fail. Pass *laghw*. Keep the signs from falling on deafness. Ask for a cool house and a good example. That cluster is already a *muqām* in this world, a station that, if God accepts it, is housed in the next. Verse 76 does not add a new duty. It adds a verdict on the duties already named: they were not a grim tax. They were the building of a beautiful remaining. Whoever finds them heavy may hear this verse as the reason they were worth their weight.

---

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:77

> **Say, "What weight would my Lord give you, were it not for your supplication? But you have denied, and it will be inevitable."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Question: What Would You Weigh Without a Call?**

The sūrah that began with a blessing upon the sending of a Criterion ends with a command to speak, *qul*, and a question that is almost a freeze of the human being: *mā yaʿbaʾu bikum rabbī lawlā duʿāʾukum* — "What weight would my Lord give you, were it not for your supplication?" *Mā yaʿbaʾu* is an idiom of regarding, caring, attaching weight. Declaratively, it is what one says of a thing one considers of no consequence. The question asks: of what consequence would you be, if it were not for your *duʿāʾ*?

Al-Rāzī: a human being's purpose is to worship God, in keeping with:

> *"I did not create jinn and mankind save to worship Me."* (Qur'an 51:56)

*Duʿāʾ* here is understood as worship, prayer, gratitude, or, as Ibn Kathīr notes, faith itself. The call — the supplication, the worship, the reaching toward God — is the thing that makes a water-creature (25:54) more than a moving shade (25:45–46). Without it, the Lord — who does not need the worlds — would have nothing in the person to regard as the purpose for which he was measured.

This is not the statement that God needs our prayer. It is the statement that we, without prayer, do not rise to the rank of what He made us for. Cattle eat and drink (25:44, 25:49) and are not blamed. Humans who only eat and drink, without *duʿāʾ*, have refused the extra that made them *bashar* under a Criterion.

**Were It Not for Your Call: Lawlā as the Thin Line**

*Lawlā duʿāʾukum* — were it not for your supplication. The line between weight and weightlessness is a calling. The servants of the Compassionate have been, for fifteen verses, a people of *duʿāʾ*: avert Hell; grant coolness in our families; make us imams. The disbelievers have been a people of other speech: fables, mockery, "what is the Compassionate?", "is this the one God sent?" Verse 77 says that the first kind of speech is the human's weight. The second is a denial that has a consequence.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Duʿāʾ is worship."* (al-Tirmidhī 2969; Abū Dāwūd 1479)

And:

> *"Nothing is more honorable before God than duʿāʾ."* (al-Tirmidhī 3370; Ibn Mājah)

And God says:

> *"Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell humbled."* (Qur'an 40:60)

Verse 77 and 40:60 are one doctrine. Pride that will not call is the inner of *nufūr* (25:60). Calling is the inner of being a servant of the Compassionate.

**"But You Have Denied, and It Will Be Inevitable"**

*Fa-qad kadhdhabtum fa-sawfa yakūnu lizāmā* — "But you have denied, and it will be inevitable." The *you* is the deniers who have filled the sūrah: deniers of the Hour (25:11), of the messenger's humanity as sufficient, of *al-Raḥmān*, of the Book as anything but *mahjūr*. *Kadhdhabtum* — you have given the lie. Then *lizām*, a thing that adheres, that is binding, inevitable. Al-Ṭabarī records readings that *it* is death, or battle, or Badr (where the Quraysh's denial met a worldly *lizām*), or the punishment of the Hereafter. Al-Rāzī leans to the last. Ibn Kathīr notes the reading: you have denied, and that denial will cling to you as punishment.

*Lizām* is kin to *gharām* (25:65), the clinging punishment. What they refused to attach to — a *duʿāʾ* that would have given them weight — is replaced by a denial that will attach to them. You will be clung to by what you chose. The servants chose a call, and peace will meet them (25:75). The deniers chose a lie, and *lizām* will meet them.

**The Sūrah's Last Word as a First Practice**

A commentary on seventy-seven verses could end in a summary. The Qur'an ends in a practice: say this. Tell them that without a call they do not weigh, and that denial adheres. The reader who has not denied, or who has denied and then taken the *illā* of verse 70, is being told his weight's location: *duʿāʾ*. Not a résumé of knowledge about al-Furqān. A calling.

So the last application is the simplest, and it gathers the sūrah. Call upon the One who sent the Criterion upon His servant. Call as the night-praying call. Call for the Fire to be averted. Call for a cool house and a good example. Call as a person who asks no wage and takes a way. Do not call upon what cannot benefit or harm. Do not let *hawā* be the one you call. Do not take the Qur'an as abandoned. Walk humbly when you have finished calling. The Lord who would give you no weight without your supplication is the Lord who, with your supplication, has already given you a Book, a messenger who ate food, a shade, a night, a rain, a door that replaces evil with good, and a Name — *al-Raḥmān* — that a people once refused to bow to, and that His servants never finished walking under.

If denial has been the day's speech, verse 70 is still in the same chapter. Turn, believe, work. The *lizām* is for a denial that remains. The weight is for a call that begins.

**Duʿāʾ as the Human’s Weight**

Al-Rāzī: a human being’s purpose is to worship God, in keeping with:

> *"I did not create jinn and mankind save to worship Me."* (Qur'an 51:56)

*Duʿāʾ* here is worship, prayer, gratitude, or, as Ibn Kathīr notes, faith itself. The call is the thing that makes a water-creature more than a moving shade. Without it, the Lord — who does not need the worlds — would have nothing in the person to regard as the purpose for which he was measured. This is not the statement that God needs our prayer. It is the statement that we, without prayer, do not rise to the rank of what He made us for. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Duʿāʾ is worship."* (al-Tirmidhī 2969)

> *"Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell humbled."* (Qur'an 40:60)

Verse 77 and 40:60 are one doctrine. Pride that will not call is the inner of *nufūr* (25:60). Calling is the inner of being a servant of the Compassionate. *Lizām* is kin to *gharām*: what they refused to attach to is replaced by a denial that will attach to them. The servants chose a call, and peace will meet them. The deniers chose a lie, and *lizām* will meet them. The last application is the simplest, and it gathers the sūrah. Call upon the One who sent the Criterion. Do not call upon what cannot benefit or harm. Walk humbly when you have finished calling.

---
